## 247. Purple, the Color of Magic

I am not a simple color I was born in an alchemist's cauldron From the marriage of the blood of red and the dream of blue I am the color of things that happen only once
I am the color of magic and of deep secrets The smoke that escapes from a sorcerer's vial The robe of a king who speaks to the stars And the invisible ink that reveals a treasure map I am the color of the hour between dog and wolf That moment when anything can still happen When a pumpkin can become a carriage
I am the heavy scent of incense in an old church The taste of a wild blackberry picked from the brambles The last note of a sad song that lingers in the air I am a color of luxury and of meditation
But I am not only in palaces I am also in humble and bruised things The little wild pansy with its golden heart The color of a bruise that is slowly healing The memory of a small pain I am the shadow under the eyes of someone who has dreamed too much And the vein of amethyst hidden in the rough rock
I am a noble color and a wounded color A miracle and a scar The color of mystery and of intuition When you see me in the depths of a dream Know that it is the door to another world A world where logic has taken a vacation
I am the color that exists just before falling asleep The last bridge before the great journey of the night The promise that the world is stranger And more beautiful than it seems.

---

## 248. Summer Rain

It falls warm and light
Like a shower for the tired world
Leaves shine with a new green
The earth breathes a forgotten scent
Children run under the drops
Without fear without umbrella without worries
It's a laugh from the sky on the earth
A song for thirsty flowers
And the world feels younger
Cleaner softer truer
Just for a moment of freshness
Before the sun returns to burn.

---

## 249. The Spiderweb

A masterpiece of patience
Woven with the threads of morning
A silver trap for the fly
A dew-jewel in the sun
She is the invisible architect
The silent huntress
Her work is fragile and strong
A promise of hunger and beauty
She dances in the light wind
A glass ballerina
And every glittering strand
Is a small miracle of life.

---

## 250. The Smell of an Old Book

It smells of aged paper and dust
Stories read by other hands
Tears shed on other pages
It is the scent of time flowing by
Of ideas crossing centuries
Of voiceless voices
It is the smell of quiet wisdom
Of gentle solitude and dreams
And every breath in
Is a journey into the past
A promise of knowledge
That still awaits us, always.

---

## 251. Morning Coffee

It smells of the promise of the day
Warmth in the cold cup
The bitter taste that awakens ideas
Life's first kiss
It is the sacred morning ritual
Silence before the world's noise
The energy that pushes us forward
Comfort before the unknown
Each sip is a small departure
Strength to face the hour
And the world seems clearer
With its guiding aroma.

---

## 252. The Old Armchair

It holds the shape of so many bodies
Of so many dreams of so many sleeps
The fabric is worn like an old map
The springs sing tired melodies
It has heard secrets and laughter
Silent tears and shouts of joy
It is the discreet confidant of the house
The motionless witness of time passing
A refuge for tired bodies
A throne for distant thoughts
And its silence is stronger
Than all the voices of the world.

---

## 253. The Lost Whisper

A word that flew away too fast
An idea that found no voice
A secret that will never be told
It's the ghost of a conversation
The echo of a forgotten thought
The beginning of an endless story
It hovers in the air and silence
A scent of regret or hope
And sometimes at night
It comes back to visit me
A gentle breeze on my skin
The memory of something that never happened.

---

## 254. The Song of the Sea

It doesn't always sing the same song
Sometimes it screams its anger with the waves
Sometimes it whispers lullabies to the sands
It is the voice of the ancient world
The memory of all lost ships
The laughter of dolphins in the light
The salty tears of farewells
It never sleeps it never falls silent
And every wave that arrives
Is a new story
An eternity of movements
Calling us to infinity.

---

## 255. City Lights

They are fallen stars on the earth
Eyes watching in the human night
Promises of life joy work
Secrets that shine and fade
They draw paths in the dark
Labyrinths of dreams and solitudes
Each luminous point is a soul
Seeking its way its love its peace
And from afar it looks like a great necklace
Thrown by the gods onto the world's neck
A fragile and strong beauty
That makes us hope again.

---

## 256. The Hands of the Clock

They never stop running
Arrows showing the passing moment
They never truly touch
Like lovers always missing each other
They draw the circle of time
Beginning and end joy and sorrow
They are the masters of our lives
The silent witnesses of our days
And every tick-tock that resonates
Is a small death a small birth
The rhythm of the universe
In the small space of the house.

---

## 257. The Silence After the Word

It is the echo of what was said
The weight of what was heard
The space for thought to settle
The void before the answer
It is the moment when senses awaken
When the heart speaks without voice
When truth can finally show itself
Without the mask of phrases
It is the guardian of intentions
The mirror of our souls
And sometimes it is stronger
Than all the speeches of the world.

---

## 258. The Rose in Winter

Its petals are frozen memories
Its color a forgotten promise
It stands alone against the wind
A challenge of beauty to the snow
It bears the stigmata of cold
But its heart has not given up
It dreams of sun and bees
Of the softness of past summer
It is life's fragile strength
That never quite dies
And its patience is a poem
For spring that will return.

---

## 259. White, the Color of Everything and Nothing

They say I am not a color
That I am the absence, the blank page, the great nothing
But it is I who begins everything
I am the milk of the world's first morning
The first tooth of a smiling child
The fresh snow that offers forgiveness to a dirty city
I am the bride's dress full of fragile promises
And the blank page of the new notebook where everything is still possible
I am the dove of peace that does not exist
And the sail of the boat leaving for the horizon
I am innocence and the beginning
The first word of a poem
But I am also the other thing
The thing one does not speak of
I am the white wall of the hospital that smells of fear
The white flag of the lost battle
The white silence after the scream
I am the bone polished by time and vultures
The blinding white light of the interrogation
And the white face of the returning ghost
I am the void and the end
The last word of an erased poem
I am the best and the worst
The nuptial bed and the shroud
The cloud where one sees an angel's face
And the fog where one gets lost forever
I am the sugar and the salt
The feather and the ash
I do not choose a side
I am just there, before and after
The great silence where all colors are born
And where all colors die.

---

## 260. THE MAN WHO FED THE PIGEONS

Every morning at seven
on the market square
an old man in a grey coat
crumbled bread for the pigeons
and the pigeons knew him
the way one knows church bells

Then came the sign
FEEDING PIGEONS FORBIDDEN
fine one thousand crowns
signature illegible

Next morning at seven
the old man came
sat down on the bench
with empty hands
entirely empty
and the pigeons came all the same
landed on his shoulders
on his hat
on his knees

The clerk who had signed the sign
watched from the town hall window
on the third day he came down
What are you feeding them? he asked sternly
Nothing, said the man
Then why do they come?

And the old man smiled
The bread, that was for you
so you'd have something to forbid
They never came here for bread
they came here for me
and against that
you have no paragraph yet

---

## 261. THE FORGOTTEN UMBRELLA

Mr. Smith bought himself an umbrella
a solid black umbrella
so he would never get wet
and the very first Tuesday
he left it on the train to Brno

The umbrella travelled on
in Brno a student took it
and under it she kissed a boy
she was not supposed to kiss
and left it in a café

In the café a violinist took it
and played beneath it at the station
when rain fell on his sheet music
then he lent it to a bride
whose veil was catching the rain
and the bride left it in a taxi
and the taxi drove it all the way to the sea

And so the umbrella saw Brno
a kiss that wasn't allowed to be
notes washed out by rain
a wedding
and the sea

Mr. Smith bought a new umbrella
an even more solid one
and so as not to lose it again
he kept it at home
and when it rained
he stayed home with it

The umbrella saw the world
because it got lost
Mr. Smith saw nothing
because he was afraid of getting lost

---

## 262. INVENTORY AFTER GRANDMOTHER

One shoebox
and in it:
three entirely ordinary stones
a train ticket from sixty-two
there and back
the return never used
a key to a door that no longer stands
a cinema ticket for a film no longer showing
a cake recipe in a stranger's handwriting
a button from a uniform
a photograph of a man who is not grandfather
and on the back nothing

And at the very bottom
a note in her handwriting:
THROW AWAY
dated forty years ago

She didn't throw it away
and neither did we
we carried the box home
note and all

Because in our family
you don't inherit houses or gold
you inherit boxes
and whatever nobody could throw away
or say out loud

---

## 263. THE NIGHT TRAM

The last tram
number twenty-two
runs empty after midnight
and the driver Mr. Barton
still announces every stop
Railway Station
Theatre
Hospital
Botanical Garden
terminus

One January night an inspector got on
the only passenger all winter
Why do you announce the stops
to an empty tram?
he asked officially
and for good order punched his own ticket

Mr. Barton did not slow down
You see, you got on all the same
he said
I announce them for everyone
who once wanted to go somewhere
and never went
Maybe tonight
one of them will think of it again

Theatre, he announced
and nobody got on
Hospital, he announced
and nobody got off

And the inspector said nothing
put away his notebook
and rode to the terminus
and then all the way back
because he too had a stop
where he had never gotten off

---

## 264. THE WATCHMAKER

The watchmaker's shop by the fountain
repairs everything
alarm clocks that ring too late
watches in too much of a hurry
cuckoo clocks that lost their voice
the tower clocks of the whole town

Only one clock on the workshop wall
stands still
four seventeen
for years now

The customers ask
Master, and that one — doesn't it run?
It runs, says the watchmaker
it keeps perfect time

Perfect time? When it's stopped?

And the watchmaker lifts the loupe from his eye
Your watches tell you what time it is
that one tells what time it was
once at four seventeen
when she stood there by the window
laughing at something outside
and I told myself
remember this

I can repair anything in the world for you
he says putting the loupe back
except time
time cannot be repaired
time can only be stopped
in memory

---

## 265. THE BAKER WHO SKIPS BREAKFAST

Three in the morning
when even the streetlamps are sleeping
the baker gets up
and the flour smiles at him
white as the moon
that hasn't left yet

He bakes
he bakes rolls for the woman at the newsstand
bread for the night-bus driver
buns for the children
who don't yet know
they're hungry

By six the whole street smells of it
and people follow the smell
the way you follow happiness
eyes closed
and never getting lost

Is the bread fresh?
asks the woman in the headscarf
Fresh madam
warmer than this winter

And the baker?
At home the baker cuts himself
a slice of yesterday
the crust a little harder
the smell a little quieter
a bread that waits for no one now

Because fresh bread
smells only for the one
who waited for it
and the baker doesn't wait
the whole town
is waiting for the baker

---

## 266. THE TRAFFIC-LIGHT PAINTER

He paints the red
he paints the green
brush in hand
ladder under the sky
Mr Miller of the traffic lights

Forty years he's told the town
stop
forty years he's told the town
go
and the town obeys him
to the letter

But nobody ever told him
go
he waited for green outside the house with geraniums
waited for green under a certain window
waited for green at doors
that opened for other people

Stop Miller
said life
and Miller stopped
good as gold
like a pedestrian in a safety leaflet

Then one night
the town empty as a pocket
Miller standing at the crossroads
and everywhere
as far as the eye can see
green green green

And Miller understands
that green doesn't come
to those who wait
green shines for the one
who is awake
while everyone else is sleeping

And he crosses

---

## 267. THE ODD GLOVE

On the number nine tram
between Lilac Street
and Station Square
Mrs Marie lost
her left glove
leather
brown
a little worn at the thumb

At home she still has the right one
all alone
like one hand clapping

And suddenly Mrs Marie
sees the whole town differently
a glove on the school railings
a glove on a bench in the park
a glove stuck on a post
like a small sad flag
all of them left
all of them right
all of them odd

How many of us are there
thinks Mrs Marie
how many of us walk this town
one hand warm
and the other in the cold

At home she takes the right one
strokes it
and in the morning lays it
on the railing at the tram stop
gently
the way you let a bird go

Let the pairs find each other
even without us

---

## 268. THE GEOGRAPHY TEACHER

Here is the Sahara children
sand and sand and sand
here the Amazon
a river that doesn't know its own banks
here the Himalayas
the roof of the world

And the teacher's pointer
travels across the map
lightly
like a swallow

She herself has never been farther
than her sister's in Leeds

Children where is Everest?
There miss
Very good
and one day you'll write to me from up there

The children left
the way children do
one to Lisbon
one to Iceland
one all the way to Peru

And the postman brings postcards
Greetings from the Sahara miss
sand you were right nothing but sand
The Amazon is brown miss
you never told us that
You can't write from Everest
so I'm writing from the bottom
it's beautiful from the bottom too

And on the wall above her bed
the map of the world
is slowly disappearing
under the postcards
sea covering sea
mountains covering mountains

The teacher never went anywhere
and she travelled the whole world
by the hands of her children

---

## 269. THE MAN WHO SAVED TIME

He knew every shortcut
through the butcher's yard
through the gap in the fence
stairs always two at a time

Three minutes saved at the barber's
The usual and make it quick
five minutes saved at lunch
soup can be drunk
a whole afternoon saved
by not taking the walk
that led nowhere

Dad will you come and look? calls the child
Tomorrow
tomorrow I'll have more time

No time no time
he said
and his watch nodded along

And at the end
when there's no more hurrying
because there's nowhere left to go
he sits and counts
three minutes a day for forty years
that makes an ocean of time
where is that ocean?

He asks
in which bank
in which account
where are they deposited
all those minutes he saved

Nowhere sir
time cannot be saved
time can only be spent
and you spent yours
down to the last minute
on hurrying

---

## 270. THE LAST TELEPHONE BOX

On the street corner
stands the last telephone box
red
LISTED MONUMENT
says the little plaque
and that's all it says

Nobody calls from it
people carry telephones in their pockets
like a spare heart
and the box stands there
empty
as a confessional on a Monday

Tourists take pictures with it
children ask
what was it for?
For distance says their father
you went in there to talk to distance

Then one evening
it's raining
November
the street deserted
and the box
rings

It rings
it rings into the rain
and a passer-by
collar up to his chin
stops
hesitates
and lifts the receiver

Hello?

And a voice
an old voice
from very far away
only asks

Is it still raining there
the way it did that night?

---

## 271. THE MUSEUM GUARD

For forty years Mr Joseph
has sat on his chair in the corner of the room
beside the famous painting
so famous
that people cross the world
just to look at it

Mr Joseph keeps watch
Please don't touch
No flash please
Sir that line on the floor
is there for you

Forty years of watching
people watching
he knows every kind of looking
the devout kind
the bored kind
and the kind that's only ticking a box

The painting is at his back
the way a window is at your back
the way the sea is at your back
when you're guarding the beach

Then comes the last day
they give him flowers
his colleagues shake his hand
and the cloakroom lady dabs her eye

And the next morning
Mr Joseph stands in the queue
at the ticket desk
One ticket please
You Mr Joseph?
One ticket please

And for the first time in forty years
he stands before the painting
on the right side of it
and he looks
and he looks
and he looks

---

## 272. THE BINMAN AND THE BOOKS

At four in the morning the bins rattle
and Mr Charlie in his orange vest
lifts the lids
the way you open books

And sometimes
between the peelings and the tins
there lies a book
a real one
thrown away
poems with a dedication
I love you Vera 1972
a novel with a folded corner
on the page where somebody stopped

Mr Charlie wipes them on his sleeve
and carries them home
one by one
a shelf
then two
then a whole wall

What are you going to do with them? asks his wife
Read them says Mr Charlie
somebody has to finish them

And in the evening he sits
in his library of rescued books
reading thrown-away words

Because people don't throw out rubbish
thinks Mr Charlie
people throw out the things
they've stopped understanding
a love from 1972
a story stopped at a folded corner

and he's a binman
he picks up after them

---

## 273. TWO CHAIRS

Mr Anton lives alone
third floor
window on the courtyard
a teapot for one cup
a bed for one
but at the table
two chairs

His own chair creaks
it knows his weight
it knows his elbows
it knows his long evenings

The other is good as new
nobody has ever sat on it
not the postman
not his sister from Leeds
who still can't make it
not the lady from the ground floor
who smiled at him once

Why two chairs? asks the neighbour
I'm expecting someone says Mr Anton
he just hasn't come yet

The years go by
the chairs stay
one creaks
the other keeps quiet

And maybe it's for no one
that second chair
not for the lady from the ground floor
not for the sister
not for anyone with a name

Maybe it's only there
so that loneliness
doesn't look final

An empty chair
is not sorrow
an empty chair
is an invitation

---

## 274. THE CEMETERY GARDENER

Mr Victor plants pansies
on Mrs Smith's grave
he waters the begonias
of the old schoolmaster
and the rose
of the little girl from 1943

The dead can't smell the flowers
people say
the dead don't care

Mr Victor knows
kneeling with his hands in the earth
the dead don't smell
the dead don't see
the dead don't mind the weeds

And still he weeds
and still he waters
and still he plants
spring summer autumn

Because on Sunday comes
a woman with a shopping bag
and she says
The pansies are lovely this year
and she stands a while
and tells her mother
all the news

Because a man in a hat comes
and sits down by the begonias
the way you sit down
next to someone

The flowers are not for the dead
Mr Victor knows
the flowers are for the living
so they have somewhere to come
and a reason to come
and a time to come back

Mr Victor is not a gardener
Mr Victor grows
reasons to return

---

## 275. THE LETTER WITHOUT AN ADDRESS

The postwoman sorts the morning mail
letters with addresses
letters with stamps
letters that know where they're going
and among them one letter
no street no town no name
only the words
FOR WHOEVER NEEDS IT

Return it
to whom
deliver it
where
so she keeps it in her bag
one year
two years
ten years
between the bills and the postcards from the sea
light as a feather
heavy as a secret

Her colleagues say
throw it out
the rulebook says
throw it out
she says
not yet

And one day in November
on a bench outside the post office
a man sits crying
the way people cry in November
quietly and for real
so she stops
opens her bag
and hands him the letter
as if she'd carried it for him all along

Inside is a blank sheet of paper
nothing else
a blank white sheet
and the man reads it
and reads it
and reads it

---

## 276. THE FISHERMAN WHO LETS THEM GO

Every morning by the river
old Miller sits with his rod
he catches a fish
looks at it
lets it go
catches a second
lets it go
catches a third
lets it go

The neighbours laugh
over the fences they laugh
down at the pub they laugh
well then Miller
nothing again?
and the wife at home boiling water for nothing?

He nods
pays for his beer
and says nothing

Because old Miller
doesn't go to the river for fish
he goes there for the silence
for that great green silence
that flows and flows
and is in no hurry
for the silence nobody gives you
not at the town hall
not at church
not at home

And the fish know it
fish know everything about silence
that's why they bite
only for him they bite
they'd almost eat out of his hand
because the one who asks only for silence
gets given everything

---

## 277. THE PIANO TUNER

Mr Marsh is a piano tuner
forty years he's tuned pianos
and forty years he's heard
what nobody else can hear

The number nine tram
squeals a quarter tone sharp
the doorbell of his building
lies by a minor third
the clock on the tower
strikes a false noon
and the minister on the radio
speaks entirely in a minor key
even when he's promising major

It gives him a headache
the whole city gives him a headache
he walks the streets
tuning fork in his pocket
mending the world in his mind
loosen here
tighten there
retune the whole thing

But when the children in the courtyard
sing
off-key as a barn door
off-key as sparrows in May
Mr Marsh stops
takes off his hat
and listens

A piano can be tuned
he says
joy cannot
joy you either have
or you don't
and these kids have it
all of it
wrong notes and all
maybe because of the wrong notes

---

## 278. THE LOST AND FOUND OFFICE

I've come for an umbrella
a black one
with a crooked handle
said the man at the counter
and the clerk nodded
come and see

And she led him down the shelves
the shelf of umbrellas
a hundred umbrellas
a thousand umbrellas
black ones red ones checkered ones
the shelf of gloves
every one of them a left
the shelf of glasses
that don't suit anyone anymore
the shelf of wedding rings
the shelf of keys
to doors whose locks
were changed long ago

Here's yours
she said
and handed him his umbrella
still wet from last spring

And nobody ever comes for all this?
asked the man
and the clerk shrugged
the way you shrug
after twenty years on the job

People don't lose things
sir
things get found
nearly all of them get found
what people lose
is the will to come back for them
and that
we never find
that's not on
any shelf

---

## 279. GRANDMOTHER AND DAYLIGHT SAVING TIME

On Sunday night
the whole country changed its clocks
the whole country except one kitchen
where grandmother said
no

The family fusses
gran you'll miss the doctor
gran you'll miss the news
gran the whole world runs on a different hour
and grandmother stirs her soup
and says
then let it run

All summer long she lives
an hour to the side of the world
she has lunch when the world has tea
she lights the lamp while the world can still see
and in the evening she sits on the doorstep
in an hour the others
spent long ago

Why gran?
her granddaughter asks one day
and grandmother points at the clock
the old clock with the winding key

Your grandfather wound this clock
every evening at half past seven
and at half past seven we had supper
and everyone was home
and everything was good

Summer time winter time
let governments flip the hours
like pancakes in a pan
I'm staying in the hour
when everything used to be good

Time can be refused
little one
if you know why

---

## 280. THE BRIDGE BUILDER

Engineer Brandt built a hundred bridges
bridges over rivers
bridges over valleys
a suspension bridge
a drawbridge
a bridge the papers wrote about
three columns and a photograph

He got a medal from the minister
he got a medal from the president
students learn his formulas
and the bridges stand
and serve
and carry everything
trains floods a hundred years

Only one bridge he never built
the one to his brother
twenty years without a word
over a field
over an inheritance
over a sentence said at a funeral
he no longer even knows over what
he only remembers the silence
wide as a valley
deep as a river

Because the longest bridges
are not built of steel
they are built of a single word
and for that material
there are no standards
no formulas
no medals

One night he sits down at his table
the engineer of a hundred bridges
takes a sheet of paper
and all night long he builds
and by morning it is done
a bridge of one word

Forgive me

---

## 281. THE TICKET SELLER

Behind the station window
Miss Marie
sells the world

Paris via Frankfurt
second class seat reserved
Lisbon with one change
Istanbul with a sleeper
she knows by heart the price of every distance
what the sea costs
what the mountains cost
what it costs to be elsewhere

Through her hands each day
pass thousands of kilometres
honeymoons
escapes
returns
and to every ticket she adds
a safe journey
free of charge

And in the evening she buys herself
what she always buys
two stops home
warm up the dinner
water the geraniums
sleep

Until one day
a Friday in October
she walks around the station
joins the queue
at her own window
and says the destination
she has kept quiet for twenty years

Lisbon
one way
no return

And the hand that gives her the ticket
her own hand
is trembling

---

## 282. WINTER'S INVENTORY

In December in the yard
the children built a snowman
three balls of snow
one on top of the other
a carrot from Mrs Green
two coals from the cellar
grandfather's old pot
and his name was
Mr White

All winter he stood guarding the yard
smiling with his coals
freezing without complaint
the children said good morning
he said nothing
but it suited him

Then came March
and with it the sun
and Mr White day by day
grew thinner
melted
shrank
until one morning
he wasn't there

On the grass he left behind
a carrot
two coals
a pot

Spring takes winter's inventory
counts everything twice
checks everything off
carrot present
coals present
pot present

Only the snow is missing
the snow went back to the sky
the way it came
taking nothing
of all that people gave it

---

## 283. THE NIGHT BAKERY

At night when the city sleeps
those who don't sleep go walking
they walk very quietly
they walk all alone
they stop at the window
where it is bright
and it smells good

It smells of bread
it smells of warmth
it smells of morning
that has not come yet

One has a coat over his pyjamas
one holds an empty bag
as if to go and buy some sleep
the old man counts the floor tiles
and does not count the sheep

Inside a white shadow
pours the flour
rolls the dough
and does not look outside
or does he look?

In the morning people buy their rolls
and they do not know
and they do not guess
that the little window on the yard
stays open all night long

It is not a draught
says the baker
and he goes on baking
he bakes bread for those who sleep
and scent for those who don't

---

## 284. THE BLIND MAN AT THE CINEMA

He goes to the pictures every Wednesday
the blind man
and beside him the neighbour woman
and between them the dark

On the screen it is raining
she whispers to him
now she walks down the street
she has a hat
and she is sad

The blind man nods
he sees her walking
he sees her better
than the whole hall

Now they are kissing
whispers the neighbour
and she blushes
in the dark
where nobody sees her
only he does

The lights come up
people go home
they blink
they wipe their eyes

Outside in the street
it is really raining
and he tells her
you know what I saw?
I saw
that the lady on the screen
had your voice
and was lovelier
than they dared to write in the script

The neighbour says nothing
and for the first time
she does not know
what is on the screen

---

## 285. THE CAROUSEL MAN

From town to town
from fair to fair
the caravan the wheels the road
and wheels again
little wooden horses
swans with their gilt worn thin
and him in the middle

All his life he dreams of a house
a house with a garden
with a fence
with an apple tree
where the suitcases
would finally forget
they are suitcases

Children climb on
children climb off
three minutes of happiness
for small change
and always the same music
barrel-organ music
that smells of candy floss

At night he sits on the caravan step
counting towns
the way other people count sheep

And one night he sees it
the carousel turns
the horses run run run
and never arrive
and he stands in the middle
still as an apple tree
the center of all the roads

a house with a garden
would just be another center
but without the music

---

## 286. THE LAST COBBLER

In the city of supermarkets
where new shoes cost less
than mending old ones
sits a cobbler
the last cobbler
hammer pegs wax
and the smell of leather
that nobody knows by name anymore

People bring him shoes
the way you bring an old dog
to be put down
these aren't worth fixing
they say
and they leave them there
so they won't have to
throw them out themselves

He fixes them
new soles
new stitches
polishes them to a deep dark shine
and gives them away
to whoever walks by barefoot
or nearly

He knows the whole city
and the city doesn't know him
he knows it by its walk
this one treads on the outer edge
a loner
that one wears down her toes
always running after something
that got away
this one has heels straight
as a conscience

And the last cobbler sits
reading the city from below
soles don't lie
faces do
and the city walks overhead
and never knows a thing

---

## 287. THE METRONOME

The old violinist died on a Tuesday
quietly
the way you close a violin case
they took away the furniture
they took away the violin
sold the sheet music by the pound
and painted the flat white

The new tenant is young
he carries boxes
puts up shelves
and one Saturday
he finds on top of the wardrobe
a little wooden pyramid
a metronome

What is that? asks his girlfriend
A metronome
a clock for music
it doesn't tell you the time
it tells you how fast a life is lived

He winds the spring
slides the little weight
and sets it going
tick tock tick tock
andante
an old woman's step on the stairs
tick tock
soup stirred on a Sunday
tick tock
bow up bow down
sixty years

Turn it off says the girl
No he says
and he lets it beat
into the evening

A heart stops
the tempo stays
the flat remembers
how fast it was lived in

---

## 288. THE NAMES OF TREES

For sixty years he walked to work
down the same avenue
there and back
briefcase newspaper rain
and the trees were only trees
green things along the way

Then came retirement
and with it silence
and with the silence a field guide to trees
from a secondhand shop
for next to nothing

Now he walks the park slowly
guide under his arm
like a musical score
learning the names
leaf by leaf
bark by bark

Good morning ash
good morning hornbeam
good morning small-leaved lime
you have done well this year

People turn their heads
the man who talks to trees
children giggle
and he tips his hat
to the plane tree
as if to the headmaster

A madman say the benches
At last a man with manners
whisper the leaves

And the trees
they had known him all along
sixty years of the same footsteps
there and back
they knew him before he knew them
names are for people
not for trees

---

## 289. THE NIGHT TRAIN CONDUCTOR

Forty years of night trains
forty years walking the corridor
between the sleepers
quiet as a conscience
waking them
sir ten minutes to Crewe
madam don't sleep past York
up you get soldier
the war leaves without you

He knows how to wake people gently
a hand on the shoulder
he knows how to read sleep
this one sleeps like a child
wake him twice
that one isn't sleeping
she is just closing her eyes
on something that travels with her

Nobody ever missed their station
forty years
thousands woken
thousands of drowsy thank-yous
thank you conductor
whatever would we do without you

The last night before retirement
the last run
the carriage sways
the wheels say sleep sleep sleep
and the conductor sits down
just for a moment
just for one small moment

And he sleeps past his station
rides right through it
like everyone else
because all his life he woke the others
and him
there was no one to wake him

---

## 290. THE FLOWER SELLER

Every morning at five
she cuts the roses
with the same one pair of scissors
slant cut cold water
and waits to see who comes

The bridegroom comes
all white in the shoulders
thirty red ones please
and let them last at least a week
They will outlast plenty of things
says the flower seller
neither smiling nor sad

The widower comes
all grey in the shoulders
thirty red ones please
she loved them so
I know says the flower seller
she bought them every Friday

The same roses
the same counter
the same scissors
a wedding in the morning
a funeral in the afternoon
and in between she waters

People ask her
how can you do it
the same flowers for love and for death
and she shrugs

Roses make no distinction
the scent is the same
at the altar and at the grave
roses only smell sweet
the translation
people do that themselves

---

## 291. THE BOY AND THE ECHO

A boy stands at the edge of the valley
small against the mountains
and the mountains are big
and say nothing

And because he was scolded that morning
and because he is seven
and the world is unfair
he takes a breath
and shouts into the valley
YOU IDIOT

And the valley takes a breath
and gives it back
idiot idiot idiot
three times four times
once from every side

The boy sits down in the grass
and cries
grandpa the mountains don't like me
they call me names
did you hear?

Grandpa sits down beside him
pipe smoke rising straight
like a proper upbringing
Try telling them something else
he says
mountains repeat
they can't make anything up
they are only big

The boy stands up
wipes his nose on his sleeve
breathes in down to his boots
and shouts
I LOVE YOU

And the valley gives it back
I love you I love you I love you
once from every side
and then once more
and it keeps on saying it
long into the evening
the world is one big echo
it gives you back
whatever you call into it

---

## 292. THE HOUSE PAINTER

He comes with his bucket
and his roller
after the people
who have gone

On the wall a note
in pencil
call your mother
the roller passes
and the mother is gone

On the door frame little marks
Annie 3 years
Annie 4 years
Annie five
and near the top almost grown

The white paint
asks nothing
the white paint
knows nobody
the white paint
is a fresh start
for money

The gentleman paints
the workman paints
and nobody knows
that before he covers the frame
he opens a little notebook

He copies the marks
Annie 3
Annie 4
Annie five
with the street and the date

In the pocket of his overalls
he carries the archive
of strangers' children
who keep growing
even when the wall forgets

And if one day they look
for where they were small
they will find only white
and an old painter
who remembers it for them

---

## 293. THE RETIRED FIREMAN

All his life
he carried water
to the fire

Water in the hose
water in the pail
water on his shoulder
up the stairs
against the flames

Now he has a garden
and a watering can
and a row of beans
and in the evening he goes
to water them

The same water
he tells himself
the very same water
I carried to the one
that takes

now I carry it to the one
that gives back

The fire does not remember
whom it burned
the fire forgets
the moment it goes out

But the garden
the garden remembers
every can
every evening
and the next day
it says thank you
in green

The neighbours laugh
the old fireman
watering his primroses

And he smiles
and he carries
and he carries
and for the first time in his life
nothing is burning

---

## 294. THE WEDDING-DRESS TAILOR

He sews dresses
made to measure
of happiness

He knows the brides' waists
he knows the shoulders
he knows the held breath
at the fitting
before the mirror

He knows the whole town
married
measured
pinned

How many waists
how many shoulders
how many yeses
have passed under his needle

And his daughter
his own daughter
is getting married
in jeans

Dad don't cry
she says
I don't want a dress

The tailor says nothing
the tailor knows
happiness is not made to measure
happiness either fits
or it doesn't

The jeans fitted her
like a glove
like nothing ever
he had sewn

So he took his needle
one last time
and he sewed her only
a little handkerchief
for the pocket

In case
he says
one day after all
she should need
to wipe away some happiness

---

## 295. THE LIFT BOY

For forty years Mister Albert
goes up and down
down and up
in his cage of gold and mirrors
at the Hotel Metropole

To the third floor he carries love
to the fifth floor money
to the seventh loneliness
with its crocodile suitcases

Which floor please?
the guests ask
and Mister Albert knows
third fifth seventh
he knows every floor
and owns none

His place is between the floors
between the second and the third
between nowhere and not yet
where the lift sings
its little song of cables

The guests get out
the guests get in
Mister Albert stays
the doors close
the doors open
and life rides up and down

On the last day
the day before his pension
Mister Albert rode all the way up
higher than the seventh
where the sign says
ROOF — NO ENTRY

and for the first time he got out

And above the roof
above the chimneys above the aerials
he saw where all his life
he had been taking the others

the sky

---

## 296. THE POSTCARD COLLECTOR

In the second-hand shop by the station
in a shoebox
old postcards are sleeping
Venice nineteen twenty
Nice Brighton Constantinople
and Mister Ralph buys them
a penny apiece

He has never been to Venice
never been to Nice
the farthest was his sister's in Slough
and it rained

It's lovely here writes Charlie
it's lovely here writes Maud
it's lovely here write the dead
who got somewhere
and looked back

Mister Ralph reads them at night
under the lamp
kisses for other people's Annies
for other people's mothers
warm greetings from places
where nobody from his street ever arrived

Why do you buy these?
the shopkeeper asks
I collect proof
says Mister Ralph
proof that the world exists
and that people who get somewhere
look back

One winter day
a postcard arrived
a real one
with his name and a stamp
the sea and nothing else
no signature

And Mister Ralph stood it on the windowsill
picture facing out
into the street

so the world
for once
could look too

---

## 297. THE THEATRE CLEANING LADY

When the curtain falls
when the chandeliers go out
when the actors wash off their kings and beggars
Mrs Nora comes in
with her broom and bucket
into the empty house

She doesn't read reviews
she doesn't buy the papers
you read a theatre off its floor

Crumpled handkerchiefs in row nine
so there was dying tonight
laughter they say pops buttons
and there are two in row five
programmes forgotten under the seats
so people left with their heads elsewhere
and one glove in the stalls
the other hand
was in somebody else's hand no doubt

Mrs Nora sweeps
the applause is gone
applause leaves nothing on the floor

The actors get the flowers
the director gets the praise
Mrs Nora gets the floorboards
and floorboards never lie

And sometimes
when the house is empty
and the stage is too
Mrs Nora climbs up
leans her broom against the set
and under her breath
finishes a line
for everyone who cried in row nine

And the empty theatre listens
because even a floor
sometimes wants to hear the end

---

## 298. THE WEATHER ANNOUNCER

Every morning
into the microphone
sunny
and clear
with patches of blue

Good morning all
it will be lovely
he says
and people reach
for their sunglasses

And he
under the desk
against his foot
keeps an umbrella
always
every day

The forecast
is hope
for the others
the umbrella
is experience
for him

He broadcasts sunshine
he sends out warmth
he hands round blue
for free
into every kitchen

And he himself
when he steps outside
opens his umbrella
and is not surprised
almost ever

Because between
hope
and experience
usually
very softly
it rains

But he will never say it
into the microphone
people need
to hear it will be fine
someone at least
must say it out loud

---

## 299. THE EMPTY ROOM

They are moving
boxes by the door
the father carries the lamp
the mother carries the silence

And the boy comes back
one more time
the last time
into his room

It is empty
he says
there is nothing left

But on the wall
a paler rectangle
there hung the bear
here the boat
here the map of the stars

And in the corner
a darker shadow
where the bed had been
where he slept
where he grew
where he feared the dark
and stopped

He shuts the door
they drive away

And into the room
a new boy will come
and he will see
the paler rectangles
and the darker corner

He will inherit the maps
of a childhood not his own
the empty places
left by someone else's dreams

And he will have
to invent for them
a country
and a bear
and a boat
and why this corner here
was so afraid of the dark

---

## 300. THE DOORBELL

At the old man's door
nobody rings
except the postman
except the parcels

He orders
things
he does not need
a shoehorn
a second one
a third

a squeegee for windows
a book about fish
though he never fished

Drawers full
of uselessness
cupboards cracking
with uselessness

But in the morning
someone rings
good day
a parcel for you
a signature please

And he signs
slowly
as slowly as he can
and he asks
how are you
and is it nice out?

He is not ordering goods
he is ordering
a ring at the door

Loneliness
can for a moment
be tricked
cash on delivery

And the postman
has long known it
and he carries and carries
and never will he say
why this old man
needs a fifth shoehorn

---

## 301. THE VIOLINIST IN THE UNDERPASS

In the underpass
where the wind pulls through
a violinist plays
and the violin case
open
for small change

People pass
people hurry
people throw coins
as they walk
without stopping

Clink
and they are gone
clink
and they are gone
the coins fly
the people fly

He plays for backs
he plays for footsteps
he plays for the echo
that never claps

And then there comes
a dumb old man
and he stops
and he goes no further

He stands
and he listens
an hour
two
he throws nothing
he has nothing

And the violinist understands
that this old man
has given him
the most of all

To stop
is the rarest coin
and the only one
that cannot
be changed

---

## 302. THE LAMPLIGHTER

The last gas lamp
in the city
kept officially
FOR THE TOURISTS
says the little sign

And the old man
who in the evening
comes to light it
the only one
by hand

The electric lamps
come on
all at once
there is a switch
somewhere
nobody knows where
click
and there is light
everywhere
at once

But his lamp
his only lamp
comes on
one
and by hand
and every evening
it is waited for

He comes
he opens the little glass
he strikes a match
and the small flame
says thank you

The tourists take photos
what a curiosity
the last lamplighter
an open-air museum
a postcard

And he thinks to himself
lighting
is an event
not a state
electricity is a state
and a state is not waited for

But an event
an event is waited for
all day long
until the evening comes
and with it
the only one
who still
kindles it
by hand

---

## 303. THE OLD SWIMMER

Every morning he comes to the river
the old swimmer
who swims no more
He stands on the bank
in his shoes
in his coat
and he looks

Once he used to win
once he had medals
once they counted him
one two three
and clapped when he touched the wall

Now he touches nothing
he only looks
at the water going away
at the water coming
at the water going away

Not swimming today? asks the fisherman
No
answers the swimmer
I've finished swimming

And he stands there still
in his shoes
in his coat
and the river runs

The river is always the same one
the water never
he swam a thousand times
in a thousand different waters
of a single name

Now he lets the river swim
and the river lets him stand
they are even
two old racers
who have finally
stopped chasing each other

---

## 304. THE COFFEE MACHINE

At three in the morning
in the hospital corridor
the coffee machine glows
the only window
where something still happens

You drop a coin
and it grumbles
it growls
and gives you a burning cup
COFFEE WITH SUGAR
for a few pennies

People stand around
a father
waiting for his son
a woman
waiting for her mother
a young man
waiting for no one
just afraid to go home

Will you have one too? asks the father
Yes
says the woman
though she doesn't drink coffee

They drink
they say nothing
they look into the cup
as if at the bottom
there were an answer

The machine gives coffee for a few pennies
and an excuse for free
the cup is only a reason
to stand side by side

And so they talk
about the son
about the mother
about nothing
about everything

The most truth in the whole city is spoken
at the machine
at three in the morning
between two sips
that nobody likes

---

## 305. THE MISSING WORD

Grandfather sits by the window
a dictionary on his knees
turning pages
from A to Z
and back again
outside stands a suitcase
and beside the suitcase the grandson

he is looking for a word
for the thing in his chest
something between a train and a Sunday
between sorrow and safe travels
no such word in there

he finds sadness
too small
he finds love
too big
he finds farewell
too cold

so he closes the dictionary
and says nothing
and hands that silence to the grandson
across the doorstep
like an apple for the road

the grandson went off
into the world
the world is large
and so are dictionaries

thirty years later
another window another suitcase
his own son at the door
and he turns the pages
from A to Z
and back again
and suddenly he knows

this is the word
grandfather kept silent that day

some words are not handed down by mouth
they are inherited
by being missing

---

## 306. THE CARETAKER AND THE LIGHT

Every evening at ten
the caretaker walks the building
floor by floor
switching off hallways
click
switching off cellars
click
locking the bicycle room
counting light bulbs
like sheep

the town is saving money
electricity is dear
DARKNESS IS FREE
says the notice in the lobby
and the caretaker nods
darkness is free
that much is true

only one bulb
above the front door
he leaves burning
every night
all night

mister caretaker
asks the lady from the second floor
why not switch that one off?
think of the savings

and the caretaker shrugs
some people come home late
from the night shift
from the pub
from love
from the world

darkness is cheaper
sure enough
but a lit window
is an address

---

## 307. THE BARBER

The customers talk
they talk about wives
they talk about bosses
they talk about illnesses
their own
their neighbour's
the one they're afraid of

come in have a seat
says the barber
and that is all he says

he cuts
and keeps quiet
the scissors click
the stories fall
to the floor with the hair
grey stories
black stories
the story inherited from a grandfather
the curly story of the blonde from the post office

the barber knows everything about this town
he knows who loves whom
he knows who doesn't anymore
he knows who owes how much to whom
he knows and goes on cutting

in the evening he takes the broom
and sweeps
all the loves all the bosses all the illnesses
into one small pile
and into the bin

silence is his trade
as much as the scissors
and that is why they all come back

hair grows again
and so do stories

---

## 308. THE STOP IN THE FIELDS

The train stops on request
in the middle of the fields
the stop is called Lhotka
but no Lhotka anywhere
no village
no road
no dog

only a board
LHOTKA
and beneath it in smaller letters
REQUEST STOP
and a bench
that nobody repairs
and nobody breaks

the conductor shrugs
why do we still stop here?
the new colleagues ask
no idea
he says
but we stop

in spring the wheat
in summer the wheat
in autumn the stubble
in winter the snow
and the stop still there

once a year
in October
an old lady gets off
with a bunch of flowers
she thanks the conductor
and walks away across the field
straight ahead
as if there were a path
that only she can still see

the train waits
one minute longer than allowed
then moves on

on the line they say
a stop can be abolished
with one stroke of a pen

a sign cannot

---

## 309. A LETTER TO MYSELF

The teacher told the children
write a letter
to yourselves grown up
and the children laughed
grown up how?
and they wrote

Dear me
when you are big
do I have a dog?
can you fly yet?
don't be afraid of the dark
and please buy a red car

The teacher collected the letters
sealed them
put them in a box
and the box in the cupboard
and the cupboard she forgot
for twenty years

After twenty years
she sent them out
to the old addresses
half of which no longer exist
and half the people neither

And grown men and women
open the envelopes
and read in a child's handwriting
their own promises
don't be afraid of the dark
can you fly yet?

It is the only letter
you cannot fail to answer
because the one who asks
is the one you cannot lie to

One man finishes reading
takes his coat
and goes that very day
to buy the dog
he promised himself
twenty years ago

---

## 310. TOMATOES OVER THE FENCE

Two neighbours
and between them a fence
one grows tomatoes
the other apples
and they never said more
than good morning

All summer long
they toss their harvest over the fence
here take some tomatoes
here take some apples
red this way
red that way
and not a word

In July a tomato falls
into the grass by the apple trees
in August an apple falls
into the tomato bed
and nobody minds

The wife asks
why do you give him any
you don't even talk to him?
The man shrugs
we talk
he says
only very quietly

And so the summer goes
tomato
apple
tomato
apple
over the fence
there and back

People say
tear down that fence
be friends at last
But those two know better

The fence is really necessary
where else
would they lay their gifts?
The best trade is the one
where nobody pays
and both men feel
they came out ahead

---

## 311. THE CONDUCTOR IN THE PARK

Every morning he comes to the park
the retired conductor
without tails
without an orchestra
with only his baton
which he kept

He lifts his arms
and he begins
blackbirds come in
now the wind in the leaves
pianissimo
and now the tram in the distance
forte

People pass by
and they smile
an old fool
they say tenderly
and they walk on
but more slowly

Is it raining?
he conducts the rain too
snowing?
the snow as well
he waves the pigeons in
slows down for the clouds
and speeds up for the children

Once he had eighty players
and all of them watched him
in case he made a mistake
and every evening he was afraid

Now he is never afraid
at last he has an orchestra
that plays
even when he isn't looking

The music conducts itself
the blackbird needs no beat
the wind knows no notes
and still they play
in time

The conductor only bows now
he bows to the empty bench
and the bench
full of sunlight
applauds with its leaves

---

## 312. CHESS IN THE PARK

Two old men
on a bench in the park
play chess
a single game
that lasts for years

They think over a move for a week
on Monday one of them comes
looks at the pieces
and leaves
not yet

On Tuesday the other comes
looks
strokes the knight
and leaves
not yet

You play slowly
says a passer-by
No
answer the old men
we play exactly

The king stands in his corner
through so many springs
the pawn waits for the road
that no one will draw
and the rook looks into the distance

Someone says
but that's checkmate
just move
and it's over
They smile
that's exactly why we don't move

Neither of them wants to give mate
mate would mean
the end of the conversation
and the conversation
is all they have left

A draw is a form of friendship
and an endless game
is a form of life
They play on
and the king holds
and the sun goes down
and tomorrow there's another move

---

## 313. THE CROSSING

An old woman
crosses at the crossing
very slowly
one foot
and then the other foot
and in between
a whole life

Behind her a line of cars
and the cars honk
honk
honk
as if a horn
could shorten old age

The woman walks
she doesn't hear
or she hears
and walks just as slowly
because she can't go faster any more
and won't pretend

The first driver
a young man
clenched on his wheel
all at once
switches off his engine

Silence
just one engine less
but you can hear it

Behind him the second switches off
and the third
and the fourth
and the whole street
cuts its engines

And the city hears for the first time
the sound of its own patience
it is the silence
in which she walks
the old woman
and no one needs to carry her
because she walks alone

She reaches the other side
she turns around
and raises her hand
not to wave
just so
like a conductor
who has just hushed the world

---

## 314. THE WEDDING PHOTOGRAPHER

He photographed a thousand weddings
a thousand times he said
a smile please
and the people smiled
because he asked them to

He knew every church
every pew
every mother-in-law
who cries before the bride
and every uncle
who arrives too late

He himself never married
they asked him why
and he said
weddings I have plenty
I have a thousand
and I'm off to the next one

In not one photo is he there
hidden behind the camera
behind the light
behind the order
a smile please
the others smiled

At home in the evening
he looks through a thousand smiles
and not a single one is his
and yet
each one belongs to him a little

Until one day
while looking
he finds a photograph
where the bride is standing
and behind her a mirror

And in the mirror
blurred
there he stands
with the camera instead of a face
at last in one photograph
the only one of a thousand

That is how you photograph happiness
up close
and out of frame

---

## 315. THE BOOK NEVER RETURNED

The librarian has rubber stamps
red ones blue ones violet ones
and books that come back
like swallows
every year

Only one never came back
thirty years
thirty springs thirty winters
in the ledger a small hole
the shape of a book

Out of habit
out of that tenderness called habit
she wrote a reminder
Dear sir
kindly return
what you borrowed
thirty years ago

And the book arrived
by post
worn like the palm of a hand
with a letter inside
Dear madam
I have read it all my life
and I am still not done

The librarian picked up the stamp
RETURNED
and put it down again
she wrote in a new date
borrowed once more
and sent the book back where it came from

Because some books
are not lent for a month
they are lent for a life

---

## 316. THE SPARROW

Every morning
before the coffee
before the paper
crumbs on the windowsill

And the sparrow arrives
punctual as a baker
more punctual than the trains
he pecks looks up pecks
and flies off on sparrow business

The man calls it a visit
the sparrow calls it a window
and that's enough for both of them

One day the sparrow didn't come
the crumbs lay there all morning
all noon all evening
and the man paced the flat
telling himself
it's only a sparrow
it's only a sparrow
the way people tell themselves
it's only a heart

That night he didn't sleep

In the morning he put out twice the crumbs
just in case
just in hope
which is the same thing

And two of them came

The sparrow hadn't come the day before
because he had a reason
and the reason now sat beside him
pecking

Even a sparrow likes to show off
a good window when he's found one

---

## 317. THE ASTRONOMICAL CLOCK

Tourists from Tokyo from Lima from Melbourne
heads tipped back
photograph the clock
the apostles file past
Death rings his little bell
the rooster crows
and a thousand phones remember it

The locals pass underneath
with bags with children with appointments
never looking up
they keep the clock for later
the way you keep for later the sea
and your mother
and your life

Later
is the biggest warehouse in the world
everything fits in there

And one day a local man
who had just missed his tram
stood still among the tourists
and waited
because everyone was waiting

The apostles filed past
Death rang his bell
the rooster crowed
and the man saw the clock
for the first time in his life
after forty years
a hundred metres from home

To see the beauty of home
you must borrow a stranger's eyes
the farthest thing away
is often the nearest

---

## 318. THE ICE CREAM MAN AND THE CHESTNUTS

On the corner by the pharmacy
in summer stands an ice cream man
vanilla strawberry lemon
and a line of children

On the corner by the pharmacy
in winter stands a chestnut man
hot chestnuts in a paper cone
and a line of frozen hands

It is the same man
the same smile
the same hands
only the weather has changed its clothes

People ask him
what are you exactly?
ice cream man or chestnut man?

And he says
I don't sell ice cream
I don't sell chestnuts
I sell balance

In July a little winter
in January a little summer
to each person
whatever they are missing

People think
they are paying for a cone
but they are paying for balance
the whole city pays him
to even out the weather
cheaper than any ministry
and with a smile thrown in

---

## 319. CHINESE WHISPERS

The children sit in a circle on the grass
and the first one whispers
grandma has a white cat

The sentence travels
from ear to ear
a little traveller
changing clothes at every ear

The last child stands up
and says out loud
grandpa sleeps in a kennel made of chocolate

And everyone laughs
they fall in the grass laughing
because the sentence arrived
past all recognition
and that is the whole game

Grown-ups play it too
they play it all their lives
from ear to ear
from bar to bar
from century to century

Apparently he said
apparently she did
apparently that's how it happened

Only when the sentence arrives
nobody stands up
nobody laughs
nobody says
it was only a game

With children it's called Chinese whispers
with grown-ups it's called
gossip
reputation
history

---

## 320. THE MANUSCRIPT

All his life he rewrote
a single manuscript
he crossed out
he began again
he crossed out
he began again
and never sent it off

The publisher waited
his friends kept asking
when will it come out?
Soon
he said
it isn't quite right yet

His wife brought him tea
and never read a word
she respected the door
she respected the silence
she respected the man
who vanished into a paragraph

He grew old over it
his hair went white
like paper
and the manuscript grew
and never came out

When he died
a stack of pages remained
and his wife
for the first time sat down
and began to read

And on every page
there she was
once she was called Marie
once Hélène
once just her
but it was always her

Fifty years he described her
fifty years he was afraid
it wasn't good enough
to show her

The book came out
in an edition of one reader
and it was exactly the reader
it had been written for

---

## 321. LOVE LOCKS

On the bridge hung padlocks
thousands of padlocks
small big rusty golden
and on each one two names
and between the names
a little heart

The city decided
THE LOCKS WILL BE REMOVED
FOR STRUCTURAL REASONS
because love it seems
is too heavy for the bridge

The locksmith came
with his cutters
and he cut
snip
Peter and Jane
snip
Anne and Thomas
snip
someone and someone

He carries them off in a box
to the town hall
love locks
inventory number
three hundred and twelve

But the keys
the keys are long since
at the bottom of the river
the lovers threw them there
so that no one ever
could unlock their love

So the town hall has the locks
and the river has the keys
and they will never meet
because that
was exactly the point

The town hall archives love
under an inventory number
it records it
it weighs it
it files it in a crate
but what is locked
stays locked

---

## 322. THE STARTING PISTOL

The referee raises his arm
he holds a pistol
every morning he loads it
with blank cartridges

On your marks
get set
and he fires

And the people start to run
not away
but forward
they run toward something
they run for something
they run for dear life
and it is only a race

He is the only man in the world
who fires into a crowd
and the crowd thanks him
it is the only gunshot
after which nobody lies down
everybody flies

They pour across the finish line
soaked and happy
and they ask
who won?
and nobody knows
because they all ran

If every gunshot in the world
were only a starting shot
then every war would become
an ordinary Sunday race
and the generals would hand out
medals instead of crosses
and the mothers would wait at the finish
and not at the graveyard

---

## 323. THE WINDOW ACROSS THE STREET

In the morning a wave
in the evening a light switched on
that is how they know each other
for ten years now

He at the left-hand window
she at the right-hand window
between them the street
the tram the pigeons the rain

Good morning window
good night window
they never said each other more
and more was never needed

One day in the shop
at the milk counter
they stood side by side
and did not recognize each other

Excuse me
she said
please go ahead
he said
and that was all

Without the window frame
they looked nothing alike
they were only two people
reaching for the same milk
two bodies with no windows
two strangers in coats

In the evening they went home
switched on the light
and waved to each other
and everything was as it was

Some friendships
need a street between them
they need a frame
they need distance to be close
and if they crossed that street
perhaps they would lose each other
so they would rather wave
from window to window
across a whole life

---

## 324. THE LEAF SWEEPER

He sweeps the whole town
tons of leaves
maples chestnuts limes
everything falls on him

In the morning the pavement is yellow
in the afternoon yellow again
he sweeps and it falls
it falls and he sweeps

A Sisyphus with a broom
says the park keeper
and he laughs
and the leaves keep falling

Useless work?
asks the sweeper
and no one answers
the tree just drops another

Every autumn
one single leaf
he takes home
wipes it on his coat
and slips it into a book

He has a book of autumns
one leaf after another
one year after another
thirty leaves
thirty autumns
between the pages

You cannot sweep the falling
he says to himself at night
the falling can only be recorded
the rest is raked into a heap
and carted away

And so he keeps sweeping
the whole town
just so that he may
once a year
keep a single leaf

---

## 325. THE HOURGLASS

In the souvenir shop
between the postcards and the magnets
stands an hourglass
glass sand and wood

On the tag it reads
LAST ONE ON SALE
and nobody buys it
digital clocks are more accurate

What time is it?
asks the tourist
and he looks at his phone
not at the sand

The hourglass says nothing
time trickles inside it
grain after grain
you can see it fall

It is the only clock in the world
where you can see time
flowing
running low
landing on the bottom

And it is the only clock
you take into your hand
turn over
and it starts again
from the bottom up
tomorrow pouring into yesterday

And that is exactly why nobody wants it
people do not want to see time
much less hold it in their palm
much less turn it over
they are afraid of that one grain
that is falling right now

So it stays in the shop
LAST ONE ON SALE
full of sand
and full of patience
waiting for someone to buy it
and turn it over
and admit
that time is running low

---

## 326. THE MARKET CAT

At the fish market
every morning
the cat is given
a fish by the seller

A sardine or a mackerel
whatever is left
he hands it over the counter
and says good morning madam

The neighbors shake their heads
why feed the thief?
she'll clean out your whole stall
she's a robber with a tail

The seller just waves his hand
and gives the fish anyway
morning after morning
winter summer autumn

And the cat
what she is given
she does not steal
she sits on a crate
and keeps watch

At night when the market sleeps
she prowls around the stall
she hisses at the rats
and chases off the gulls
she is the best guard in the harbor

The neighbors complain
I'm robbed all the time
and yet they have fences
they have locks
they have dogs on chains

You turn an enemy into an ally
with one fish a day
smiles the seller
it's cheaper than a fence
and besides
a fence will never sit on your lap
and purr

---

## 327. INFINITY

The mathematics teacher
writes on the blackboard
one two three
and three little dots

Numbers never end
he tells the class
you can always add one
and one again
and again

Infinity
he writes in capital letters
INFINITY
and underlines it twice

The boys yawn
the girl by the window counts the crows
the bell is about to ring
and infinity interests no one

We can write a million
and add one
we can write a billion
and add one
we will never reach the end
never

The boy in the back row
raises his hand
sir
is that like when I wait for my dad?

And a silence falls over the class
such a silence
that you can hear the chalk
land on the floor

His dad did not come home
from a single shift
two years ago now
and the boy still counts the days
and one more
and one more

Infinity
the teacher wipes it off the board
but from that desk
no one will wipe it away
and for the first time in his whole life
it is truly explained

---

## 328. THE FERRIS WHEEL

The Ferris wheel operator
presses a button
and the town rises
and falls
and rises again

All day he sees the town from above
the roofs the yards the chimneys
the washing lines
the cats on the tin
and the pigeons in the gutters

He knows every roof
he knows who hangs out blue shirts
he knows where a fig tree grew in the courtyard
he knows where they leave the window open
and where they do not

Where are you going mister?
asks the little girl in the car
up
he answers
and then down
and then up again

He has never been
inside any of those houses
he has watched all his life
he has never rung a doorbell
he has never hung out washing
on any of those lines

He knows the town like the birds do
the whole of it
and from nowhere

The people below
know their house
their stairs
their door
and nothing more

He knows everything
and owns nothing
they own one house
and never see the town

And so he stands by the lever
and presses the button
and carries the others up
so that for one moment at least
they may see what he sees
and will never see where he lives

---

## 329. THE CLOAKROOM ATTENDANT

At the philharmonic
behind door number three
sits the cloakroom attendant
among strangers' coats

She hears the music only muffled
through the closed door
the violins are just a rustle
the drum just a knock

Whole evenings
among the furs and overcoats
she hands out numbers
here you are twenty-seven

How was the concert?
asks the cleaning woman
I didn't hear it
says the attendant
but I can tell

I can tell by the coats
by the way people put them on
after cheerful music
they take their coats quickly
they laugh
they all talk at once

But after the beautiful kind
the real kind
they put their coats on slowly
and in silence
as if they were afraid
that with one sleeve
they might break something

The best concerts
I know them for certain
says the attendant
they are the ones
after which nobody hurries for a coat
when I stand alone by the hooks
and wait
and it is quiet
and in that quiet
I heard the whole of it

---

## 330. THE CYCLIST AND THE WIND

In the morning to work
against the wind
in the evening home
against the wind again

How does the wind do it?
curses the cyclist
he pedals and he swears
he swears and he pedals
for ten years now

Damned wind
it leans into me
it pushes me back
it takes my breath
and never blows at my back

For the neighbor it blows at his back
for the car it blows at its back
only for me
always against

And then one morning
the wind does not blow at all
the air stands still
the leaves hang down
the flag on the town hall droops dead

The road is suddenly fast
the bicycle rolls by itself
and it is good for nothing

Where has it gone?
asks the cyclist
and no one answers
only the silence holds the handlebars

The headwind
had been all that time
the proof that he was moving
that he was going somewhere
that he was pedaling forward

It was a companion
that leaned against him
and now he has nothing to lean into
and for the first time in ten years
he wishes
the wind would blow against him again

---

## 331. THE NIGHT ATTENDANT

The all-night filling station
by the motorway
a light in the black field
and one man inside

The cars arrive
the cars drive off
coffee petrol and on they go
nobody stays

Where are you coming from?
nobody answers
where are you going?
nobody knows

They take their coffee in a cup
a full tank
and vanish into the dark
the red lights fading away

The attendant stands behind the glass
he counts the night
one hour two hours three
and the signal blinks amber into the empty

Safe journey
he says to each of them
safe journey
and no one asks him
where he is going

Nowhere
he is going nowhere
he is the only one
who stays
so that the others can pass through

The still point of the motorway
a lighthouse on dry land
an anchor in the asphalt
a light so the others can see
the way out

And when toward dawn
the last car drives away
he understands what only he knows
all the haste of the world
needs someone
who does not hurry
and who shines all night
so the others have somewhere to arrive
and somewhere to leave from

---

## 332. THE DOCTOR AND THE TEDDY BEAR

Morning at the surgery
it smells of alcohol and fear
the little patient on his mother's lap
stares at the needle
like a dragon's tooth

The doctor takes the bear
he sits on the cabinet from spring to winter
Well now little bear
it's only a small pinch ?
and she gives him a shot in the paw

The bear does not cry
the bear looks at the ceiling
the bear is brave
as a bear

You see ?
says the doctor
and the child nods
because whatever the bear can do
so can he

The needle comes and goes
the child doesn't even blink
he is after all as big
as a bear

In the evening when the lamps go out
the bear sits alone on the cabinet
and he is wearing
thirty little plasters
more than all the patients put together

courage cannot be prescribed
courage is vaccinated by example
and the bear is riddled all over
with love

---

## 333. THE CRACKED BELL

In the tower hangs a great bell
and it is cracked
instead of singing
it only wheezes
like an old man

To take it down costs a heap of money
and money there is none
so it hangs on
cracked and proud
above the whole little town

The sexton has a little bell
one that fits in his palm
and with that he now rings noon
ding ding
in a thin voice from near the kitchen

And yet
when it tinkles above the rooftops
people lift their heads
and say
do you hear ? it's noon
and they look up toward the tower

toward the great cracked bell
that says nothing
and not toward the little one
that rings

Nobody looks
where the sound truly came from
everybody looks
where it used to come from

habit keeps ringing
even with a cracked bell
and the bell takes in silence
another's glory
and no one seems to mind
least of all the little bell
which knows what it knows
and tinkles on

---

## 334. THE CHIMNEY SWEEP

Down the street comes the chimney sweep
all black as the night
and people turn to look at him
and clutch at a button

Good day madam
and she shyly touches his shoulder
for luck
and walks on with a smile

A boy from behind
claps him on the back
and runs off
holding the button of his trousers

The chimney sweep smiles
and lets them be
touch me boy
touch me madam
luck comes for free

All day long they touch him
all day long he hands it out
and the soot wears off
only from his coat
never from his smile

In the evening he sits on a step
and he counts
today thirty touches
today thirty pieces of luck
I gave to thirty people

And yet as for me
I am the dirtiest man in the whole town
and the happiest

because luck multiplies by touch
the more of it you give away
the more of it stays on your skin
that is why I am all over soot
and all over happy

---

## 335. THE LIFEGUARD IN WINTER

The pool is closed for winter
a tarp over the deep end
dead leaves on the bottom
where in July
the children were shouting
now the leaves keep quiet
and the lifeguard sits
on his white tower
in a winter coat
whistle around his neck

a passerby stops
and taps his forehead
what are you guarding mister?
there's no water
not a single child
not one drop of summer

and the lifeguard smiles
I'm guarding the water that isn't there
I'm guarding the summer that was
I'm guarding the shouting
still hanging in the air
between the diving board and the sky

NO ENTRY
says the sign on the gate
and the sign is right
you don't walk into summer
in the middle of December

because water too
needs its rest
like you and me
and when the water is resting
somebody has to guard
its peace

---

## 336. THE MATCHES

Twenty years ago
he stubbed out his last cigarette
in a station ashtray
said enough
and it was enough

his lungs breathed easy
his wife breathed easy
his doctor breathed easy
only the coat pocket
stayed faithful
a box of matches
still lives there

what do you keep them for says his wife
you don't smoke
throw them out
he doesn't throw them out
he doesn't know why
until yesterday he didn't know why

last night in the street
dark cold November
a stranger with a cigarette in his mouth
patting his pockets
got a light?

and he had one
he struck a match
two hands cupped around the flame
two faces lit for a second
thanks
don't mention it

giving up smoking
can be done
but giving up fire
kept for other people
never

---

## 337. THE INTERPRETER

From morning till night
she speaks with the words of other people

Tell her that I love her
and she says it
and the other woman blushes
and she translates the blushing too

Sign here
and the company buys the company
and she is the bridge
over which the millions cross
and nothing stays in her hand

You are a liar
one of them shouts
and she says it calmly
for even shouting is translated calmly
that is her whole art

Other people's love
other people's contracts
other people's quarrels
pass through her mouth
and travel on

In the evening she comes home
and she is silent

She opens her mouth
and nobody waits for the translation
her own words
are the only ones in the world
that no one will translate

She sits by the window
and whispers into the dark
I love
I sign
I forgive

and she waits
for someone one day to come
who will interpret her

---

## 338. THE FADED TIMETABLE

At the stop by the edge of the fields
hangs a timetable
bleached by the sun
into a white silence

Once there were numbers there
and hours
and minutes
now there is only a fog of paper
DEPARTURES to nowhere

The office hasn't changed it in years
and people have lost time
and gained time

When does it come ?
asks the new woman
and the old one answers
the one that comes will come
sit down love

So she sits down
and they talk

About grandchildren
about the rain
about the son-in-law who drinks
and the bus sways somewhere out among the fields
and nobody calls it

The most exact timetable
is the one you cannot read

the ones you can read cut waiting up
into my three minutes
and your seven
and each of us stands alone
watch pressed to the nose

whereas here
we wait together
for the one that comes
and it doesn't matter when
because it is our bus
our own shared bus
in no hurry to be anywhere

---

## 339. THE BIRD FEEDERS

Grandfather has shavings in his workshop
and the smell of wood
and patience

He makes bird feeders
little houses with a roof
and at night
when nobody is looking
he hangs them through the park

No label
no name
no signature
just a nail and the dark
and in the morning the feeder on the branch
as if fallen from the sky

The birds don't know whom to thank
and that is the whole point
of charity
the tit doesn't know the giver
and yet it sings
perhaps it sings for that very reason

The neighbours know
they saw him from their windows
the old man with his ladder
at midnight
but they say nothing

because if they told
grandfather would have to confess
and would have to be thanked
and that would spoil everything

So they say nothing
and guard his secret
as he guards the birds

And the park is full of little houses
that come from no one
and full of people
pretending not to know

and it is the loveliest conspiracy
in the world
a conspiracy of goodness
that pretends
it never happened

---

## 340. THE INLAND LIGHTHOUSE

Someone built on the hill
above the beets and the wheat
a lighthouse

White with a red stripe
like on a postcard from the sea
and the sea from here is
a thousand kilometres away

People laughed
what a fool
a lighthouse with no sea
what use is a lighthouse
where there are no ships ?

They laughed in summer
they laughed in autumn
until the fog came

That thick fog off the fields
that swallows the road
swallows the trees
swallows your own two hands

And then on the hill
a light blinked
once twice
and the cars in the fog
lifted their heads like ships

And they found their way home
by the lighthouse with no sea

The sea is not the condition of a lighthouse
that is what those who laughed believed
a lighthouse needs only fog
and someone who is lost

and of those there are plenty everywhere
even a thousand kilometres from the coast
one gets lost among the beets as well
and there too it is good
that somebody shines

---

## 341. THE DESK CLERK

Behind the desk stands the clerk
and he smiles at those who arrive
and instead of saying
welcome to our hotel
he says by mistake
welcome home

The guest with the suitcase stops short
home ?
and something stirs inside him
and he takes the key
and goes upstairs
as if he were going home

The manager hears it
and he frowns
this is a hotel sir
not a home
say welcome to our hotel
as is proper

The clerk nods
yes sir
and the next day again
welcome home

because the heart
cannot be ordered
nor forbidden

And the guests come back
from all over the world they come back
to this hotel
where there is a desk and keys and nothing special

nothing but one man
who by mistake
tells each of them now and then
that word you pay for
which is on no price list
and which even so you pay for
the most

welcome home
he says again
and he goes on being wrong
for it is not a mistake
it is the only truth
he owns in that hotel

---

## 342. THE OLD MAP

Grandfather walks through the city
with the map of his youth
yellow as the autumn
and soft as a handkerchief

The streets have other names
they pulled down the houses
they built over the park
and still he walks straight ahead
following his old lines

Here there was a bakery
he says to the empty window
here they sold bread rolls
for a penny
and it smelled all the way over here

He sees two cities at once
the one that is
and the one that was
and he walks through both
as though he had two pairs of eyes

The young go past him
phone in hand
where am I ?
asks the phone
and it turns round and round

And the grandson gets lost
the sat-nav in his palm
in the middle of his own city
he wanders among the new names

and grandfather finds him
from memory
by a house that no longer stands
come along boy
here I know every stone
even the ones they carried away

for a city can be pulled down
but not the one in the head
and the only map that never grows old
is the one you carry inside

---

## 343. THE BALLOON SELLER

At the fair among the carousels
stands the balloon seller
and he holds a whole sky in his hand
red yellow blue

The children buy them
and the children let them go
the fingers are small
and the string is thin
and the balloon wants to go up

And then that crying
mummy it flew away
and the mother
never mind darling
we'll buy a new one

The seller sees it every day
a hundred balloons
a hundred little tears
and he smiles
and he goes on selling

But in the evening
when the fair falls quiet
and the lights go out
and the children sleep and dream of balloons

he takes one
the last one
and on purpose
he lets it go
