better than her own
she talks of them at the hairdresser's
like the neighbours upstairs

And then one day
a Tuesday
for the last time

THANK YOU FOR WATCHING WITH US

the credits roll upward
and carry off Clare the doctor the kind one and the scoundrel
somewhere behind the screen

Grandmother sits
her hands in her lap
and she is in mourning
for people who never were

The neighbour would say but they were made up
grandmother knows what she knows
they have gone
and the flat is once again
four people emptier

---

## 437. THE CHILD AND THE MOON

Grandpa look
the moon is following me
I go left and it goes left
I go right and it goes right
I run
and it runs too

Why does it follow me grandpa?

The grandpa could say
it's a hundred thousand miles away
it's a cold stone
it isn't following you
it isn't going anywhere

But the grandpa watches the little one
holding the moon by the hand
across the whole sky

and he says nothing

For he knows how long it is afterward
once you learn
that the moon follows no one
that the stars are only fires
long ago gone out
that all of it was just a dream

So he says
yes my boy
it follows you
because it loves you
and it's afraid that on the way home
you might get lost

And the child walks
and the moon follows him
and the grandpa follows them both
so that for at least one year
the boy will have someone
who faithfully walks beside him

---

## 438. THE MAN WHO NEVER HURRIED

In the city where everyone runs
one man walks slowly

The clerks overtake him
the errand-boys overtake him
the man with the briefcase overtakes him
and the woman with the pram
and the dog without a master

All of them somewhere
he nowhere

He stops at a shop window
where there are clocks
fifty clocks
and each tells a different time
and it's all the same to him

He watches a pigeon
peck at a crumb
a puddle of coffee
forming on the pavement
an old woman
counting the steps

No one notices him
because he stands still
and what stands still
is as if it weren't there

And then he comes to the crossing
where the light is red

And they are all there
the clerks the errand-boys
the man with the briefcase the woman with the pram
and the dog has caught up with its master

They have all caught up with him again
at that red light
out of breath

and he says to them softly
you see
we have come just as far
only you along the way
you saw nothing

---

## 439. THE WOMAN WHO TALKED TO HER PLANTS

Good morning geranium
did you sleep well
and you pelargonium
don't sulk
it'll rain one of these days

The neighbour behind her window
taps her forehead
look at that one talking to the flowers again

Good morning basil
how you've grown
and you rosemary
don't pout
you'll have water enough

The neighbour in his shirtsleeves
shakes his head
the poor woman
alone as a fencepost
she talks to the soil

But that balcony
it blooms
it blooms like nothing else in the whole house
the petunias spill over the railing
and the people below stop
and ask whose it is

And she waters
and she chatters
and she listens to what no one else hears

Maybe the plants aren't listening
who knows
maybe it doesn't matter

But whoever says to someone in the morning
good morning
did you sleep well
that one blooms as well

---

## 440. THE OLD MAN AND ONE SEA

Once in his life he saw the sea
just once
and ever since he tells of it

The grandson sits on his stool
and listens
grandpa what was the sea like

And the old man begins

It was blue like
and here he always pauses
blue like what
once he says like your grandmother's eyes
another time like the ink at school
another time like ice when it cracks

And the waves

Once they were tall as houses
and grandpa ran
another time they only lapped
and tickled at his feet
another time the surface was
like glass
and nothing happened at all

The grandson notices
grandpa last time it was different

And the old man smiles

What can you do my boy
the sea has its tide going out and in
even in memory
each time it comes another
and each time it is whole

It's enough to see it once

and you have it for a hundred tellings
and not a single time
will you see it the same

---

## 441. THE COLLECTOR OF SUNSETS

Every evening he goes out on the balcony
and photographs the sunset

Monday's was pink
Tuesday's blood-red
Wednesday's almost nothing
the clouds ate it
Thursday's golden like a medal
Friday's purple like a bruise

He has thousands of them
in the phone
in the drawer
in a shoebox

Who will tell him
it's the same sun after all
always the same sunset
what's the use of so many photos

And he opens the box
and shows

Look
this one isn't like that one
and that one not like the other
no two of them are the same
not a single time
in a whole lifetime
has the sun gone down twice alike

They tried to make him believe
that a day is like a day
that one is like another
that it all repeats

But he has the proof
a thousand proofs
in a shoebox

that nothing
but nothing at all
ever repeats

and whoever pays attention to endings
has no two days the same

---

## 442. TWO SISTERS AND ONE WATCH

Their mother left one watch
small and gold
with a cracked glass
and two daughters

It couldn't be fairly split in two
so they said to each other
we'll take turns
half a year you
half a year you

In June Martha wears it
in December Helen
and at the handing-over
they meet in their mother's kitchen
at the table that stayed

They pour themselves a coffee
and pass the watch across
and somehow they linger
and they talk
of the children of their backs of the winter
and of their mother

Year after year
twice a year
at that table

One day Helen says
you know
this watch doesn't even keep good time
it runs slow

And Martha laughs
it runs slow
our mother was no fool

she didn't leave us a ring
that only one of us would wear
she left us a watch
that has to be passed on

she arranged it
so that her girls
would have to see each other
at least twice a year

---

## 443. THE UMBRELLA MENDER

In the age when umbrellas are thrown away
because it's cheaper than the mending
he sits in his shop window
the old umbrella mender

A rib here
a wire there
a drop of oil on the spindle
and the umbrella opens again
like a flower

People pass by
and wonder
who still mends an umbrella
a new one costs next to nothing

But they come

A woman arrives
with a man's umbrella
black big heavy
and she says
it was my husband's
mend it for me please
I don't want a new one

A son arrives
with his father's umbrella
with the handle bent
bent exactly
the way his father held it

And the mender understands

He doesn't mend the cloth
nor the ribs
nor the spindle

he mends that piece of wood
that remembers
the grip of a hand
that is no longer here

and that's why they come to him
even though a new one costs next to nothing

no one can buy a new grip
of that hand

---

## 444. THE BARBER AND THE FIRST CUT

Each time he cuts a child for the first time
the mother keeps her hands in her lap
and she trembles

And the barber takes the first lock
the very first
and he doesn't let it fall among the others

He tucks it into an envelope
writes the name and the date
and puts it in a drawer

Peter three years old
Anna two and a half
Thomas four and he cried

The drawer fills up
year after year
locks fair dark red
the beginnings of a whole town

The years go by
the barber grows old
the mirror grows dim

And one day a man comes in
tall in a suit
bald

and he says awkwardly
this may sound foolish
but they told me you cut my hair first
my mother said you kept it

And the barber opens the drawer

and looks
Peter three years old

and he hands the tall man
the envelope with the lock
of golden hair
he will never have again

Here you are
he says
your beginning
I kept it for you
all this time

---

## 445. THE EMPTY TRAM TO THE DEPOT

The last tram
rolls to the depot
empty
completely empty

Nobody rings
nobody gets off
nobody asks
when will we be there

The stops go by
LAST STOP
LAST STOP
and nobody gets off

The seats keep quiet
the poles shine
the tickets sleep in the machine
like children

And the driver
the old driver
looks in his mirror
and sees the emptiness

So he sings
softly at first
then out loud
off-key and happy

He sings of the sea
where he has never been
of the girl
who did not marry him

Because for the first time all day
nobody hears him
for the first time in his life
the tram is only his

Tomorrow he will carry people again
to work to work to work
but tonight
the line is finally free

---

## 446. GRANDFATHER'S SHOES

The grandson got grandfather's shoes
black and heavy
polished like a mirror
one last time by grandfather's hand

He tried them on
and sank into them
as into two boats
anchored in the hall

His foot swims in them
no heel anywhere
no toe anywhere
he is alone in them as in a house

Mother says
you'll grow into them
father says
it takes time

Put on two pairs of socks
grandmother said
and she turned to the window
and looked outside a long while

The boy walks down the hallway
clack clack clack
the shoes slide off him
grandfather's walk slides off him

He set them by the bed
toes toward the door
as if in the morning
they might go somewhere alone

And every night
he lies down beside them barefoot
and measures
how much he has grown already

In a few years
they will fit him just right
and he will walk through the city
and he will not know

that he walks
in his grandfather's steps
over the cobbles
that remember him

---

## 447. THE TREASURE MAP WITHOUT THE CROSS

They found a treasure map
yellowed in a box
with paths and arrows
and the word HERE

But the cross
that one got lost
it faded from the paper
like a tear

Where is here?
asks Annie
here is everywhere
says Tommy

So they dig
they dig the garden
from fence to fence
from morning to night

They dug up a worm
pink and sleepy
they dug up a stone
that looked like a heart

They dug up a shard
blue as the sea
and a root
tangled like a question

They dug up an old key
to no door at all
and a button
from a coat with no owner

Mother calls them to lunch
they do not hear
they have earth under their nails
and the whole world in their palms

The garden is dug up
the neighbor shakes his head
where is the treasure? he asks
and the children laugh

The treasure was in the digging
says Annie that evening
and she falls asleep dirty and rich
the map beneath her pillow

---

## 448. THE CANDLE THAT BURNED OUT

On the cake stood a candle
a single one
and it burned
for the whole room

Close your eyes
they said
and make a wish
and tell it to no one

Silence
full of seconds
full of wishes
full of wax

And then
all at once
they drew a breath
and blew

The candle went out
went out completely
the little body broke
the wick turned black

Someone said what a shame
someone said one more
someone was already cutting the cake
and laughing

But above the cake
above the heads
above the laughter
the smoke was rising

A thin grey ribbon
upward
to the ceiling
to the ceiling and beyond

And it carried the wish
carefully
as a mother carries a child
across the dark river

The candle did not die in vain
it died so that
the wish
could finally fly away

Some endings
are not endings
they are doors
opened outward

---

## 449. THE NOTEBOOK WITH ONE SENTENCE

At the secondhand shop among the books
lay a notebook
a cheap notebook
with a dog-eared corner

Inside a single sentence
written in pencil
TODAY I DECIDED TO LIVE DIFFERENTLY
and then nothing

Page after page
empty
white
waiting

Who wrote it?
no one knows
when did he write it?
no one knows

He decided
and then?
he left the shop of time
and left the sentence all alone

A customer bought it
for a few coins
carried it home
and sat a long while

Then he took a pencil
an old pencil
and under the sentence
he wrote a second one

AND I AM STILL DOING IT TODAY
he wrote
and he smiled
to himself

And in the morning
he carried the notebook back
and set it among the books
the dog-eared corner facing out

Some books
are not written by one person
they are written by a relay of strangers
who will never meet

The empty pages
are not an ending
they are an invitation
written for you

---

## 450. TWO DROPS ON THE WINDOW

It is raining
and two children sit by the window
they are bored
and they stare at the glass

I bet you
said the girl
that my drop
runs down first

And I bet mine does
said the boy
and he pressed his nose
to the cold glass

They chose two drops
two fat drops
at the top edge of the window
and they waited

Three two one go
Mine is moving
No mine is moving
You cheated

The drops trembled
hesitated
stopped
as if they were listening

Mine is winning
No mine is
wait
what is it doing

Because halfway down
the two drops
met
and merged into one

A single drop
fat and heavy
slid to the sill
alone

And the children fell silent
who won?
nobody knew
and it did not matter

The race ended
in an embrace
whoever rushes the same way
in the end becomes one

---

## 451. THE OLD CIRCUS ELEPHANT

The circus went bankrupt
they folded the big top
and the lions left by train
somewhere to the north

Only the elephant remained
the old elephant
his skin like a map
his eyes like wells

They let him into a meadow
green and wide
no fence no chain
no people

Go they told him
you are free
the grass is yours
the sky is yours

And the elephant
took a step
and then another
and he turned

He walked in a circle
round and round
in a ring as big as the arena
that stayed in his legs

The untouched grass rippled
all around
only in the middle
he trampled the old circle

The farmers watched
why won't he go farther?
he is free after all
he has the whole world after all

But the elephant
did not know the straight line
all his life they taught him the circle
and the circle is hard to unlearn

Every morning he walked in a circle
and trumpeted softly
to an old music
that no one played anymore

And freedom lay all around him
green and patient
and waited
for him to learn it

---

## 452. THE GARDEN GATE THAT CREAKS

The garden gate creaks
for long years now
it creaks at every opening
it creaks at every closing

Oil it
says the neighbor
I have oil
I'll bring you some

No thank you
says the old man
and he goes back into the house
and lets the gate creak

The neighbors shake their heads
what a miser
hasn't he a few drops?
can't he hear that racket?

But the old man hears
he hears well
he sits by the window
and waits for the creak

Because one creak
he knows better than all
light and quick
like a laugh

That is how the gate opens
for his daughter
when on Sundays she comes back
from far from the city from life

The others creak in the ordinary way
the postman the neighbor the wind
but that creak of hers
he knows even blind

An oiled gate
would keep silent
and he would not know
when to lift his head

The creak is the doorbell of the heart
says the old man softly
and he does not mend
even what is broken

---

## 453. THE LAST APPLE ON THE TREE

The harvest is over
the crates are full
the apples red and round
have gone to the cellar

And on the tree
right at the top
one has stayed
a single apple

You forgot it
calls the grandson
that one up there
I'll pass you the ladder

No says the orchardman
and he looks up
and rubs his hands
used to the trunk

I did not forget it
I left it there
it is too high
says the grandson

It is not too high
says the orchardman
it is just right
for whoever comes after us

The titmouse will come
and the blackbird
and the frost will give it sugar
and the wind will give it a song

Whoever harvests everything
strips tomorrow too
whoever harvests everything
forgets the guests

And so above the empty orchard
one apple shines
like a small sun
like the tithe of generosity

And the birds see it from afar
and they know
at this farmhouse
no one is forgotten

---

## 454. THE STAR THAT NO LONGER SHINES

The astronomer took the children up the hill
far from the lamps
far from the town
close to the sky

Look
he said
that bright one there
the blue one on the left

How beautiful it is
said the little girl
it shines more than all of them
and it winks at me

Yes it shines
said the astronomer
and he gripped his telescope
and was silent a while

But that star
has not shone for a long time
it went out
when our grandfathers were small

How can that be?
the children ask
but we can see it
but it is right there

Its light traveled a long way
a thousand years through the dark
a thousand years on the road
to arrive today

And it will travel farther
another thousand years
to people not yet born
onto hills that do not yet exist

Someone can be long gone
and still shine for us
the ending and the light
pass each other in time without meeting

So do not forget
said the astronomer softly
you too will shine one day
for whom nobody knows yet

---

## 455. ONE UMBRELLA FOR TWO

It's raining
and they have a single umbrella
small
round
barely enough for one

they walk pressed together
shoulder against shoulder
and each of them has
one shoulder wet
and one shoulder dry

the outside shoulder streams
the inside shoulder warms

You're not getting wet? he asks
And you you're not getting wet? she asks

and both of them lie
both have a drowned sleeve
both are smiling

Tilt it more over yourself
No over you
No over you

and on they go
tipping that little circle
this way and that
as if handing each other
a scrap of dry sky

It rains on the whole city
it rains on those who own
big umbrellas
and walk through the middle of the dry
alone

And those two
wet by half
warmed twice over
because whoever shares an umbrella
takes the rain from one side only
and with the other side
leans on someone

---

## 456. THE POSTWOMAN AND ONE HOUSE

To that house on the corner
she carries only flyers
sales on washing machines
insurance offers
furniture catalogues

never a letter
never a card
never an envelope
written by hand

Does that one get any mail? asks the neighbor
No says the postwoman
there you only slide
paper into tin

the box is always
half open
like a mouth
that waits for a word
and gets an advert

Year after year
flyers
sales
catalogues
and the name on the box
fades
until you can barely read it

One day in December
the postwoman stops
buys herself a postcard
with a cat in the snow
and writes it
herself

Keep well
from someone passing by

and slips it into that box
among the washing machine sales

because a lonely mailbox
doesn't need the whole world
it needs one hand
that remembers

---

## 457. THE PIANIST IN THE HOTEL LOBBY

He plays in the lobby
among the potted palms
among the leather armchairs
among people hurrying
to their suitcases
to the stairs
to their lives

no one listens

he plays softly
a waltz
then another
the notes fall
into the carpet
into the empty

Who are you playing for? asks the manager
For no one? he says
but he smiles
because he's lying

behind the desk stands
the night clerk
young
tired
his night is just as long
as the pianist's

and he listens
even staring at the guest book
even counting the keys
he listens with one ear
the one that doesn't sleep

At midnight the pianist stops
and the clerk lifts his head
One more? he asks
the slow one

and the pianist plays
the slow one
for him alone

because an empty lobby
is not empty
when two keep watch in it
and music needs only
one ear
that doesn't sleep

---

## 458. THE COIN IN THE COBBLES

Between two cobblestones
a coin is stuck
trodden
into the mud
into the years

people bend down for it
scratch at it with a nail
It won't come says one
It's holding says another
and they walk on

it stays there
a little yellow moon
in the grey stone

What's it worth? asks the child
Nothing says the father
since no one can pry it out

but the child looks
and the coin shines

the baker steps on it
the postwoman steps on it
the whole city steps on it
every sole
polishes it a little

year after year
it grows brighter
it glows like a small sun
driven into the ground

Do you see the riches? says the child
the kind no one owns
the kind that belong to all

because a wealth
that no one touches
belongs to the street
and the richest of all
is the pavement
where the poor walk

---

## 459. SOMEONE ELSE'S FLOWERS

The neighbor left
on holiday
to the sea
and he forgot
the flowers on his sill

geraniums
begonias
one drowsy fig tree

They'll dry out says the woman downstairs
They'll dry out says the mailman
and on they go

but the man next door
every evening
takes the watering can
climbs over the railing
and secretly waters
someone else's window

Whose are they? his daughter asks
I don't know he says
but they're thirsty

he waters them on Friday
he waters them on Sunday
he never signs
he never rings

The neighbor comes back
tanned
a suitcase of seashells
and he's amazed

They survived?
I thought they were done for

and the man next door
says nothing
and smiles into his can

because kindness
is often done
under a borrowed name
with no thanks
and watering
knows no fences

---

## 460. THE GIVEN STONE

The child came in from outside
with dirty knees
and in his hand
a stone

ordinary
grey
rounded by the river

For you he says
it's a treasure

The mother looks
at the stone
at the hand

A treasure? she asks
and what's inside it?

Nothing says the child
I just found it
and it was pretty
and I thought of you right away

The mother takes it
weighs it in her hand
cold
heavy
quite ordinary

and slips it into her pocket

She carries it on Fridays
she carries it in winter
she carries it for years
among the keys and the coins
she rubs it with her thumb
waiting for the tram
waiting for the doctor
waiting for nothing

people have rings
people have gold
she has a river stone

because worth
does not come from the thing
worth comes from the hand
that gives
and the heaviest stone in the world
is the one
given with love

---

## 461. THE LAST SNOWFLAKE

Winter is ending
the snow melts
the roofs drip
the puddles grow

and the last snowflake falls
one
the last
of the whole winter

Catch it says the brother
I'll catch it says the child
and holds out a palm

the flake comes down
slowly
it turns
as if unwilling

and lands
on the warm palm

I've got it cries the child
I've got the last snowflake!

but the palm is warm
and the flake
is only a drop

Where did it go? asks the child
It was here
it was white
and now it's water

and the child cries
cries that he didn't save it
that he held it
and it vanished

The mother wipes the tear
with that same palm

You did save it
she says
just not the way you wanted

because some kinds of beauty
can only be had
by letting them go
and to save a snowflake
means
to let it melt

---

## 462. THE WALLED-UP DOOR

In the old wall
a door remains
bricked up
during the works

an outline
an arch
a stone of another color
and no handle

it leads nowhere

Where did it lead? asks the child
Nowhere says the mason
nowhere now
behind it there's a brick
and behind the brick another brick

but people
passing in front of it
lower their voices

the man who's arguing
falls silent
the lovers stop laughing
the old woman nods hello

Why are you whispering? asks the child
I don't know says the old woman
it's just that by a door
you go quiet

because a door
even walled up
still promises
somewhere

behind it perhaps there was a room
behind it perhaps there was a garden
behind it perhaps there was someone
who was waiting

and people feel it
even through the brick

you can wall up a door
you can wall up a window
you can wall up a whole house

but possibility
cannot be walled up

---

## 463. THE OLD BRIDGE AND THE NEW

Across the river
they built a new bridge
wide
concrete
four lanes
traffic lights
signs

and beside it
they left the old one
of stone
narrow
closed to cars

Tear it down says the official
It'd be a shame says the mason
and they leave it standing

over the new bridge
trucks pass
buses pass
the whole city passes
in one long rush
hurrying

on the old bridge
there is no one

only in the evening
two arrive
lean on the railing
look into the water

Where are you going? asks the constable
Nowhere they say
we're already here

then comes an old man
to feed the pigeons
then comes a painter
with his easel
then comes a child
throwing twigs into the current
Which one gets there first?

because progress
takes the traffic
and the past
keeps the beauty

and every city
needs one bridge
where nobody
is in a hurry

---

## 464. THE COLLECTION OF POEMS

The poet has written
the last line
of the last poem

the manuscript lies on the table
thick
finished
two hundred and five poems
stacked
like bricks

Is it done? he asks himself
It is he answers
and doesn't believe it

he looks at that pile
at those years
at those mornings at the table
at those pencils gnawed to the middle

and he feels sad
because a finished thing
suddenly goes mute

then he stands up
he opens the window
Let the wind read it he says

and the wind comes in
takes the first sheet
then the second
then ten
then a hundred

and the poems fly
over the rooftops
over the squares
over the river

one lands in a mailbox
one in a passerby's pocket
one in a puddle
one on the lap of an old woman
on a bench
who happened to have nothing to read

The poet looks out the window
the empty table
the empty hands
the full air

and for the first time
he smiles

because a poem
does not belong to the one who wrote it
a poem belongs
to the one who finds it

and a collection is finished
only on the day
the author
lets it go from his hand

---

## 465. THE KNIFE GRINDER

Down the street comes a bicycle with a grindstone
and the bell announces ahead of it
KNIVES SCISSORS RAZORS SHARPENED
and the houses open up
like pocket knives

The butcher brings his heavy knives
the seamstress her wedding-dress scissors
the barber his father's razor
the grandmother her little apple knife
she has kept since her wedding day
and the boys a jackknife with a stag-horn handle
found down by the river

Nobody wants anything dull
a dull knife tears bread instead of cutting it
dull scissors chew the cloth
a dull razor insults the face
a dull scythe insults the grass

And the grinder pedals
the stone spins
sparks skip across the cobbles
a little penny fireworks show
and the knives go home
sharp and satisfied
as after a good night's sleep

In the evening when the bell has gone quiet
the last customer comes
and unwraps from a rag a soldier's knife

Sharpen it?
asks the grinder

The opposite
says the man
dull it
it comes from the war
and it still cuts too well
at night I can hear it

And the grinder says nothing
he pedals
and grinds it flat
a long time
longer than all the knives of the town put together
until it is smooth and blind
like a river pebble

What do I owe you?
asks the man

Nothing
says the grinder folding up his stone
all day long I have been sharpening
and once a day
a man should dull something
so this town can get some sleep

---

## 466. THE GLAZIER

He walks the town with panes on his back
transparent wings of window glass
and he calls out long and slow
GLAAAZIER
and the streets answer him with their cracks

Here boys with a ball
there frost that painted too hard
there a window from a quarrel
last night a door slammed
a little too close to the heart

People apologize
people are ashamed of broken windows
Don't you worry madam
says the glazier with a wink
the boys with the ball feed me
the frost feeds me
even your quarrel feeds me
long life to them all

One day his apprentice asks him
Master do you really love cracks?
I do
says the glazier

Because of the bread?
Not only

And he takes the apprentice to the edge of town
to a villa with a garden
the windows whole
not one crack in thirty years

Here nobody ever broke a window
says the glazier
because here nobody ran with a ball
nobody slammed a door
nobody leaned out too far
to wave at someone

Glass only cracks where people live
he says shifting the panes on his back
unbroken windows
are just another name
for an empty house

And he walks on calling
GLAAAZIER
and he wishes all the windows of the town
a long life
and every now and then
a good hard shot with a ball

---

## 467. THE BOOKBINDER

The workshop smells of glue leather and thread
on the table lies a hospital of books
grandmother's cookbook collapsed at the cakes
a hymnal that sang for a hundred years
a world atlas with America torn away
and fairy tales read by three generations
that fell apart from love

The bookbinder takes them in her hands
like a doctor
here the binding gave way
here the thread snapped
here the spine
books break at the spine
where they were opened most

A man has brought his grandmother's cookbook
he holds it in both hands
as if it might fly off page by page
Can it still be saved?
It can
says the bookbinder
anything can
as long as no pages are lost

And what is it really that holds it together
asks the man
a book like this

Thread
says the bookbinder
plain linen thread
glue and cloth
but that only holds the paper

And the book?
A book is held by whoever keeps coming back to it
a book nobody reads
falls apart even when it's new
look
and she lifts the fairy tales from the table
this one is in shreds
and it holds together better than all the rest

The man pays and turns at the door
And people?
what are people sewn with?

Thread too
says the bookbinder threading her needle
only you can't see it
and it also tears in the place
where a person opens most

---

## 468. THE ORGANIST WITHOUT AN ORGAN

On the church door hangs a sign
CLOSED FOR TECHNICAL REASONS
the technical reasons are in their tenth year
the office has the key
the office has office hours
and office hours have no time

Inside the organ is silent
three thousand pipes full of darkness
and the organist walks past
every Sunday at ten
as he always did
he stands a moment at the door
then goes off through the town

Mister organist
what do you play on now
asks the baker's boy

On the town
says the organist
listen
the wind in the chimneys
those are the flutes
the draught under the arcades
the principal
the tram on the bridge
the pedal basses
the rain on the tin roofs
the tremolo
and the pigeons on the ledge
vox humana
even if they are only pigeons

And it's true
when the wind comes up off the river
the whole town plays
chimneys houses wires doorways
and the organist in the middle of the street
closes his eyes
and his fingers run through the air
stop after stop

They locked the church
he says
they locked the organ
but music is only air
that remembered something
and air cannot be locked

Inside the church there is silence
and technical reasons
but on Sundays at ten
when the wind blows
the whole town plays the mass
and nobody can close it down
because you cannot hang
a sign on the air

---

## 469. THE TAXI DRIVER WHO TURNS OFF THE METER

The taxi waits at the station rank
and the meter counts
kilometres minutes waiting time
all of it fairly
penny by penny

But sometimes
the driver reaches out his hand
and click
the meter goes dark

At three in the morning to the maternity ward
when the man beside him clutches a little suitcase
and has forgotten how breathing works
click

The old man who wants to see the house
that isn't there any more
just a field and one pear tree
click

The woman with two bags
who says drive
just drive
and says nothing else
click

The young man in a borrowed black suit
going to the first funeral of his life
click

The young colleague at the rank shakes his head
You'll never get rich like that
I won't
says the driver

Then why do you do it?

The meter is an honest machine
says the driver
it counts kilometres
and it counts them well
but some rides are not in kilometres
the one to the maternity ward is measured in a whole life
the one to the pear tree in fifty years going back
and the one with the two bags
that one can't be measured at all

And who is going to pay you for them?

They're already paid
says the driver starting the engine
some rides were paid for long ago
before the passenger ever got in
only with those
you must not count

---

## 470. THE SUMMER CINEMA PROJECTIONIST

The open-air cinema behind the brewery
a screen stretched between two poplars
three hundred chairs under the stars
at the back the projection booth
and in the booth the projectionist
last season
next year they say a car park

No rain of spectators tonight
says the ticket lady closing her window
three tickets today
yesterday none

And the projectionist projects anyway
for three
for one
for nobody
the reel turns
the beam flies over the empty chairs
and in the beam dance the moths and the dust
the most faithful audience in the world

A man from the town hall came to look
Who are you showing this for
when nobody came?

For the moths
says the projectionist
for the poplars
and for the screen
forty years it has carried
lovers soldiers horses and the sea
a film that is never shown
dies in its can
would you let the sea die?

The man from the town hall writes that down
and leaves

On the last night of the season
the ticket lady sells a single ticket
to the projectionist
he loads the reel
starts the machine
runs down the steps
and sits in the middle of three hundred chairs

Forty years he showed films
and only today
for the first time
does he see one whole
from the right side

And the moths watch with him
and the poplars rustle along with the music
and when THE END appears on the screen
it isn't true
it's only a caption

---

## 471. THE MUSIC BOX REPAIRMAN

In the courtyard workshop a hundred small musics are sleeping
music boxes with cranks
ballerinas stopped mid-pirouette
brass cylinders studded with pins
like a starry sky
and combs that know one single song
but know it by heart

People bring them in broken
this one is silent
this one has lost three notes
and most often
this one is stuck
the same note over and over
one single note
like a hiccup
like a reproach

A woman brought in her mother's box
Swan Lake
stuck on one note since winter
It plays and plays that one place
says the woman
and I can't listen to it any more
and I don't know how to switch it off

The repairman winds the box
and listens
the note repeats
always the same
always the same way

It isn't an illness
says the repairman
just one bent pin on the cylinder
you see
the melody came to a certain place
and cannot get past it
so it goes back
and tries again
and again
like all of us

Then he takes his tweezers
straightens the pin
by a hair
and winds the spring

And Swan Lake stirs
passes the place
as if it were nothing
plays on
to the very end
and fades

The woman is crying
not for the note that kept sticking
but for the ones
that came after it

There there
says the repairman handing her the box
a melody must be allowed to finish
especially a sad one
finished it hurts once
stuck it hurts forever

---

## 472. THE STATION PORTER

At the station by platform two
stands a porter with his trolley
forty years now
and more fates have passed through his hands
than through the priest's and the doctor's put together

He knows the newlyweds' suitcase
light and skittish
almost empty
after all they mostly carry each other

He knows the travelling salesman's case
battered at the corners
like him

He knows the emigrant's suitcase
a whole flat pressed into thirty kilos
the eiderdown the photos the father's clock

He knows the soldier's case
the student's case tied up with string
the actress's trunk that weighs more than the actress
and the doctor's little bag
where somebody's life lies sleeping

The young stationmaster asks him
You always know where people are going?

Not where
says the porter
I only know whether they're going toward something
or away from something

And how can you tell?

By the weight
a suitcase going toward something is light
even when it's heavy
a suitcase going away from something is heavy
even when there's almost nothing in it

The heaviest suitcase of my life
was small and nearly empty
a comb a shirt and a photograph
I carried it for a lady to the morning train
and my arm ached for a week

What was in it?

Nothing
says the porter
that's just it nothing
everything else she had left at home

The weight of a suitcase isn't measured in kilos
he says taking hold of his trolley
it's measured by everything
that stayed behind at home

---

## 473. THE SIGN PAINTER

The sign painter stands on his ladder
scraping an old shop sign
the new owner calls up from below
Paint over all of it
it's going to say PHARMACY

And under the paint letters appear
GROCERIES
and under the groceries
HALLIDAY'S FINE FOODS
and under the fine foods
BAKERY FRANCIS CHAMBERS AND SON
and under the bakery
only a golden shadow of lettering
whose name nobody can read any more

The whole street is written over under the paint
like an old envelope
address on top of address
nothing but addressees who moved away
to somewhere the post does not deliver

At the bakery the sign painter stops
that R with a little tail like a swallow
he knows that R
only one man ever made an R like that
his father
the ladder may have stood exactly here
and up on it a young fellow
whistling

Have you covered it all?
calls the owner from below

All covered
says the sign painter
and he paints the new name
black and gleaming
proper work properly done
only he thins the paint
a little more than usual

Businesses die
he says to himself up on the ladder
names just move one layer down
and one day another fellow will stand here with a scraper
and I want him
to have something to read

---

## 474. THE BEEKEEPER ON THE ROOF

On the roof of a tower block
eight floors above the street
between the aerials and the lift shafts
stand three beehives
and Mr Smith with a veil over his face

The neighbours shake their heads
Honey from the city? they say
from all this concrete?
from all this smoke?
honey comes from the countryside
like grandfather
like Sunday

The bees don't know that
in the morning they fly out over the street
and see what the neighbours never see
the lindens by the post office
the acacias along the tracks
Mrs Horton's balcony full of window boxes
mulleins on the demolition site
little gardens behind the garages
a whole city in bloom
that believes itself grey

In autumn Mr Smith rings the doorbells
a jar of honey in each hand
and on the box a sign
DON'T BELIEVE IT? TASTE IT

Mrs Horton tastes
and raises her eyebrows
How strange
it tastes like our balcony

Of course it does, says the beekeeper
your window boxes eighth floor
the linden by the post office ground floor
the whole street in one spoonful

And the neighbours stand in the corridor
jars of honey in their hands
and for the first time in all these years
they taste the house they live in
and it is sweet

---

## 475. THE HEDGEHOG AND THE AUTUMN LEAVES

All September the hedgehog made his bed
leaf by leaf
maple for sleeping
oak for a cover
linden under his head
a quilt for the whole winter

On Monday came the men in orange
with brooms and bags
and a paper from the town hall
DECREE ON AUTUMN CLEANING

They swept up the maple
swept up the oak
swept up the linden
they loaded winter into bags
and carted it off to the dump

The hedgehog stood on the bare lawn
and said nothing
hedgehogs don't go to town hall
hedgehogs don't file complaints
a hedgehog has only his spines
and against a decree that isn't enough

Only one sweeper
the oldest one
left in the corner of the park behind the bench
one pile unswept
he forgot it carefully
all afternoon he kept forgetting it
and in the evening he came back to check
that it was properly forgotten

In his report he wrote
sector twelve cleaned
except for the corner
the corner could not be finished
for technical reasons

And the technical reason
was already snuffling in the corner
burrowing into the oak and the maple
and sleeping
all winter it slept
on the only mistake
the town hall made that year
on purpose

---

## 476. THE MOLE AND THE GARDENER

The gardener had a lawn like a billiard table
English style
mowed on Sunday mornings
measured by eye and by ruler
the pride of the whole street

And under the lawn lived a mole
who knew nothing about billiards
and everything about soil

Morning after morning a new molehill
black as spite
and the gardener with his spade
with his hose
with traps from the hardware store
with a smoke bomb from the neighbour
waged the war
that gardeners have waged since forever
and that moles since forever have won

This is my garden
he shouted into the grass
I planted it
I water it
I mow it

And from below silence
only corridors of silence
a mole doesn't argue
a mole digs

Then one summer the drought came
the lawns on the street turned yellow
one after another
billiard tables of straw

Only that one lawn stayed green
drilled through and through
aired down to the deep
the rain from June remembered it
in the tunnels

And the gardener stood with his hose hanging
and for the first time in his life
he knelt down in the grass
and said in a downward direction
all right
you understand the cellar
I understand the roof
and the garden
the garden is the whole house

---

## 477. THE FOX IN THE CITY

At three in the morning the city belongs to the fox
the traffic lights blink amber
just for her
and she walks down the middle of the street
as if she'd come to pick up the keys

At the restaurant she inspects the bin
half a chicken
a whole loaf
an apple with nothing wrong with it
strange hunters these people
thinks the fox
they hunt all day long
and in the evening they throw away the catch

The shop windows stay lit
over coats that nobody wears
in the windows a blue flicker
people sitting at fires that give no warmth
and the dogs
the dogs sleep on cushions behind doors
and whimper in their dreams
because they can smell it
this street without a leash

The fox doesn't judge
the fox just takes notes
they built themselves a forest of stone
and are scared of the dark in it
they invented time
and it keeps running out on them
they shut themselves in dens
and they call the dens home

Only by the station she slows down
on a bench a man is sleeping
newspapers instead of leaves
this one is one of mine
thinks the fox
he sleeps outside too
he didn't fit in either

And before daybreak
she vanishes through the park
lies down under a bush and sleeps
and leaves them the city
lends it to them whole
until three in the morning

---

## 478. THE HEN WHO FLEW

In the Smiths' yard it happened on a Tuesday
the dog was chasing the hen
the hen was running
the fence was close
the wings remembered

Two metres
maybe three
she flew
over the ground over her shadow over her own fear
and she landed in the nettles
but she flew

The yard assembled that very evening
the rooster summed it up from the dunghill
hens do not fly
that is a scientific fact
the ducks nodded
the geese hissed their agreement

It was a jump, said the old goose
a jump with flapping
every one of us knows that

You didn't see it, said the hen
I was up there
I saw the henhouse roof from above
I saw all of you from above
you are smaller than you think

That settled it
whoever claims we are small
is a liar

Since then she doesn't speak of it
she pecks her grain like the others
only sometimes in the evening
she looks up over the fence
where the swallows pass

And the swallows know
swallows see everything from above
the yard the fence and that Tuesday
and when they fly past
they tip a wing to her
the way you greet
a colleague

---

## 479. THE OLD CARP IN THE FOUNTAIN

In the fountain on the square lives a carp
big as a forearm
grey as the cobblestones
an old resident

They bought him in December from the barrel
carried him home in a plastic bag
the bathtub was already filled
and in the kitchen the knife was being sharpened

But the child cried
and cried so well
that in the night the whole family
carried him in silence to the square
and let him go in the fountain
among the stone tritons

Since then he has survived everything
twenty Christmases
twenty carps that ended otherwise

People come to the fountain
and throw coins
they turn them in their fingers
close their eyes
make a wish
and toss

The carp swims among the wishes
the bottom glitters
love health money
the jackpot a trip around the world
the floor of the fountain is paved with desires

The children ask
will it come true?
and the parents say
only if you tell no one

The carp alone says nothing
and none of them knows
that one wish has already come true
that it swims there before their eyes
scaly and grey
for the twentieth year

the wish of a child
who one night in December
cried so loud
that Christmas itself
gave in

---

## 480. THE BATS IN THE CHURCH ATTIC

To the church on the hill
only three grandmothers and a draught still come
the priest drives in on the last Sunday of the month
the organist died
the bell rings only for funerals

But up in the attic
above the vault with its painted heaven
hang the bats
a whole parish of bats
head down
patient as candles

They sleep through all the sermons
but they keep watch with all the stars
they ask for nothing
they promise nothing
they are simply there
which of all the prayers
is the hardest one

The sexton wanted to drive them out
vermin he said
droppings on the beams
squeaking in the rafters
but the priest shook his head
let them be
they are the last ones
who haven't moved away

People get christened married buried
they lock the heavy door behind them
and go home
the bats stay
in December in July
they keep a watch under the roof
that nobody asked for

They hang on and they don't let go
the priest tells the grandmothers
all winter darkness and frost
and they hang on and they don't let go
I have no better definition of faith

And if God one day looked down
from above as is his habit
through the slate through the painted heaven
he would see the empty pews
and the parish of bats
and since from above everything is upside down
it would be exactly them
the only ones in the whole church
standing upright

---

## 481. THE SNAIL AND THE RACE

On the path along the gardens
a race was run that morning
nobody had announced it
but everybody ran

The ant overtook him
the lizard overtook him
the dog overtook him
and even the centipede
who has so many legs
he doesn't even have to try

Children on bicycles rang their bells
out of the way snail out of the way
you'll never get anywhere like that

Where? asked the snail
but the children were already gone

At noon the postman overtook him
in the afternoon a pensioner with a cane
and towards evening even the shadow
stretching out towards the east

All hurrying the same way
home
to shut the door behind them
and say at last

Only the snail was in no hurry
he carried his house on his back
all of it
kitchen bedroom roof
and wherever he liked it
there he was home

At night he slept in the middle of the path
while the windows went dark one by one
and if he could speak
he would have said
I didn't win
but I am the only one
who crossed the finish line
right at the start

---

## 482. THE MAGPIE AND THE WEDDING RING

She left it on the ledge by the sink
just for a moment
just while she did the dishes
a small gold band
with a date and a name engraved inside

A magpie doesn't read dates
a magpie doesn't read names
a magpie sees a shine
and a shine is a shine

Three seconds of wings
and all that was left of the ring
was a wet little circle on the sill

She cried all evening
thirty years she said
thirty years I never took it off
and now it's gone

The husband said nothing
and in the morning he borrowed a ladder

Up in the poplar in the nest
lay a treasure
a lemonade cap
a scrap of silver foil
a silver button
a piece of Christmas tinsel
and in the middle of it all
thirty years of marriage

He took the ring
and left the foil where it was
so she would have something too

Down in the kitchen he went down
on one knee that creaked by now
and slid the ring on her finger a second time
like back then
except both their hands were shaking

And as she laughed and cried at once
he said
you see
the magpie only stole the shine
that's the only thing that can be stolen
the rest
those thirty years
we carry somewhere else
where no magpie can reach

---

## 483. THE SQUIRREL AND THE FORGOTTEN NUTS

All autumn she worked
a hazelnut here
an acorn there
a beechnut under a stone
and she carefully remembered it all

Or so she thought

In winter she found the one under the spruce
and the one by the stump
but the third
the fifth
the ninth
the earth might as well have swallowed them

Silly squirrel laugh the chickadees
she forgot where she hid her supper

Except in spring
a little oak came up from the lawn
a hazel a bit further on
a beech by the fence
exactly there
where the squirrel forgot

The old gardener sees it every year
he leans on his rake and tells his grandson
you see that oak by the path
a hundred years old
which means a hundred years ago
some squirrel forgot
where it put its lunch

And the whole forest? asks the grandson

The whole forest
nods the gardener
the whole forest is forgotten nuts
memory feeds you for one winter
forgetting
plants trees

And up in the branches of that oak
planted by her great-great-grandmother
by losing it
a squirrel is hiding a nut right now
and already can't remember where

And that is just as well

---

## 484. THE BLACKBIRD WHO SANG IN DECEMBER

On the seventeenth of December
at half past four in the dark
a blackbird on an aerial
launched into his whole spring song
from beginning to end
leaving nothing out

The people at the bus stop raised their heads
out of their scarves and their phones

He's gone mad said a man in a hat
he's got the date wrong
It's the streetlights said a woman with shopping bags
they shine on him all night
and he thinks it's dawn
It's this warm winter said a student
nature is out of joint
it's a warning

Only a little girl asked
And what if he knows something?

The grown-ups smiled
how could he know
a blackbird has no calendar

Exactly said the little girl
that's why he can't get it wrong

The bus arrived
the doors opened
the doors closed
and it left three people lighter
because they stayed standing
under the aerial
in December
listening to spring

Whether the blackbird was mistaken
or knew something more
nobody ever found out
spring came as always
at the end of March
pretending
it had never dropped by in December

Only those three from the bus stop
ever since then every December
walk past the aerial
slowly
just in case

---

## 485. THE GRAMOPHONE AND ONE RECORD

The gramophone in the corner of the room
plays one record
always the same one
for years now

The grandson asks
Grandpa why don't you buy another?
The shop has crates of them
jazz waltzes the whole world
for next to nothing

Grandpa lifts the needle
gently
the way you lift a sleeping kitten

This record
has a scratch in the third song
the needle skips there every time
as if it stumbled on a doorstep

That's a flaw though says the grandson
they give your money back for that

It's not a flaw says grandpa
it's the evening
your grandmother danced in this room
and bumped the little gramophone table
laughing as she did it
and the record wrote it all down

A new record would play music
clean music without one mistake
this one plays that evening
little table and all
laughter and all

And so the gramophone in the corner
plays one record
and when the needle skips
grandpa half closes his eyes

In that spot it isn't the needle skipping
it's the heart

---

## 486. THE TYPEWRITER MISSING A LETTER

An old typewriter
types every letter
except one
the M jammed
some thirty years ago

Buy a new one said the dealer
this thing belongs in a museum

But the old man bought nothing
he learned to go the long way round
instead of sea he writes ocean
instead of a man in love he writes a lost man
instead of I love you he writes
you know

It can be done he says
language is wide as a river
there's always another bank

Only one word
cannot be gone around
Marie

Marie is Marie
no language in the world
has a second word for Marie

And so each time
a letter arrives at her name
the old man rolls the page up a little
takes his fountain pen
and writes the missing letter in by hand

His letters are straight
and silver as railway tracks
and right in the middle of them
one letter slightly trembling
warm
alive

The whole letter is typed by machine
only she
only she is written by hand

---

## 487. THE SUITCASE COVERED IN STICKERS

In the attic under the beam
sleeps a suitcase
covered in stickers
HOTEL BRISTOL VIENNA
GRAND HOTEL NAPOLI
ISTANBUL
LISBOA
and one with a camel

The granddaughter runs her finger over it
as over a globe
Grandpa you've been everywhere
why don't you go anywhere anymore?

Grandpa slowly turns the suitcase around
You see here on the side
this empty spot?

I was saving it
for the most beautiful city
I looked for it all my life
Vienna was all whipped cream
Naples shouted its beauty from every window
Istanbul smelled so good nobody slept
and in Lisbon I cried
and to this day I don't know why

But the sticker of the most beautiful city
isn't sold at any kiosk in the world

Why not?

Because the most beautiful city
is known by one thing only
it's where you unpack the suitcase
carry it up to the attic
and don't need it for fifty years

The granddaughter looks around
at the old attic
the beams the roof above her head

So that's why the spot is empty

It isn't empty says grandpa
that is the sticker
it just can only be read
from the inside

---

## 488. DAD'S READING GLASSES

Dad left few things behind
a watch that kept going
a coat that fit his brother
a small box of fishing hooks
and reading glasses
in a case with one worn corner

The son put them on
just like that
to try
the way you try on another man's life

And the world went blurry
the window melted
the street vanished in fog
the mountains beyond the town dissolved

Only the near stayed sharp
his own hands
the newspaper on the table
the photo in its frame
the crumbs beside the bread

So this is how dad saw
the son says to himself
the distances blurred
and up close every little letter

All my life I blamed him
for not caring about the world
for never asking about my travels
only how are you
what are you eating
aren't you cold

Dad didn't see far
dad saw near
and near
is where we were sitting

Now the son wears those glasses at night
when the newsprint starts to swim
they fit him at the very first try
as if the optician had ground them for two

Eyes are inherited
only slowly
you have to grow into them

---

## 489. THE NIGHT-SHIFT THERMOS

Thirty years of night shifts
thirty years of three in the morning
boiler room shop floor gatehouse
and in the bag a thermos
green dented
with a lid that serves as a cup

At three in the morning
the town is cold as a door handle
the dark stands leaning on the gate
and a man could almost believe
there's no one left in the world

Then you unscrew the lid
and the thermos steams
hot tea
hot after twelve hours
sugared exactly right

The maker printed on it
KEEPS HEAT 12 HOURS
thirty years says the workman
and counting

Now he's retired
the thermos stands on the shelf
and sometimes he wakes at three
just like that
out of habit
the body remembers its shifts

One night he asked his wife
how that tea
stayed hot the whole night through

A thermos holds heat says his wife
but it doesn't make any
someone has to pour the heat in
evening after evening
for thirty years

The workman holds the lid in both hands
like back then on the shop floor
All those years I thought
it was a good thermos

It was says his wife
pouring the tea
but even a thermos
is only a messenger

---

## 490. GREAT-GRANDMOTHER'S SEWING MACHINE

In the corner of the bedroom stands
a sewing machine
treadle-worked
black with the gold letters SINGER
great-grandmother's

It sewed the wedding dress of twenty-nine
little shirts for four children
cut from a single bolt of cloth
aprons and trousseau monograms
curtains for three kitchens

In the war it turned coats inside out
the lining became the face
and for a little while
the poverty didn't show

It shortened trousers handed down
lengthened sleeves before winter
and the black dress it sewed twice
that one is not spoken of at home

Now on top of it stand
a plant in its pot
photos in silver frames
wedding christening wedding
and no one in the family remembers
the true rhythm of the treadle
heel toe heel toe

But it doesn't sew anymore says the son-in-law
it just takes up space

It doesn't take up space says grandmother
running her palm along the iron
it holds it

And maybe she is right
a hundred years it held the family together
stitch by stitch
dress by dress

Now it sews no longer
and holds the family still
photo beside photo
like an altar
where the service goes on

---

## 491. LAST YEAR'S CALENDAR

In the kitchen hangs a new calendar
it does its job honestly
page after page it runs
right now July
with a picture of a flowering meadow

But in the hallway
still hangs last year's
open at October
last year's October
with a picture of rowan berries

Visitors correct it with their eyes
That one can come down now
they say carefully
it's the new year after all
and half of it gone

You forgot to take it down

We didn't forget
says the lady of the house
and wipes the dust off the rowan berries

That October
someone still walked down this hallway
someone who cannot
be turned to November

But time moves on
say the visitors

Let it move says the lady of the house
through the kitchen let it move
there we don't stand in its way
but one wall in the house
one wall has the right to stand still

And so July ripens in the kitchen
meadows harvests and snow take turns
and in the hallway
the same rowan berries bloom on

The only month in the world
that never ended
because nobody turned it

---

## 492. THE CRACKED CUP

In the cupboard behind glass
stand the cups for guests
white with a blue rim
whole
without a single flaw
six of them
like soldiers on parade

And in front on a hook
hangs one cracked cup
glued back together
the crack like lightning
across a whole porcelain sky

We don't give it to guests
a guest might say
good heavens it's broken
and he would be right
and he wouldn't be

The daughter asked one day
mum why do you drink from the cracked one
we have whole ones after all

And mum turned the cup in her hands
This one cracked the morning
grandpa died
it slipped from my fingers
and dad glued it that evening
two hours he sat over the pieces
not speaking
that was his way of crying

Whole cups says mum
can still be broken
this one has that behind it
there is nothing in the world
you can drink from more calmly

And that is why the guests
get porcelain white as snow
and we at home get this one
with the lightning

Guests are given
what is whole
the family is given
what is true

---

## 493. THE SLED IN SUMMER

July
the asphalt melting in the yard
wasps besieging the apricots
and in the cellar under the stairs
between the preserves and the old bicycle
a sled is waiting

Wooden
with rusty runners
the rope coiled up
like a sleeping snake

The boy finds it
while hunting for his ball
drags it out to the yard
sits on it
in the middle of summer
and doesn't move

A sled in the grass
a boat on dry land

Mum why won't it go?
Wait for the snow
How long?
Half a year
Half a year!

For the boy an eternity
for the sled nothing at all

A sled knows how to wait
it's the sled's trade
eleven months of dark
for three weeks of glory
and not so much as a creak

Everything in the cellar is waiting anyway
the preserves wait for the Sunday cake
the skis wait for the mountains
the tinsel waits for Christmas
things never ask how much longer
things know
that everything has its snow

And then one December morning
the boy looks out the window
and flies down the stairs
straight to the cellar

The sled is ready
of course it's ready
it was ready the whole time

It was only winter
that was taking its time

---

## 494. THE OFFICIAL STAMP

All day long it jumps
from ink pad to paper
from ink pad to paper
thump and it's the truth
thump and it's official
thump and you may be born
thump and you may build
thump and you may be buried

Without it a paper is just paper
an application just a plea
a certificate just words
and words are not to be trusted

One Friday around four
the clerk's tired hand slipped
and the stamp came down beside the form
right onto the desk

A round imprint
with the emblem in the middle
in the plain wood

The clerk froze
she wanted to wipe it off
and then she looked at it
for a long time

For the first time in twenty years it struck her
how much of the world
runs around without a stamp
rain falls without a permit
starlings head south without a form
love has no file number
and yet it comes into force
on the day of delivery

Birth has a stamp
marriage has a stamp
death has a stamp
only the life in between
walks the world uncertified
and strangely enough it counts

She kept the desk
never had it replaced
it stands there to this day
the only piece of furniture in the building
officially certified
to exist

The people queuing in the corridor
exist meanwhile just like that
at their own risk

---

## 495. THE WAITING ROOM

The doctor's waiting room
smells of iodine and old paper
three coats on the coat rack
that have never met

A man with his arm in a sling
a girl coughing into her sleeve
an old lady with a shopping bag
sitting exactly one chair apart
as politeness requires

Magazines on the table
the princess is getting married
the princess divorced long ago
the horoscope says what to do in August
which August it was
nobody knows anymore
a recipe for perfect Christmas cookies
from a Christmas long since digested

Nobody speaks
and yet they understand each other
one cough answers another
one sigh another sigh
it is a shared silence
like a blanket over the knees

Next, calls the nurse

You go first, the old lady tells the man with the arm
I'm in no hurry
and then to the girl
go on, dear, you have school
I have all the time in the world

When the waiting room is empty
the doctor comes to the door
Mrs Smith, nothing wrong again?

Nothing, doctor
at home the silence is only mine
here for a while it was ours

---

## 496. THE LAST NEWSSTAND

The newsstand on the corner
is small as a confessional
and Mr Barton has kept it
for fifty years

The sports paper for Mr Taylor
crosswords for Mrs Blake
the political daily for the doctor
who curses as he reads it
and takes it all the same

Mr Barton knows everyone by name
and by name he also knows the ones
who come no longer
Mr Taylor left in April
his sports paper lay on the counter for a week
before Mr Barton admitted to himself
that Mr Taylor no longer needed the scores

The young don't buy papers
they carry the news in their pockets
faster than ink
Mr Barton, what's paper for
when it's all on the phone?

It's all there, nods Mr Barton
only in there nobody says
good morning Mr Smith
is your mother home from the hospital yet?

One Monday the newsstand is closed
Tuesday too
on Wednesday a sign appears in the window
written in a stranger's hand

The news that the last newsstand had closed
ran in no newspaper
it went from mouth to mouth
along the corner the street the square
the way news used to travel
before

---

## 497. THE CARPET RACK

In the courtyard between the houses
stands the iron carpet rack
painted green once
the green no longer remembers

On Friday mornings the women came
carpets over their shoulders
and beat them
dust flew up into the sun
and in it you could see
a whole week of one household

Nobody beats carpets anymore
they're vacuumed at home in silence
the dust is carried out in sealed bags
like a secret

But the children
the children hang from it upside down
doing rolls and flips and cartwheels
perching on it like sparrows
settling from up there
all the affairs of the courtyard

In spring a man came with a clipboard
measured the rack and wrote
installation no longer serves its purpose
removal recommended

No purpose? asks a girl
hanging upside down at that very moment
it serves
it holds the sky down
and the earth up
no other installation in the courtyard can do that

The man with the clipboard looked at the sky below
and for the first time in years
his head spun
He left the box empty

The rack still stands
the only installation in the courtyard
ever to be promoted
from work
to play

---

## 498. THE PASSAGEWAY

Between Long Street and Short Street
there is a passageway
it isn't on the maps
it isn't on the signs
the GPS swears there's a wall there

And yet every day it carries
a hundred pairs of feet
the baker's wife shortening her morning
schoolboys stretching their way home from school
a man with a dog
the dog knows it better than the man

The passage has seen
a first kiss in the alcove
a last quarrel in the same spot
a man sheltering from the rain
who met his future wife
sheltering from the same rain
a boy crying there so they wouldn't see at home
a thief ran through it once
and the constable ran through after him
both knew the way
both were local

A tourist stands over his map
shaking his head
there's nothing here, he tells his phone
and the phone agrees

And past him goes a grandmother with her shopping
she vanishes into the wall
as if she walked through it every day
because she walks through it every day

A city has arteries
those are on the maps
and then it has veins
that only the body knows

---

## 499. THE SECOND-HAND BOOKSHOP

In the second-hand bookshop by the bridge
the books smell
of being finished
or left unfinished
you can tell by the spine

On the first page of the first volume
I love you, Christmas 1961
price in pencil twenty crowns

In a book of verses about the sea
To my Annie for her graduation, Dad
Annie may have grandchildren by now
and her graduation lies here
for fifteen

In a cookbook
Good luck at the stove, Mother
next to the recipe for beef in cream sauce
a stain of beef in cream sauce
the recipe proved itself

A customer leafs through
and suddenly looks up
Sir, this dedication
couldn't it be erased?
It spoils the book

The bookseller adjusts his glasses
It couldn't, young man
and even if it could
I wouldn't do it

That lady from sixty-one
may be gone by now
may have long forgotten
but somebody loved her at Christmas
and wrote it down
and it's the only sentence in the whole book
that doesn't come from another book

The book costs twenty crowns
the dedication comes free
same as back then

---

## 500. THE PLAYGROUND AFTER DARK

The playground after dark
is like a theatre after the show
the sets still standing
the actors gone to bed

The sandbox full of footprints
small as a palm
a sandcake flagged with a popsicle stick
a forgotten shovel
red
it will be looked for in the morning

The climbing frame holds the shape
of the last jump
the goal without a net remembers
all eleven goals of the day
even the tenth
that caused the argument

And the swing
the swing is swinging
by itself
back and forth
back and forth
less and less

Physics says wind
a mother at the window says come to bed
the night watchman says there's nothing there
and walks on just in case

But the swing knows what it knows
it is swinging out the day
the way a bell keeps ringing
after no one strikes it

It stops at the exact moment
when at the last window of the neighborhood
the last child falls asleep

And in the morning
first feet on the sand
first hands on the chains
wind it up again
like a clock

---

## 501. THE POST OFFICE ONE MINUTE BEFORE CLOSING

The post office closes at six
at five fifty-nine
the clerk already holds the keys
the rubber stamp is asleep
the parcel scale yawns

And then the door flies open
a man
he has been running
you can tell by his breath
in his hand a letter
held like a bird
so it won't fly away

Are you still taking mail?
We're closing, sir

I know
that's why I ran

The clerk looks at the clock
then at the envelope
the address written with care
and yet crossed out once
not a familiar name in a woman's hand
a woman's name in a man's hand

You've been writing this letter a long time, haven't you

Three years, says the man
it was written in one evening
three years I carried it in my pocket
and this morning I told myself
today
or never
and all day long it was almost never

The clerk takes the letter
the stamp wakes up
today's date falls on the postage
like a signature

One minute to six, she says and smiles
you made it

Letters posted one minute before closing
weigh more than parcels
the others could have waited till tomorrow
this one would not have survived
the night

---

## 502. THE MANNEQUIN IN THE WINDOW

In the fashion house on the square
a mannequin stands in the window
thirty years now
the same smile
the same gaze
through the glass and out

They dress her by the seasons
in January the fur coat
in April the raincoat
in June the flowered dress
in September the coat again
the mannequin doesn't object
fashion always comes back
she knows that better than anyone

The shopgirls have come and gone
three generations
the first one walks past with a cane now
and always stops at the glass
Hello, she says without a sound
it still looks good on you

The mannequin looks out
thirty years in the same direction
she saw parades and funerals
she saw children become mothers
and mothers grandmothers
the people outside change clothes by the seasons too
only their smiles don't last as long

Then the shop closed down
the goods were carted away
the mannequin they left
she stands in the empty window
undressed
still smiling

And only now
have people noticed her
only now do they look away
for thirty years they looked at the dresses
never knowing
that all along
someone was looking at them

---

## 503. THE VILLAGE LOUDSPEAKER

At noon the loudspeaker crackles
on the pole by the chapel
and the village lifts its head

Announcements
a child's glove was found outside the shop
blue with a white thumb
will the owner please come to the shop

The poultry farm announces
pullets for sale on Friday

The village office reminds you
sirens will be tested on Sunday
do not be alarmed

And further
it is with deep sorrow that we announce
the passing of
Mr Anthony Warren carpenter
the funeral is Friday at two

All in one voice
in one tone
the glove the pullets Mr Warren
a stranger would find it odd
is death announced like a glove?

It is
the village is a single ear
and what is lost
is lost to everyone
the glove for a week
Mr Warren for good
the tone is the same
because a loss is a loss
only the size differs

Only once did the voice break
in the middle of an announcement
it is with deep sorrow that we announce
the passing of
Mrs Marie Dalton

and then a pause
long as the whole village

the announcer's wife
the voice finished quietly
and the village at its midday plates
put down its spoons
and stood
the way one stands in church

Since that day everyone knows
why gloves and people are announced the same
that tone must be held with both hands
so it doesn't break
because you never know
whose name you will be reading
tomorrow

---

## 504. THE STAIRCASE WITHOUT A LIFT

House number twelve has no lift
it has a staircase
ninety-six steps
four floors
and on every floor two lives

On the ground floor the lady who bakes
her scent rises free of charge
all the way to the fourth
on the first the man with the cello
he plays slow things
the stairs hear them whole

On the second the young couple
they take the stairs two at a time going up
three at a time coming down
once they carried a pram up
and since then they count the steps too

On the third nobody
the flat is for rent
the steps outside it are the quietest

And on the fourth Mr Gregory
eighty-two years old
ninety-six steps
half an hour to the top
one stop on every landing
at the baking lady's floor he breathes in
at the cellist's he listens
at the young couple's he smiles
at the empty flat he takes off his hat

The doctor tells him
Mr Gregory, change flats
take the ground floor
spare your heart

And Mr Gregory shakes his head
Doctor
on the ground floor I'd be home at once
and then what?
This way it takes me an hour to come home
and on the way I meet the whole house
the scent the music the children the silence

The young ones run the stairs in a minute
and know nothing about them
I know every step by name
the step that creaks
the step that keeps quiet
the step where I start taking off my shoes

A lift would carry me up fast
like a parcel
I'd rather climb slowly
like a house
that over four floors is learning
to be the sky

---

## 505. FOG OVER THE RIVER

This morning the river breathed in
so deeply
that it breathed out the whole city

First the towers vanished
then the bridges
then the houses on the embankment
finally the sidewalk across the street

The sounds stayed
a tram rings from nowhere
a bakery smells from nowhere
footsteps come from nowhere
and pass you before
you learn whose they are

The world is suddenly small
within arm's reach
the lamppost rises out of the milk
only once you can touch it
and behind you it's already gone

Along the embankment a white cane taps
straight ahead without hesitating
Aren't you afraid of getting lost?
asks someone nobody can see

Me? laughs the man with the cane
I've walked this way for thirty years
listen and follow me
today at last the city is
the way I know it

And so he walks in front
and behind him footsteps
a whole invisible line
across a bridge that isn't there
toward a shore that may yet be

At noon the fog lifted
the towers went back to their places
the bridges the houses the sidewalk across
people breathed a sigh of relief
and stopped listening

Everything could be seen again
and everything was far away again

---

## 506. RAINBOW OVER THE HOUSING ESTATE

All day it rained on the estate
conscientiously and in gray
gray rain on gray buildings
everything as it should be

Toward evening the sun came out
and over the concrete blocks a rainbow
stretched from one tower to the next
seven colors
no scaffolding no permit

The boy on the fifth floor shouts
Mom come to the window
our building's in color

The lady with the shopping bags
sets them on the sidewalk and looks
the bus driver stops
a little past the stop
and nobody minds

Only the man with the briefcase doesn't look up
A rainbow, he says, so what
a rainbow won't pay the rent
and he walks on about his business

The tenants' meeting has voted for years
on a new facade
and there's never any money
and now all the buildings got one
at once
for free
in colors the meeting
never dared dream of

It lasted four minutes
maybe five
then the rainbow faded
and the buildings were gray again

Only the boy on the fifth floor
kept looking a while longer
then told his mother
Don't worry
gray is only for a while too
it's just a longer while

---

## 507. THE PUDDLE

After the rain there stayed on the square
a puddle
big as a table
deep as the sky

The grown-ups walk around it
in a wide arc
they look at their shoes
they look at their watches
that puddle again says a man with a briefcase
somebody should do something about it

Cars drive right through it
without knowing
they are driving through the sky
they splash the clouds onto the pavement
the clouds trickle slowly back
and the sky settles down again
patient as water

Only a child in red rubber boots
stops at the edge
and leans over it like a well
Mum the church tower is in there
and it's upside down
and a bird is flying in it
and a cloud that looks like a dog
and there's you

Come on it's only dirty water
says the mother
looking at her phone
where the sky is shining too
but only the forecast

By evening the puddle had dried
the sky went back up
to where the grown-ups
never look either

The child knows it
and that is why he'll wait
for the next rain

---

## 508. FROST ON THE WINDOWPANE

In the night a painter came
nobody heard him ring
he has no name no ladder
no studio no price list
he paints only when everyone is asleep
and only on windows

By morning he had painted on the glass
ferns that grow nowhere
a forest that stands nowhere
silver gardens
feathers of ice birds
a whole exhibition
ADMISSION FREE

The child wakes up first
and stands in pyjamas at the window
as if in a gallery
Granny come and look
someone painted us a forest in the night

And because he wants to point out
the little path between the ferns
he breathes on the glass

And the forest is gone
only a round porthole remains
and in it an ordinary yard
dustbins and bare trees

Grandmother brings the tea
Don't cry
the painter isn't angry
he paints the same thing every freezing night
a hundred years now
and every morning someone wipes it away with a breath

Then why does he paint it

That's exactly why says grandmother
if it lasted
it would only be a curtain

---

## 509. THE GALE AND THE PAPER KITE

A gale like nothing in years
bends the poplars over the town
carries off hats and umbrellas
and on the hill stands a boy
holding a kite on a string
a kite of newspaper
with a tail made of his mother's ribbons

The wind leans in
the string sings
it sings higher and higher
and then it snaps

The kite climbs
higher than the church
higher than the crows
a little paper dot
and then nothing

It tore loose on me
the boy says to the old man
walking his dog on the hill
it tore loose and it's gone

It didn't tear loose says the old man
it let go

Is there a difference?

A big one says the man
what tears loose falls
what lets go flies

But I wanted to keep it

I know
we all want to hold
what flies

The boy walks home
an empty spool in his hand
and a piece of string
He doesn't throw it away
because at the other end
even if you can't see it anymore
the sky is still tied on

---

## 510. THE FIRST STORM OF THE YEAR

All winter and all spring
the sky did nothing but whisper
and suddenly in May
it clears its throat
till the glasses rattle in the cupboard

First thunder
and everyone to the windows
the lady from the ground floor with a dish towel in her hand
the schoolteacher with his tea half drunk
the barber with his customer half trimmed
the children with their noses on the glass

The dogs knew it first
a whole hour before
now they're under the table
and the cat behind the wardrobe
and nobody laughs at them

The children count
one two three
and thunder
three kilometres reports the father
as if the storm were a train
and he knew the timetable

Then the rain comes
all at once
ropes of water
the street disappears
and in the archway stand people
who never once said hello
and now they are silent together
which is more

And then it's over
the dogs crawl out
the windows open
and the air
the air is suddenly new
washed and ironed
exchanged piece for piece
without a word without forms

And the people from the archway drift off
and greet each other
for the first time in years
it took the sky shouting
for them to notice
they were standing side by side

---

## 511. INDIAN SUMMER

Summer doesn't leave by train
it has no suitcases
no platform
it goes away on foot
and slowly
like someone who keeps turning around

A silver thread above the stubble
a second thread across the lane
a third one lands on your sleeve
that's the spiders moving summer out

They wrap it in silver paper
thread by thread
day by day
and send it off by wind
airmail
no address and no stamp
it will deliver itself
next year

The sun is still warm
but slanting now
like a hand waving goodbye
wasps stagger in the fallen pears
the smoke of potato haulms
rises straight as a candle
and the swallows sit on the wires
like notes
at the end of a song

Grandpa when does summer end
asks the child

It doesn't end says the grandfather
it says goodbye
and that's the whole difference
what ends slams the door
what says goodbye comes back

And when a gossamer thread
lands on your cheek
don't brush it off
that's summer taking its leave of you
in person

---

## 512. HAIL ON THE GREENHOUSE

In June on a scorching afternoon
the sky turned black
and let down hail
hailstones big as pigeon eggs
five minutes
the storm needed no more

The gardener walks over the shards
counting out loud
twelve panes
fifteen
twenty-two
the tomatoes bruised
the lettuces in ribbons
only the seedlings under the bench
act as if nothing had happened

The neighbour calls over the fence
What a disaster eh?

It's a bill says the gardener
disaster looks different

And what are you going to do

Sweep says the gardener
then reglaze
the glazier is a friend
by Saturday there'll be new glass

And the sky?
Who will reglaze the sky for you?

The gardener looks up
through the hole in the greenhouse roof
where between the black clouds
