a patch of blue is already shining

The sky reglazes itself
he says
look
it's working on it now
and it never charges
not for the glass
not for the labour

---

## 513. THE MELTING SNOWMAN

He stood by the gate since December
a saucepan on his head
a carrot in the middle of his face
two pieces of coal
and a broom under his arm
like a soldier guarding the garden
who never asks for pay

In March he began to lose weight
a little every day
first the saucepan fell
then he leaned over
as if taking a bow
and one morning
all that was left on the lawn
was a carrot
two pieces of coal
an old broom
and a wet patch
where the grass is greener than anywhere else

Did he die? asks the child

Snow doesn't die says the grandfather
snow only changes its address
right now it lives in the brook
next week in the river
in summer it will be a cloud
and in December it will come back
all white
as if freshly ironed

The child gathers up the carrot
the coals and the saucepan
and puts them away in a shoebox

What are you doing?

I'm keeping his things for him
says the child
people who go far away
always leave their keys
with the neighbours

---

## 514. THE SMELL OF RAIN ON HOT ASPHALT

All day the city glowed
like a stovetop
trams melted along their rails
pigeons sat in the shade of statues
and the air shimmered over the car hoods
DO NOT FEED THE PIGEONS
KEEP OFF THE GRASS
even the signs were thirsty

Then in the evening the first drop fell
big as a coin
and hissed on the asphalt
as on a red-hot pan
a second a third a tenth

And suddenly that smell
that nobody knows how to name
and everybody knows

The clerk with his briefcase lifts his nose
the shopgirl steps out of her shop
the driver rolls down his window
and everyone remembers something
holidays at grandmother's
a garden after a storm
things they never lived through
and miss all the same

That was the city remembering
that it is earth
under the asphalt under the cables
under the car park under the cellars
the soil is still lying there
a hundred years in the dark
and it remembers the rain

And that smell
it isn't the rain
it's a greeting from below
the earth telling the rain
I am here
I am still here
they only paved me over

---

## 515. THE FIRST WHISTLE

Grandfather can whistle like a blackbird
whistle like a train
whistle for the neighbours' dog
and even for the waiter in the café
which is not done
and that is exactly why he does it

The grandson is six
and nothing comes from his lips but air
a small silent wind
that calls nobody

All afternoon on the doorstep
the boy purses his mouth like grandfather
tongue down says grandfather
not that far down
blow softer
blow like you're cooling soup
blow like you don't want to wake your mother

Nothing
nothing again
only air
and the tears are not far off

Grandfather is in no hurry
grandfathers are never in a hurry
that is why grandsons love them
he peels an apple and says
it took me a whole summer to learn
and in the end a blackbird taught me
because he kept laughing at me

And then
between one nothing and the next nothing
it happens
a thin note
crooked
tiny
but a note

The boy is startled by his own mouth
Grandpa did you hear that?
I heard says grandfather
and he says nothing more
because the blackbird on the fence
has just answered

---

## 516. THE DRESS IN THE BOX

High up in the wardrobe
above the sweaters above the bed linen
above the box with the taxes
there is one more box
white
its lid tied with a ribbon

It is opened rarely
on an anniversary
after a big spring cleaning
and sometimes for no reason
when it rains outside
and the radio plays something old

The woman pulls up a chair
takes the box down
folds back the tissue paper
and there it lies
lace gone slightly yellow
small buttons like a row of moons
and a scent
they no longer sell anywhere

She never puts it on
never even holds it against herself
she only lays her palm
on all that white
the way you lay a palm
on the forehead of a sleeping child

Her daughter asked her once
mum why don't you give it away
it wouldn't fit you anymore

It wouldn't fit
says the woman
folding the tissue paper back
but that's not why I keep it
I don't keep it to wear it

A dress isn't worn all your life
she says tying the ribbon again
a dress is kept
so that day
has somewhere to live

---

## 517. THE FIRST GREY HAIR

In the morning at the bathroom mirror
between the brushes and the bottles
between the steam and the hurry
the woman finds it

One hair
white as a thread from another sweater
insolent
shining away there
in the middle of all that dark brown
as if it belonged

She leans towards the mirror
fingers already ready
the tweezers wait on the shelf
executions are held in the morning

The man stands in the doorway with his coffee
and watches
What says the woman
I've got my first grey hair
and stop looking

And the man takes a sip of coffee
and says
Don't take it from me

Don't take it from me
that one is mine
it grew on you that winter
when the little one was sick
and you didn't sleep for three nights
and every morning you told me
everything was fine

The woman stands there
fingers in the air
the tweezers stay on the shelf

Since then they multiply
like stars when evening comes
and she doesn't count them

Only sometimes at the mirror
she finds a new one
and wonders
which night this one
grew for him

---

## 518. DANCING IN THE KITCHEN

On the stove the soup is bubbling
on the board an onion waits
half chopped
the knife is resting
on the radio between the news and the weather
suddenly
that song

The one from that summer
when they had one umbrella
and half a rent

The man wipes his hands on the tea towel
and bows
ridiculous
ceremonious
like a waiter in a film
May I have this dance

But the dinner
says the woman
Dinner can wait
says the man
songs can't wait
songs last three minutes

And so they dance
between the table and the fridge
between the chair and the stove
in slippers
a hip bumps the saucepan
the steam plays fog for them
the cat watches from the windowsill
like an audience
that already knows the programme

The neighbour downstairs lifts her head
from her knitting
they've dropped something again
she says
and she doesn't know
that up there above her ceiling
there is right now a summer
forty years old

The song ends
the announcer reads the weather
cloudy tomorrow
the soup has boiled over a little

Let it boil over
says the woman stirring
youth too only comes back
for three minutes
and it too
always burns something

---

## 519. THE INHERITED LAUGH

The father left behind
a watch that needs winding
a raincoat
a drawer of screws
that will come in handy someday
and a laugh

The laugh did not stay in the drawer
it moved into the son
whole
with the breath at the beginning
with the little choke at the end
with the hand
that has to slap the knee

The son doesn't know it
laughs don't know themselves

Visitors come
the aunt from Brno the old colleague the neighbour
they sit around the cake
somebody says something funny
and the son laughs

And the aunt from Brno goes pale
the colleague puts down his spoon
the neighbour looks at the door
as if someone were about to come in

Good lord says the aunt
that's him all over
it can't be
my heart nearly stopped

Only the mother doesn't raise her head
she calmly pours more coffee
and pushes the cake towards her son
let him go on laughing

The visitors are frightened
because they hear a dead man

She hears a living one
she has known it for a long time
death takes everything at once
but it only knows how to give back
in small pieces

And it begins with the laugh

---

## 520. THE LAST FAMILY PHOTOGRAPH

It's a bad photo
you have to admit

Grandfather is talking
so his mouth is open
the aunt has just blinked
the children are fighting over the dog
the dog is a blur
because he alone understood
you should keep moving while you can
grandmother is looking left
where somebody out of frame
has just said something funny
and the uncle is holding a cake
already missing a corner

Nobody is looking at the camera
nobody is standing straight
nobody said cheese

Because nobody knew
it was the last time

If they had known
they would have stood in a row
combed
solemn
arms at their sides
as on an official document
if they had known
it would be a photograph of sorrow

This way it's a photograph of happiness
of one ordinary afternoon
that doesn't look at the camera
because it's far too busy
with the cake
with the dog
with whatever someone on the left is saying

Last photos are always careless

That is how you can tell
that nobody was saying goodbye
that everybody thought
next time
next time we'll take a proper one

---

## 521. THE CROOKED STAR

In the box of decorations
everything is ordered like a jeweller's shop
glass baubles from great-grandmother
swaddled in cotton wool
little angels from Vienna
bells that really ring
a garland measured to the ceiling

And on top
in a matchbox
a star

Made of paper
crooked
one point longer than the others
the gold paper is peeling
in the glue you can see a thumbprint
about the size of a bean

Made in nursery school
twenty-seven years ago
its author at present
is paying off a mortgage in another town

And yet every year
before the baubles from Vienna
before the angels
before the bells that really ring
it is the one hung on the tree
THE CROOKED STAR
on the best branch
the one at eye level

Guests are sometimes surprised
right at the front? that scrap of paper?
when you have such beautiful ornaments

We do
says the mother
and straightens the star's longer point
which will bend again anyway

But the stars in the sky
are crooked too
if you saw them up close
they peel as well
somewhere they too have a thumbprint

They only look perfect
from a distance
like children
once they've moved away

---

## 522. THE SIDES OF THE BED

For forty years
he slept on the door side
and she by the window

There was never a vote on it
it was simply so
since the first night in the rented room
where the bed stood differently
and the door creaked

He by the door
just in case
in case of a burglar
in case a child cried in the night
in case the world got a bad idea
it would have to get past him first

She by the window
because she liked hearing the rain
and he liked hearing
her listening to the rain

Forty years
each their own hollow in the mattress
each their own night table
his detective novels and nose drops
her love stories and hairpins

And then one autumn
without a word they changed sides

The children laughed
mum dad have you gone mad
at your age

Not mad

Only the burglars don't come anymore
the door has been quiet for years
the children cry in other cities
into their own telephones

But the window
through the window now at night
something bigger than a burglar looks in

So they swapped
a sentry remains a sentry
for forty years he stood between her and the world
now he lies
between her and the sky

---

## 523. THE HAND ON THE SADDLE

The street behind the house
Sunday morning
a bicycle two sizes too big
so it will last
and the father

The father holds the saddle
and runs
Pedal he says
don't look down
look ahead
I've got you

The bicycle sways
the world sways
the bell rings by itself
out of fear

Have you got me? shouts the boy
I've got you shouts the father

And he runs
on a Sunday
in his Sunday shoes
he runs past the fences
past the dustbins
past Mrs Smith with her shopping bag
he runs and he holds

And then he only runs

And then he doesn't even run

He stands at the end of the street
hands in his pockets
and watches
his son riding
alone
straight
away

Have you got me? shouts the boy
because he's afraid to turn around

I've got you
calls the father
from a distance that is no longer enough

And that is all
that is the whole trade
run
hold
let go
and say nothing

Many years later
in a room with white walls
the father asks quietly
Have you got me?

I've got you says the son

and it is the same hand holding him
and it is the same lie
and just like back then
it is the truth

---

## 524. THE PHONE AT SEVEN

Every Sunday at seven
the telephone rings
and it is his mother

She asks
whether he eats
whether he dresses warmly
whether he has found himself a girl
and whether there's a draught from the window

He answers
I eat
I dress
mum please
and there's no draught from the window

Meanwhile he watches the football
with the sound off
meanwhile he stirs the soup
meanwhile he nods a head
she cannot see

Sunday after Sunday
year after year
at seven
so exactly
he could have set his watch by her
and he never did

Then that March came
and the Sundays at seven went silent

For a year they said nothing
the telephone stood in the hall
like a monument

And then one Sunday
at seven precisely
it rings

He drops the wooden spoon
three steps to the hall
heart like a bell

Hello

Silence
then an old voice
Petey?
Are you eating properly?
Are you dressing warmly?

Wrong number
someone else's grandmother
another Sunday of another son
who didn't pick up

He ought to say
madam you have the wrong number

And he says
I eat regularly
I dress warmly
and there's no draught from the window

Because on Sundays at seven
there are no wrong numbers
only mothers
and sons
who still feel like talking

---

## 525. SECOND VIOLIN

Forty years in the same quartet
forty years second violin
the first plays the melody
the one people hum on their way home
the second plays all the rest
the part nobody hums
and nobody can walk without

After the concert a journalist asks
doesn't it hurt
a whole life second
a whole life in the shadow

And the violinist lays the instrument in its case
the way you tuck in a child
Try building a bridge
from one bank of the river only
he says

Once the first violin was ill
and he played the melody
all evening he flew up there
alone in the light
and the whole time he could hear
one voice missing below
a melody is beautiful
but it gets cold
when no one is breathing under it

The first violin gets the flowers
the second gets the beat
the first takes the bow
the second holds the tuning
even through the applause
silently

Whoever holds the harmony
holds everything
only you can't hear it
the way you can't hear a bridge
as long as you're walking across

---

## 526. THE PAGE TURNER

She sits to the left of the pianist
half a chair behind
in a black dress
so as not to be seen
and she is seen
only when she stands

She doesn't play a single note
and knows the whole concert by heart
she breathes with the pianist
counts the bars like steps on a tightrope
and at the exact second
not a hair too soon
not a hair too late
she carries the music over the edge of the paper

Applause
the pianist bows
once twice three times
meanwhile she gathers the scores
and leaves sideways
through the small door

In the cloakroom a little girl asks
mummy who was that lady
who wasn't doing anything

And the pianist who overheard
turns around in his tailcoat
Everyone thinks I play alone
he says
but between any two pages
there is an abyss
and she is the bridge

Nobody claps for her
because it all went well
they would clap for her
only if the music fell
and the music never fell
not once
in thirty years

---

## 527. THE BACK ROW OF THE THEATRE

In the front row sit those
who want to be seen
in the back row those
who want to see

Students with tickets for pennies
the usherette finally on her break
the fireman on duty
lovers who don't care what's playing
and once in a while
the author of the play himself
incognito
hat on his knees

An old actress comes to sit here
since she stopped acting
From the stage you see nothing
she says
the spotlights burn your eyes
and the audience is just a darkness
that breathes now and then

From here I see everything
the stage and the house
I see the cardboard king
and I see the gentleman in row five
who weeps during his monologue
for real

The play is performed on the stage
but it happens in the house
in row sixteen in the middle
in row seven by the aisle
wherever someone has just been
struck by a line

That's why I sit at the back
says the actress
from the front row you see the theatre
from the back row you see
what theatre can do

---

## 528. THE FRESCO RESTORER

High on the scaffolding under the vault
the restorer lifts away centuries
with a cotton swab
centimetre by centimetre
candle smoke
the dust of processions
the pious overpainting of zealous repairs

Under the grime angels surface
a blue dearer than gold
a gold dearer than humility
saints with eyes wide open
a sky four hundred years old

And then at the edge of a cloud
where no one can see from the nave
she finds a fingerprint in the plaster
the painter leaned on the fresh wall
towards evening probably
in the year sixteen twelve
maybe his back was aching
maybe his wife was laughing down below

The committee asks from the floor
have you found anything of value

A signature
she answers

She sets her finger beside the print
gently
a millimetre away
the way you shake a hand
across four centuries

He was painting eternity
she says later for the report
angels saints heavens
all of it for eternity
but the most alive thing of all
after four hundred years
is the touch of a tired man
who for one second forgot
he was making eternity

Eternity gets painted
the touch remains

---

## 529. THE STREET PORTRAITIST

On the bridge sits the portraitist
a sign propped against his suitcase
PORTRAIT IN FIVE MINUTES

He draws tourists and lovers
and flatters everyone a little
straightens the nose
softens the chin
lights up the eyes
irons out the years

But you're lying
says a student with a sketchbook under his arm
that's not what they look like

The portraitist doesn't put down his charcoal
I draw them
the way their dog sees them
the way their mother saw them
the way they see themselves
the second before the bathroom light comes on
That's not a lie
that's an advance

One day an old lady comes
sits up straight as in school
and says
draw me as I am
all of it
the years too

And he draws for a long time
longer than five minutes
wrinkle after wrinkle
the way you read a letter

The old lady looks at the paper
and says nothing for a long while
That's the first time
she says at last
anyone has really
looked at me

How much do I owe you

Nothing
says the portraitist packing up his charcoals
the pretty ones I sell
the true ones I give away
hardly anyone wants them anyway

---

## 530. THE CELLO ON THE TRAM

Evening tram number nine
tired people
wet umbrellas
everyone staring into their phone
or into their own nowhere

At the theatre stop a cellist gets on
and with him a big black case
wide as a grown man
he settles it carefully on a seat
by the window
so it can see out

The inspector works his way down the aisle
tickets please
and this gentleman here
he points at the case

The cellist hands over two tickets
This gentleman was born in seventeen ninety
he says
but he travels full fare

The tram begins to smile
first the lady with her shopping
then two boys in hoodies
then even the inspector
who gives the case a salute
and moves on

And what's inside
asks a little girl across the aisle

A tree
says the cellist
it grew a hundred years in the mountains
listening to the wind
and then it learned to sing
now it rides the number nine
like the rest of us

The little girl nods
the tram rattles over the bridge
and all those tired people
look at the black case
which won't play a single note the whole way
and still they feel lighter

At the terminus the cellist gets off last
Good night sir
the driver tells him in the mirror
and to you too maestro

---

## 531. THE PROOFREADER

Forty years of red pencil
forty years of other people's mistakes
swapped letters
dropped commas
sentences that set out and never arrived
words split in the worst possible place

She has corrected novels and train timetables
ministers' speeches
and washing machine manuals
millions of errors
and not one of them was hers

One day a manuscript arrives
an old man's memoirs
on the last page it says
dedicated to my wife
who reads over my shoulder

But the wife has been dead thirty years
it says so in the very first chapter

The proofreader raises her red pencil
this should be past tense
says the rule
who read over my shoulder
that is the correct form

The pencil hangs a moment over the paper
like a wasp over a glass

And then the proofreader erases her mark
the only one in forty years

Because grammar knows
the past tense and the present tense
but not the third one
the tense of those who are gone
and read over your shoulder anyway

Some mistakes get corrected
she says closing the manuscript
and some are left to live
because they are truer
than the rule

---

## 532. THE FIREMEN'S BAND

On Sunday on the village square
the firemen's band is playing
the baker on trumpet
the blacksmith on the helicon
the butcher on the drum
and the fire chief conducting
with the hands that usually hold the hose

The trumpet comes in late
the clarinet runs ahead
the helicon goes its own way
and they all always meet
at the end of the bar
like a family at supper

The square is full
grandmothers on chairs brought from home
children on shoulders
dogs between legs
and pies on the long table

A gentleman from the city arrives
stops by the beer tap
and murmurs to his neighbour
but they're playing out of tune
can you hear it
every second note

The neighbour nods and refills his glass
The philharmonic plays for the ears
he says
these fellows play for the whole village
that trumpet there pulled
the Vasek girl out of the pond last year
that drum fixes roofs
and the only false playing
is playing you don't mean
these men mean it dead seriously
every wrong note comes from the heart

The band strikes up another march
off the mark again
and the whole square starts dancing
exactly in the rhythm
that isn't there
and that everyone can hear all the same

---

## 533. THE RETIRED DANCER

At the stop of the number three bus
an old lady stands with her shopping bag
it's raining
the bus is late
people huddle under the shelter

She stands by the pole
and holds it lightly
too lightly to be holding on
heels together toes turned out
chin level as if carrying a crown
and in her wrist
quite unnoticeably
a port de bras

None of the people waiting know
that this pole is at this very moment
the barre in the studio on Island Street
the mirrors the piano the shouting ballet mistress
and being eighteen
nobody knows
that this lady with her bag of potatoes
once died eight nights a week
as a swan
and always rose again

Only one little girl is watching
and then she too takes hold of the pole
heels together toes turned out
chin up

The old lady smiles
says nothing
just lifts her hand
one centimetre higher
so there is something to copy

The bus arrives
and she boards it
three steps
the way one enters a stage
the driver doesn't know why
but he waits one extra second
before closing the doors

The body forgets nothing
only the audience changes
and a bus stop in the rain
is a stage too
when someone is waiting there
who once knew how to fly

---

## 534. THE LULLABY SINGER

Every evening she sings to the children
the most grateful audience in the world
which always falls asleep
in the middle of the second verse

She sings about sleepy-time
about little stars
about the little black horse
and the children breathe more slowly
eyelids grow heavy
a small hand lets go of the plush dog
and the concert is over
before the song has ended

And she keeps singing
the third verse
the fourth
softly all the way through
even the long note at the end
that no one hears anymore

Who do you sing the ending for
her husband asks one night from the doorway
they're asleep

Who for
she says switching off the lamp

For the dark so it knows
we lend it the child for one night only
for the room so it remembers the tune
in case the child should wake

And also
she says already in the hallway
for a little girl
who always used to fall asleep
in the middle of the second verse
and all her life has wanted to know
how it turned out

The beginnings of songs are for children
the endings are for us
for those who already know
how songs end
and still want to hear every night
that they end well

---

## 535. THE LAST NIGHT FLIGHT

The airport at one in the morning
goes out piece by piece
the departure board has played its last hand
the luggage belts stand still
the perfume shop sleeps behind its grille
the coffee machine keeps its light on
all alone for no one

The cleaning woman rides her machine
across a hall as big as a town square
the customs man yawns
even the sniffer dog is asleep

Only the tower is still lit
and across the screen a dot is traveling
the last night flight
high above the mountains

And the controller watches the dot
and wonders who is sitting in it
maybe a woman flying to a funeral
rehearsing her first sentence in silence
maybe a man coming home after twenty years
afraid he's forgotten the name of his street
maybe a child flying for the first time
asleep with its forehead on the window
above a land full of small lights
one of which is home

Hello this is the tower
you may descend
the runway is yours

And for that one little dot
the whole airport lights up again
the runway the hall the belts
and the sign ARRIVALS above the doors

because no one is allowed to land in the dark
and no one in this world
should ever come back
to a house gone dark

---

## 536. THE HITCHHIKER WITH A SIGN SAYING ANYWHERE

At the edge of the road past the last house
stands a hitchhiker
holding a sign made from a banana box
and on it in big letters
ANYWHERE

The first car stops
Where are you headed?
Anywhere
That's not a direction young man
It's the best direction in the world
says the hitchhiker
the only one you can't miss

I'm only going as far as Slough
says the driver apologetically
Slough is anywhere
says the hitchhiker
but the driver is already rolling up his window
Slough suddenly seems very small to him

A second car stops a third a fifth
and they all ask the same thing
Anywhere sure but where exactly?
and the hitchhiker lifts his sign a little higher
and the drivers shrug
I'm only going to my mother-in-law's
I'm only picking up cement
I'm only going home
and off they drive
and each one in his rearview mirror
carries away a piece of that word

Toward evening an old car pulls over
the driver leans across
opens the door
and asks nothing at all

Where are you going? asks the hitchhiker
No idea says the driver
I've been driving since morning that's all
Then that makes two of us
says the hitchhiker and gets in

And the sign lies on the back seat
ANYWHERE
and for the first time all day
it points exactly the right way

---

## 537. THE FERRYMAN

Across the river between two steeples
the ferryman goes
fifty years now
there and back
there and back

He has ferried brides in white
and coffins in black
the doctor at midnight
goslings wheelbarrows an accordion
whole fairs with the carousel in crates
and once a runaway horse
that stood on the ferry
still as a statue

People ask him
and you never stayed on the other side?
it's only a stone's throw

Never says the ferryman

Not even for one night?

Not even one

But why?

And the ferryman leans on his pole
When I stand here
the other side is that one
when I stand there
the other side is this one
fifty years I've been crossing to the other side
and I've never once arrived

But in the middle of the river
he says pushing off
in the middle of the river I am neither here nor there
and that is the only place in the world
nobody can take from me
because it's on no map
and you can't build on it
you can only cross

---

## 538. THE ROAD THAT LED NOWHERE

Past the village a road begins
an honest road with milestones
with an avenue of old cherry trees
and a little bridge over the brook

It starts out grandly like all roads do
and ends in a field
in the middle of the oats
it simply stops
like a sentence someone never finished

Once they say it was meant to reach a town
that got built somewhere else
or a bridge that changed its mind
nobody remembers now
the plans went yellow in the district archive

The authorities twice tried to strike it off
a road without a destination shall be cancelled
it says so in the regulations
but there was never money for that
and so it keeps on leading
nowhere

And people walk it
lovers because they meet no one there
except other lovers
and those don't count
an old man with a cane because he has nowhere to get to
a lady with a dog that chases wind through the oats
children on bikes riding to the end of the world
and home in time for supper

Every other road drives you somewhere
to work to the office to the train
only this one is in no hurry at all
and that is why it walks so well

And at the very end in the oats
the grass is trodden down
from all the people standing there looking
because from there
you get the finest view
of nowhere

---

## 539. THE BOUNDARY STONE

On the bank between Upper End and Lower End
a stone sits in the grass
an old boundary stone
a U carved on one side
an L carved on the other
and a cross on top
so there'd be no mistake

Once upon a time it was serious
the mayors took the meadow to court
the farmers brawled at the parish fair
sealed documents sleep in the archive
with the surveyor's oath
that the line runs exactly here
for all eternity

Only the grass can't read
it grows across the line in both directions
the swallows fly from one land register to the other
without a single stamp
the rain rains on both villages at once
the hare has its burrow right on the border
head in Upper End rump in Lower End
and it sleeps just fine

And the cows
the cows don't read maps
the Upper End cow grazes Lower End grass
and in the evening she gives milk
and that milk is one milk only
equally white in both villages

Only people know where the line is
and children
children know it best of all
they sit on the stone with their sandwiches
because whoever sits on the stone
is neither in Upper End nor in Lower End
and in both villages
that is the best seat there is

---

## 540. THE HIGHWAY DINER

A diner between two exits
a hundred kilometers from everywhere
nobody lives here
nobody comes here
everyone only passes through

The waitress brings coffee and the soup of the day
and in five minutes she learns
what people elsewhere won't tell in a lifetime

The trucker shows her a photo of the daughter
he sees once a month
the woman by the window is driving away from someone
you can tell by how long
she stirs a coffee with no sugar in it
the traveling salesman tells jokes
so as not to hear himself for a while
and the soldier on leave says nothing
and that too is a whole story

They tell her everything
because they will never see her again
a confessional with a menu
and here the absolution comes with free refills

Once somebody asked her
don't you get sad out here?
everyone drives away
and you stay behind

And the waitress wiped the table
You people drive one and the same highway all your lives
and you call that traveling
I stand here behind this counter
and the whole world passes through my hands
a thousand stories a day
five minutes each
and not one of them tells me its ending

And that's just as well she said
straightening the sugar bowls
the endings I make up myself
and all of them
every single one
turn out happy

---

## 541. THE LAST STOP OF THE CABLE CAR

The cable car climbs out of the valley
over the spruces over the clearing
over the last shingled cottage
where somebody is still waving

And up on the ridge
is the last stop
a tin shed a great wheel
and a sign saying END OF THE LINE

Here the wires end
beyond there is nothing at all
just grass hugging the ground
stones
and the wind that has lived here forever

An old man with a thermos runs the station
he greets the cabins like old acquaintances
and all day long he answers
one single question

It doesn't go any further?
It doesn't
And what's further on?
Further on is the wind

The tourists take pictures by the sign
eat their sandwiches on the sheltered side of the shed
and ride back down

But now and then somebody stays standing
looking at the ridge above the station
where no cable leads anymore

You can't ride up there says the old man
up there you can only walk
and higher still
higher still you can only look

Everything in this world stretches only as far as it will
he says screwing his thermos shut
wires rails telephone lines
they always stop a little short of the summit
the last stretch everyone must climb alone
and the very last stretch
cannot be climbed at all
it can only be stood in silence
while you let the wind
take your cap
as the price of admission

---

## 542. THE MAN WHO CAME BACK THE SAME WAY

Every morning at ten past seven
he left the house
down Station Street across the footbridge
past the bakery and past the church
to work
and at five in the afternoon
back along the very same way

Thirty years

The neighbors told him
man at least buy yourself a bicycle
discover another street
you'll wear a groove in that one
the world is wide

I know he said
that's exactly why

In the morning the bakery smelled of bread
in the evening other people's dinners drifted from open windows
in the morning the church was on his right hand
in the evening on his left
on Monday there was a puddle on the footbridge
on Tuesday it had turned into a mirror
on Wednesday it was gone
in spring the lilacs overtook him on the way
in winter he walked in his own morning footprints
and they never quite fit

One day his grandson asked him
grandpa don't you get tired
of always the same road?

And the man laughed
I have never once in my life
walked the same road
in the morning a man goes down it
who doesn't yet know how the day will end
in the evening a man comes back
who does
they walk on the same stones
and each takes a different road

And if you really knew how to walk
he said taking the boy's hand
one street would be enough
for the whole world

---

## 543. THE METRO MAP OF A FOREIGN CITY

In the drawer under the rubber bands
under the old glasses and the bills
lies a folded map
the metro of a foreign city
brought home forty years ago
from a single journey

Lines colored like crayons
the blue one runs along the river
the yellow one circles the center
like a cat around a warm stove
the red one heads for the airport
and nowhere else

And the names of the stations
sound like lines of verse
Porte des Lilas
the Gate of Lilacs
Château d'Eau
the Castle of Water
Filles du Calvaire
the Daughters of Calvary
whoever thought those up
was no clerk
or maybe he was
but on Sundays he wrote poems

In the evening he spreads the map on the table
and travels by finger
boards at the Gate of Lilacs
changes trains where they advise you not to
because the connecting corridor they say never ends
and he happens to like corridors that never end
he never gets lost
he is never delayed
and he rides for free

He spent three days in that city once
he was twenty
and it rained
that is all
and that is a great deal

Why don't you go back?
people ask

And he folds the map along its faded creases
A different metro runs there now
they've renamed the stations extended the lines
but in this map
the summer I was twenty is still running
perfectly on time
every five minutes

---

## 544. THE POSTCARD THAT ARRIVED YEARS LATE

When they took apart the old sorting machine at the post office
a postcard fell out from behind the tin
the sea a lighthouse two sailboats
and a postmark thirty years old

It's beautiful here
the water is warm
I think of you
I'll be back soon
Your Anna

The official procedure is clear
addressee unknown return to sender
only the sender
no longer has any address
and where she has moved to
there are no deliveries

Bin it said the supervisor
thirty years past the deadline
nobody's waiting for that anymore

But the old postman slipped it into his bag
what has once been written must arrive
that isn't in the regulations
that's in postmen

He asked at the tobacconist's asked at the butcher's
asked at the registry office
the house was rebuilt strange names on the doorbells
after a week he finally tracked down
a retirement home
in another town by a park
he went there on a Sunday
on a ticket he paid for himself

Mail for you sir
he said and handed him the postcard

The old man put on his glasses
and read for a very long time
for so few words

She never came back you know
he said at last
I know said the postman
But she wrote that she would
And what is written stands
said the postman
that's why we carry it

And the old man stood the postcard
on his night table sea side out
and from that evening on he went on waiting
only no longer just like that
now he had it in writing
with an official stamp

---

## 545. THE MAN WHO COUNTED THE STARS

On the roof of the house by the station
a man sits every night
blanket thermos notebook
and above his head a sky
full of unfinished work

One two three
he says softly
seventy-four seventy-five
then a cloud drifts over
and it's all gone
tomorrow again

The neighbours have him figured out
a quiet well-mannered madman
who harms no one
except the stars
they say you shouldn't count them

One night the baker's boy
climbs up from the ground floor
Mister so how many are there?
I don't know says the man
in thirty years I've never got
past two hundred and eight

And that doesn't break your heart?

The man pours him tea from the thermos
Look he says
the baker too every single day
they eat up all his bread
and in the morning he bakes more
and nobody tells him
it's pointless

But bread is for eating

Exactly says the man
and stars are for counting
not for finishing the count
if one day I counted them all
the sky would be done
and things that are done
people stop looking at

Since that night
the baker's boy counts too
from his window on the ground floor
you can see just a ribbon of sky
seven eight nine stars
but starting over
that he already knows
like a grown-up

---

## 546. THE DREAM THAT KEPT RETURNING

Mrs Edith has one dream
for forty years always the same
the garden behind her childhood house
a line full of white washing
and her mother with her back turned
pinning up sheets against the wind

The dream has its rules
Edith stands by the currant bush
she must not speak
she must not move
and before her mother turns around
the alarm clock rings
every time

She knows the dream by heart
the way you know a prayer
she used to tell it good evening
she used to tell it you again

Then one night in March
everything is as always
the garden the line the wind
except one sheet
is blue

Such a small thing
who would notice
Edith notices
she's been looking for forty years

And her mother without turning
says
hand me a clothes-peg

And Edith takes a step
her first step in forty years
and hands it over

In the morning she wakes
with an empty palm
but the palm remembers
that small piece of wood

Since that night she sleeps without dreams
the way you sleep when the work is done
and in her apron pocket she keeps a peg
in case some day again
the wind should blow

---

## 547. RADIO FOR THE SLEEPLESS

At two in the morning a voice speaks on the radio
soft as a kitchen lamp
playing songs for no one
that is for everyone
who isn't sleeping

The baker woman listens already up
the watchman listens not yet in bed
the mother with a child on her shoulder
the truck driver between two towns
the old man whose bed is too big
and the boy afraid to switch off the light

None of them knows about the others
each one thinks
I am the last person awake in the world
and the voice on the radio speaks
as if sitting in each of their rooms
separately

One night the voice says
I don't actually know if you're out there
in twenty years nobody has written to me
so I'll ask you just one thing
if you can hear me
turn your light on for a moment
then turn it off

And the host steps to the studio window
high above the sleeping city

And the city blinks
here and there and over there too
by the station on the ground floor under the tower
windows like fireflies
a whole constellation of the sleepless

Good night he says into the microphone
good morning rather
and for the first time in twenty years
he isn't saying it to the dark

---

## 548. THE ALARM CLOCK THAT DIDN'T RING ON SUNDAY

Mr Walter has a tin alarm clock
handed down from his father
he winds it every evening
with the little key at the back
two turns not a hair more

The clock rings at half past four
a whole lifetime
Monday for the foundry
Tuesday for the foundry
Wednesday Thursday Friday
Saturday for the market
and Sunday
Sunday too
because Mr Walter forgets to switch it off
and then lies with his eyes closed
listening to the bells of Saint James
which ring so much better

One Saturday evening
there was a party at the neighbour's
accordion wine stories of the old days
and Mr Walter came home late
and fell asleep in his shoes
and never wound the clock

On Sunday he woke at nine
the sun already sat on the floor
like a guest nobody had invited
who still arrived on time

Mr Walter took fright
then remembered it was Sunday
then looked at the alarm clock
and the clock kept quiet
hands folded behind its back
pretending nothing at all

You could have told me sooner
says Mr Walter
that there's no ringing on Sundays

The clock said nothing
alarm clocks don't talk
alarm clocks simply know

Since then Mr Walter
doesn't wind it on Saturday nights
he calls it an agreement
and on Sunday mornings he lies in
listening to the bells of Saint James
while on the bedside table
rests a little tin heart
that one day a week
takes orders from no one

---

## 549. THE MINUTE OF SILENCE

At the stadium across the river
before the Sunday match
the loudspeaker said
let us observe a minute of silence
for Mr Josef
who for forty years
chalked the lines of this pitch

And five thousand people stood up
and there was silence

For the referee it lasted exactly a minute
he was watching his stopwatch
that's what referees are for

For the goalkeeper it lasted one penalty
the one Mr Josef once saved
in the youth team
the only one of his life

For the boys on the terraces it lasted forever
because boys don't know how to stand still
not even for a second

For the sausage seller it was short
he'd barely taken off his cap
before putting it back on

For the widow in row twelve
it lasted thirty-nine years
from the wedding to yesterday
she walked the whole length of it
there and back
and still had some left over

And for the pigeons on the grandstand roof
it didn't last at all
pigeons keep their own count

Then the referee blew his whistle
the only whistle
Mr Josef never heard
and the ball set off across the grass
along the fresh white lines
that somebody else
had drawn this morning
slightly crooked

And the silence
the silence sat down in the empty seat
in row twelve
and watched the match to the end

---

## 550. THE LAST DAY OF THE HOLIDAYS

On the thirty-first of August
the days are long as a whole summer
and short as nothing at all

The Crowe boy knows it
so from morning on he's rushing
one last time to the river
one last time up the cherry tree
though the cherries are long gone
one last ride on his bike in a single go
out to the transformer and back
no hands

Time runs strangely that day
the morning lasts a week
the afternoon blinks and is gone
and the evening smells of September
a smell known only
to children and schoolteachers

In the hallway the schoolbag is waiting
brand new gleaming
sitting by the door like a dog
that knows tomorrow means the vet
and wags its tail anyway

Mother calls from the window
come home we're up early tomorrow
and that tomorrow is a longer word
than the whole of July

Grown-ups don't see that day
for grown-ups the thirty-first
is just the day before the first
they have forgotten
that once they too could hear
summer swinging shut
like a great gate

But the Crowe boy hears it
so that evening in his pencil case
between the brand-new pens
he hides a pebble from the river
still warm with sun

And tomorrow at his desk
when the teacher asks
what did you do this summer
he'll say nothing
and in his hand under the desk
the river will keep flowing

---

## 551. THE HOUR BETWEEN NIGHT AND MORNING

There is one hour
that appears in no timetable
night has already signed the handover papers
morning has not yet reported for duty
and the city belongs
to whom exactly

To bakers with flour on their eyelids
to cats inspecting the dustbins
to the driver of the first tram
still running empty
just for himself
to the nurse coming off the night shift
to the postwoman going on the early one
they pass each other on the bridge
and nod
though they've never been introduced

And to those who do not sleep
poets without poems
lovers without lovers
old men homesick
for something that has no name

At that hour everyone is on first-name terms
with their eyes
the night baker hands over a roll
straight across the counter still hot
and won't take money
at this hour nobody pays
at this hour you belong

Then the first alarm clock rings
somewhere on the third floor
and it's all over

The sun comes out the people come out
proper well-rested hurrying
and the brotherhood dissolves
like sugar in coffee

Except sometimes at noon
at the crossing by the post office
the baker and a sleepless poet
nod to each other

And nobody around can work out
how on earth they know each other
those two

---

## 552. HOW CHILDREN FALL ASLEEP

Children fall asleep in the middle of a sentence
and then the dinosaur went
and then
and then nothing
they're asleep

They fall asleep with a toy car in hand
halfway round the bend
with a storybook open on their belly
one shoe off
and the other for another day

They fall asleep on the tram
head on a stranger's sleeve
at the table over their soup
on the carpet in the middle of building
a bridge out of blocks
they'll calmly finish in the morning

They never decide to sleep
it simply ambushes them
like rain on a day out
they fall into sleep
the way you fall into grass
straight down
without backing up

Grown-ups have forgotten how

Grown-ups fall asleep
the way a shop gets closed
check the locks
switch off go through the accounts one last time
stack the worries on their shelves
one sheep two sheep
three hundred and twelve sheep
and sleep nowhere in sight
because sleep does not call
on those who are doing inventory

Except sometimes
on a train at half past four
a grown-up falls asleep mid-sentence
head on the shoulder of a stranger
and suddenly he has the face
he had when he was six

And the whole compartment goes quiet
and the stranger sits up straight
not breathing
standing guard over his sleep
and his shoulder won't start to ache
till after Colchester

---

## 553. THE NIGHT WATCHMAN WHO READ POETRY

A warehouse on the edge of town
three hundred boxes on pallets
wire fence floodlight gatehouse
and in the gatehouse a small lamp
and under the lamp a watchman
and in the watchman's hands
a slim book of verse
from the second-hand shop for pennies

Rounds at ten at midnight at two
torch keys stamp in the logbook
NOTHING TO REPORT
and in between
poems

One night the inspector turns up
a young man in an ironed shirt
What's that you're reading on duty?
Verse says the watchman
And do you know what you're guarding?
I do says the watchman
three hundred boxes

And do you know what's inside them?

No says the watchman
in fifteen years I've never opened one
that's not what I'm here for
I guard them so they stay shut

And he taps the little book

This one is full of boxes too
thirty poems
and nobody knows what's inside them either
until they're opened
the only difference is
I guard the boxes so that no one opens them
and the poems
so that someone does

The inspector writes in his report
ALL QUIET ON SITE
and drives away

And the watchman pours himself tea
and goes on reading
and around the gatehouse stands the night
three hundred boxes of darkness
and in just one of them
a light is on

---

## 554. THE SECOND HAND

On the clock there are three hands

The hour hand is the director
fat slow full of dignity
in a whole day it hardly moves
and yet the world sets itself by it

The minute hand is the office clerk
shuffling from counter to counter
looking terribly busy
once an hour it walks around the office
and goes back to standing still

And then there is the third one
thin and red
no title no rubber stamp
the only one you can see running

It runs all day all night
no lunch break no Sundays
sixty steps every minute
three thousand six hundred an hour
you try counting that

And when someone asks the time
nobody ever says
it's fifteen seconds
they say half past two
the director and the clerk
collect all the glory

On the tower clocks
they abolished it altogether
from that height they said
you wouldn't see it anyway
struck off
the way the errand boy is cropped
from the firm's Christmas photo

And yet

When the doctor holds a sick man's wrist
he doesn't look at the director
he counts her little steps

When people wait for midnight
for the start for the return for the miracle
every eye runs with her

And when the clock stops
she's the one who shows it
she's always the first to know
the smallest one
the one who did
all the work of time

---

## 555. THE BOX MARKED OTHER

Form number seven
offers you options
employed unemployed retired
married single divorced widowed
owner tenant
believer non-believer
and at the very bottom
one small box
OTHER

The man at the counter holds his pen
and thinks
he worked but every which way
he loved but not according to the papers
he lived wherever they let him
he believed mostly in the mornings
so he ticks
other
other
other
other

You can't do that sir
says the clerk
you have to choose something
I did choose
says the man
all my life I've been choosing other

The system won't take it
Then let the system come to the counter
says the man
and next to the box marked other
where it says please specify
he writes in small letters
rain accordion Marie

And the clerk looks at those three words
a long time
then she takes her stamp
and stamps his whole life
valid indefinitely

---

## 556. COMPLAINTS IN VERSE

The complaints department of the transit authority
received a letter
Dear Sirs, your number nine bus
pulled away right under my nose
taking a piece of Tuesday with it
I request a refund
either of the Tuesday or of the driver's smile
whichever is in stock

The clerks gathered over the letter
there is no paragraph for this
no form for this
no rubber stamp seems right
so the letter travelled
from floor to floor
and on every floor
somebody smiled reading it
which in an office building
counts as an exceptional incident

The man kept writing
he complained about a hole in the road
in a sonnet
about the rise in ticket prices
in a ballad with a refrain
about the month of November
just because
November seemed long to him

And years later an answer came
written by the youngest clerk
after office hours
and in verse
Dear sir
that bus is beyond catching now
it has left for good
we are therefore refunding your minutes
in full
regrettably we pay in no other currency
please spend them
on something beautiful

---

## 557. NUMBER FORTY-SEVEN

The ticket machine by the door
hands out numbers
to anyone who reaches for one
to the doctor and the man out of work
to the lady in furs and the boy in overalls
the same to all
in the order they arrive

The man with number forty-seven
sits on a plastic chair
watching the glowing board
forty-four
forty-five

Beside him a mother with a child
that's crying
she holds number sixty-two
and the counters close at four

Meanwhile at counter three
a gentleman slips ahead
a gentleman who knows the supervisor
the people in line say nothing
the people in line are good at saying nothing

Forty-six

The man looks at his ticket
then at the crying child
and hands the mother
his number forty-seven
and takes her sixty-two

The board chimes
and the machine by the door
keeps printing numbers
one after another
fairly
the only one in the whole building
that takes people
just as they come

---

## 558. KEEP OFF THE GRASS

In the middle of the park stands a sign
KEEP OFF THE GRASS
brand new
black letters on white
driven into the ground
exactly where
the grass is greenest

The park keeper paints it every spring
in summer he wipes off the dust with his handkerchief
in winter he sweeps away the snow
it's a fine sign
he says
it keeps order around here

And all around the sign
since nobody walks there
daisies grow
one beside another
white and unhurried
a whole meadow of daisies
right at the foot of the ban

A little girl asks the keeper
mister and those flowers
can they read?

No, says the keeper
flowers can't read

Then why do they grow just here

And the keeper looks at the sign
and for the first time in years he sees it
the grass is slowly climbing the post
creeping up along the letters
another year or two
and it will have read the whole thing
the only one
really walking on it

---

## 559. THE LOYALTY CARD

In a man's wallet
between his ID card and a photo of the grandchildren
a little card
BRIDGE CAFÉ
ten stamps and the tenth coffee free

Nine small blue cups
punched one after another
nine mornings
nine times
good morning Mr Smith
the usual?
the usual

The first stamp on the day it rained
the third when his granddaughter was born
the fifth when nobody said a word
and the coffee was good all the same
the seventh when the girl behind the counter was crying
and he pretended not to see
and left a little extra change

The ninth on a Friday
all weekend he looked forward to the tenth

On Monday a paper on the door
CLOSED FOR GOOD
THANK YOU FOR YOUR CUSTOM

The man stands at the glass
behind which you can still make out the chairs
legs up on the tables
then he takes out his wallet
and slides the card carefully back
between the ID and the grandchildren

Throw it away he will not
one stamp is missing
and as long as it's missing
someone in this world owes him a coffee
and the bridge café
still
just a little
exists

---

## 560. MAKE A U-TURN

In the traveling salesman's car
a woman's voice is speaking
in three hundred meters turn right
continue straight ahead
in two kilometers
you will reach your destination

Destinations
a warehouse on an industrial estate
an office in Plzeň
a parking lot by the motorway
a hotel that looks like every hotel
twenty years of destinations

One Tuesday between Prague and Brno
the man misses an exit
and the voice says calmly
make a U-turn

He misses another
make a U-turn

And suddenly the man asks out loud
of the empty car
where am I actually going

The voice doesn't answer
voices from machines don't answer
questions like that
they only repeat with patience
make a U-turn

All right, says the man
all right
and at the next exit he turns around
all of him
twenty years and all

He drives north instead of south
past road signs he doesn't know
to the village where he was born
and where he hadn't been since the funeral

He stops by a garden with a rusty gate
and switches off the engine

You have reached your destination
says the voice
and for the first time in twenty years
it is right

---

## 561. THE SELF-CHECKOUT

At the supermarket they closed till number four
the one with Marie
who knew half the neighborhood by name
and in her place now stands a machine
with a green light
PLEASE SCAN YOUR FIRST ITEM

An old lady comes to it every Tuesday
rolls milk one apple
and chats away
well now little machine
windy again out there
the two of us can tell
you by your wires me by my knees

UNEXPECTED ITEM
IN THE BAGGING AREA

There there, the lady soothes it
it's only my old shopping bag
it has survived worse

The young man in the queue behind her
rolls his eyes at the ceiling
the lady is talking to a shop
who is she really talking to

To Marie who stays home now
to the husband who died in April
to the daughter across the ocean
who calls on Sundays
when there's time

PLEASE WAIT
HELP IS ON THE WAY

No need to call anyone
says the old lady stroking the screen
we'll manage this together

And the machine blinks and blinks
UNEXPECTED ITEM
IN THE BAGGING AREA
and in its way it is right
something is lying there that isn't in the catalogue
between the rolls and the milk
one whole quiet Tuesday of it
and out of everyone in the store
only the machine
has noticed

---

## 562. GRANDMA'S FIRST SMILEY

They bought grandmother a telephone
smarter than the whole family
and for a week her grandson explained
you write here
you erase here
and here granny
here are the smileys

Grandmother put on her glasses
and looked at that meadow of little faces
yellow and round
laughing crying sleeping
one of them even kissing a heart

Then she sat all evening in the kitchen
holding the phone in both hands
the way you hold a fledgling
and wrote the first message of her life

In the morning the grandson's phone beeps
GRANDMA
and in the message not a single word
just a heart
and right after it
a little face in tears

He calls her granny
you sent me crying
that's for when a person is sad
you meant to send a smile

Of course I did
says grandmother
I must have pressed it wrong

And then a small silence
and then grandmother says
or maybe I pressed it right
because I wanted to smile at you
and somehow doing it
who knows why
made me want to cry
that's something you young ones don't know yet
you'll learn it

---

## 563. THE INSTRUCTION MANUAL

In the drawer under the television
sleep the instruction manuals
the manual for the washing machine
the one for the hair dryer
the one for the kettle
the one for the vacuum whose bags they stopped making
the one for the television hauled away years ago
seven languages
two-year warranty
nobody has ever read them

The washer starts with the big knob
the hair dryer has only two settings
you can live in this world
without reading

But one evening the power goes out
and the man hunts in the drawer for candles
and finds the manuals
and by candlelight he reads
for what else is there by candlelight

Do not expose the appliance to impact
do not immerse it in water
clean with a soft cloth
no harsh products

And in the chapter TROUBLESHOOTING
a table
the appliance gives no warmth
the appliance makes no sound
the appliance does not respond
and for all three the same first advice
first check
that the appliance is connected

The man lifts his eyes from the page
the flat is dark
someone is walking on the floor above
his son's number he knows by heart
and hasn't dialed since Christmas

Seven languages
and all of them say the same thing
no warmth no sound no response
first check
that it is connected

---

## 564. THE SATISFACTION SURVEY

At ten in the evening the phone buzzes
RATE YOUR DAY TODAY
from one to five stars
your opinion matters to us

The man sits on the edge of the bed
and remembers honestly

It rained in the morning
one star
but that smell of wet pavement
five

He missed his tram
one
but in the next one
over its mother's shoulder
a stranger's child smiled at him
five

At work a meeting about meetings
no stars
spreadsheets don't have stars

At noon a soup almost like his mother's
in the afternoon a call with his sister
they quarreled
one
they made up
five
and maybe they quarreled only
to have something to make up for

And in the evening a stranger stood on the bridge
looking down at the water
the man stopped beside him
and they were silent together
until the stranger said
well I'll be going
and went home
how many stars for that
and in which box

The man looks at the five empty stars
a day cannot be averaged
a day can at most be lived
in the end he lights up all five

And the screen asks
WHAT COULD WE IMPROVE?
and the man thinks for a long time
and then writes
nothing
just let there be another one tomorrow

---

## 565. THE STATUE THAT FACED ONE WAY

On the square stands a statue
some general or a poet
nobody remembers now
pigeons make no distinctions

For a hundred years it has faced one way
down the avenue toward the river
it knows by heart
the bakery window
the corner of the savings bank
and a piece of sky above the bridge

It has seen funerals and parades
flags that kept changing
carriages trams cars
everything passed before its eyes
in one direction
like the river

Only one thing it doesn't know
what is behind it

Because behind it there is a park
a small park with a bench
the statue knows it only by ear
laughter without a face
a violin in June
the creak of the bench
when two people move closer
and the silence
that only lovers know how to make

In the evening over its shoulder
fall long shadows
foreign and slow
that is all of the park
it will ever own

It asked the pigeons
What is behind me?
A bench says the pigeon
And on it?
Everything that doesn't fit
in front of monuments

The statue would turn around
but the pedestal

And then one evening
it watches the people
walking home from work down the avenue
all of them straight ahead
not one turns around
and yet nothing
is stopping them

Their necks are entirely free
the statue says to itself
and they carry them straight
like me

at least I have an excuse
I am made of bronze

---

## 566. THE UNFINISHED SCARF

In the basket by the armchair
a ball of blue wool
and an unfinished scarf
the needles stuck mid-row
just as she set them down
when she went to answer the door
and never came back

The aunt says
I'll finish it
I know the pattern
it practically knits itself

Don't you dare
says the mother
and no more is said about it

The scarf stays in the basket
winter follows winter
the moths got the sweaters
they never touched the scarf
even moths understood something

One day the granddaughter asks
why doesn't anyone finish it
it would keep somebody warm

And the mother takes the needles in her palm
gently as if they were sleeping
A finished scarf is a finished thing
she says
finished things get folded into the wardrobe
and that's the end of them

But an unfinished row
is somebody
who has only stepped away from her work
somebody who'll be back any minute
to sit down by the armchair
and say
now where was I

---

## 567. THE ROCKING HORSE IN THE ATTIC

In the attic among the suitcases
stands a rocking horse
mane of hemp rope
one eye still painted
the other chipped away
they carried him up
when the children outgrew him
which is to say thirty years ago

Sometimes at night
he creaks
and rocks for a while
all by himself

That's a draft
says the father
the roof isn't tight
physics
nothing more

The mother doesn't like going up to the attic
not that she's afraid
she just doesn't care
to run into physics

One day the grandson climbs the ladder
to fetch the Christmas ornaments
and sees the horse rocking
quite slowly
quite quietly
as if someone very light
were sitting on him

Downstairs he tells them
the father shrugs
a draft

Maybe
says the mother putting wood on the fire
but a draft is only wind after all
wind that lost its way in the house
and found in the attic
the last thing left
that still wants to play with it

---

## 568. THE FOUNTAIN PEN

In the desk drawer
lies a fountain pen
black with a gold nib
worn smooth by fingers

It signed two weddings
one as groom one as witness
the deed to the house
garden and mortgage included
a job application
a resignation letter
excuse notes for the children
congratulations condolences
and at the end one piece of paper
that is not spoken of at home

These days anything will do for signing
a ballpoint from the insurance company
a finger on a screen
a box to tick
nothing more

The grandson finds the pen while tidying
Does it still write?
he opens it
dips the nib into the little bottle
that had been waiting too

And the pen writes
as if the years had never happened
it writes his name
in one stroke without hesitating

You see says the grandfather
a pen makes no distinctions
it signed love and the bank
a birth and the other thing
all in the same ink

Because a pen doesn't know what it's signing
he says and closes the drawer
it only knows whose hand it is
and the hand
the hand knows everything

---

## 569. THE LIGHT SWITCH

The parents' house is up for sale
the son comes to walk through it one last time
the agent hands him the keys
the electricity's been cut
he warns
take a flashlight

He didn't take a flashlight

He steps into the dark hallway
and his hand all by itself
before he can even think
flies to the doorframe
a little to the left
a small cold square
the light switch

Click
no light comes on
that's all right

The hand goes on
three steps straight ahead
mind the stair that creaks
second switch around the corner
radiator don't touch it burns
the kitchen handle sticks
lift and push

The body walks the house
in the dark
and stumbles nowhere
the head has forgotten dates and quarrels
the head has forgotten so much
the body nothing

Outside he returns the keys
You didn't take anything?
the agent wonders
people always carry something off

I did
says the son
and raises his hand
but don't worry
nobody in there will miss it

---

## 570. THE NIGHT LAUNDROMAT

At two in the morning the only lit window
on the whole street is on the corner
SELF-SERVICE LAUNDRY
OPEN ALL NIGHT

Inside three people
who don't know each other
a nurse after her shift
a boy with his sheets in a plastic bag
and a lady in a coat
thrown over her nightgown

They don't speak
they sit on plastic chairs
watching the drums
like children watching a carousel

In one the whites go round
in another the blues
in the third a man's entire week
Monday's shirt
Wednesday's dish towels
the socks of every day

The boy leans toward the nurse
You can't sleep either?
I could, says the nurse
but at home nothing turns

So they sit in silence
three strangers
staring into the same carousel
the way people once stared into fires

The lady in the coat gets up
takes out the warm laundry
and holds it a while in her arms

If only you could
she says into the silence
put a whole day into the drum
forty minutes at sixty degrees
and take it out
clean and warm
and fold it up for tomorrow

---

## 571. DEW ON THE GRASS

Every morning before six
the meadow opens its jewellery shop
no bars no display cases no insurance
a million brilliants laid out in the grass
a diamond on every blade
pearls strung along the spiderwebs
sapphires in the cups of plantain leaves

No shop assistant
no till
no sign saying DO NOT TOUCH

The jeweller from the square walks by each morning
and stops at the fence
If I had this in my window
he says
I'd be the richest man in the county

But you have it
says the meadow
you only need to bend down

But the jeweller is in a hurry
at nine he opens his shop
and in the shop he has a safe
and in the safe little stones
that last

At nine o'clock the sun arrives
the greatest thief under heaven
no gloves no crowbar
it gathers everything
brilliants pearls sapphires
down to the last drop

Nobody reports it to the police
The meadow just shrugs its blades
Tomorrow they deliver fresh stock
it says
unlike the jeweller I know
that the most precious things
are the ones that cannot be kept

---

## 572. WIND IN THE CHIMNEY

The young couple bought an old house
and the first night they didn't sleep
Someone is singing in the chimney
she says
And the stairs creak
he says
and the doors as well
we'll call the mason in the morning

The mason came
walked around the house
tapped the chimney
listened for a while

There's nothing here for me to fix
he said
The chimney is sound
the stairs are sound
it's only the wind talking to the house
and the house talking back

Sixty years together
the wind and this house
at that age you don't sing for strangers anymore
you sing out of habit
The wind asks
the house creaks
and that's that

My old folks were the same
said the mason in the doorway
In the evening dad just said hmm
mum just said well yes
and everything was said
that young people say in an hour

That night the young couple laughed
then lay in the dark and listened
the wind asking
the house answering
slowly without hurry
like those who already know
the other one won't leave

And many years from now
on some windy night
she will only say hmm
and he will only say well yes
and the house above them
will give a contented creak

---

## 573. ICICLES

Everything in the world grows upward
grass chimneys children prices
only icicles grow head down
winter's armoury
hung beneath the gutter

A boy breaks one off
the longest one
a sword
and at once he is a knight
and at once he has a war

He swings his sword at winter
a slash here
a thrust there
winter says nothing
winter is enormous
and the knight has one mitten with a wet thumb

Soon the sword is sweating
soon the sword is shorter
soon the sword is a dagger
the dagger a splinter
the splinter a drop
in an empty palm

The boy is crying
I killed my sword
he says
I held it too tight

But the grandmother at the gate laughs
You silly little soldier
don't cry
it was never your sword
it was winter's
all season long it forged them by the thousand
and handed them out to children

A weapon that melts in your hand
says the grandmother
that's winter for you
it only arms you
if you promise
the war will be over
before the thaw

---

## 574. GRASS BETWEEN THE RAILS

Under regulation D-17 paragraph 3
the trackbed shall be kept free of vegetation

The grass between the rails
never read the regulation
it grows among the sleepers
at the worst address in town
gravel instead of soil
diesel instead of rain
and twice an hour a thunder
rolling over its head

The express to Paris
the express from Paris
people at the windows with important destinations
conferences weddings aeroplanes
nobody looks down

Once a year come the men in orange
with the regulation weedkiller
and the grass turns yellow
and a report is signed
VEGETATION REMOVED

By spring it is back
green insolence
between platform five and platform six

The stationmaster with thirty years of service
said it once to a young colleague
See her there?
They've cancelled everything here
the night train the local the buffet the post office
she's the only service still running

And sure enough
when they closed the line beyond the town
and the rails rusted
and the timetables dissolved in the rain
the grass stayed at its platform
It overgrew the sleepers overgrew the rails
and raised its seed-heads
right where all its life
the trains had rolled above its head

It was never in a hurry to get anywhere
and it travelled farthest of all

---

## 575. ECLIPSE OF THE SUN

At a quarter to eleven
the whole town the quarrelling town
the litigating town
the town of two parishes three pubs
and one endless dispute over parking

stopped
and looked up

The butcher next to the vegetarian
the landlord next to the tenant who owes him
the constable next to the graffiti kid
the bank director next to the man
who wouldn't return his good morning
the lady from the counter next to everyone
she had ever told COME BACK TOMORROW

All with their heads tipped back
all in the same cardboard glasses
all suddenly equally small
under one nibbled sun

Three minutes of night at noon
The birds went quiet
the coffee machine glowed in the dusk
and nobody blamed anybody for anything
because every mouth was hanging open

Then the sun came back
whole
Light like something freshly washed

And the town lowered its heads
put its everyday faces back on
and dispersed to its disputes

Only one boy asked his mother
Mum when is the next one?
In sixty years
said the mother

That's a pity
said the boy
that's a long time to wait
just to be kind together

---

## 576. THREE O'CLOCK ON A NOVEMBER AFTERNOON

In November the dark arrives before the children leave school
At three it waits at the gate
like an impatient grandmother
a whole hour early

The bell rings
and the children walk out into an evening
that came without an appointment
satchels lanterns woolly hats
and already the first street lamps overhead

Miss
why is it night when it isn't night yet?
asks a small girl in the cloakroom

It isn't night
says the teacher buttoning the girl's coat
it's just the sun in November
cutting down its hours
It teaches all year too
it gets tired too

The children walk home through the dark like miners
little shifts coming up from the pit of the day
and because of them the windows
light up earlier
the soups simmer earlier
the mothers watch from the windows earlier

And maybe that is exactly why
the teacher says to the caretaker
while locking up the classroom
November is the month of lit kitchens

In June the children come home into sunshine
and nobody looks out for them
In November they come home into darkness
and the whole street turns its lights on
just to see them coming

The dark isn't wicked
says the caretaker switching off
the dark only turns the lamps
into a proper welcome

---

## 577. JUNE DOWNPOUR

It fell without warning
a June sky has no patience
and the street emptied out
like a necklace snapping

Under the awning of the pastry shop
suddenly pressed together
a bank clerk with his briefcase
a girl with blue hair
a postwoman
a gentleman with a dachshund
and the dachshund

Five people and one dog
who otherwise would never have met
not at a wedding not at a funeral
not in the queue at the fishmonger's

The first minute they are silent
each watching a different rain

The second minute the man with the dog says
Well the gardens needed that
and everyone nods
even the dachshund

The third minute the clerk lends
his handkerchief to the girl with blue hair
because the blue is running down her neck
and it is his best handkerchief
and he doesn't want it back

The fourth minute the postwoman laughs
at something the girl said
and the man with the dog joins in
and they all laugh
the laughter of people who never introduced themselves

The fifth minute the rain eases
raises the curtain
and the show is over

They part in four directions
no addresses no names no promises
the bank the post round home the blue hair

The sky writes in its notebook
introduced a few more of them today
and has nothing to add
Summer carries on

---

## 578. THE NIGHT OF FALLING STARS

An August night on the hill outside town
blankets thermoses tilted heads
the whole town lying on its back
waiting for something to fall

There
somebody shouts
and a hundred people hurry to make a wish

Health for the little one
let him call back
let it work out
let her come home
let it finally stop hurting

And meanwhile up above
a stone is falling
A small cold stone
that flew through space for millions of years
knew nobody
promised nothing
and now burns up in two seconds
over one hill full of people

The physicist on his blanket would say
the star couldn't care less about your wish
it's just a pebble and friction

But the girl beside him asks
Then who gets what out of it?

The physicist is silent a long time
and then very quietly
The stone gets nothing
that's true
But you just admitted to yourself
what it is you really want
That almost never happens to people

Sometimes something
has to fall out of the sky
for a person to shine a light
into themselves for one second

There
somebody shouts
and the physicist hurries to make a wish

---

## 579. CIRCLES ON THE POND

A boy on the south bank
finds a stone
flat and just right for the palm
and throws it into the middle of the pond
just because
because he is seven
and the stone was there

Plop
and the stone is gone

But water keeps nothing to itself
Water broadcasts everything
First circle second third
the circles set out from the plop
like news that can't be stopped
wider and wider
softer and softer

The boy is already running home for his snack
and his circles are still travelling

They brush the reeds
rock the ducklings
lift and lower the fisherman's float
the fisherman looks up
nobody anywhere

And on the north bank
a twenty-minute walk away
an old lady sits on a bench
and watches the very last circle
arrive at her feet
no more than a wrinkle on the water
faint as breath
but it reaches her

Where have you come from
she asks it

The circle doesn't answer
circles carry no return address

So the old lady smiles at the whole pond
just in case
because somebody on the far side
may be waving right now
without knowing at whom

---

## 580. THE MOON BY DAY

At half past ten in the morning
the moon hangs in the blue sky
pale as after a night shift
a thumbprint on the clean heavens

Nobody looks
Today the show belongs to the sun
the sun gets the applause the sunglasses the ice cream
the crowded terraces the crowded beaches

And the moon?
The moon nothing
The moon hangs there and shines
into broad daylight
which is like singing
in a station full of express trains

A small girl at the bus stop spots it
Mum the moon overslept
it forgot to go home

No
says her mother
it never goes home
it's up there all the time
even now even at noon
you just can't see it
because the sun talks too loud

And at night?

At night the sun goes down
all the loud ones fall asleep
and suddenly it turns out
somebody was there all along

The little girl stares up for a long time

That's like grandma
she says at last
She sits in her corner all day
and nobody notices her
and at night when I'm scared
she shines the brightest of all

And the moon up there
pale unpaid patient
goes on shining into the wide day
It doesn't shine because anyone sees it
It shines because it is the moon

---

## 581. THE FATHER AND THE TIE

Sunday morning at the mirror
a father and a son
and between them a tie
navy blue with stripes
borrowed from adulthood

The wide end over the narrow
says the father
once around
up through the back
and down through the loop

The son is eight
and the knot looks like
anything in the world
except a knot

Never mind says the father
a knot isn't learned with hands
a knot is learned with years

And the years did the teaching
the first dance
the first job interview
the wedding
the funerals of strangers
and each time the son's hands remembered
the wide end over the narrow
once around
up through the back
and down through the loop

Today is Sunday again
the mirror is the same one
it just hangs in a smaller flat
the father holds the tie
and his hands tremble
as if they carried all those years

Come here says the son
and stands behind him
the way his father once stood behind him

Not too tight says the father
Don't worry says the son
a man taught me
at a mirror
one Sunday

In the mirror stand two men
and one knot
that holds
while everything else
slowly comes untied

---

## 582. THE OUTGROWN SLEEVES

Evening under the lamp
mother unpicks a hem
the coat bought in September
is too short by January
children grow faster than fabric

In the sleeves waits a reserve
two fingers of darker blue
the sun had no time to drink
a spare piece of winter
just in case

Children grow out of shoes
grow out of beds
grow out of bedtime stories
mum nobody says that anymore
grow out of the kitchen doorframe
where the pencil marks
climb over one another

Mother lets out
whatever can be let out
and what cannot be let out
will one day give way on its own

Then came a winter
when the sleeves lasted till spring
and after it a winter
when the coats went away
with the arms still in them
to other cities

Tonight the lamp is on again
and again a seam is being unpicked
only the other way round
father's coat has grown too big
old age grows inward

The needle isn't surprised
the needle sews
both directions with the same stitch
and always leaves a reserve
just in case
because love needs
room to grow

---

## 583. THE LAST CARRIAGE

In the last carriage ride
the ones who ran until the very last second
coat over the arm and heart in the throat
the ones who can't stand a crowd
a soldier on leave
a student with a guitar
and one old man
who picks the last carriage on purpose

Everyone pushes toward the front
closer to the engine
closer to the future
from the first carriage you get off first
they say
the first carriage sees the station soonest

Sir, asks the conductor
why do you always ride in the back

And the man points out the window
From here you can see the rails
slowly meeting again behind us
the station waving goodbye
instead of welcoming
the level crossing lowering its barriers
and lifting them at once like a hat
the cowshed the church and the three poplars
how long they stand in place
watching us go
here the countryside doesn't run to meet you
here the countryside takes its leave

Up front sit the people in a hurry
