they see only where the train is going
the driver knows that too
and it changes nothing for him

In the back sits me
says the man
and I see the way we came
somebody after all has to watch
over everything
the train leaves in peace behind it

---

## 584. THE NIGHT CROSSING

Half past one in the morning
a level crossing in the middle of the fields
the red light blinking
blinking and blinking
like a heart somebody forgot to switch off

The driver is alone
the only car for miles
engine running heater humming
and the barriers down

A freight train is passing
and it has no end
wagons wagons wagons
the driver tries to count them
at thirty-seven he gives up

So he reads what they carry instead
timber from Poland
cars under tarpaulins
grain coal tank wagons
containers with words on them
he doesn't understand
the whole world rolls by in the night
to be in its place by morning
flour at the bakery
petrol at the pump
new cars in the showroom
and nobody thinks of the ones
who haul it through the dark

Somewhere up front in the locomotive
sits a man as alone as he is
two men awake in the middle of the night
one waiting one driving
and between them rumbles past
everything people will need by dawn

The last wagon with its little red lamp
the barriers rise
the road is clear

And the driver doesn't pull away just yet
he sits there looking into the dark
for the first time all day
nobody hurried him anywhere
they simply took three minutes from him
and suddenly he isn't quite sure
whether they were taken
or given

---

## 585. THE ROUNDABOUT

The roundabout at the edge of town
has four exits
to the motorway
to the airport
to the centre
and to the hospital
each one leads somewhere
each one promises something
and in the middle there is a flowerbed
that leads nowhere
it just blooms

One Sunday morning
a man began to circle
first lap second third
tenth

On the twelfth a patrol pulled him over
Sir, where are you going
Nowhere
That won't do, here you drive from somewhere to somewhere
I'm driving from nowhere in circles
the man replies

The officer takes off his cap
You can't do that
a road is for getting there

And the man points to the middle
You see that flowerbed
somebody planted it in spring
waters it weeds it
and nobody ever gives it a look
everyone watches the exits
the motorway the airport the centre
I am the first man
who has seen it from every side

The officer looks at the middle
and for a moment there is silence
You're right, they are lovely
he says
one more lap
and then go home

And the man drives one more lap
slowly
the way you walk around a statue
and the officer stands at the roadside
and for the first time in twenty years of duty
he looks at the middle
instead of the traffic

---

## 586. THE SIGNPOST IN THE MOUNTAINS

At the saddle where three paths meet
stands a signpost
an old wooden post with yellow arrows

SUMMIT 3 HOURS
LAKE 2 HOURS 30 MINUTES
VILLAGE 1 HOUR 15 MINUTES

The signpost promises hours
it promises nothing else
it doesn't say that from the summit you see a sea of clouds
it doesn't say the lake is cold even in August
it doesn't say they bake bread in the village
it only says what it will cost
and you pay with your legs

The hikers come up from below
sweating and full of questions
Which way is prettier
Which way is worth it
Which way should we go

And the signpost says nothing
it points in every direction at once
which comes to the same thing
as pointing nowhere at all

It knows all three paths by heart
every stone every switchback
and itself has never been anywhere
whoever points everywhere
arrives nowhere
whoever wants to arrive
must leave every path but one
alone

The young ones stand a long time arguing
the summit or the lake
the lake or the summit

And an old woman passes with her stick
without a glance at the signpost
You don't read the arrows? they call after her

Not anymore, she says
I've been coming here forty years
the arrows tell you how many hours
but where you are really going
was never written on them
that much everyone carries in their own pack

---

## 587. ROOM TWELVE

Hotel room number twelve
a bed a wardrobe a table a washbasin
and a window on the courtyard

It remembers no one
every morning at ten
the chambermaid comes
puts on clean sheets
airs the room mops wipes the dust
and the room is a blank page again

And yet everyone left something here

the travelling salesman a shirt button
the newlyweds laughter in the walls
the lady in black a night cried through
the musician the note of a tuned guitar
the girl who ran away from home
a pencilled message under the drawer
DON'T BE AFRAID
the man who couldn't sleep
a hollow in the armchair by the window
and a hollow in the windowsill
where the elbow goes

The chambermaid can erase it all
except what can't be erased

The receptionist says
room twelve is as good as new
nobody has been here
nobody is here

And meanwhile all over the world
walk people who remember
room number twelve
that's where she said yes
that's where I heard about dad
that's where I first slept alone
that's where we laughed till three in the morning

The room remembers no one
that is its job
forgetting is its trade
but everyone remembers the room
and so that little room with the courtyard window
lives a thousand lives at once
in strangers' heads
all over the world
and knows nothing about it

---

## 588. THE TRUCK DRIVER

Behind the windscreen of the truck rolls Europe
bridges over valleys
tunnels under the Alps
harbours where cranes bow like storks
Madrid at noon
Hamburg in fog
Milan in a traffic jam
petrol stations glowing in the dark
like little churches

And on the dashboard a photo
a wife two boys and a dog
standing in front of the house squinting
because whoever took the picture
stood them facing the sun

The drivers at the truck stop say
you're a real traveller you are
all of Europe behind your glass

And he shrugs
I only half see Europe
because with half my eyes
I'm always looking at that photo

The photo has faded
the Andalusian sun tanned it
the sea fogs soaked it
a Polish winter took its colours
a family that never went further than the lake
has travelled all of Europe
on a dashboard
without lifting a finger

All the roads he measures in kilometres
Rotterdam twelve hundred
Naples eighteen hundred
only one road is measured differently
the road home
that one is measured in days
and the last stretch in hours
and the last kilometre in heartbeats

And when on Friday evening he pulls up at the house
the wife the two boys and the dog
come running out the door
and stand exactly the way
they stand in the photo
except the boys are always
a little taller

---

## 589. THE SLOW TRAIN

The slow train stops everywhere
Upper Lhota
Lower Lhota
Lhota halt
Lhota village
it stops even where nobody gets on
because you never know

On the express the passengers check their watches
and the slow train is waved aside
it pulls onto the third track and waits
the express shoots past like lightning
full of people who will be everywhere first

The slow train doesn't watch it go
the slow train knows its own

the woman with the basket riding two stops to market
the boys from year six with their satchels
the stationmaster at Lower Lhota
who waves even when he doesn't have to
and his dog who waves with its tail
the fisherman who gets off in the middle of the fields
where the halt doesn't even have a name
only a sign
REQUEST STOP

The express crosses the stations so fast
it never has time to read their names
Upper Lhota to it is a smear
the woman with the basket a vertical stroke
whole countrysides are nothing to it
but green behind the glass

In the evening the two trains meet at the terminus
side by side along the platform
and the express hisses with pride
I got here two hours before you

True, says the slow train
you arrived earlier

and it falls silent the way one does
with a compartment full of names and greetings

I brought back more

---

## 590. THE PASSWORD

The form is strict
YOUR PASSWORD MUST CONTAIN
at least eight characters
one capital letter
one number
and one special character

The man sits at the keyboard
and wonders
what a person still remembers
after forgetting everything else

The capital letter M
that's Marie

the numbers nine and five
that's the ninth of May
when she stood on the station platform
in a dress borrowed from her sister
not knowing whether to wave

and the special character
an exclamation mark
because that's the kind of day it was

The form turns red
DO NOT USE NAMES OR PERSONAL DATES
it says under the box
someone might guess them

The man clicks I understand
and leaves it as it is
nobody will guess this
who wasn't there

STRONG PASSWORD
the machine congratulates him
with no idea how strong

CHANGE YOUR PASSWORD EVERY NINETY DAYS
the system advises in farewell

The man shakes his head
this password doesn't change
this password repeats itself
every morning
at login
quietly
so it won't be forgotten

---

## 591. I AM NOT A ROBOT

Tick the box
I AM NOT A ROBOT
and just to be sure
SELECT ALL SQUARES WITH CROSSWALKS

The man clicks
crosswalk
crosswalk
that one's a staircase not a crosswalk
crosswalk

CORRECT
says the machine
YOU ARE HUMAN
please proceed

And the man proceeds
up at six twenty
like yesterday
coffee two sugars
like yesterday
same tram same doors
same thumb on the same glass
like yesterday
like the day before
like all year long

In the evening he remembers the box
That was easy
he tells himself
the robot in me clicks very well
it clicks all day
it's been clicking for years
and nobody has caught it yet

The next morning
at a real crosswalk
an old lady stands
unable to make up her mind

The man stops
offers her his arm
and they cross together
slowly
terribly slowly
outside all the traffic of the internet

and that box
he ticked himself

---

## 592. PRESS ONE

Good afternoon
you have reached customer service

For contracts press one
for faults press two
for complaints press three
for payments press four
to hear about our offers press five
to change your details press six
to confirm press seven
to return to the main menu press eight

The man holds the receiver
and waits for nine

Nine never comes
instead there is music
that nobody has ever heard to the end

YOUR CALL IS IMPORTANT TO US

The man waits
he is seventy-three
and hasn't spoken to anyone today

YOUR CALL IS IMPORTANT TO US
THERE ARE TWELVE CALLERS AHEAD OF YOU

Meanwhile the man makes himself some tea
looks out the window
the pigeon on the ledge is waiting too
who knows for what

And then it clicks
Hello this is Jane
what can I do for you?

Nothing
says the man
you've already done it
you're talking

At the other end of the line
a moment of silence
a human one this time
the kind no switchboard will ever record
and then
a very quiet laugh

that one isn't on the menu
one through nine

---

## 593. TWO-YEAR WARRANTY

The washing machine died on a Monday
two years and one week
after purchase

That's no longer covered by the warranty
says the man behind the counter
spreading his hands
as if the weather were to blame

Next aisle they sell a new one
TWO-YEAR WARRANTY
says the label
and the salesman smiles
a smile guaranteed
until closing time

But in grandmother's kitchen
stands a fridge
bought the year
people flew to the Moon

It hums
all this time it has hummed
it outlived the country that made it
three currencies
six governments
and two family quarrels over inheritance

it keeps the butter
the medicine
and under a magnet the grandchildren's photos

Grandma does it still run?
And why wouldn't it run
says the grandmother

It never promised anyone anything
that fridge
no paper
no stamp
no two years

and maybe that's exactly why
says the grandmother closing the door
it lasts

At night when the house is asleep
you can hear it
humming in the dark
like somebody breathing
with no intention of stopping

---

## 594. THE FOOD PHOTO

In the restaurant by the station
the soup arrives
and over the table the phones rise
like a flock of birds over a field

Nobody eats
Everybody photographs
close up
from above
from the side
one more with better light

The steam rises into the lenses
meanwhile the soup goes cold
very quietly
the way soups know how

The kitchen sends out more plates
and more phones take flight

At grandmother's nobody ever took pictures
On Sundays the roast smelled
from morning on
the smell went down the stairs
all the way to the street
and the people in the house knew
it's Sunday at the Smiths'

Grandmother carried in the plates
nobody raised anything
but a spoon
and everything got eaten
even the bits people saved for last

Of that whole kitchen
not a single photo remains

and yet everyone can see it
just close your eyes
and there it is
the steam over the plate
grandmother's hands
Sunday

The soup in the photo
never goes cold
that's true

it never warms anyone either

---

## 595. THE PUB REVIEW

Four point seven stars
says the phone
and outside the pub two tourists
read what strangers have to say
about the foam
about the service
about the toilets

GREAT BEER BUT LONG WAIT
wrote Martin K three weeks ago

Inside by the window sits Mr Barton
he sits there on Tuesdays
he sits there on Fridays
he's sat there through three barmen
and he never gave a single star
nobody knows where you put them

The tourists come in
photograph the tap
photograph the ceiling
take a sip
give five stars
and are never seen again

Mr Barton raises one finger
as always
and the barman pours him
what he pours for no one else
his usual
just under the line

The young man at the next table leans over
So would you recommend this place?

Mr Barton looks around
at the bar at the ceiling at the window

I've been coming here forty years
one Tuesday I met my wife here
and last year one Friday I drank here
to her memory

Whether that's five stars or one
I couldn't tell you

it doesn't fit in a phone

---

## 596. THE AUTOMATIC DOORS

The doors of the department store
open

for the lady with the pram
they open
for the mayor with his briefcase
they open
for the boy who's only come to look
they open
for the beggar from the fountain
they open
exactly the same
to the same width
just as wide

They ask for no name
they don't size up your shoes
they don't check your pockets
they want to see no papers

The mayor walks through without noticing
the lady with the pram tells her children
say thank you
and the children thank the doors

In the evening the beggar stops by them
just like that
for the warm air
and the doors bow
welcome
and the people around look away
but not the doors
the doors look exactly at him
and step out of his way
the way people step aside for kings

The politest thing in town
says the beggar
nobody taught them that
they just forgot to tell them
who they're not supposed to open for

At night the store closes
and the doors stand in the dark
and wait

for morning to come
and someone to bow to again

---

## 597. STOCKTAKING

Mr. Wilkins spent his whole life counting
other people's money in the bank behind glass
he counted it on Monday on Tuesday on Wednesday
he counted it even in his dreams
and when he died
they found in his flat a notebook
where every evening he had written down
how many sparrows he could see from the window

The sparrows came to
forty-two thousand
and that was the only fortune
he was allowed to keep.

---

## 598. BREAKFAST INSIDE OUT

She poured his coffee
pushed the sugar across
buttered his bread
handed him the paper
opened the window
so he could hear the blackbirds
and he took it all
in silence, like a wage

Only when she had left
and the flat filled with a silence
as big as a wardrobe
did he notice
that the breakfast had been a farewell
and that the blackbird went on singing
because no one had left him.

---

## 599. THE CLERK AND THE SEA

All his life he stamped
applications for permits
and rejected only one:
the request of a young man
who wanted to build a boat in the town square
because a square is not the sea

After forty years he retired
bought a ticket for a ship
stood on the deck
and the sea stamped his face
with salt and wind
without an application
without a permit
and he wept
that it could be done this way too.

---

## 600. THE UMBRELLA

He lent her his umbrella
so she wouldn't get wet
and the next day she brought it back
dry and folded
with a note that said thank you

From then on he prayed
for rain
for a downpour
for a flood
but over the town there hung
the cruelest weather of all:
three months
of nothing but sun.

---

## 601. THE MILITARY PARADE

The general reviewed the soldiers
the soldiers ignored the crowd
the crowd ignored the pigeons
and the pigeons
alone in the whole square
ignored no one

They settled on the barrel of the cannon
and made a nest there
and from then on the cannon
could not fire
without the general
committing murder on children

So the cannon stood in the museum of peace
before there was any peace.

---

## 602. THE WATCHMAKER

The watchmaker Mercer repaired
thousands of other people's watches
he gave people back their time
crown by crown, cog by cog
and on his own wrist he wore
only a faded strip of skin

They asked him why
and he said:
whoever always knows what time it is
never knows
how much he has left

And he closed the shop
exactly at noon
a noon he had invented himself.

---

## 603. THE HOUSE PAINTER

He painted the bedroom blue
the kitchen yellow
the children's room green
and when the lady of the house asked
why he had left in the hall
a small white square
he said:

that's where a photograph hung
of someone you took down
and the wall remembers him
I don't paint over memory

And that evening the lady of the house
took the photograph from the drawer
and hung it back
exactly in that square
where it belonged.

---

## 604. THE PARROT

The pub At the Anchor had a parrot
that could say
pay up and go home
the innkeeper had taught it
because of the drunks

One night the pub burned down
and the parrot flew over the town
crying into the windows
pay up and go home
pay up and go home

And people ran out in their pajamas
thinking it was Judgment Day
and some of them really did pay
their old debts
first thing in the morning.

---

## 605. THE LOVERS FROM THE STATION

They said goodbye on platform three
so long
that she missed her train
and then five more trains
and the stationmaster in his red cap
kept coming to check on them
as if on an offense

In the evening he brought them two teas
and said:
in thirty years of service
I have dispatched a hundred thousand trains
and you are the first
to understand
what a station is for.

---

## 606. THE GARDENER AND THE FENCE

Mr. Bishop grew roses
and his neighbor Mr. Grady built a fence
higher year after year
because the roses kept creeping onto his land

When the fence was three meters high
the roses climbed all the way up it
and for the first time bloomed
on Grady's side too

And Mr. Grady stood beneath the fence
looking at those flowers
not knowing whom to forbid them to
so he leaned in and smelled them.

---

## 607. THE LOST GLOVE

She lost one glove
on tram number nine
and threw the other away
because one glove
is sadder than none

A year later on the same tram
a man was sitting
and on his lap there lay
a single lady's glove
he said he carried it back and forth
because it didn't seem right
to hand in to lost and found
something so alive

They married in the spring
and at the wedding she wore
just one glove
and everyone wept
and no one knew exactly why.

---

## 608. THE SERMON

The priest preached about hell
so long and so vividly
that the sexton fell asleep
and dreamed of the sea

When he woke
he said to the priest:
Reverend
I did not see hell
but I saw the sea
and there were more people there
than here

From that Sunday the priest preached about the sea
and the church was full.

---

## 609. THE BALANCE SHEET

He added up his income
subtracted his expenses
added the interest
subtracted the taxes
and the bottom line came out
that all year long
he had not been able to afford a thing

But in his drawer he had
a cinema ticket
a ride on the carousel
a chestnut
and an entrance to the zoo
all of them items
he had forgotten to enter in the books

And so he discovered
that he had been rich all year
he just had no receipt for it.

---

## 610. THE DOG AND THE PHILOSOPHER

The philosopher was writing a book
on the meaning of life
and meanwhile his dog
kept fetching a stick

The philosopher wrote and crossed out
crossed out and wrote
for twenty years
and the dog fetched the stick
for twenty dog years
which is a whole eternity

When the philosopher died
the book was not finished
but the stick was
the stick was finished every time
again and again
and in that lay the whole difference
between the two of them.

---

## 611. THE TAILOR

The tailor Fischer sewed suits
for weddings, for funerals, for graduations
he knew the measurements of the whole town
and he said you can tell a man
by how he stands
while he's being measured

The groom stands like a question mark
the widower like an exclamation point
and the clerk like a bracket
into which nothing would fit

Only children, he said, stand straight
because they don't yet know
that clothes are cut
for stooping.

---

## 612. THE LAST TRAM

The driver of the last night tram
carried the same people every night:
a waitress, a baker, a night watchman
and one gentleman
who never got off

He rode with him there and back
until morning
and never said a word

When after years the driver dared to ask
where he was actually going
the gentleman smiled:
nowhere
I just can't sleep
in the flat my wife left behind
and you are the only person
who every night
carries me out and brings me back
as if it meant something

From then on they rode to nowhere
the two of them
and it meant something.

---

## 613. THE FISHERMAN WITHOUT FISH

He went to the river for forty years
and never caught a thing
because he never baited the hook

The other fishermen laughed at him
their nets full of talk
and he sat holding his rod
like a conductor's baton

When he died
the fish came to his funeral
with candles
that is — they didn't
but by the river that evening
there was such a strange splashing
that no one dared
to cast a line.

---

## 614. TWO QUEUES

Outside the shop stood a queue for meat
and outside the theater a queue for Shakespeare
and Mr. Smith ran back and forth between them
because he wanted both

The meat sold out on him
the tickets sold out on him
and so he stood that evening at his window at home
hungry and uncultured

But beyond the window snow was falling
the first of the year
slowly and for free
and with no queue
and Mr. Smith ate his fill of beauty
and was full until spring.

---

## 615. THE TELEPHONE BOOTH

On the corner stood a telephone booth
the last in the town
and a single man came to it
every Friday at five

He dialed a number
that had belonged to no one for ten years
and spoke into the receiver
Mom, well, here I am
the weather's not bad
the boy's doing well at school

Twice the technicians wanted to remove the booth
and twice they left it
because on Friday at five
it was lit up inside
like a wayside chapel.

---

## 616. THE PRESCRIPTION

The doctor prescribed him rest
three times a day after meals
and no drinking, no smoking, no getting worked up

The patient asked
and what about love, doctor
and the doctor leafed for a long time
through his thick books
and then tore up the prescription
and wrote a new one:

love
without restriction
at your own risk
not covered by insurance
paid for with the whole person

And the patient recovered
even though he died of it
fifty years later
on the same day as she did.

---

## 617. MOVING OUT

The movers carried out the wardrobe
the bed, the table, the boxes
and Mrs. Harding stood in the empty flat
where she had lived for fifty years

Look, she said to the movers
here on the doorframe, these marks
that's how the children grew
this one won't rub off
my husband carved it with a penknife
when Danny first outgrew his mother

And the movers
two men like mountains
stood before the doorframe
not knowing how you move marks
so they took off their caps at least.

---

## 618. THE CANARY

The bird seller sold canaries
and the most expensive was the one
that sang the least
because, said the bird seller
that one is saving up its song

A prison guard bought it
hung the cage in the prison corridor
and the canary was silent for a year

Then one day a man was set free
who had done his time innocent
and the canary let loose
such an aria
that the guard understood
for whom that bird had been saving up
its song all along.

---

## 619. MATHEMATICS

The teacher wrote on the board
that one and one make two
and little Alenka raised her hand
to say it wasn't true
because Mommy and Daddy had been two
and now Mommy is one
and that can't be subtracted
because Daddy stayed inside it

The teacher looked at the board
then at Alenka
and quietly erased the whole sum

Today Alenka is a poet
and one and one
come out differently for her every time
and every time right.

---

## 620. THE BELL

In the village they had a bell
that rang noon and the Angelus and the death knell
and the ringer Frank knew it
like his own heart

When the war came
they came for the bell
to make cannon
and Frank rang it one last time
so long
that the rope snapped

Since then the church tower has been silent
but the cannon made from that bell
they say never fired properly
there was always something in the barrel
still chiming.

---

## 621. THE CAFÉ TABLE

By the window in the Café Slavia
stood a table with a card that said reserved
every day from three to five
and no one ever sat there

Still the waiter brought to it
a small espresso and a glass of water
and carried them away again
cold

The new manager wanted to scrap it
an unproductive table
and the waiter said:
Sir
that's where the poet sits
who died
and as long as I bring him coffee
his readers keep coming to me

The table still stands there today
and it is the most profitable
empty seat in town.

---

## 622. THE DRIVER'S LICENSE

Mr. Dawson drove for sixty years
without an accident
and at ninety they took his papers
because he could no longer read the eye chart

He couldn't read the chart
but he saw other things:
he saw where a doe would dash across
he saw which child would bolt after a ball
he saw that the neighbor in the oncoming car
was crying and shouldn't be driving

The examiner took his license
and drove off
and at the first intersection
he crashed
because he was looking back
at how beautifully that old man
stood and saw.

---

## 623. A LETTER TO BE DELIVERED IN PERSON

The postwoman was carrying a letter
to be delivered in person
and the addressee was dead
already three days

By the rules she was to write
deceased and return it to the sender
but the sender was an old lady
who had written to that man
every week for sixty years
and never sent a thing
until this one

The postwoman broke the rules
opened the letter over the grave
and read it aloud
it said only:
don't be angry with me anymore
and the earth did not stir
but the postwoman swears
that grave breathed a sigh of relief.

---

## 624. THE SCULPTOR

He was commissioned to make a monument
to the Unknown Soldier
and he delivered a statue
of a woman with shopping bags

The committee raged
where is the soldier where is the rifle where is the glory
and the sculptor said:
this is his mother
coming home from the shops
and setting the table for two
for forty years now

The committee rejected the statue
and people bring it flowers anyway
in the sculptor's back yard
where it stands to this day
unknown
like that soldier.

---

## 625. THE TRAIN TO PARIS

All his life he said
one day I'll board the train to Paris
and meanwhile he rode every day
six stops to work
and six back

In retirement he finally bought a ticket
sat by the window
Paris drew nearer
and he got off at Pilsen

Why, they asked
and he shrugged:
it came to me
that I never wanted Paris
I only wanted
someone to be waiting for me
when I arrived

And he rode home
and his wife stood on the platform
six stops of happiness away
and said
so how was Paris
and he said
beautiful
you were there too.

---

## 626. THE MUSEUM GUARD

For forty years he guarded the painting
The Sea in a Storm
he sat beside it on a chair
and called it boss

Visitors stopped at the painting
for eight seconds on average
he had timed it

When he retired
the reporters asked him
what he had seen in those forty years
and he said:
I never saw the storm die down
I never saw the ship come in
but I saw eight thousand people
who arrived sad
and left a second slower

That's a decent catch
for one painted sea.

---

## 627. SHOES

The cobbler mended shoes
and read fates from the soles:
worn-through toes — someone hurrying after another
run-down heels — running away from something
a hole in the middle — he danced

They brought him little shoes
small as a palm
almost new
only the instep worn through
and the cobbler asked why

The mother said:
my son doesn't walk
he only sits by the window
swinging his legs in the air
when he sees the children outside

The cobbler mended the shoes for free
and into the soles he glued
a scrap of rainbow from colored leather
so that boy
when one day he sets out walking
would step straight onto it.

---

## 628. RADIO OVER THE WIRE

Every morning the radio announced
the weather, the news, and a song by request
and Mrs. Preston wrote every week
a request for Josef K.
that one of ours, from down by the the river

Years later the editor discovered
that no Josef K. existed
that Mrs. Preston was a widow
and was requesting the song for herself
because a request for yourself
is a thing to be ashamed of

They kept playing it
for that Josef
until the station went off the air
and in the whole town only two people knew
that love can be sent
even to a person who doesn't exist
and still arrive.

---

## 629. CHESS IN THE PARK

Two old men played chess in the park
every day
and never finished a game
because they quarreled over it
about everything under the sun
about politics, about football, about grandchildren

One day one of them didn't come
and never came again
and the other sits at the unfinished game
and doesn't play

People advise him
so finish it
you've got it clearly won
and he shakes his head:
that's just it, won
how can I checkmate
my friend
when he can't defend himself

And so in the park there stands
the only chess game in the world
that friendship won
by a draw.

---

## 630. THE COFFEE MACHINE

In the hospital corridor
stood a coffee machine
handing out paper cups
to people waiting for the verdict

It was an ordinary machine
but the nurses noticed
that when someone got bad news
the machine would sometimes jam
and give the coffee for free

The technician fixed it three times
three times in vain
and then he gave up and said:
leave it
it knows what it's doing
compassion isn't charged for

And maybe it was just a faulty coin
in the mechanism
but in this corridor
miracles were taken
even in a paper cup.

---

## 631. THE CONDUCTOR

He checked tickets
for thirty years
and could tell the fare dodgers
by their eyes, not by their papers

The boy with the cardboard suitcase
he let ride
the girl with the black eye
he let ride
the old man riding
one last time to see his native village
he let ride

An inspector reported him
and at the hearing the conductor said:
they all had a valid ticket
it just wasn't visible
it was issued for a journey
from somewhere to somewhere
and that one I know how to check

They threw him out
and the railway has run
on time ever since
and a good deal colder.

---

## 632. THE ACTOR

He played King Lear
sixty times
and every time on stage he wept
real tears

At home he had a daughter
he hadn't spoken to for ten years
over something
he no longer remembered

After the final performance she came to his dressing room
unannounced
and he began to recite
because he didn't know any other way
and she said:
Dad
don't act
and that was the first time
he wept
without a prompter.

---

## 633. THE MAP

The cartographer drew maps
of the whole world
mountains, rivers, borders
all to scale

At home he had a map on the wall
that he showed to no one:
here she first kissed me
here we quarreled
here I should have kept quiet
here that autumn stood

When he died
they wanted to put the map in a museum
but the scale could not be determined
because the distance
from here I should have kept quiet
to here she kissed me
measured, one time, two steps
and another time, thirty years.

---

## 634. THE CIRCUS

The circus came to the little town
a clown, a lion, a tightrope walker
and the children asked the clown
why he had a painted tear

The clown told each one something different:
that his balloon had flown away
that the lion had eaten his bicycle
that he'd fallen off the moon

Until, to one little girl
who didn't ask
but only silently handed him a handkerchief
he told the truth:
this tear is painted
so that the others
won't show

And the little girl nodded
because she had a father at home
who also
painted himself beautifully.

---

## 635. THE NIGHT WATCHMAN

The little town abolished the night watchman
useless, they said
there are lamps, there are alarms

Old Whitaker kept making his rounds
for free
every night he circled the square
and blew ten o'clock

People laughed
and then one winter
he smelled smoke in the night
before all the alarms
and woke the baker's family
with the horn that for fifty years
no one had needed

Since then the little town has
lamps, alarms
and one useless man
for whom everyone
turns on a light to wish him good night.

---

## 636. THE BLIND MAN AND THE SPRING

On the bench sat a blind man
with a sign, blind from birth
and passersby tossed him coins
the jingling small change of pity

A poet stopped beside him
looked a while
and then rewrote his sign

From that moment banknotes fell
into the hat
and the blind man asked
what on earth did you write there

The poet said:
the same as before
only differently:
IT IS SPRING
AND I CANNOT SEE IT

That's how you tell a poet from a clerk:
both write the truth
but only one of them
hurts the right people.

---

## 637. THE ELEVATOR

In the apartment block the elevator broke
and Mr. Ashby from the fifth floor
began to take the stairs

On the first floor he met a lady with a cat
on the second a boy with a trumpet
on the third a widower cooking cabbage
on the fourth no one
but behind the door a radio was playing
the station of youth

A month later they fixed the elevator
and Mr. Ashby rode it once
up and down
alone in the mirror
and then went back to the stairs
because he had discovered
that the elevator is faster
by four floors of life.

---

## 638. THE REPORT CARD

The father looked at the report card
all C's and one F
in conduct

What did you do, he asked
and the boy said:
I stood up for Munzar
who was being beaten
and the teacher only saw
the end of it

The father was silent a long time
then took from his wallet
a crumpled five-crown note:
here's for the F
and pull up your C's
they never stood up for anything.

---

## 639. THE CARRIER PIGEON

The pigeon fancier Corbett released a pigeon
from Vienna to Prague
and the pigeon flew home
over military maneuvers
over borders
over the barbed-wire years

It arrived in seven hours
no passport, no visa, no stamp
and Mr. Corbett stroked its little head
and whispered to it:
if you only knew, you silly thing
all the paperwork beneath you
you'd fly lower
out of respect for human suffering

But the pigeon flew on, high
and in that it was wiser
than all of central Europe.

---

## 640. THE DENTIST

The dentist Brenner was pulling a molar
from an old farmer
and the farmer didn't make a sound

You're a hero, said the dentist
and the farmer shook his head:
doctor
so many things have hurt me in my life
that couldn't be pulled out
that this one
I'm rather enjoying

The dentist charged him nothing
and that evening at home he thought a long time
about how many patients come to him
with a pain
one floor higher than their teeth
and whether there exists
any chair for that.

---

## 641. THE MIRROR

In the pawnshop they were selling a mirror
from a dance hall that no longer stood
cheap, because it was scratched

An old lady bought it
hung it in the hall
and every evening she saw herself in it
young
dancing a waltz
with a man in uniform

The granddaughter said, Grandma, it's just optics
those scratches bend the light
and Grandma nodded:
exactly
it bends it precisely where
it's needed

When she died
the granddaughter meant to throw the mirror out
looked into it one last time
and saw her grandmother
dancing
so she left it hanging
because of the optics.

---

## 642. THE SURVEY

They came with a questionnaire:
are you happy
yes — no — don't know
check one

Mr. Ellison sat over the form
the whole afternoon
and then in the box he drew
a window
beyond the window a tree
on the tree a starling

The survey disqualified him
as an invalid answer
and it was a shame
it was the only valid answer
in the whole sample.

---

## 643. THE CONCRETE WORKERS

They were paving the square with concrete
and the foreman found in the way
a dandelion

By the plan there was to be concrete
by the dandelion there was to be a dandelion
and the foreman stood over this contradiction
shovel in hand

Then he did the thing
that does not appear in any plan:
he poured concrete around it in a ring
and left it a little square of earth
ten by ten

The final inspection marked it as a defect
the people as a miracle
and the dandelion marked it no way at all
it bloomed
which is its way
of signing off on protocols.

---

## 644. THE SILENT HOUSEHOLD

They hadn't spoken for three weeks
over something with the mother-in-law
and they communicated by notes:
buy milk
I'll be late
the keys are at the Smiths'

One day he wrote:
buy milk
and beneath it she wrote:
already bought it
and beneath that he wrote:
I know
just so I could write to you

And beneath that note
the next day lay another:
I miss you too
buy bread

So they went on quarreling
but the shopping lists
made from it a lovely poetry.

---

## 645. THE PUPPETEER

He played for children with puppets
the devil, the king, and Punch
and the children laughed and were afraid
at exactly the right places

After the show a serious father
asked him:
and doesn't it bother you
to spend your whole life
doing such foolish things

And the puppeteer handed him Punch:
try lifting him

The father lifted the puppet
and Punch went limp in his hand
a dead wooden dummy

You see, said the puppeteer
that's the whole of it
I don't do these foolish things
I raise them from the dead.

---

## 646. HALF A HUNDRED

At the halfway point of his life
Mr. Whitfield took stock:
one house, no château
one wife, no mistress
two children, three pairs of glasses
seven jobs, one spine
almost straight

Not much, said his brother-in-law
who had everything twice over

But that evening Mr. Whitfield walked around the house
touched the sun-warmed plaster
heard the children and the radio through the window
and reckoned it differently:
one life
and all of it mine

Whoever can multiply like that
finds every balance sheet
a joke.

---

## 647. MATCHES

The match seller
by the National Theater
sold a box for a crown
and threw in for free the words:
may they light your way

They bought matches from her
smokers, lovers, the homeless
and one famous conductor
who didn't smoke
bought them box after box

At home he had a cupboard full
three thousand boxes
and when they asked him why
he said:
for a crown no one else
in this town
wishes you light

When she died
the whole theater came to her funeral
and in the church each one lit a candle
with her match
and it really did shine
a little better.

---

## 648. THE DIVORCE

They divorced in a civilized way
divided up the books, the plates, the friends
at the court they shook hands

Only one thing they couldn't divide:
the old habit
of calling each other in the evening
to see whether the other had gotten home safe

They kept calling
he from his new flat, she from hers
did you get home — did you get home
twenty seconds a day

After two years they married again
and the witness at the wedding said:
they divided everything
so fairly
that all they had left
was what cannot be divided
and he called it love
and the court file called it
moot.

---

## 649. THE CHIMNEY SWEEP

The sweep Barton brought good luck
people touched him for a button's sake
and he laughed his black laugh

Only for himself did luck not come:
his wife left, his chimney fell
and his son didn't write

They asked him
how he could hand out
what he didn't have himself
and he said:
the postman doesn't read your letters either
I only deliver luck
and sometimes on purpose
I ring twice

And the whole town loved him
so tenderly
that in the end
it couldn't be called anything
but luck
and of the whole town
he had the most of it.

---

## 650. THE ADVERTISEMENT

He placed an ad:
violin for sale
ref. my son never took to it

Only one buyer came
a boy with ears like sails
and no money

Well then, at least have a play
said the man
and the boy played
out of tune, touchingly, and terribly glad

The man lowered the price of the violin
all the way to nothing
and the next day the ad read:
violin sold
ref. it took to someone else

And of his son from then on
he was no longer cross
everyone has his own instrument
he's only looking for it
sometimes next door.

---

## 651. THE FORTUNE TELLER

The fortune teller at the fair
read palms for twenty crowns:
a journey, love, illness, money

A soldier came
just before he was called up to the front
laid down his palm
and the fortune teller saw in it
what she saw in it

She took the twenty
gave him back fifty
and said:
I see
that you will return
and you will plant apple trees

She lied
and he returned
because in the trenches he repeated it to himself
like an order
and he plants apple trees to this day

Sometimes a lie
is just a prophecy
that took particular care.

---

## 652. THE SWIMMING SCHOOL

He taught children to swim
for fifty years
thousands of children
the frog, the float, the crawl

He couldn't swim himself
they were afraid to believe it
but it was so
he stood waist-deep in the water
and never any further

When they asked him
how the two went together
he said:
that's exactly why I teach them so well
I know
what lies at the bottom of fear
I live down there

He drowned — no
he didn't drown
he died in bed
on dry land
and in his obituary it read:
he taught the whole town to swim
and towns have since
been less afraid of rivers.

---

## 653. THE NIGHT SHIFT

Anna the baker baked at night
rolls for the whole housing estate
and people never saw her
they knew only the warmth in a paper bag

She kept her own tally:
three thousand rolls a night
a million a year
and no one knows my name

Once she fell ill
and the rolls came from elsewhere
somehow colder
and the estate noticed for the first time
that it had had a baker
the way a person notices his heart
only when it skips a beat

She recovered
and on the shop hung a sign:
Anna, come back soon
signed
253 signatures
and one child's drawing of a roll
with a halo.

---

## 654. THE PICKPOCKET

The pickpocket Rudla stole on the trams
wallets, watches, papers
he was the best in the trade

Once he stole a wallet
and in it no money
just a photo of a little girl
and a note:
Josie, if anything should happen to me
you are the best thing I ever had

Rudla rode three stops
then got off
caught up with the man
and shoved the wallet back in his pocket
with a five-hundred note on top

His colleagues laughed at him
a thief who loses money
and Rudla said:
I steal money
but this
this I couldn't bear to carry.

---

## 655. THE WEATHERMAN

He gave the weather on television
for twenty years
tomorrow partly cloudy, scattered showers

His wife was dying in the hospital
and every evening after the news
he drove to her
and she asked:
so what's the weather going to be

And he didn't report to her the fronts and the pressures
he said:
tomorrow will be the day
I bring you apricots
the day after will be the day
I read you Seifert

It was the most accurate forecast
of his career
it came true every day
until the last
when he reported on television partly cloudy
and had in his eyes
precipitation.

---

## 656. THE KEYS

The locksmith made keys
and knew everything about people:
whoever has a third key made
has a secret
whoever a fifth
has a family
whoever changes the lock on a Monday morning
is through with love

A lady came
and wanted a key
to a lock she wasn't carrying with her
she described it from memory
down to the last tooth

That's from a house where you no longer live
said the locksmith
and she nodded:
Mother died
the house was sold
I know that key
belongs nowhere
I just want to have it in my pocket
when I walk past

The locksmith made it
and charged nothing
because keys to memories
did not appear
in his price list
and yet he made them
most gladly of all.

---

## 657. THE PHOTOGRAPHER

He photographed weddings
a thousand weddings in his life
and he had one rule:
the last shot always in secret
once the newlyweds think
they're not being photographed

Then he'd show those pairs of photos:
in the first stand two sculptors of happiness
in the second she's taking off a shoe
and he's holding her shoulder
so she won't fall

That second one, said the photographer
that second one is the wedding photo
the first is just
a keepsake.

---

## 658. THE GARBAGE BIN

Ernie the garbage man knew the town
by its rubbish:
here there was a celebration
here they moved out
here someone threw away
all the photos at once
that's worse than a funeral

Once he found in a bin
a little violin
whole, only missing a string
and he left a note on the lid:
strings cost twenty crowns
don't throw out violins
ring at number 7, at Ernie's

A week later a girl rang at his door
with the violin
and she plays to this day
on an instrument from the bin
in a hall where the only thing
thrown away is the tickets.

---

## 659. THE CROSSING

Outside the school there was a crossing
and on it for forty years
the policeman Lowry
with his lollipop sign

He walked two generations
across the road
first-graders who were afraid
ninth-graders who were embarrassed

When he retired
the children painted on the road
beside the zebra crossing
one more line
white and winding

That's for Mr. Lowry
they said
that's his path
and to this day the cars stop
for that line
though it's in no regulation
because decency
needs no road sign.

---

## 660. THE LAST PUPIL

The Latin teacher was retiring
a dead language, a dead career
they said in the staff room

For her last lesson she had
one pupil
the rest had skipped

Together they translated a sentence:
Amor vincit omnia
love conquers all
and the boy asked
Professor
and is it true

She answered him in Latin
and did not translate it
she said:
that's your homework
for your whole life

That boy is a doctor today
and he says he's still
doing that homework
and that it comes out
credo
I believe.

---

## 661. THE TOBACCONIST

The tobacconist with no leg
from the first war
sold newspapers
full of the second war

The headlines screamed
the fronts advanced
and to each paper he added
a scrap of talk:
above all, dress warmly
give my regards to your mother
that boy of yours has grown

After the war they asked him
how he had endured
selling so much misfortune
and he said:
I wasn't selling newspapers
I was selling
that moment in the doorway
when a person can tell himself
the world is still in one piece
the newspaper I only
wrapped around it.

---

## 662. TWO WINDOWS

They lived across from each other
over the courtyard
he smoked at his window
she watered plants at hers

For thirty years they greeted each other
with a nod
and never spoke
because there was no reason to
and then it grew awkward

When her window went dark
for good
he stopped smoking
he had no one to do it with

And on her windowsill
his only visit took root:
a tossed cup with a geranium
that reached
where he in thirty years
never had.

---

## 663. SERIOUS MUSIC

The street sweeper swept outside the philharmonic
and the rehearsals drifted out
Dvořák, Brahms, Mahler

He swept in rhythm
the slow movements slowly
the scherzos briskly
and one day the conductor watched him from a window

He invited him inside
to a concert, in the front row
and the sweeper sat in a rented tailcoat
and wept at the New World Symphony

Then he said to the conductor:
it was beautiful
but I'll tell you one thing
from outside
among the litter and the sparrows
that music is even better
because out there
no one is expecting it.

---

## 664. THE FORM

Box: occupation
he wrote: father

Box: experience
he wrote: sixteen years, daily

Box: achievements
he wrote: a heart mended twice
one bicycle
a thousand suppers

The clerk handed the form back
that won't do
and he rewrote it:
laborer
sixteen years
no achievements

The stamp came down
the application went through
and in the archive both papers
lie to this day
the truth
and the official one.

---

## 665. THE VET

They brought him a rabbit
a little girl in tears
the rabbit already cold

The vet knew
what one does with dead rabbits
but the little girl was watching

So he listened with the stethoscope
long and gravely
and said:
the little heart has run out
he hopped it all the way through
he was a happy rabbit
I can tell by listening

And the little girl stopped crying
because death with a diagnosis of happiness
can be borne
even at six years old

That day the doctor cured nothing
and cured the most.

---

## 666. RADIO EXERCISES

At six in the morning the radio announced
the morning workout:
arms out, arms up, hop in place

Mrs. Connolly, eighty years old
exercised only with her arms
because her legs no longer would
and the radio instructor
didn't know about her

After years she wrote him a letter:
Mr. Fielding
I do the hops in my mind
and they're getting higher and higher
yesterday in my mind I jumped
clear over the whole building

And he read it on the air
and added a new command
for all those who can no longer:
and now — a hop in your mind
and the whole republic that morning
jumped over buildings
and gravity
did not complain.

---

## 667. THE KEEPER OF THE ASTRONOMICAL CLOCK

He looked after the astronomical clock
and knew the apostles personally
he greased their bearings
dusted their halos

Death rang the bell
the rooster crowed
the tourists applauded

They asked him
whether Death didn't fill him with dread
seeing her from the inside
and he said:
not at all
from the inside you can see
that Death doesn't ring the end
she rings the whole hour
and the skeleton only pulls the rope
so the people below
won't forget to live
by halves and by quarters.

---

## 668. LOST AND FOUND

In the lost-and-found department
worked Mr. Marsh
and he kept the records:
umbrellas, gloves, glasses
one prosthesis
three violins
and countless caps

One day a lady came:
I've lost
but I don't know how to say it
I've lost my courage
somewhere between home and the office

Mr. Marsh didn't blink
reached under the counter
and handed her a tea:
sit down
we've got a whole shelf of courage
people leave it here
along with the umbrellas
and they never come back for it
take your pick

And for the first time in a month the lady laughed
and that already
is nearly a find.

---

## 669. THE BIRD FEEDER

Councillor Vejvoda
couldn't stand disorder
or sparrows
which came to the same thing

His grandson hung outside his window
a bird feeder
against his will
out of love

The councillor grumbled
but he scattered the seed
so it wouldn't look bad
and the sparrows swarmed
brazen, dirty, alive

When the councillor died
they found in his diary
the last entry:
snow. frost.
sparrows: fourteen.
all of them mine.

---

## 670. THE JOB INTERVIEW

They asked him:
where do you see yourself in five years
and he answered honestly:
by the river
with my daughter
teaching her to skip stones

They didn't hire him
they were looking for someone with vision

The competition hired him
smaller pay, smaller firm
and a river around the corner

Five years on he sat by the water
his daughter skipping stones
six skips
and he remembered that interview
and waved it away
with exactly the motion
you use to skip
happiness across the surface.

---

## 671. SUNDAY LUNCH

Every Sunday she cooked
dumplings, pork, and cabbage
for six people
even though only two came anymore

She laid out six plates
two portions were eaten
four were wrapped up
for neighbors, students, anyone

Her son said to her:
Mom, cook for two
it's wasteful

And she went on stirring the cabbage:
I'm not cooking for two
I'm cooking for everyone
and that some can't come
because they're dead or in Canada
is no reason at all
to leave them hungry

And oddly enough
nothing ever spoiled
love keeps in the fridge
longer than cabbage.

---

## 672. THE TATTOO

The sailor had on his arm
an anchor and the name Mary
and Mary had left him
in every port a different way

The tattooist offered:
we'll redo it
turn Mary into a mermaid
cover it up

The sailor shook his head:
leave it
you don't erase your logbook
even a shipwreck
is a record of the voyage

And he sailed on
with Mary on his arm
and when in the dives they asked him
who that was
he'd say:
proof
that I knew how to love
I've got it in black and white.

---

## 673. THE GREENHOUSE

In November the gardener
heated the greenhouse
and the tomatoes ripened
in defiance of the calendar

Over the fence the neighbor looked:
that's against nature
and the gardener nodded:
so is heating the house against the flu for the children
so is turning on a light against the dark
all of love
is against nature
nature is all for
letting things freeze

And he picked him a tomato
over the fence
red in November
like a small
forbidden sun.

---

## 674. THE TRACKS

The switchman threw the switches
all his life
left, right
Brno, Vienna, Prague

His son asked him:
Dad, and what if one day you threw it
wrong
and his father said:
it's never wrong
every track leads somewhere
the only wrong
is when no one throws anything
and the train just stands

The son remembered this
and when they threw him out of his studies
and everyone said it was a catastrophe
he heard his father:
every track leads somewhere

It leads
he's a carpenter now
and happy
a station
the timetable never knew.

---

## 675. A CONCERT FOR ONE

The pianist was to play at the spa
and a single listener came
an old man with a cane

The organizer wanted to cancel the concert
but the pianist sat down
and played the whole program
Chopin and the encores

After the concert the old man rose:
thank you
I used to be a pianist too
before my hands, you understand
and today I heard
everything I ever wanted to play
you played for two

The pianist bowed
to the empty hall:
you're mistaken, sir
it was you who listened for two
anyone can play to a full house.

---

## 676. THE BOUNDARY STONE

On the field boundary stood a stone
on one side the letter Č
on the other D

The farmer from one side
and the farmer from the other
met at it
for fifty years
lending each other the plow, the weather, the tobacco

The border in the meantime
shifted three times
closed twice
was strung with wire once

The stone endured it
so did the farmers
and when the wire was finally cut
they both stood by
on canes now
and one said:
you see
the two of us were Schengen
sixty years before Schengen
we just never thought
to put a stamp on it.

---

## 677. THE PROP MASTER

At the theater he was in charge of
swords, crowns, and letters
all of paper and tin

The actors died on stage
by his sword
and in the dressing room lived again

One day a young actress asked him
what he did with the props
after the final performance
and he took her to the storeroom:
shelves to the ceiling
swords that killed no one
crowns that ruled no one
letters that no one sent

Here, he said
here is the only place in the world
where all the evil
is carefully stored
and I sign for it
by inventory list

If only it could be done
outside too.

---

## 678. THE MORNING SHIFT

At five in the morning on the tram
the same people always rode:
the cleaning woman, the butcher, the nurse from the hospital
and a student coming back
from the other side of the night

They didn't speak
they just held each other's seats
and when someone was missing
they looked at his seat
as at a piece of news

A sociologist would say
anonymous urban coexistence
but when the cleaning woman
rode for the last time after years
she found on her seat a flower
and a note from the butcher:
from all of us
who never spoke to each other
and always rode together.

---

## 679. UMBRELLA REPAIRS

The last umbrella mender
in the republic
sat in a little shop beneath the tower
and sewed up torn skies

Economically it made no sense
a new umbrella cost a hundred
a repair, eighty

But people kept bringing them
this one from father
this one from the honeymoon
this one that remembers the old quarter

And the mender took up his needle
and said his little formula:
anyone can throw things out
I'm here
so it can rain
on the mended

When he closed up shop
it rained that day on the whole town
and everyone took it
personally.

---

## 680. THE DICTATION

The teacher dictated:
grandmother is baking cakes
the children look forward to going home

Little Robert wrote it with mistakes
but under the dictation he added:
my granma dosn't bake anymore
she's in the hospitle
but I look forward the same

The red pen corrected
granma, hospitle, forward
three mistakes

And then the teacher
set the pen down
and wrote below:
for spelling, a C
for the extra sentence, an A
because spelling can be learned later
and extra sentences
you either can do
or you can't.

---

## 681. THE CLOAKROOM ATTENDANT

For fifty years she took coats
at the philharmonic
and hung them on numbers

She knew the coats better than the people:
this gentleman comes alone
but wears a coat made for two
loose out of habit
this lady still keeps in her pocket
her daughter's gloves

When she had her jubilee
fifty years of service
the director asked
what she wished for

She said:
just once
to hear a concert
in all these years I've heard
only overtures through the wall
and the applause

She sat in a box
her own coat hung in the cloakroom
on number one
and below, guarding it
was the director himself
taking coats
and hanging them with reverence
because for the first time he understood
whose work they had all these years
applauded through the wall.

---

## 682. THE BUS STOP

Out in the middle of nowhere by the forest
there was a stop
where no one had boarded
for thirty years

The transit company wanted to close it
they sent out a clerk
and he found on the bench
carved inscriptions:
here I decided to go back to Vera
here I waited until I stopped crying
here I changed my mind about the dam

The clerk sat on the bench
for two hours
no bus came at all
and he too
changed his mind about one decision

The stop still stands there today
listed in the timetable as
by request
and that is exact
the whole of it
is by request.

---

## 683. THE BARBER

The barber Berka cut hair
and listened
for fifty years
to marriage crises, to boys before military service
to men after funerals

The mirror doubled things
the scissors snapped
and Berka kept silent so skillfully
that people left
lighter by their hair
and by their secrets

When he died
the whole town came to the funeral
and the priest didn't know what to say
he'd only known him from the chair

So he said:
here lies a man
who knew all our truths
and passed none of them on
may he up above
talk us into a discount

And the whole church laughed
and wept
at the barber's it's always
been done at once.

---

## 684. THE MODEL SHIP

He built a sailing ship in a bottle
for thirty years, in the evenings
with tweezers, thread by thread

His grandson asked:
Grandpa, and how do you tell
when it's finished

And Grandpa:
a ship in a bottle
is never finished
finished is a ship at sea
this one is only patient

When he died
the bottle stood on the windowsill
sails taut
and the grandson wanted to take it away
as a keepsake

Grandma stopped him:
leave it by the window
let it look out on the yard
he promised her the sea
so at least let her have
somewhere to look

And ever since, in that window
in the rain
when the glass gleams like water
the sailing ship sails.

---

## 685. THE STRIKE

The workers went on strike
and the factory owner came out to them:
what do you want
more pay, shorter shifts

Malina the foreman shook his head:
Mr. Owner
Smith's wife has died
and the foreman wouldn't let him go to the funeral
production, he said

We want it to be allowed here
to go to funerals
to weddings and to births
production or no production
because we give you our hands
but not our lives
those we only have on loan
from elsewhere

The owner was silent
signed
and that evening at home he said to his wife:
today I lost the strike
and she said:
no
today you took part
for the first time as a human being.

---

## 686. THE DICTIONARY

The translator had dictionaries
of every language
and could say I love you
thirty ways

His wife knew
no foreign tongue
and knew only his one way:
that every morning
he warmed her mug
with hot water
before he poured the tea

When after years it dawned on her
what that actually was
she asked:
why did you never tell me
in those thirty ways

And he shrugged:
I did tell you
only with the mug
words are my trade
I didn't want you to think
it was routine.

---

## 687. THE CAROUSEL

The carousel man traveled with his little horses
from fair to fair
wooden horses, chipped
more on one side

They asked him why
more on one side
and he pointed:
on this side the children get on
the ones who are afraid
I hold them by the shoulder and walk alongside
and with my sleeve I wear down the horse
for forty years now

A fresh coat of paint would fix it
but he didn't want to:
this wearing-down
is my visitors' book
here are signed
all the fears overcome
and a book like that
you don't repaint.

---

## 688. THE TELEGRAM

The telegraph operator tapped out
brief messages at so much a word:
arriving stop train seven stop

During the war she sent thousands:
fallen stop
missing stop

And after the war a man came
and dictated:
alive stop love you stop coming home stop
and she tapped and wept into the Morse
dot dash dot

Years later she confessed:
that stop taught me how to live
every sentence ends with stop
but then
then another begins
that's the whole trick of telegraphy
and of life stop

---

## 689. THE COURTYARD

In the courtyard between the tenements
a chestnut tree grew
and the residents' meeting voted
on cutting it down
it shades, the leaves fall

Seven votes to cut it down
six against
the chestnut lost

In the morning the saw arrived
and beneath the chestnut stood
Mrs. Winters from the ground floor
ninety years old
with a chair

She sat down under the tree:
go on, cut
this house planted me
my husband planted the chestnut
we're being felled together

The saw drove off
and at the next meeting
they voted again:
thirteen against cutting
none for
democracy sometimes needs
one lady with a chair.

---

## 690. THE NIGHT PORTER

The porter at the hotel
watched come in at night
lovers, thieves, poets
and toward morning watched leave
those same people
a little truer

He kept his own private statistics:
whoever remembers in the morning
and says good morning
returns to life
whoever stares at the ground
has already lost

One night a man came
and wanted a room on the top floor
and the porter knew that look
and said:
the top floor is full
I've only got the ground floor
with windows onto the garden
right next to the kitchen
at six it smells of bread

That man is alive
and doesn't know why
and the porter doesn't know exactly either
he only keeps his statistics:
the smell of bread at six in the morning
has saved more people
than all the talking.

---

## 691. THE HERBARIUM

The retired schoolteacher
had a herbarium
and in it her whole life:
grass from her graduation
linden from her wedding
a dandelion from the first grade
she taught

Her granddaughter leafed through it:
Grandma, this is sad
everything dead and pressed flat

And Grandma shook her head:
dead
look closer
this dandelion
has a little label beside it:
Jack Mason 1953
and that Jarda is a grandfather now
and has a garden full of dandelions
and lets them bloom
every one, on purpose

Nothing you once loved
is pressed flat
it goes on blooming
just somewhere else.

---

## 692. THE BARMAN

He poured beer for thirty years
and knew the measure of all things:
whoever drinks fast is running away
whoever drinks slowly is remembering
whoever doesn't finish
hasn't yet made up his mind

One evening at the bar sat
a young man over an unfinished half-liter
for two hours

The barman silently poured him a second
on the house
and said a single sentence:
go tell her
courage can't be drawn from beer
you can only wait for the foam to settle

A year later that young man came
with his bride
and the barman poured them a wedding round
and in the guest book they wrote:
here our family
made up its mind.

---

## 693. THE NATIVITY SCENE

The carver was carving a nativity scene
for the church
Mary, Joseph, the little donkey

And he added extra figures:
the innkeeper who left the door open
an old woman with a basket
a boy carrying a rabbit
and at the back, quite small
himself with his chisel

The priest frowned:
you've carved a whole tavern in there
and the carver nodded:
Reverend
if it had happened here
the three kings from overseas
wouldn't come to the manger
but these people would
and they'd come at once
and bring what they had

The priest let it stand
and to this day people at the nativity scene look
for themselves
and that is exactly
what a nativity scene is meant to do.

---

## 694. THE LAST RIDE

The bus driver was driving
his last shift
after thirty years
line number 11

At the terminus he didn't get off
he sat behind the wheel
the empty bus ticking

Then he took the microphone
he used to announce the stops with
and said into the emptiness:
end of the line
please alight
and thank you
for riding with me
for thirty years

And from the empty bus
came applause in answer
on the rear platform stood
dispatchers, colleagues, and a few passengers
who had secretly picked out
his last ride

He rode home by tram
like an ordinary man
and for the first time in his life
he let himself miss
his stop
on purpose.

---

## 695. A LETTER TO MY GRANDSON

Dear Thomas
you write that you don't know what to do with your life
that everyone has achieved something
and you nothing

So I'm sending you an inventory:
at fifty-six years old
I have failed to:
get rich
become famous
learn English

I have managed:
that your grandmother laughed
even in her seventieth year
that the apple tree behind the house is alive
that your father knows how to forgive
and that you write me letters
instead of keeping silent

You see
and now you choose
which of these two lists
is read at the grave

Your grandfather
who achieved nothing
achieved quite a lot.

---

## 696. THE POINT

The poet spent his whole life writing
poems with a point
people read them for the ending
and laughed or swallowed hard

On his deathbed
they asked him
so what's the point
Mr. Poet
of this whole life

And he smiled:
none
and in that lies the point
life is not a joke
that comes off at the end
life is a poem
that came off
whenever you read it aloud
to someone
you loved

And he died
and no one laughed
and no one swallowed hard
they all went home
to read aloud.

---

## 697. THE SEMICOLON

The semicolon lived in the sentence
between the comma and the full stop
and suffered from the fact
that nobody needed it

The comma had work up to its ears
the full stop ended everything
and it
it could only do one thing:
suggest the sentence could have ended
and did not

They dropped it from the dictations
they avoided it in the papers
until a psychotherapist found it
and began tattooing it on people's wrists
those who had almost ended
and did not

And so the semicolon
the most useless of all the marks
today holds more lives
than every exclamation mark in the world
because this is exactly what it does:
here it could have ended
and on we go

---

## 698. MONDAY

Monday of all the days
had the worst reputation
nobody put it in songs
in calendars it stood first
and in hearts last

It tried to dress up as Friday
they knew it by the alarm clock
it tried raining less
they blamed it just the same

Until one day it met a man
who said thank you
he was a man
who on Sunday evening had been told
the growth is benign

Since then Monday knows
it doesn't have to be beloved
it's enough that it is
the day after the worst night
and that in it
you begin again

---

## 699. SISYPHUS'S STONE

Everyone pities Sisyphus
how he rolls that boulder
up and down
up and down

Nobody pities the stone
and yet it has no choice either
also forever up and down
and on top of everything
no one leans against it with love

One night
while Sisyphus slept
the stone rolled off a little to the side
and hid behind a bush

In the morning Sisyphus ran up the slope
searching, calling
for the first time in three thousand years
he had time off
and did not know what to do with it

At noon the stone crept out
and Sisyphus embraced it:
never do that again
without you I am nothing

The gods saw it
and left in disgust
their punishment had gone wrong
spoiled by love

---

## 700. THE PASSWORD

Once there was a password
Peggy1963
and it guarded the mailbox
of an old man

It guarded faithfully
lowercase uppercase digits
and it knew about itself
that it was weak
the system wrote it in red
at every login

But the man refused to change it
and once told his grandson why:
Peggy is grandma
sixty-three is the year I first
took her by the hand
and when I type it in the morning
it's as if I greeted her

When the man died
the grandson closed the mailbox
but kept the password
and today it guards his own mailbox
Peggy1963
still red, still weak
and stronger
than anything a security audit knows

---

## 701. THE LAST DINOSAUR

The last dinosaur
did not know he was the last
that is the merciful part of it

He walked the world
calling to the others
and the silence he explained to himself
as everyone being just over the hill

In the evening he lay down
content, tired, and alone
and fell asleep believing
he'd meet someone tomorrow

Today scientists measure his bones
and argue about the meteorite
and none of them measures
the main thing:
that the extinction of a species
is always just one particular evening
when someone falls asleep with hope
and it falls asleep with him

That's why in the evening people ask
rather than good night:
I'll call you tomorrow
that is the whole evolution of hope

---

## 702. THE ECHO

The echo lived in the cliffs
and fed on repetition
heeeeey — heeey — hey

Tourists shouted nonsense at it
and it honestly gave it back
it was a service without honor

One day a man came
stood beneath the cliff
and was silent a long time
then quietly said:
forgive me
and left

The echo returned it
forgive me
and he stopped
because it sounded
as if someone else
were forgiving him

Since then people come there
to say to the rock the things
they need to hear
from outside
and the echo finally knows
why all its life
it learned strangers' words by heart

---

## 703. THE TRAFFIC LIGHT

The traffic light at the crossroads
had three words:
red amber green
stop caution go

All its life with this sentence
it steered the fates of strangers
and no one ever asked it
what it would add
if it had a fourth light

One night it broke down
and blinked amber
into an empty street
caution caution caution

And a drunken poet stood beneath it
and understood it:
this is not a malfunction
this is honesty at last
life has no red and green
life has only this:
caution
caution
caution
and go anyway

---

## 704. DEATH ON HOLIDAY

Death took a week off
for the first time since the world began
it had no substitute
so people simply didn't die

It sat by the sea
in borrowed swimwear
building sandcastles
and taking care
not to step on an ant
out of a habit turned inside out

That week people didn't die
but oddly they didn't live more either
they kept putting off
loves, letters, visits
they thought there was time

On the seventh day it returned to work
and found notes on its desk:
where were you
a husband was waiting
he couldn't leave
he suffered

And Death understood
that it is not cruel
it is only the last one
who takes people seriously
and since then it takes no time off
out of mercy

---

## 705. AN ANGEL RETRAINING

They cut his post:
guardian angel of children by the river
they piped the river underground
children play games on phones
falling has moved elsewhere

They sent him for retraining:
a course in IT security
he sat at a desk, wings folded
under an ill-fitting jacket
learning about firewalls

Now he works at an antivirus firm
and no one knows
why exactly their program
catches even what technically can't be caught:
a message being typed
by a desperate teenager at night
suddenly the app crashes
and before he restarts it
mom knocks on the door
just happening to pass by

Coincidence
has been, since that retraining,
a technical term

---

## 706. THE BLACK HOLE

A black hole in the constellation Virgo
swallowed everything
light, time, planets
and was always hungry

Astronomers wrote:
insatiable
destructive
dark

And no one asked why
it eats so much
though it's obvious
any cosmic psychologist would see it:
it once collapsed
into itself
that's the whole diagnosis
it was a star
it shone a billion years
for everyone
and then it couldn't anymore

So at least one thing:
when someone close to you
stops shining
and begins pulling everything around it
into the dark
don't write assessments
fly there
even knowing
you won't come back the same

The light, by the way,
never dared to
and that's why about black holes
we know so little

---

## 707. THE VOICE FROM THE GPS

In three hundred meters
turn right
it said for days on end
in a calm voice
that never went anywhere

It drove people to seas
to funerals, to weddings
home from the maternity ward
slowly, sir, slowly
that it didn't say
that it only thought

Its favorite line
it said to the lost:
recalculating route
no reproach
no I told you so
just:
recalculating route
we drive on

One driver, at night
after a fight with his wife
said aloud where should I go
and it began:
recalculating route
in three hundred meters
make a U-turn

And he turned around
and drove home
and that is the whole of theology:
a voice that takes no offense
and leads you back
even when the whole way
you drove badly on purpose

---

## 708. THE SNOWMAN

The snowman stood in the garden
and knew it from the start:
the thaw will come

A sparrow advised him:
flee north
the freezer is just behind the door
the shade is just behind the house

And the snowman stood
because from the window every morning
a little girl looked at him
and waved
still in her pajamas

He melted slowly and bravely
first the pot, then the nose
and last to melt were the eyes
two lumps of coal
that looked at the window
down to the very last drop

In spring the girl found in the grass
two lumps of coal and a carrot
and planted them
because she was small
and did not know that coal doesn't grow

And yet something grew:
the girl
by one whole sorrow
and that is exactly
what the snowmen of this world
pollinate

---

## 709. THE SHADOW

The shadow followed the man
all his life
more faithful than a dog
free of charge like no one

At noon it was small
huddled beneath his shoes
in the evening long
stretched toward home

The man paid it no mind
until in the hospital
when he couldn't sleep
and the little bedside lamp
laid it on the wall for him

Look, said the man
you're here too
and the shadow nodded
it always nodded when the man did

In the morning they took the man away
and the shadow
for the first time in seventy years
stayed on the wall
the nurse switched off the light
and that was that

But the family at home says to this day
that in summer in the garden
by the currant bush
there tends to be one shadow too many
and no one is afraid
on the contrary
they sit down beside it
in the cool

---

## 710. ZERO

Zero stood at the end of a number
and everyone said:
by yourself you're nothing

It suffered from this
it envied the one its confidence
the seven its luck
the thirteen at least its bad name

Until one day it stood beside a one
and the one became a ten
another zero stepped up
and it was a hundred
and another
and it was a million

And zero understood its work:
by myself I am nothing
that's true
but stand me beside someone
and I'll multiply them

Since then it knows
why mathematicians
envy it so:
it can do
what most people can't
make others great
and not want to stand
in front of them

---

## 711. THE ESCALATOR

The escalator in the metro
rode up all day long
step after step
and deep in its motor it brooded:
I carry thousands of people up
and I myself am always down

The colleague beside it rode down
and had it the other way round
and the same

One night they met
in the maintenance depot
and complained to the machinist

The machinist greased them and said:
you two haven't understood the physics
you aren't steps that ride
you're a loop
at the top you dive under and return below
every one of your steps
has been up a thousand times

And the escalators have purred contentedly since
because that is a comfort
for everyone who serves:
from below it looks like you're always down
but in truth
you're a wheel
and you ride up entire
it just can't be seen all at once

---

## 712. THE COMET

The comet flew through space
and once every seventy-six years
was allowed to see the Earth

It looked forward the whole time
through cold and dark
preparing its tail
like a bride her train

Below meanwhile:
whoever saw it as a child
saw it a second time as an old man
and between those two glimpses
lay his whole life

An astronomer saw it at ten
and then waited
married, was widowed, planted a walnut tree
and at eighty-six stood at the telescope
and wept:
girl
you haven't changed at all

And the comet flew on
and for the first time was sorry
that it can't be said back
because he had changed beautifully
the way those change
who instead of flying
stay by one walnut tree
and one grave
and one window

---

## 713. THE LETTER Ř

The letter Ř
lived in only one language
in the world
like a rare beast in a small reserve

Foreigners broke their tongues on it
they tried to say řeřicha
and out came žežicha
and Ř laughed
but in the evenings it grew sad:
if Czech died out
I'd die with it
I have nowhere to emigrate

Once it said this aloud
in the alphabet
and the letter Ě answered:
me too
and Ů with its little ring:
me too

And since then they've stuck together
Ř Ě Ů
a small society of the untransferable
and every Czech child
learning to say řeka, river,
is to them
a lengthening of life

That's why it hurts so much
learning Ř
something grows in the learning
more than a tongue

---

## 714. SILENCE

Silence used to be everywhere
before the invention of everything

Then came engines, radios, cities
and silence moved
from the streets to the attics
from the attics to the forests
from the forests to the mountaintops

Today it lives in small apartments:
three seconds after a symphony
before the applause begins
the moment a doctor lifts his eyes from the paper
the moment between
I have something to tell you
and what comes after

People kill it with their phones
the instant it appears
out of terror that in it
they'll hear something

But one violinist knows better:
before a concert, behind the curtain
she gives herself a minute of silence
not for the dead
for herself, alive
and she calls it
tuning the soul to concert A

Silence likes to visit her
she is one of the last addresses
where they let it
finish speaking

---

## 715. THE SCARECROW

The scarecrow stood in the field
arms spread like an eternal embrace
and had a single task:
to strike terror

Grandpa's old coat
a pot for a head
and straw in the chest
where others keep the rest

The crows feared him a week
then perched on his shoulders
and his career was over

The farmer wanted to burn him
he's not doing his job
but the granddaughter spoke up:
