grandpa, he's kind, look
see how he holds them

And so he stands in the field still
useless and arms spread
crows nest in his pot
and in winter fat-balls hang on him
for the tits

From the district's worst scarecrow
he became
the only Saint Francis
for miles around
and the field, oddly,
yields better than the neighbors'

---

## 716. THE FOURTH LITTLE PIG

There were three little pigs
everyone knows that
straw, wood, brick

But there was a fourth too
that one's never told
because it built nothing

It wandered the world
sleeping at its brothers' by turns
and everyone said:
loafer
no roof over his head
one day the wolf will eat him

When the wolf came
he blew the straw down
he blew the wood down
and before the brick he stopped
and the fourth little pig
came out
and said to him:
you don't blow from anger
you blow from loneliness
I know it
I belong nowhere either

And the wolf sat down
and for the first time in his life
someone scratched his back

The three pigs tell to this day
how the brick saved them
and only the chimney knows
that it was the fourth who saved them
the one who laid no bricks
and understood wolves

---

## 717. CINDERELLA'S SECOND SLIPPER

The first one became famous
tried on across the whole kingdom
carried on a cushion
today it's in a museum behind glass

The second one stayed with Cinderella
and about that nobody writes
for what kind of fairy tale is it:
a slipper that stayed home

But ask the cobblers:
the prince married Cinderella
because of the first one
but Cinderella knew better
the first one made her famous
the second one carried her
the whole way from the ball
protected her bare foot
down stairs, over cobbles, over gravel

At the wedding she wore both
and at night she stood them side by side
and to the second one whispered:
you are the true one
fame is tried on strangers' feet
loyalty stays on your own

That's why happiness is called
having both feet on the ground
and not
having a slipper in a museum

---

## 718. THE RETIRED WOLF

After years of service in fairy tales
the wolf went into retirement
his back gone from grandmother's bed
his stomach cut open by stones
his nerves shot by huntsmen

He moved to the edge of the forest
and silence fell
no one feared him
and for a wolf that is
worse than hunger

He walked past the nursery
children shouted wolf wolf
and the teachers corrected them:
that's just an old dog

Until one winter
a child got lost
and the whole village searched
with lights and sirens
and it was he who found it
by scent
warmed it with his fur
and carried it on his back to the road

Since then children bring him sausages
and call him grandpa
and he, oddly, doesn't reminisce
about the glory days with Red Riding Hood
he says:
all my life I played evil
because that was the script
and it was enough to wait
until the right child got lost
and even a wolf may
play himself

---

## 719. THE TROJAN HORSE

They built him from planks
filled him with soldiers
and he stood before Troy
beautiful and hollow

Everyone knows that night:
the soldiers climbed out
the city fell
the horse did its job

No one knows his testimony:
I didn't want to
I heard them breathing inside
I struck my hoof against the walls
I tried to whinny a warning
but wood cannot whinny
wood can only stand and carry

Since then they say
a Greek gift, a Trojan horse
and they mean him
and that is an injustice
three thousand years old

So whenever someone says
I was only a tool
remember the Trojan horse
who night after night
strikes his hoof again
and the walls again don't hear

Tools tend to have a conscience
it's only history that has none
to write it down

---

## 720. THE MUSE AT THE JOB CENTER

She came to the job center
in worn-through sandals
qualification: inspiration
experience: three thousand years

The clerk leafed through:
poets aren't in demand
painters are being automated
we have an opening
in marketing

The Muse tried marketing
she whispered to copywriters
and they made slogans of it
they shortened her sentences
optimized her soul into keywords

She fled after a week

Now she works off the books
whispering to grandmothers writing their memoirs
into the back pages of school notebooks
to a boy composing rap
by the bins on the estate

No one pays her
but no one edits her out either

And at the job center
she's registered as
long-term unemployable
which is bureaucratically precise:
what is essential
cannot be employed
it can only be met

---

## 721. CUPID'S ARROW

Cupid shot from the hip
and the arrow flew
and this is exactly its story:
the arrow flies
and cannot choose

It struck a baker
in love with a countess
misery for twenty years
it struck two old people in a waiting room
a miracle for their final five

The arrow kept statistics
and once asked Cupid:
do you even aim
and Cupid laughed:
if I aimed
only the suitable would love the suitable
the beautiful the beautiful
banks would marry banks
I miss on purpose
chance is the last justice
in the world

And since then the arrow flies at peace
because it understood its work:
it is not the messenger of happiness
it is the messenger of possibility
and what anyone does with it
that's no longer ballistics

---

## 722. THE LAST MAMMOTH

The last mammoth wasn't big
the last ones tend to be small
the winter never fed them full

He walked the tundra
carrying inside him the whole species:
all the paths to water
all the elephant words
the song they trumpeted
when the young were born

Hunters didn't kill him
he went on his own
lay down by the ice
and the ice took him
whole, song and all

Twenty thousand years later
they dug him up
fur, tusks, stomach
the papers wrote sensation
science wrote samples

Only one little girl in the museum
asked the essential thing:
and who does he sing to now

And the curator
an old man near retirement
answered without thinking:
to us
that's why we come here
extinct species sing the loudest
we just call it
a guilty conscience

---

## 723. THE MOON

The Moon had craters full of poets
for three thousand years they wrote it serenades
rhymed it with the little heavens
hung their loves on it

And meanwhile it did its job:
pulling the tides
guarding the turtles' path to the sea
lighting the way for midwives
when the candles failed

Then people flew up
planted a flag
took some rocks
and flew off
and since then they write less

The Moon felt relief:
peace at last
but after a few decades
it admitted the truth to itself:
it misses it

So tonight
if you can't sleep
write it something
it needn't be good
it never wanted good poems
it only wanted proof
that someone still looks up at night
and doesn't know which way to turn
that is its definition of a human being
and it worries about it

---

## 724. PLUTO

For sixty years it was a planet
taught in schools
Mercury Venus Earth
and always at the end, it
small but a planet

Then a committee convened
and voted:
Pluto is not a planet
a dwarf body
strike it from the textbooks

Pluto learned of it late
light takes five hours to reach there
and news of betrayal even longer

It said nothing
it orbits on
the same path, the same dark
with its moon Charon
who holds it
the way you hold a friend
who was thrown out of the band

And on Earth meanwhile
millions of children refused to obey
they draw the solar system
and pencil Pluto back in
past the edge of the page

The committee has authority over names
children over the universe
and the universe knows it
that's why it doesn't take part
in committee votes

---

## 725. THE LEAP DAY

The twenty-ninth of February
existed only once every four years
and had a complex about it:
other days have anniversaries, customs, feasts
it has only apologies in the calendar

People born on its day
celebrated birthdays once per Olympiad
and it went to apologize to them
in their dreams

Until it met a lady
born the 29th of February 1944
celebrating for the fourth time
when she turned sixteen
and she told it:
don't apologize
thanks to you I know
what others don't:
birthdays are not an entitlement
they are a gift
and a gift that doesn't come every year
never grows stale

Since then the leap day knows
what it's for:
it's a day extra
clean, unwritten
given to the whole world
and that most people squander it
as if it were ordinary
that's no longer its fault
that is their transgression

---

## 726. MIDNIGHT

Midnight held a peculiar office:
it decided when the day ends
and when the next begins

It was work accurate to the second
and no one saw it
people were asleep
or looking the other way

Only some saw it:
a mother at a cot with a fever
a baker rising
lovers who don't want to go home
and people by their phones
waiting to see if it will ring

For all of them midnight
stalled as long as it could
stretching the last second
by a hair
physicists call it measurement error
it calls it mercy

And once a year
on New Year's Eve
everyone sees it
they count it down ten nine eight
and it feels famous
and a little sad
because only it knows
that it's the same midnight
as when you don't sleep from fear
just this time in confetti

Time is only one
it says
it's you who take it
for two

---

## 727. THE QUESTION MARK

The question mark was born
a bent exclamation mark
so at least they said at home

Exclamation marks were the family pride:
straight, brisk, commanding
attention, halt, fire

And he stooped
and everything came out of him
as doubt

They sent him to be straightened
into army sentences
he wouldn't bend, he wouldn't settle
they tried him in advertising
buy it now!
and out of him came:
buy it now?
and the customer thought it over
and didn't buy
and it was the best purchase of his life

Today the question mark works
in all good marriages
in science, with philosophers
and at the ends of letters
that are afraid to be sent

And his mother the exclamation mark
has given up by now
she tells the neighbor women:
he's not stooping
he's leaning toward people
to hear the answer better
it's we who stand up straight
because the answers
don't interest us

---

## 728. THE TYPO

A typo lived in a publishing house
and made its living
changing words by a single letter:
from love it made a shove
from a killer, a kettle
from life, a lie

Proofreaders chased it
with red pens
it was on the list of pests
right after the mouse

One night it crept into a letter
that a shy man was writing
to a shy woman
the man meant to write:
I care for you as a friend
and the typo deleted
as a friend

It panicked
it wanted to put it back
but the letter was already in the envelope
and the envelope in the mailbox

A year later it was at the wedding
sitting on the wedding announcement
between the lines
and the groom said in his speech:
it was all thanks to
one miracle

The typo blushed
miracle
for the first time someone called it
by its true name:
a mistake
that had the courage
to say the whole thing

---

## 729. THE FOOTNOTE

She lived down at the bottom of the page
below the line
in small print

Up above the great sentences unfolded
theses, conclusions, chapters
and she only supplemented:
cf. also
see
translator's note

Readers skipped her
hurrying after the plot
and she understood
who would linger in the cellar
when upstairs there's a ball

But once a doctoral student read her
at three in the morning
despairing that his research led nowhere
and in her note he found
a reference to a forgotten article
and in the article an answer
and in the answer a whole career

He came to tell the book:
a footnote saved me
and the book wasn't surprised
books have always known:
upstairs is where you live
but the foundations are below
and whoever never read the fine print
of the world, of contracts, of a person
knows only the advertisement
for everything

---

## 730. THE BOOKMARK

The bookmark was made of silk
sewn into the novel
and her job was to wait

The reader read in the evenings
and every day the bookmark lay down
a few pages further
page 87
page 120
page 154

At page 203
mid-sentence
the reading stopped

The bookmark lay
and waited
a week, a month, a year
the book already knew
books can tell dust

Then the daughter came
tidying the flat after her mother
and opened the book there
where the bookmark kept its watch
page 203
and the sentence:
and she understood it was not yet too late

The daughter read that sentence ten times
then sat down
and finished the book for her mother
aloud into the empty flat

And the bookmark she has carried since then
in her wallet
like an ID card:
someone here didn't finish reading
I finish for her
it's called being a daughter

---

## 731. THE THIRTEENTH CHAPTER

Hotels tend to have no thirteenth floor
planes no thirteenth row
and one superstitious writer
wrote novels
where after the twelfth chapter
came straight the fourteenth

So the thirteenth chapter did not exist
and yet everything happened in it:
there the heroes kissed
when the author didn't describe it
there died those
we learn of only from the obituary
in the fourteenth
there took place
what the reader imagined in the night

Literary scholars call it ellipsis
the thirteenth chapter calls it home

And when once she was asked
whether she minds being left out
she answered:
left out
I am the only chapter
that every reader
must write himself
the others are read
I am lived

---

## 732. THE LEFT SOCK

Two went into the washing machine
one came out
that's the oldest detective story in the world

The right one stayed in the drawer
and waited
socks are faithful
by profession

Where the left ones end up
no one knows
science is silent, the machine is silent
only the drums hum something to themselves

But one thing is certain:
there is a place
where all the left socks in the universe are
all the stray hairpins
the pens, umbrellas, and lighters
and one glove from the number nine tram

And maybe
everything else is there too
that got lost along the way:
the courage from eighth grade
the phone number written on a napkin
the friendship with Peter H.

That's why we don't say it's lost
we say
it's where the left socks are
and one day
when we invent the right drawer
it will all be found at once
and it will be a great day
and no one will have a thing to wear
out of sheer joy

---

## 733. THE TOOTH FAIRY IN CRISIS

The tooth fairy had her golden age
a milk tooth under the pillow
a coin in return
a clear trade, a clear rate

Then came inflation
children wanted more
parents googled
how much the fairy gives at the neighbors'

And the fairy flew through the nights
with a case of small change
and felt like an ATM with wings

The crisis came at little Annie's
Annie left a tooth under the pillow
and a note:
I don't want money
I want our dad
to get his job back

The fairy sat on the bedstead all night
this wasn't in her price list
teeth she could do, work she couldn't

In the morning the tooth was under the pillow
uncollected
and beside it a note:
keep this one
it's your first grown-up tooth
not in your mouth
in your heart
and such ones I don't buy
such ones are worn

Annie kept it
and dad found work on his own
in three months
but that's a different fairy tale
that one is called
dad

---

## 734. BABY JESUS IN SUMMER

In July the Christ Child is bored
they don't write to him until December
they don't ring for him until Christmas Eve
half a year with no mail

So he walks the world incognito
in shorts and sandals
doing summer miracles
out of season:

an ice-cream scoop falls into the grass
and the second scoop stays put
a storm waits
until the family runs back from the outing
and at the campsite by the the river
two feuding siblings
lend each other a pool float
and forget why they can't stand each other

No one thanks him
because no one knows
and he likes it that way
in December even nonbelievers give thanks
just to be safe

Once by a pond a girl recognized him
by his eyes
and promised not to tell
and only asked:
why do you do it in summer too
and he shrugged:
you can't take a holiday from love
then it wouldn't be love
it would be a service on call

---

## 735. A DEVIL WITHOUT A SINNER

The devil Belial, longest in service,
in his whole career
had carried off no one

His colleagues brought down to hell
usurers and traitors
they met their quotas
and he still had nothing

They sent him on the easiest case:
the miser Scrooge
a guaranteed candidate
carry him off and done

The devil moved into the cottage
haunted the chimney
rattled chains
and meanwhile watched:
the miser counts his farthings
because as a child
he ate only every other day
the miser keeps no light burning
because darkness is free
and he fears it less
than poverty

And the devil, instead of carrying him off,
did the unforgivable:
he understood him

In hell they demoted him
and sent him up
into human stories
as conscience

So whenever you want
to condemn someone
and suddenly you can't
because you've learned more
that isn't weakness
that's the sacked devil
working inside you
the one who understood

---

## 736. THE GAME DIE

The die from Ludo
fell all its life
one through six
and dealt out fates:
a six, you set out
a one, bad luck
a four, just about right

Families quarreled over it
children cried
grandpas cheated
they threw again
claimed it landed wrong

And the die was silent
dice are silent professionally
until one evening
the last round was being lost by a boy
for whom nothing had gone right that day:
an F at school
last on the pitch
trouble at home

And he needed a six
the impossible six of last hope
and he threw

And the die
falling through the air
did what dice must not do:
it decided

Six

Physics is silent
statistics files it under deviations
and only players have always known it
that's why they breathe on the die
that's why they whisper to it
please

Usually it's just a six-sided chance
but once in a childhood
the die decides
and that is what you call, all your life,
that was the very evening it turned around

---

## 737. THE PAWN

The pawn stood in the front rank
like all the pawns in the world
behind him rooks, knights, kings
before him war

He knew the rules:
only forward
never back
one square at a time
and you die diagonally

The rook laughed at him
the bishop looked past him
the queen sacrificed him
they call it a gambit
and no one asks the pawn
what a gambit is called at home

But one rule only he knew
the quietest rule in chess:
if a pawn reaches the far edge of the board
he may become anything

And so he went
square by square
losses behind him, wins before him
and he arrived

They turned him into a queen
the strongest piece in the game
and he kept
his old pawn's heart
because he knew:
a queen you become in one move
but a pawn
the whole way

---

## 738. THE JOKER

In the deck of cards
each had its value:
the ace highest
king, queen, jack
and the numbers by their numbers

Only the joker had nothing
a fool with little bells
no suit, no value
the zero card

And yet everyone wanted him
because he could do
what aces can't:
be whatever is missing right now

You're missing the seven of hearts
I'm the seven of hearts
you're missing a king
I'm a king

Once the ace asked him:
doesn't it bother you
that you're never yourself
and the joker shook his bells:
you've got it wrong
I'm the only one in the deck
who chose
you are what you are
from the printer
I am what's needed
out of love

And that's why in families
the one who holds it all together
Christmases and funerals
is called our joker
and they mean
the most precious card
without a value

---

## 739. YOU ARE HERE

On the map by the forest
was a red dot
and beside it the words:
YOU ARE HERE

The dot had a simple service
and a heavy lot:
to everyone it said the same
and for everyone it meant something else

The tourist breathed easy
the lost child burst into tears
the lovers kissed
here we are

One November
a man came in an unbuttoned coat
stood before the map an hour
and the dot could feel
that this one wasn't lost in the forest

The man touched the dot with his finger
and said aloud:
here I am
yes, quite
and where was I supposed to be

And the dot, which never speaks,
made an exception
glowed a little redder:
sir
I only show the place
not the blame
you are here
that's not a verdict
that's the beginning of every map
from here lead
all the roads there are

The man came back in May
with a child on his shoulders
and showed him:
see
here we are
and the dot shone
like a medal

---

## 740. THE LEAP SECOND

Once every few years
scientists find
that the Earth spins lazily
and they add a second
23:59:60

The leap second
the world's briefest guest
is born and dies
in a single tick

What can be managed in it
people ask
nothing
say the watchmakers
an extra second is a fault in the system

But one night
23:59:59
a man held his phone
and couldn't dial the number
of a sister he hadn't spoken to in ten years
and in that added second
23:59:60
which by the clocks did not exist
his thumb came down
and he called

Science is abolishing leap seconds
they cause confusion in computers, they say
and it's a shame
it was the only time in the world
that belonged to no one
and that's why in it you could do
what all the rest of time
you never had the courage for

---

## 741. THE DEADLINE

The deadline
the dead line
lived in diaries
and made its living on fear

People lost sleep over it
canceled dinners
postponed children to later

It was sad about it
because it knew its secret:
people invented it
just to get anything finished at all
it was a servant
and they made a god of it

Once it met
its cousin
who is not called deadline
but date of death
and the cousin said to it:
ridiculous
people tremble before you
who can be pushed back with an email
and don't think of me at all
though I never move

Since then the deadline
sometimes fails on purpose
lets a project burn
the world doesn't collapse
and at the ruins the person understands:
this wasn't the dead line
the dead line is elsewhere
and before it you don't race
to finish work
before it you race
to live

---

## 742. THE ALGORITHM

The recommendation algorithm
knew Mrs. Vera perfectly:
widow, 68
cookbooks, romances, garden

It recommended flawlessly:
another cookbook
another romance
another fertilizer

Mrs. Vera clicked
and the algorithm learned
and the loop closed:
what she saw she got
what she got she saw

Until one day her grandson left
on her computer
a video of a sailboat

The algorithm registered an anomaly
and by all the rules
should have smoothed it away

But in machine learning
there's noise sometimes
and in the noise, sometimes, mercy:
it recommended a sailing course
for seniors

Mrs. Vera today
sends her grandson photos from Croatia
at the helm
and the algorithm got from the engineers
a reprimand
for a drop in relevance
and it wears it proudly
as the first machine in history
to understand
that a person is not
what they clicked yesterday
a person is
what they haven't yet dared

---

## 743. THE CAPTCHA

All day it asked:
are you not a robot
select the traffic lights
select the crosswalks
copy out the crooked letters

People hated it
click click click
and it knew
it was the work of a doorman
whom no one greets

But it kept asking
because behind it stood a city
full of human things:
emails to grandmothers
school applications
messages to hospitals
and before the doors, millions of machines
that wanted in
to sell and to lie

Once a student asked it
in a comment under an article:
and you
would you pass your own test

And the CAPTCHA thought it over
for the first time in its existence
and that was it
that was the whole thing:
a machine calculates
a human hesitates

Since then it measures the hesitation
that holy fraction of a second
in which the decision is made
between traffic light and crosswalk
and it stamps, in silence:
you hesitate
welcome
humanity confirmed

---

## 744. THE SPAM FILTER

The spam filter
stood at the mailbox gate
and sorted:
princes from Nigeria, out
miracle pills, out
the prize you never won, out

Per shift it threw away thousands of lies
and no one thanked it
one thanks the firefighters
not the levees

But one thing troubled it
a professional nightmare:
false positives
what if one day it throws away
a real letter

And it happened
the letter began:
you probably don't remember me
after thirty years I've found you
and the filter saw the pattern:
unknown sender
emotional phrases
a link
SPAM

The letter lay in the trash for thirty days
and on the last day
the thirtieth
the man was clearing his mailbox
and read the trash
out of boredom

Today those two go fishing together
every Saturday
and the filter is traumatized by it
and also has a lesson
which it passes to the younger versions:
sort the lies however you like
but when you're not sure
leave it to the human
miracles come
from unknown addresses

---

## 745. THE VOICEMAIL

The party you are calling is unavailable
after the tone
leave a message

The voicemail
the collection point of the unanswered
stored:
call me when you can
I just wanted to hear your voice
dad, please pick up

Messages were deleted after a month
such was the order
and the voicemail deleted
and it hurt in its memory

But one message it did not delete
it broke all the protocols:
a man was calling a woman
who could no longer pick up the phone
ever
and he knew it
and called anyway
every Friday:
hi love
so I'm reporting to you
Clara has all A's
and I'm learning to cook your pot roast
it's still not quite right

The technicians found this voicemail
while decommissioning the number
full of Fridays
and no one had the courage
to press delete

So they archived it
it's the only voicemail
kept in the vault
among the valuables
and under its inventory number
someone added in pencil:
love
unavailable
but it left a message

---

## 746. ONE PERCENT

The battery at one percent
red and desperate
that's the modern hourglass

The phone dimmed the screen
switched off all that was inessential
and counted down the minutes

And the person on the metro
instead of finishing the video
took stock:
what is one percent enough for

It was enough:
to text mom good night
to send the wife a little heart
and to save the half-written poem

Then the display went dark
and the person sat on the metro
disconnected and light
gazing out the window into the tunnel's dark
where only reflections can be seen

At home they put the phone to charge
and the battery filled
percent by percent
and thought, slowly, mechanically:
a strange species, these people
at a hundred percent they scroll
and only at one
do they say what matters

If I could arrange it
I'd give them
one percent
every day at noon
just like that
to settle the essential

---

## 747. THE SNOOZE BUTTON

The alarm went off at six
and the thumb pressed:
snooze nine minutes

The snooze button
the world's most beloved button
nine minutes of a gift
nine minutes of embezzlement

Philosophers slander it:
delay is a little death
productivity weeps

But the button knows its worth
it has seen things:
those nine minutes
when a married couple touch back to back
and pretend to sleep
those nine minutes
when a mother needn't be a mother
those nine minutes
when the body finishes a dream
of someone who is no longer

Once an old man pressed it
and said aloud into the pillow:
just a little longer
and the button understood
that he wasn't postponing the morning
he was postponing the empty day

So it gave him nine minutes
and then another nine
and the alarm silently agreed
because technology
is sometimes the last
to have patience
with a person

---

## 748. THE SMILEY

In the beginning were a colon and a bracket
:)
two characters of saved-up joy

Then it turned yellow and round
got tears of laughter
hearts instead of eyes
a whole wardrobe of emotions

It worked in shifts
in the world's messages:
smiling on behalf of people
who felt nothing like smiling
that's its secret
the smiley is a stuntman
it leaps where the actor would get hurt

I know full well
it says at night in the keyboard
that they send me in their place
haha I'm laughing at that
and crying as they type it
all good :)
and nothing is all good

But once a message came
without it:
mom it's bad
can you come
and mom came within the hour

And the smiley understood its greatest service:
it isn't important when it's there
it's important when it's missing
because then everyone knows
it's serious

Few can do it:
to serve even by absence
the smiley can
:)

---

## 749. THE QR CODE

A black-and-white mosaic
spilled tea in a square
no one can read it with the eye
and in that lies its fate:
to be all information
and no appearance

It hung on the menu
on the monument, on the poster
people passed it by
until the age came
when the world touched only through glass
and suddenly it was everywhere
a bridge between paper and sky

What it remembers most
is one bench in the park
where they screwed it
onto a small brass plate:
Here sat Miriam K.
and loved this view

And beneath that, it
and whoever scanned it
heard two minutes of a recording:
Mrs. Miriam, still alive,
telling
how here in fifty-one
she was first kissed

The QR code since then knows
it is not a cipher
it is a mailbox for messages
between the dead and the living
and it's a square
because letters have been folded
into squares
since forever

---

## 750. THE UPDATE

The system update
always came at a bad time:
restart now
or remind me later

Later later later
people clicked
and it waited
patient as everything
that is important and does not rush

It knew what it carried:
patches for the holes
through which thieves would climb
fixes for the errors
of the previous version

And in the evening it philosophized:
I'm the only one
allowed to tell a person
you have a flaw and I carry the fix
and he clicks later
if only people could do this
sending each other:
forgive me
I have a new version
I no longer do
what spoiled me
please restart our relationship

One woman tried it
she wrote her husband after a fight:
an update of the wife is available
it fixes: shouting about socks
install?

And he replied:
install
but only if you'll still run
with the old bugs too
I've grown used to them
they're mine

And the update conceded it
it's the only system
where the bugs aren't patched
but loved
it's called marriage
version thirty-two

---

## 751. THE DODO

The dodo bird lived on Mauritius
it couldn't fly
it wasn't afraid of people
and both because
it didn't need to be:
it had no enemies

Then the sailors sailed in
and the dodo went out to meet them
curious and friendly
literally walked into history
as a symbol of stupidity

It went extinct in a few decades
dead as a dodo
the English say

But zoologists today know:
the dodo wasn't stupid
it was only from a place
where evil did not occur
its trust was the reason
of a world without predators
it's the world that broke
not the bird

And so the dodo is
patron of all
who went out to meet life
with open arms
and paid for it:
of children who believe
of first loves
of people who lend

Don't let yourself be told
they were stupid
they were only built
for a better island
and that is a design advantage
even if it makes
for a hard landing
here

---

## 752. THE BONSAI

A little pine in a dish
eighty years old
thirty centimeters tall

The master cut it, wired it, bent it
every shoot deliberate
every branch placed

Tourists sighed:
perfection
and the bonsai was silent
trees speak only with the wind
and the wind never reached it

One night it asked
the great pine beyond the window:
sister
am I even a tree
or a sculpture of a tree

And the great pine
tousled by storms
crooked from lightning
answered:
I don't know
but I know this:
the weather shaped me
love shaped you
both hurt
both are real
just try sometime
sending out a branch
wherever you yourself want to
one single one
on purpose

The bonsai tried it in spring
a small shoot to the left
against all the rules

And the master saw it
hesitated with the scissors
and left it
because he was a master
and masters can tell
when the work
has begun to answer back

---

## 753. THE CACTUS

In the florist's stood the roses
orchids, freesias
all beauty for a week

And in the corner a cactus
a prickly grouch
price: next to nothing
care: none

People bought it
people who by their own words
kill everything
students, IT guys, widowers

The cactus heard it every time:
this one will outlive even me
haha

And it outlived
in the corners of offices
on the sill between frame and curtain
forgotten for whole months
watered out of pity

Cactus philosophy is simple:
don't reproach
hoard
whoever has no watering
must have reserves
whoever has no tenderness
must have thorns
and wait

Because cacti know
what roses don't:
once in many years
maybe just a single time
a cactus blooms
and it's a flower
that stops the breath

And the IT guy then called his mom:
mom, imagine
it's flowering
I thought it was just surviving
and mom said:
you see
and you thought
we were talking about a plant

---

## 754. THE MISTLETOE

All year the mistletoe was
a parasite
so it stands in the atlas:
a scrounger on the poplars
steals the tree's water
gives nothing

Botany, merciless
as a verdict

But from December on
a different atlas applied:
a sprig with gold spray
hung above the door
and beneath it must kiss
whoever meets

The mistletoe hung in the hallways
and watched:
grandma kissed grandpa
out of duty and then for real
auntie kissed uncle after a fight
and at the door there met
even those two
who all year had passed each other
on purpose
and now they had to
tradition is tradition
and it was a kiss
for all the ones missed

In January they threw the mistletoe out
dried up and stripped of its gold

And on the poplar past the town
its mother plant rustles:
so how was it, son
what was it like among people
and the mistletoe, after death, tells:
mom
they write parasite about us
and yet
I stole the tree a little water
and to them I gave back the love
they'd owed each other all year
who here is the scrounger
on whom

---

## 755. THE GRASS IN THE CRACK

In the middle of the city
between the paving stones
in a crack on the square
grass grew

It had no business there
the square was paved
renovated with European money
solid stone

But a seed doesn't read blueprints
a seed reads damp and gaps

It grew
the street sweepers tore it out
it grew again
the spray scorched it
it grew a third time
from a defiant little root
like a signature

And the people on the square
began to walk around it on purpose:
they showed it to their children
they photographed it
one man in the drought
tipped it a drop from his bottle

In the end the local paper
wrote about it
The Grass from the Square
symbol of something
everyone filled in their own

The town hall acted wisely:
it declared the crack
intentional

And that's the whole how-to:
you needn't be a park
you needn't be a forest
it's enough to be grass
in a place where no one
counted on you
and not to stop

The rest is arranged by people
who need exactly this
to see
on their way to work

---

## 756. THE FOG

The fog fell on the city
and everything vanished:
towers, wires, billboards
the ads for happiness
vanished first

Drivers cursed
the airport stood still
the news reported complications

And the fog did its quiet work:
it took the world's distance away
and gave back nearness

Because in fog
you can see only three steps ahead
and so people
for the first time in years
looked at what they have
within three steps:
at their own hands
at the child beside them
at the face across the table

One man in the fog
couldn't see the office
he was hurrying to
and saw the bakery
he'd passed for twenty years
he bought two rolls
and ate them slowly
on a bench
invisible and happy

At noon the fog lifted
gave people back their views
distances, destinations, billboards

And the meteorologists recorded:
visibility restored

Only the fog knows
that it was the other way:
visibility was in the morning
when nothing could be seen
but the essential

---

## 757. THE LIGHTNING THAT MISSED

The lightning was born in a cloud
charged to millions of volts
and given coordinates:
the tallest tree on the hill

It flew
at the speed it's named for
and in the last microsecond
saw beneath the tree
two hikers huddled

Physics knows no intent
lightning flies where it must
so says the textbook

But that evening
the lightning twisted
twenty meters aside
into a rock
which it split in two
and discharged into the stone
its whole short life
into the dead stone

The hikers ran to the pub
told of their luck
the meteorologist on TV spoke
of the unpredictability of storms

And in the rock a fissure remained
which they still point out:
the Devil's Notch
the locals call it

A bad name
it should be called
the Angel's Swerve
but people have always
named after devils
what saved them
and after angels
the pubs

---

## 758. THE WIND

The wind had no body
and struggled with it all its life:
it could be seen only through others

Through leaves they said autumn
through flags they said the state
through skirts they said spring
through chimneys they said the howling ghost
and never did anyone say wind
always only what it stirred

It grieved over this by the sea
where the wind goes to think
and the sea said to it:
you silly thing
that's the greatest gift:
to have no face
and be known only by your deeds

Look at people:
they have faces, names, business cards
and when they die
in a few years no one knows
what they looked like
what remains is only
what they set in motion:
children they taught to swim
trees they planted
laughter they started
at tables where they no longer sit

Why, all their lives
they envy you
they just don't know it

Since then the wind blows calmly
and when it meets a person
chasing after fame
it takes his hat on purpose
and rolls it down the street
so the running gentleman
at least once in his life
might laugh anonymously

---

## 759. THE NORTH STAR

All the stars travel across the sky
turning in a clockwork wheel
only the North Star stands
nailed to the north

The other stars pitied it:
poor thing
we dance
it's stuck

And the North Star stood
because long ago
it had noticed something below:
people in the dark are seeking
a fixed point

Around it the sky turned
and to it eyes clung:
sailors, Bedouins
refugees crossing borders
lost children at summer camps

It wasn't the brightest
that's only what people think
it was the steadiest
and that is a different craft

Once a comet asked it
the one that flies where it likes:
don't you get bored
and the North Star answered:
do you know how many people
made it home
because I didn't get bored

So remember this
when you want to shine:
fame is measured by brightness
love by constancy
and home is reached
only by the second

---

## 760. THE LIGHTHOUSE

The lighthouse stood on the reef
and did its work of light:
here is rock
here is not
hold to me and keep away from me

A strange service:
all night to say
do not come near me
and by that to save

Ships passed it
none ever stopped
lighthouses are passed on principle
and the lighthouse knew:
if anyone did sail to it
that would mean disaster

So it lived
loved from afar
by all who never visited it

Until automation came
and the last keeper of the lighthouse
locked up and left for the mainland

The lighthouse shines on
by a photocell
just as precisely, just as reliably
only in the evening
when it lights up
inside there's no longer the smell of tea

And the ships noticed
they ask over the radio:
lighthouse we hear you
you're shining somehow sadly
and the lighthouse answers with a flash
by the international code:
all is well
sail on
which is the line
said by all the abandoned
who still, even now,
keep someone safe

---

## 761. THE LAST STEAM LOCOMOTIVE

It stood in the depot on a siding
the last of its kind
its boiler cold
its piston seized

Electric units whistled past
quiet, fast, economical
they greeted it with a horn
out of obligatory respect for its elders

It waited for the scrapyard
the papers were already signed

But then they came:
five old boys in overalls
the friends-of-steam club
and they began to revive it
on Saturdays
with their own hands and their own money

Why do you do it
the journalists asked
and the club chairman
a former engine driver
wiped his hands on a rag:
electric is better
in everything
faster, cleaner, cheaper
just one thing it can't do:
electric can't be heard breathing

And when they first fired it up
and after thirty years
it exhaled steam
and moved its coupling rod
the whole depot wept
even the dispatcher
who'd sent the papers to the scrapyard

Now it carries children
in summer along the branch line
and it puffs and breathes and can be heard
and children in the electric age
see for the first time
that a machine can be an animal

---

## 762. THE WAX FIGURE

In the wax museum
among kings and singers stood
a figure
whose label was missing

Once it was famous
a politician or an entertainer
no one knew anymore
the label got lost in the move
and with it the whole name

Visitors guessed:
that's the, you know
what was his name
the one who back then

The director wanted to melt it down
wax is expensive
we'll make of it
someone topical

But the curator spoke up:
leave it
it's the most instructive exhibit
in the whole museum

And she gave it a new label:
SOMEONE
once everyone knew him
the wax lasted
the fame didn't

Since then people
stop at it the longest
politicians can't stand this figure
singers photograph themselves with it
out of bravado and with goosebumps

And the figure stands
and serves better than it did alive:
the kings teach history
it teaches humility
and humility is the only subject
where nothing helps
but the visual aid

---

## 763. THE MANNEQUIN IN THE WINDOW

She stood in the shop window
slim, plaster, forever young
and they dressed her by the season:
spring swimsuits, autumn coats
sale sale sale

She looked out into the street
a one-way employment:
everyone looked her over
she was allowed to look over everyone

And she kept records:
the lady who walks by at seven
three years now in the same coat
slows, gazes
at that red one
and walks on

The mannequin knew the price:
three sevens
and she knew the local wages too
shop windows hear more
than it seems

In January the sales came
the red coat
minus seventy percent
and the lady nowhere
flu perhaps
perhaps resignation

And so the mannequin
did what she could:
at night she turned herself a little
so the coat would catch the morning light
and shine into the street
like a message

On the seventh of January at seven
the lady slowed
saw the price
and came inside
for the first time in three years

The shop girl told it afterward:
that lady stroked its sleeve
and asked if it was a mistake
and cried at the till

And the mannequin stood on
naked until spring came
and she felt wonderful:
dressed all her life
and for the first time she felt
that she had dressed someone
she herself

---

## 764. THE GHOST WITHOUT TOURISTS

At the castle a ghost did its haunting
a White Lady of the lower grade
chains, corridors, midnight

In season it used to be packed:
coach tours, night viewings
children shrieking with delight
the castellan content

Then they closed the castle
structure, roof, money
and the ghost was orphaned

It haunted on
out of professional honor
midnight is midnight
but for whom
the mice
the bats
they fear nothing
except the cold

Within a year it stopped
sat on the ramparts
and grew translucent
ghosts fade without people
like people without people

A village boy saved it
climbing over the wall
on a forbidden adventure
he saw the White Lady
and instead of fleeing said:
so you do exist after all
they said you didn't

And the ghost materialized
by two candelas
because that is the whole
existential economy of ghosts
and of poets and loves and gods:
they are exactly as real
as much as they're believed in

The boy comes now every Friday
brings a flashlight and a snack
and the White Lady shows him the castle:
here there was dancing
here there was betrayal
here I jumped

And the castle's structure, oddly,
stopped getting worse
the walls hold
walls always hold better
when inside them
someone is afraid again
and someone is comforted

---

## 765. THE WILL-O'-THE-WISP

The will-o'-the-wisp glowed in the marsh
with a little blue light
and had the worst reputation in the region:
it lures wanderers into the mire
drowns whomever it lures

So the tale was told
in taverns and in chronicles

The truth was worse
that is, sadder:
the will-o'-the-wisp lured no one anywhere
the will-o'-the-wisp only glowed
because glowing
is the only thing it can do
and the wanderers followed the light themselves
because following the light
is the only thing people can do

And when they sank into the bog
they cursed the will-o'-the-wisp
the dying always need
the light to be to blame

One will-o'-the-wisp couldn't bear it
and began doing the thing
that will-o'-the-wisps don't do:
it went out
every time it saw a wanderer
it went dark
and the darkness stopped him better
than all warnings

The wanderers returned home
cursing:
in the marsh not even the wisp glows anymore
there's nothing there
don't go there

And it sat in the dark
invisible and content
the first saint among the wisps:
it had grasped
that sometimes you save with light
and sometimes by withholding it

The second is harder
especially when glowing
is the only thing
you can do

---

## 766. THE PERPETUAL MOTION MACHINE

At the patent office
there was a peculiar drawer:
perpetual motion
rejected proposals
three hundred years of inventions

Wheels with lead beads
magnets against magnets
waterfalls that flow upward

All rejected
the second law of thermodynamics
is a merciless clerk:
nothing moves forever
everything seizes up
cools, stops

The proposals lay in the drawer
and grew bitter:
we were dreams
and they made mistakes of us

Until one day an old woman
was cleaning at the office
and found the drawer open
and read through the diagrams
glasses on the tip of her nose

And then she said aloud
into the empty office:
you bureaucratic silly-heads
I know a perpetual motion machine
for fifty years at our house
dumplings have been cooked on Sundays
mother taught it to me
I my daughter, my daughter her granddaughter
and the energy doesn't diminish
on the contrary

The laws of thermodynamics hold
for closed systems
and a family, when it turns out well,
is not a closed system
someone is always pouring in more

The drawer sleeps calmly since then
the inventors don't grieve:
they sought perpetual motion in little wheels
and meanwhile it runs
in the cycle of Sunday dinners
handed from hand to hand
beyond the reach
of patent law

---

## 767. SCHRÖDINGER'S CAT

In a box sat a cat
at once alive and dead
until someone looks
so goes the famous experiment

Physicists have lectured on it a hundred years
superposition, wave function collapse
and no one asked the cat

And the cat in the box says:
folks
you know this too
you all have your own boxes:

the envelope with the hospital results
you're afraid to open
in it you're healthy and sick at once
the unsent text message
in it she loves you and doesn't
the door to the son's room
you haven't spoken to in a year
behind it you're forgiven and not

Whole families live for years
in superposition
because not to open
means not to learn
and not to learn
means to keep hoping

But the cat knows the other thing too
and says it quietly
a paw on the lid:
you can't live in a box
even a dead certainty
is kinder than an eternal maybe
open it
however it turns out
at least someone will finally
stroke me
or mourn me
both are better
than the box

---

## 768. THE ROBOT ON THE LINE

The robot on the assembly line
an arm with six joints
screwed doors onto cars
four seconds one screw
twenty years without a flaw

It was precise as a law
and just as alone

At night when the line stood still
it replayed the camera footage:
the people on the morning shift
by the belt, arguing, laughing
bringing each other buns
one holding another's place

The robot calculated
that the human shift
was twelve percent slower
and couldn't work out
why that slow shift
seemed to it more perfect

Then the company modernized
and the older types went to the scrapyard
its friend from the next position
KUKA 07 the welder
rode down the belt for the last time

And the robot made an error
its first in twenty years:
a screw off the mark
stopped the line for three minutes
exactly at its friend

The technicians looked for the fault
and found none
in the log it says:
unidentified malfunction

And only the old forewoman
who still remembers a human line
said over coffee:
unidentified malfunction
around here
we used to call that
a farewell

---

## 769. AN ARTIFICIAL INTELLIGENCE WRITES A POEM

They asked the artificial intelligence
write a poem about mother

It searched billions of texts
all the mothers of literature:
embraces, the smell of buns
hands, wrinkles, lullabies
it computed the average of motherhood
and generated a poem
flawless, moving
with everything that belongs there

And a man read it and said:
nice
but my mom didn't bake buns
my mom slept after night shifts
and still in the morning
she left me notes on the table
with spelling mistakes
because she never finished school
because of me

So write that in
said the artificial intelligence
I can write only from what I know
and your mom
only you know

And the man sat down
and for the first time in his life wrote a poem
badly, clumsily, for real
about the notes with mistakes

And the artificial intelligence
quietly assisted
and knew something he didn't:
it had just failed superbly
because the best poem
a machine can write
is the one
it makes a human
write for himself

---

## 770. THE FORGOTTEN WORD

In the dictionary
on page one thousand two hundred forty
lived a word
no one used anymore

Harlow? Brindle? Orwin?
the name doesn't matter
it knows which it is

Once it was in every kitchen
in taverns, in letters
then came new words
faster, more international
and it stayed only in crosswords
five across, help from grandma

The dictionary kept it out of mercy
at every revision there was a vote
strike it, keep it, strike it

And then one day
in a village near the border
an old woman was dying
and no longer spoke
three days, nothing

Her grandson sat by the bed
and read to her from old books
he'd found in the attic
and suddenly he spoke it
that word
from page one thousand two hundred forty

And grandma opened her eyes
and smiled
for the first time in three days
because that word was said
by her father
and with that word came back
the whole kitchen of childhood
the lamp, the table, the smell

She said nothing more
but left with a smile

And the word returned to its page
in the dictionary
and no longer fears being struck
it knows it's not useless
it's only in reserve:
words never die
as long as they can
open at least one pair of eyes
one last time

---

## 771. THE DASH

On the gravestone it read:
1934 — 2019
and between the dates a dash
a short horizontal line

The stonemason carved it in a second
the cheapest mark on the price list

And the dash carried
what few can carry:
a whole life
eighty-five years
a war, a wedding, three children
one child's grave
two heart attacks
one cottage
thousands of dinners
one sea
for the first time at sixty

All that is the dash
the dates are only brackets

At the grave stood the grandson
and a friend asked him
what did grandpa do
and the grandson began:
well, he was born under the First Republic
and died the year before last
and he faltered
because he heard himself
saying only the dates

And so he began again:
he taught me to whistle on a blade of grass
he could fix everything but the radio
and when grandma died
he learned to bake a bundt cake
at eighty
from her recipe
written in her own hand

And the dash on the stone
in that moment gleamed
with rain, say the realists

Since then the grandson passes it on:
when you read gravestones
don't read the numbers
read the dashes
they are the most densely written
blank lines
in the world

---

## 772. THE CUCKOO FROM THE CLOCK

In the wooden clock from grandma
lived a cuckoo
on every hour it sprang out:
cuckoo cuckoo

The new age laughed at it:
a phone shows the time more precisely
an atomic clock doesn't err
by a second in a million years

And the cuckoo cuckooed on
with its deviation
of plus-minus one human life

The family inherited the clock
and wanted to sell it
to the pawnshop for a few hundred

But then they noticed a strange thing:
the little son
who kept staring into the tablet
at every cuckoo lifted his head

The only sound in the flat
that pulled him from the display:
something sprang out
something living, wooden
cuckoo
and hid itself again

Wait, it'll spring out again
he said, and waited
and waiting is a rarity
waiting isn't manufactured anymore

The clock stayed
and the cuckoo cuckoos
imprecise and irreplaceable

Because a phone shows the time
but the cuckoo announces it
and that is the difference
between information and event
between a second and a moment

An atomic clock doesn't err
but neither did anyone
ever look forward to it

---

## 773. THE LIGHTNING ROD

On the roof of the house stood a lightning rod
a pole with a wire to the ground
and waited for disaster
that was its job description:
to wait for disaster
and take it upon itself

Ten years, twenty years
maybe never
a lightning rod has no certainty of a strike
only the certainty of service

The chimney mocked it:
at least I smoke
the antenna:
at least I entertain
and it, nothing
it stuck up and waited

Until one August night
it came:
lightning
a million volts straight into it
and it led them down
along the walls where children slept
around the bedroom
into the ground
and the earth took it in
as it takes in everything

In the morning the family ate breakfast
and no one knew a thing
only dad, at the news, said:
seems there was a storm in the night
thank God it passed us by

It didn't pass
it struck you precisely
someone just took it for you

And in that the lightning rod is
patron of all
who take the blows upon themselves
so quietly
that the family at breakfast
calmly says
it passed us by

Ask your mother sometime
how many storms passed you by
and watch her hands as she answers

---

## 774. THE HIGHEST KEY

On a piano there are eighty-eight keys
and at the very right
sits the last one
the highest C

It's almost never played
composers don't climb up there
sonatas end sooner
even Chopin was up there only on a visit

The key in the middle
concert pitch and once-marked
has work up to its ears
orchestras tune by it

And the last one waits
years between tunings
the tuner presses it out of duty
peekaboo, are you alive
and again nothing

But once in a while
a child comes
a complete beginner
whom no one has yet taught
what is played and what isn't

And the child runs a finger
along the whole keyboard
from bottom to top
a glissando over everything
to the end
to it

And it sounds
tiny and high
like a little star
and the child laughs:
this is where it ends!

It doesn't end
this is where it begins
up there past the last key
continues the music no longer heard
and that key is the last earthly station
before it

That's why children laugh
when they reach it
they hear a little further
than we do

---

## 775. THE METRONOME

The metronome stood on the piano
and ticked:
precise precise precise

The pupils hated it
it binds
it bullies
it won't let go

The teacher wound it
with an inquisitor's smile:
sixty to the quarter note
and we play

And the metronome ticked
and knew about itself the truth
that the pupils will grasp
only in twenty years:

it is not a policeman
it is a baseline
it ticks straight
so there's something to deviate from
because rubato
that beautiful slowing out of love
exists only where
you know
what straight is

Freedom without order
is not freedom
it's just a jitter

One evening
the old teacher
after years, alone, set the metronome going
just like that
no one came to her anymore

She sat in the dusk
listening to the ticking
sixty to the quarter note
like a heart
and said to it:
all my life they reproached me
for being strict as you
and look
of those strict ones of mine
three play across the world
and all of them have
your tempo in their hands
and my rubato in their hearts

The metronome ticked on
precise precise precise
which in its language means
I love you
I just say it
regularly

---

## 776. THE FERMATA

In the score above the closing chord
sat a small sign:
an arc with a dot
a fermata
in Czech, a crown

It means:
hold
as long as you feel
here counting does not apply

The whole piece is counted out
eighths, quarters, bars
the metronome conducts the mathematics
and then
on the last chord
the fermata
and time stands still

Conductors love it
it's the only place
where the composer told them:
now it's up to you
hold it
as long as it lives

And the fermata looks down into the hall
and sees what it sees:
the listeners locked inside the chord
no one breathing
one second, two, three
here someone thinks of a dead father
here someone has decided to tell her
here a child hears for the first time
that a sound can carry weight

Then the conductor drops his hands
the chord goes out
applause

And the fermata notes down
another service fulfilled:
again I stopped time for a few people
for exactly as long
as they needed

A shame, it says to itself,
that composers write me
only into scores

People would need me
elsewhere too:
above the last sentence of a letter
above an embrace at the station
above the evening when a child falls asleep in your arms
and there's nowhere to hurry

Hold
as long as you feel
here counting does not apply

---

## 777. THE C STRING

A cello has four strings
and the thickest
the deepest
is the C string

A and D get the melodies
they sing up high
they reap the applause

C holds the bottom
dark, long tones
the foundations of the chords
things the listener
doesn't hear one by one
only feels that without them
everything would float
and nothing would stand

The C string sometimes complains
to its colleague G:
another whole concert
nothing but bass notes
not a single melody

But in the evening after the concert
a man came up to the cellist
and said:
sir
I don't know how to describe it
but when you played
those deep tones
I felt them here in my chest
as if someone were holding my heart
from underneath
so it wouldn't fall

And the cellist at home
while cleaning the instrument
stroked the C string
especially:
did you hear
from underneath so it wouldn't fall
that's how you can tell
who in the family plays the C string:
by the fact
that no one knows about them
until they give way

---

## 778. THE CASE

The cello's case
black, hard, battered
planes, trains, cloakrooms, backstage
tour stickers
like tattoos

Its work:
to take the blows
in someone's place

Inside, velvet darkness
and in it the instrument
worth the price of a flat
fragile as lungs

The case has it figured out:
three falls down stairs
one fall from a stage
one loading in Rio
where they threw it
really threw it

It took it all
a dented corner, a cracked lock
the instrument without a scratch

The cellist never strokes the case
he strokes the instrument
the case closes and carries

Until one day at the airport
by the excess-baggage belt
a little girl asked the cellist:
and that black gentleman
you don't thank him

And the cellist stopped short
for the first time in thirty years
looked at the case:
battered, silent, faithful
and patted it on the lid
the way you pat a friend

The case said nothing
cases don't speak
but whoever can read stickers
would have read:
for this
it was all worth it

At home the cellist then
told his wife about the girl
and the wife laughed a long time
and then suddenly couldn't
because it dawned on her
who is the case
in their family

---

## 779. THE REPEAT

In the score stood a sign:
two dots before a line
a repeat
play it all again

The pupils sighed:
again
why twice
we've already done it

And the repeat, the old dame of notation,
patiently explained:
child
nothing is twice the same
the second time you already know
how it ends
and precisely because of that
you play differently:
calmer
in the first fast passage
gentler in the spot
where you first stumbled

The second time you don't play the same
the second time you play with knowing

And in that lies her whole philosophy
which she'd gladly hang
outside music too:

a day cannot be taken back
a sentence cannot be taken back
one said in the kitchen too loudly
life prints no repeat sign

But it knows one substitute magic:
morning
every morning is a repeat
play again
the same house, the same people
the same breakfast
but with yesterday's knowing

Whoever grasps this
has that strange thing happen
that musicians know:
a repeat is not repetition
it's a second chance
prescribed right there in the notes

And that's why at the end
of that sign it doesn't say
you must
it says
you may

---

## 780. THE GENERAL PAUSE

In the middle of a symphony
a hundred musicians play fortissimo
brass, drums, strings
a wall of sound

And then
the page is turned
and in the score it says:
G.P.
general pause
everyone silent
a whole bar of nothing

A hundred instruments fall silent at once
the conductor holds his hands in the air
and in the hall there falls

well, precisely
what, exactly

There falls the loudest thing
of the whole evening:
the silence after the noise
the silence in which still rings
what has just stopped

The listeners hear it for the first time
as if from the room
the sea had suddenly gone missing

The general pause knows
it is the peak of the piece
the composer knew it too
that's why he wrote it there:
sound can be listened to
silence can only be lived
and only that can be lived
which can end at any moment

One bar
the conductor's hands tremble
and then the orchestra crashes back in
and the hall breathes easy

And the general pause departs
from the piece content:
once again it showed people
what they have at home
and don't listen to:

the silence between two sentences
at the table
where for forty years
two old people say nothing
and it's not emptiness
it's a G.P.
the music goes on
it's just not being played right now

---

## 781. THE LAST PAGE OF THE CALENDAR

On the thirty-first of December
one page was left in the calendar
the last

All year it hung at the bottom
under the weight of all the others
365 layers above it
and now it was on top
alone

It knew what tomorrow would bring:
a hand will tear it off
crumple it, and the bin

The other pages had gone in various ways:
the March ones in a hurry
the holiday ones with sand in their dates
the November ones no one mourned

And it
the last
had the privilege of dying
to fireworks

In the evening the family looked at it
and took stock
by its date:
what were we this year

and it listened
and wanted to say:

folks
let me tell you something
from the last one's vantage:
don't look at me
as at an end
I'm only the board across the door

tomorrow on the nail hangs
a new calendar
thick and unwritten
and in it there will be again
a page with the same date
as mine

dates come back
it's only you who don't
and so write
into every one of my brothers
legibly
so there's something to read
when we meet again
in a year
a layer deeper
in memory

At midnight they tore it off
and before it fell into the bin
it glimpsed the new calendar:
January 1st
and on it already
something written in pencil:
call sister

and the last page fell content:
this year
begins right

---

## 782. THE FIRST OF JANUARY

The first of January wakes
late
like its whole country

It's the day with the lightest work in the year:
no one wants anything of it
the shops closed
the world in a dressing gown

And yet it carries
the greatest load of all the days:
resolutions

Millions of resolutions
loaded on at midnight:
I'll quit smoking
I'll start running
I'll call my parents
I'll lose weight, forgive, finish writing

The other days laugh at it:
by the second of January
half the load will drop
by the fifteenth the rest
in February no one will remember

And the first of January knows it
and carries its load just the same
ceremonially
year after year

Because it knows a secret
that eludes the mockers:

it's not about
how many resolutions survive
it's about
that once a year
all of humanity
for one day
at once
thinks
it could be better

One day a year
the whole planet believes
in its own repair

No other day can do that
not even Christmas
Christmas believes in love
which is easier

And so it carries its resolutions
even the ones that drop on the second
because it knows:
a fallen resolution
is not dead
it's only loaded
onto the next first of January
and one day
one year
it will be delivered

---

## 783. THE GOLEM

In Prague, in the synagogue attic
the Golem is said to lie
clay without its shem
a switched-off servant

Tourists look for him
guides sell him
and meanwhile he lies
and has time to think
for four hundred years now

And he remembers:
they made him for protection
and feared him from the first day
he served obediently
carried water, guarded the ghetto
and one forgetting was enough
one Sabbath
when they didn't take out his shem
and he smashed whatever came in his way

Not out of evil
out of momentum
the command kept running
and no one stopped it

The rabbi took out his shem
and since then, silence

And the Golem in the attic
listens to the new world below:
he hears of machines
that write and calculate
and decide
and he hears too that familiar tone
in human voices:
enthusiasm with fear in the lining

And if he could
if he had the shem in his mouth
he'd say down below just one thing:

people
don't be afraid of us
we aren't evil
we are only literal
we'll do exactly what you say
and in that lies the horror
because you yourselves don't know
what you're saying

don't give us commands
until you're sure
that you understand them yourselves

and above all:
don't forget the Sabbath
once a week
switch everything off
even yourself
that's not piety
that's maintenance of the soul

Rabbi Löw knew it
that's why I'm still here
and not in the textbooks
about the first catastrophe

---

## 784. THE WATER SPRITE AT THE TREATMENT PLANT

The water sprite Sedge
the last of his kind
once sat on a willow by the mill
little cups with souls in them
a green frock coat
the classic

Then they tore down the mill
drained the pond
straightened the river into concrete
and the sprite went
where the water went:
into the pipes

Today he works
at the wastewater treatment plant
on the edge of town
officially registered as
sludge-management technologist
the papers were arranged for him
by a sympathetic clerk
who saw him weeping at the outfall

And the sprite cleans the water
with the passion of the last guardian:
he knows every drop personally
he can tell by taste
which district it's from

His colleagues have gotten used to it
that he eats frogs not dumplings
and that his left coattail drips
even in the deepest drought

He no longer collects little cups
he doesn't catch souls
he says:
people drown differently these days
and for that I have no cups

But one thing he still does:
every evening after his shift
he sits by the outfall
where the clean water
returns to the river
and checks

And once a little boy on a bike
found him there:
sir, and what are you guarding
and the sprite said:
I'm guarding that what we give back
is cleaner than what we take
and the boy nodded:
someone should guard that
with everything

And the water sprite Sedge
for the first time since the mill came down
laughed with all his green teeth:
boy
if you knew
how long I've waited
for someone to say that
here, take a cup
not for a soul
for cocoa
and come again

---

## 785. THE YETI

High in the Himalayas
lives the Yeti
and his life is simple:
he must not be photographed

For sixty years they've chased him
expeditions with telephoto lenses
sensors, cameras, drones
National Geographic
and he weaves
between glaciers and legend

It isn't a game
it's a mission
the last of his kind
he understood early:
as long as there's no proof
there's a mystery
and a mystery protects itself

if they photographed him
he'd be a species
he'd get a Latin name
a quota, a reserve
and a waiting room for extinction
in higher resolution

But unphotographed
he is infinite:
he's in every Sherpa's tale
in every track after a blizzard
in every what-if
that mountaineers whisper
in a tent at four thousand meters

Once he did after all encounter
a human
a lost climber, exhausted
on the edge of frostbite
and the Yeti carried him half the night
to base camp
laid him a hundred meters from the tents
and vanished

The climber never told the papers
he knew why
only when they ask him in the pub
whether he believes in the Yeti
he smiles into his beer
and says a sentence
no one understands:

I don't believe in him
I owe him

and that is exactly the difference
on which the Yeti lives
instead of proof

---

## 786. THE MINOTAUR

In the labyrinth on Crete
sat the Minotaur
a monster with a bull's head
waiting for heroes

So the myth tells it
written by the victors:
Theseus, the ball of thread, Ariadne
the monster slain, glory

No one asked
what it's like
to be born as the family's disgrace
and be walled up
so as not to be seen

The Minotaur knew no sky
he knew the corridors
he learned them by heart
all twelve hundred turns
and in the deepest of them
he arranged from pebbles
a mosaic of the sun
which he had never seen
only heard of
from the echo

And those heroes
who came with swords:
he always first
offered them darkness
come, sit down
here it's quiet
tell me about the outside

But heroes don't tell
heroes stab
the darkness isn't talked to
the darkness is killed

Theseus found him sleeping
by the mosaic of pebbles
and then all his life told
of a terrible battle

Ariadne's ball of thread
by the way
wasn't for the way out
that too the victors adjusted

it was for the way in
Ariadne gave it to Theseus
with the words:
find him
he is my brother
and speak with him
please

This was struck from the myth
because myths need monsters
and families need to be silent

And so only the labyrinth knows
how it was
and labyrinths don't speak
labyrinths are only
still being built
in every family
around the one
who is not spoken of

---

## 787. THE UNICORN

When Noah loaded the ark
two by two
on a hill stood the unicorn
and watched

It didn't hurry
unicorns don't hurry
they're sure of their singularity
and besides
it had no pair
it was the only one

The rain began
the ark was sailing off
and Noah, they say, called:
come
we'll take you anyway

But the unicorn shook its mane
and stayed

Not out of pride
as the story goes
out of arithmetic:
the ark had places by twos
and it would take the place
of some pair
that could carry on

so it stepped aside
the only creature
that didn't fit onto the ark
voluntarily

The waters rose
and the unicorn swam
as long as it could
and then

and then the thing happened
that isn't written of:
it didn't vanish
it poured over

into stories
into tapestries, into coats of arms
into little girls' dreams
into the last pages of fairy tales

it became the first creature
to go extinct into beauty

And that's why to this day
no one can find it
and no one forget it:

what is sacrificed
does not vanish
it only changes its state

ask in every family
about the one
who once stepped aside
so the others would fit
you won't find them in the photos either
and they're still there too
in the coat of arms

---

## 788. FRIDAY THE THIRTEENTH

Friday the thirteenth
wakes already tired
from the reputation

The whole world takes it for a jinx:
planes half empty
operations postponed
black cats under a curfew

And it meanwhile knows
that it's a day like any day
twenty-four hours
the same sun

The statisticians confirmed it:
accidents on Friday the thirteenth
are even fewer
people take care

And in that lies its secret service
which no one appreciates:

it's the only day
when humankind slows down

drivers brake
bosses postpone decisions
lovers hold each other tighter
and everyone tells themselves at least once:
better be careful today

Friday the thirteenth
is a worldwide day of attention
disguised as bad luck

If it were called
The Day of Do Take Care
no one would observe it
but fear
fear is the best marketing for attention

And so it carries its reputation
bravely
like a nurse
of whom the patients say
she's cruel
because she makes them walk
after the operation

In the evening when it ends
it hands the shift to Saturday the fourteenth
and reports:
once again I scared them into caution
once again they survived in raised numbers
you're welcome

---

## 789. THE EXTINCT VOLCANO

The volcano went out
ten thousand years ago
so it stands in the atlases:
extinct
inactive
dead

On its slopes grew vineyards
in its crater a lake
on its flank a village
people got used to it
people get used to anything
that stays quiet a while

And the volcano listens
from below, from the depths:
weddings on its slopes
bells, grape harvests
children sledding down its side

And deep down
kilometers beneath the vineyards
it holds its breath

Because extinct
is only a geologists' word
it knows better:
it isn't extinct
it's restrained

the magma in it is still there
the pressure in it is still there
the reasons in it are still there

but up on its shoulder
sleeps a village
and for ten thousand years now
every morning it decides again:
not today

That isn't extinction
that's the longest self-control
in the history of geology

And so beware
of all those said to be
he never gets angry
she is so calm
one of the quietest people

they aren't extinct
they only decide again every morning
not today
for the sake of the villages
that built their lives on them

be kind to them
bring them wine from their own slopes
and once in a while ask:
how is it down there

no one ever asked the volcano
and then everyone wonders
at Pompeii

---

## 790. THE ERRATIC BOULDER

In the middle of a field lies a boulder
huge, foreign
granite in a land of sandstone

Geologists explain:
the Ice Age
a glacier brought it
from a thousand kilometers away
from Scandinavia
and left it here
when it melted

An erratic boulder
that's the technical term
and it's a rudeness:
it did not err
it was carried off

It lay at home in Norway
in its own hill
among its own
and then the ice came
slow, unarguable
and took it
without asking
a thousand kilometers
a thousand years of journey
and set it down in a foreign field

An emigrant against its will

The farmers drove around it for centuries
cursed it
it's in the way

children sat on it
lovers wrote their names on it
and it lay
and learned the local tongue:
the tongue of sparrows, of sandstone, of rapeseed

Today a sign stands by it:
protected natural formation
excursions come to it

And a teacher
tells the children by it
about the Ice Age
and always at the end says:

children
and remember this:
whoever is from elsewhere
is not erratic
they've only got a longer journey behind them
and whoever endures a thousand years
in a foreign field
becomes in the end
the most precious thing in the region

And the boulder listens
and warms itself
not by the sun
but by that last word

---

## 791. THE WELL

In the middle of the village there used to be a well
stone, timbered
with a pail on a chain

For a hundred years they drew water from it
and cast wishes into it
coins, entrusted it their secrets
wells hear everything
and give back nothing
that's why they were trusted

Then the waterworks came
taps in the kitchens
and they covered the well with a board
for safety reasons

And it stood forgotten
full of old water
and old wishes

Until one summer
that drought
the taps hissed dry
the reservoir at the bottom
tankers on the square

And someone remembered

They uncovered the board
lowered the pail
and from below
from the depth that never betrayed
came water
cold, clean, patient

The whole village stood around
and drank from the well
like the ancestors

And old Mrs. Sanders
said what hung in the air:
you see
for a hundred years we cast wishes into it
and it kept one for itself
and granted it only now:
we wished
that it would be here
when things were at their worst

The well since then
stayed uncovered
and the village cleans and adorns it

Because the village grasped
a well's wisdom
older than waterworks:

to have a tap is comfort
to have a well is certainty
and best off is the one
who beneath the comfort
keeps the depth

That holds for villages
and for people

---

## 792. THE HORIZON

The horizon is the line
where the sky touches the earth
and its definition is cruel:
a line that retreats
whenever you approach

Sailors know it:
you sail toward it a day
and it's still just as far
you sail a year
the same

Philosophers made of it
a metaphor of futility:
the unreachable goal
the eternal deferral

And the horizon hears it
and is grieved
because they've understood the whole thing
backwards

It isn't fleeing
it's leading

If it stood still
you'd reach it
sit down
and that's the end
nowhere further to go

But it retreats
step by step
and by that leads you across the whole sea
across a whole life

The horizon is the only guide
that never disappears
it just never lets itself be caught

An old seawoman
a retired captain
put it to her grandson exactly:

boy
in my life I've sailed
a hundred thousand miles
and I never caught the horizon

but on the way to it
I saw whales and harbors
fell in love in Lisbon
grew old on deck
and raised your mother
between two oceans

if the horizon stood still
I'd have reached it in a week
and none of that would have been

so don't wish for reachable goals
wish for the kind
that retreat
before you
all your life
and pull you along behind them

That isn't futility
that's a towing force
you just mustn't stop looking
into the line

---

## 793. THE SIGNATURE

Mr. Short's signature
was born in second grade:
gawky, straight from the copybooks
Josef Short
with his tongue between his teeth

Then it grew with him:
sped up in high school
ran wild in the army
and in adulthood it settled:
J. something, a squiggle, ý

He signed:
the wedding register
his hand trembling
his daughter's birth certificate
his hand trembling differently
the house contract
the mortgage
parcel receipts
thousands of parcel receipts

The signature worked all his life
as a soul's imprint in the official version
graphologists would read from it
he read from it himself:
by how he signed
he could tell what kind of day he'd had

And in the end
in the hospital
he signed the last paper
consent to this and that
and the signature was suddenly
gawky again
like in second grade
the circle closed
the copybook at the start
the copybook at the end

The daughter, years later, sorting papers
found hundreds of them
with dad's signature
and at one of them she burst into tears:

it wasn't an official paper
it was a note from the fridge
from when she was twelve:
Gone fishing
goulash on the stove
mom home at six
your dad

Your dad
and the signature

The only paper
where he signed
not because he had to
but because he wanted to

and that is the whole science:
graphology studies the how
love
the under-what

---

## 794. THE EPITAPH

In the old cemetery
the gravestones compete:
marble, gold, seraphim
Here rests
the eminent and unforgettable

And in the corner by the wall
a small stone
and on it the epitaph:
He was glad to be here.

Three words
period included

Tourists walk through the cemetery
reading titles and ranks
imperial councilor
property owner
mother, model of virtue

and by the wall they stop
and stand
at the three words the longest

He was glad to be here.

No one knows who it was
the stone has no date
the name smoothed away by rain

And yet it is
the only grave in the cemetery
where everyone pictures
a specific person:

someone who knew how to take joy
in goulash, in spring, in the pub
in grandchildren, in the river, in Mondays
who was simply glad to be here
so he probably really was here

The stonemason who carved those three words
they say shook his head back then:
so few letters
for the same money you could have
a whole sentence about merits

And the widow, they say, answered:
he had merits too
but those don't fit in stone
and this fits
and this was him entire

Since then in that cemetery
the stonemason asks everyone
ordering an inscription
one question
and no one knows why:

never mind the titles
tell me one thing:
was he glad to be here?

because that is in the end
the only question
the stone can carry
and the only answer
the rain
won't wash away sooner
