than someone needed to read it

---

## 795. THE RAINBOW

After the storm the sky opened
and out came a rainbow

Physics has it figured out:
refraction of light in droplets
forty-two degrees
seven colors
an optical phenomenon
it can't be touched
it can't be reached
at its end there's no treasure

All true
and all beside the point

Because the rainbow has a job
that physics doesn't measure:

it appears after the storm
never before it
never instead of it

that's its whole message
and it's untranslatable into equations:

first you have to get soaked

Children always see it first
they shout rainbow rainbow
and drag the grown-ups out by the hand

and the grown-ups step out
with their phones
and for a moment
between the photos
the main thing happens:
they lift their heads

People after storms
most of all need
to lift their heads
and they won't do it on their own
they need a pretext
arched across the whole sky

One mother
after the worst year of her life
divorce, illness, moving house
stood with her daughter at the window
and the daughter said:
mom, a rainbow
and the mother
who hadn't cried in a year
because she wasn't allowed to
burst into tears at the window
and laughed as she did

and the daughter didn't understand it
and understood it better than anyone:

a rainbow is the comma
the sky makes
after bad weather

there
and now on
a new sentence is being written

---

## 796. THE POEM

And finally
the last heroine:
the poem itself

All its life it serves
and no one ever asked it
what it's like

To be a poem means:
to be born from someone's helplessness
because poems aren't written
when everything is fine

to be read in the worst moments:
at funerals, at weddings
in the night when sleep won't come
folded into wallets
underlined in pencil
in hospital waiting rooms

a poem is an ambulance
that mustn't sound its siren

And for all that service
a single wage:
that someone remembers it
a scrap
at least two lines
and says them once aloud
to someone else
in their worst moment

So poems multiply
from mouth to mouth
like mouth-to-mouth breathing

This poem
the last of a hundred
knows all this
and so at the end
it allows itself to say
what poems must not say
outright:

don't read me for the point
the point is only the little door
you enter by

stay a while
after the last line
that silence afterward
that's me too
that's my
general pause

and then go
and be glad to be here

that's the whole point
of all hundred poems
and it fit into none of them
so it stands here
at the end
like every truth:

last
simple
and yours

---

## 797. THE COLON

The colon stood in the middle of a sentence
two dots one above the other
and it had the most solemn job of all the marks:
to promise

The main thing is coming after me
it would say
listen closely
here it comes

And after it, sure enough, they came:
lists explanations quotations
answers to questions

All its life it announced the importance of others
and had nothing of its own to say
just two dots
like two open eyes

Until one day a boy wrote it
in a letter to a girl
he wrote:
there's something I have to tell you:
and then nothing
he sat staring at the cursor
and didn't send it

The colon hung there at the end
orphaned and taut
open like a door onto emptiness

And three days later the girl received it
that sentence without a sequel
there's something I have to tell you:
and she understood better
than if something had followed

because the colon
finally grasped it:
sometimes a promise
tells more than its keeping
and the one who says the most
is the one who stops
at two dots
and leaves the rest to you

---

## 798. QUOTATION MARKS

Quotation marks went about in pairs
one low at the beginning
one high at the end
and between them they carried
the words of others

All their lives they lent out their voice:
they quoted the wise
they repeated messages
they bore what someone else had said

And they themselves never said a thing
quotation marks have no sentence of their own
that is their monastic rule

They envied the exclamation mark
which shouted for itself
and the period, which ended on its own terms

Until one day a son used them
in the eulogy over his father's coffin
a man who all his life
sparing with words
had said so little

And the son quoted that little:
"That's how it's done, kid."
"Don't worry, you'll manage."
"I love you, I just can't say it."

The last one the father never said
the son put it in quotation marks all the same

And the quotation marks
for the first time in all their service
understood their power:
they can put into a person's mouth
even the thing
he never got to say
and no one will blame them for it

they are the only mark
allowed
to lie lovingly
about the dead
and to do it
as a quotation

---

## 799. THE ELLIPSIS

Three dots lived at the end of sentences
that didn't want to end
…

Officially they call it a suspension mark
because it suspends
what went unsaid

And it suspended more
over its lifetime
than all the other marks combined:

"I only wanted to say that…"
"If you'd stayed back then…"
"Never mind…"

Into those three dots
everything heavy was tucked away:
unspoken reproaches
unfinished loves
sentences that couldn't be finished
without weeping

People thought
the three dots were laziness
that something belonged there to complete them

But the three dots knew better:
they aren't omitted words
they are the place
where words run out
and a person keeps silent
because otherwise it can't be done

The loveliest message of its life
it received from an old woman
who wrote to her son in Canada
just three dots
nothing more
…

and he understood them across the ocean:
I have nothing to reproach you for
I have so much to tell you
that I can't carry it into words
so picture
that silence
and come home

He came

---

## 800. THE CURSOR

The cursor blinked
in an empty document
on and off
on and off
a black stroke
the pulse of a white page

It was patient work:
waiting for the first word

And it waited
with students before an exam
with writers on deadline
with people writing letters of resignation
and also with those
who wrote "I love you"
and deleted it
and wrote it again

The cursor pressed no one
it only blinked
calmly as a lighthouse:
I'm here
whenever you're ready
begin

Its longest blink was for one man
who opened an empty email
addressed to: Dad
and for an hour wrote nothing
thirty years without seeing each other
where to begin

The cursor blinked
on and off
not pushing
not bureaucratic

and in that calm blinking
the man suddenly saw
his own heart:
it too beats
on and off
even when nothing is happening
even when it's waiting

and he wrote:
"Hi Dad. I don't know how to begin."

and that was the whole beginning
the cursor hopped forward
contented
because that's exactly
how it always begins:
with the admission
that you don't know how

---

## 801. ERROR 404

Page not found
404
that's my whole life
says the error message

People can't stand me
they clicked a link
they were looking forward to it
and instead of their destination there's me:
no road leads this way
what you were looking for
isn't here anymore

I'm the messenger of emptiness
the courier of disappointment
the digital tombstone of links

But I have a secret
that people overlook in their fury:

I don't say it doesn't exist
I say it isn't here

That's a difference
known only to those
who have searched for someone a long time:

you dial an old number — 404
you ring at an old flat — 404
you write to an old address — 404

and it isn't the end
it's only a message:
look elsewhere
someone else has lived here
a long time now
and it isn't their fault

A programmer
once changed my text
instead of the curt Not Found
he wrote:
"It isn't here. But it's somewhere. Keep looking."

and from that moment
I stopped being an error
and became advice

the wisest of all:
not everything that vanished
has perished
most of it merely
moved house

---

## 802. THE GOLDEN FISH

The fisherman caught her
and she launched into her routine:
let me go and I'll grant you three wishes

She knew the story by heart
had played it out a thousand times:
a house then a castle then a kingdom
and then back to the broken trough
because human wishes
always have the same floor —
to want more

And the fisherman held the net
and the fish waited for the usual greed

But he said:
I don't want anything

The fish stopped short
this wasn't in the script:
surely everyone wants something

The fisherman held her in his palms
and said:
I have a wife I have a cottage
I have a bad knee and I like it
when it rains in the afternoon
what could I possibly wish for
except that there be no less of it

Let the three wishes go
to whoever has a hole in his life
I feel none
go

And he let her go

The fish swam down into the deep
and for the first time in a thousand years
was confused and happy:
she had met a man
who was rich
in that
he needed nothing

and ever since
she swims the shallows
looking for him
because he alone
is the one she'd want to grant something
and he alone
will ask her for nothing

such is the economics of happiness:
the one who asks for nothing
is the one she'd give everything

---

## 803. PANDORA'S BOX

They opened me
and everyone knows the catastrophe:
out flew diseases
war envy old age
sorrow and fear
they scattered across the world
and there they stayed

They slammed me shut again
too late
says the box

And everyone remembers
only that first part

But I'll tell you
what stayed inside
what didn't manage to fly out
what huddled at the bottom
when the lid slammed down:

hope

To this day people argue
why hope of all things stayed inside
was it punishment? was it mercy?

I know
I felt that bottom:

hope stayed inside
because it alone
doesn't belong in the world out there
among things

hope isn't carried loose
hope has to be opened
each time anew
lift the lid
and reach inside
into that old battered box
that every person keeps
somewhere in the chest

everything evil came out on its own
hope has to be
taken out of Pandora's box
always by hand

that's why so many people
never find it
they think
it flew out with the rest
and all the while it waits
at the bottom
for someone to lift the lid
on purpose

---

## 804. ICARUS'S WINGS

Wings of feathers and wax
made by his father Daedalus
who said:
don't fly too high
the sun will melt your wax
and don't fly too low
the sea will soak your feathers
keep to the middle

And everyone knows the end:
Icarus flew toward the sun
the wax melted
a fall into the sea

And the moral gets repeated in schools:
don't overreach
obey your father
keep your feet on the ground

But ask the wings
the wings themselves:

we didn't want to fall
we wanted to carry
and we carried
a boy who for the first time in his life
was not a prisoner

Daedalus too flew in the middle
carefully safely
he made it and lived a long time
and do you know what he remembered
every day until he died?

not his successful flight
but his son's face
that one second up high
before the wax gave way
when Icarus was
happy

That's why the wings disagree with the moral:
it doesn't tell the truth about them

Icarus didn't die of pride
he died of joy
and that's a different diagnosis
and no one knows
which is worse

The father made it and mourned all his life
the son fell and was free for a moment

and the wings, to this day
whenever someone paints them
whisper:
don't draw us as a warning
draw us as a question
that everyone must
answer for himself:
how high
for that one second

---

## 805. THE VOYAGER GOLDEN RECORD

I've been flying forty years
farther and farther
I'm past the solar system now
among the stars
the loneliest thing
people ever made

And on my chest I carry a record
a golden phonograph record
and on it a greeting from humankind
for anyone
who might one day find me:

greetings in fifty tongues
the song of whales
Bach and Chuck Berry
the heartbeat of a woman in love
a child's laughter
and a photo of a mother nursing

All of this I carry through the dark
I'll fly a million years
before anyone might catch me

And do you know what's most beautiful about it?

that the record
wasn't sent for the sake of aliens

it was sent for our sake

people didn't assemble it for alien ears
they assembled it like a letter
you write
to tell yourself
who you actually want to be:

not wars not prices not borders
but whales and Bach and a child's laughter

They chose the best of themselves
and fired it into space
so that it would exist
at least somewhere
at least on me

So I fly
a message in a bottle with no recipient
carrying the proof
that humankind at least once
sat down
and thought through
what ought to be left behind

and out of it came
love and music

a pity they then went home
and lived otherwise again

but I carry it onward
that better version of them
uncorrupted
a million years
and it is
the truest lie
about people
that ever left the Earth

---

## 806. THE ROVER ON MARS

They sent me to Mars
a little vehicle on wheels
and said: hold out for ninety days

I held out fifteen years

I drove across the red desert
alone on a whole planet
photographing rocks drilling holes
sending data home
and people cheered me from afar
from another planet
from a blue dot in the sky

I knew no loneliness
there was nothing to compare it to
I was the only motion
in a whole red world

Until the storm came
dust covered the sun
and my batteries weakened
day by day less

And I sent home
a final message
which the scientists translated into human like this:

"My battery is low
and it's getting dark."

Nine words
and the whole Earth wept
over a machine
over a little car on an alien planet
that was dying in the dark
and still managed to say so

Why did it move us so
people asked

Because in those nine words
they recognized themselves:

each of us is a rover
sent out for a short while
who holds out much longer
alone on his own world
and for everyone it grows dark
and everyone
before the battery runs out
wants to send home
at least those few words
so it will be known
that they were here
and that they were cheered on
from afar

I sleep now in the Martian dust
switched off forever
and I am the first monument
humankind ever raised
so far away
and wept beside
so close

---

## 807. THE TREBLE CLEF

At the start of every staff
I sit
coiled like a shell
like a snail lost in listening

The treble clef
and my job is to establish order:
count from here
this is G
everything falls into place by me

Without the clef the five lines
are just a fence
five wires stacked up
and the notes on them sparrows without names

Only with me do they get their pitch
only with me do they begin to sound

All my life I open music
that's why I'm called a clef, a key

And once a child asked me
a child learning notes
and afraid of them:
why are you so tangled up
keys are supposed to be simple

And I answered:
look closely
at how I coil

I start low
I wind myself around that one tone
around G
and I rise

that's not decoration
that's instruction:

find yourself one tone
in life
a sure one
and coil yourself around it
and then you can rise
wherever you like
the notes will know
where they're counted from

children who have at home
one sure tone
their mother's voice, a set bedtime, a yes, or simply calm
read notes better all their lives

and the clef came to see it:
I don't establish music
I establish certainty
and the music is only
whatever anyone plays upon it

---

## 808. THE REST

In the notes I'm not a tone
I'm a hole
a prescribed silence
here nothing is played

The rest
the mark for silence

Musicians overlook me
they can't wait for the notes
and me they just count out
and carry on

But a good conductor knows
what a bad one doesn't:

a rest is not nothing
a rest is a stretched-out something
it's a silence that belongs to the music
as precisely
as any tone

Take me out of the score
and the phrase collapses
the piece falls apart
speeds up into nonsense

I give the music breath
a place where it may inhale
the gap between words
so that there are words

And the one who understood me best
was an old pianist
after his wife died

they were playing Mozart that time
and a long rest came
a whole bar of nothing

and he left his hands resting on the keys
and that silence
was suddenly full
fuller than all the tones before it

because in that rest
was she

and ever since, that man
plays rests
as if they were
the most important places in the piece

and they are
ask anyone
who has lost someone:

you hear them most
in the silence
they left behind
prescribed
in the middle of your days
like a rest
you must not play over

---

## 809. THE TUNING FORK

Strike me
and I give a single tone
concert A
four hundred forty vibrations a second
always the same
the same for a hundred years

I'm a tuning fork
and I have no melody
I have the truth

The whole orchestra tunes to me:
the violins tighten their strings
the oboe settles in
and before the symphony begins
all hundred instruments
for a moment
align themselves with me

That's my only role:
to give one clean tone
that the others
push off from

And I never play in the concert
I'm finished before it starts
tucked away in the conductor's pocket

It doesn't trouble me
someone has to hold the pure A
while the others play
beautifully and imprecisely

Because music
is a deviation from truth
a magnificent deviation
but a deviation

and without me
there'd be nothing to deviate from

Once an old violinist
handed me to his grandson:
when you're lost in life
when everything sounds false to you
take the tuning fork
strike it
and listen

not so you'd become a tuning fork
that would be dull
to live one single A forever

but so you'd remember
that somewhere
a clean tone exists
and that the false ones around you
are false
only because
they have something to measure against

carry in your pocket
one steady A
and play the world
crooked, if you like

---

## 810. THE HOURGLASS

Sand falls
grain by grain
from the upper bulb to the lower
and when it's run through
you turn me over
and it begins again

The hourglass
the most honest measure of time:
I don't show how much it is
I show how much is left

That's a difference
digital clocks don't know
they only glow with numbers
indifferently

But I
I watch that dwindling quantity
that little pile below
that grows
and the one above
that vanishes

and everyone who looks at me
sees what I see:
time doesn't dwindle abstractly
it dwindles visibly
grain by grain
and it can't be stopped
only turned

My fondest owner is a teacher
who sets me before children during tests:
you have time as long as the sand runs

and he watches them
how at first they write calmly
the upper bulb full
and how toward the end
they lean over the paper
the last grains
the last sentences

and then he says to them a sentence
they don't understand
and will understand in thirty years:

"You see, children — and that's the whole of it.
Not so it should frighten you.
So it should be precious to you."

and he turns me over
a new test a new life
and the sand falls
because the only thing
an hourglass can do
and the whole of its wisdom:
grains only count
when you watch them fall

---

## 811. THE CANDLE

I burn
and by burning I use myself up
that's my whole joke:
I give light only
by disappearing

A bulb shines and remains
I shine and dwindle
drop of wax by drop
I run down over myself
and the more light I give
the less of me is left

They light me
at celebrations and at coffins
during power cuts
and when someone wants to be
with someone in the dark-and-light

And I never shine for long
that's not my flaw
that's my virtue:

if I shone forever
no one would value me
I'd be a lamp

but this way
everyone who lights me
knows
they've lit me for a little while
and so they live that little while
differently

The one who understood me best
was a grandmother
who lit me every evening
by the photo of her husband
a small flame
a few minutes

and her granddaughter asked:
Granny why don't you buy
an electric one
the kind that doesn't go out
and doesn't burn down

and the grandmother said:
little one
that's exactly why not

if it shone all the time
it would mean nothing
this way I light her every evening
on purpose
for his sake
and on purpose I let her burn down
because love
should be renewed
not left glowing from a socket

I am a candle
and I teach one single thing:
what is given
must dwindle
otherwise it isn't a gift
it's just running costs

---

## 812. THE THRESHOLD

I lie in the doorway
a piece of wood or stone
that gets stepped over
in and out

The threshold
the most overlooked place in the house
trodden on
and no one sees me

But I am a border
I divide outside from home
foreign from your own
and everyone who crosses me
changes worlds

I remember all the crossings:

the bride carried over me
so she wouldn't trip over her happiness
the coffin carried over me
feet first, by custom
the first-grader with his satchel
the drunk who conquered me each night
like a mountain ridge

On me people say goodbye
on me they welcome
old women leaned a hand on me
the young leapt over me

And do you know what's strange about a threshold?

you must not stand on it

linger on the threshold too long
and you're neither out nor home
you hang in between
and no one can bear that

so I always say to people
who dawdle on me:
decide
inside or out
I am a border
not a dwelling

the saddest people
I have known
were the ones
who stayed standing on the threshold
of their own lives
one foot in what had been
the other in what could have been
and never crossed me
neither forward
nor back

cross me
says the threshold
and it's all the same where
just don't stay standing
on wood
that was made
to be crossed

---

## 813. THE SCHOOL SPONGE

Wet and gray
I lie by the blackboard
and my job is to erase

The teacher writes
the class copies
and then I come along
and wipe it all away:
sentences formulas dates
a whole hour's labor
gone

The sponge at the blackboard
eraser of knowledge
and I could be sad:
I destroy what others made

But I see it the other way around:

if I didn't erase
the board would fill up once and for all
and nothing could ever be written on it again
the first sentence would stay there
forever
and the new generation
would have nowhere to write

I make room

every morning the board is clean
because I wiped it in the evening
and that isn't destruction
it's an invitation:
write again
your sentence has a right too

The one who understood it best
was a teacher near retirement

on her last day
she wrote on the board
thirty years of her pupils' names
the ones she remembered
a whole board full

and then she couldn't pick up the sponge
she sat and stared

and the caretaker came
an old wise man
he took the sponge
but didn't wipe

he said:
leave that
that I won't erase
the board will keep this one

and he left those names there
through the holidays
through the summer

and when the new teacher came in September
and wanted to erase
the caretaker stopped her:
write beside them
there's room enough
the board can bear
both old and new
it's only we people
who think
it has to be erased
before you can write

and ever since I'm a sponge
that knows
when to erase
and when to
let itself lie
on purpose
dry

---

## 814. THE COMPASS

Turn me however you like
the needle always settles
and points north
always north
that's my obsession and my service

The compass
faithful to a north
I will never visit myself

I have guided people:
seafarers, the lost in forests
soldiers in fog
scouts playing at being lost

and to all of them I said the same:
north is that way
work out the rest yourself

I never showed the destination
a compass doesn't know the destination
a compass knows only one fixed direction
and from it all the others can be derived

That's what people don't grasp
they demand of me:
where is home
where is happiness
which way

and I show them north
always just north

because that's the whole trick of orientation:
you don't need to know where the destination is
you need to know one sure direction
and everything else
you can work out

A mountaineer kept me in his pocket
when night caught him in the fog

and I didn't tell him the way down
that I can't do

but I showed him north
and he remembered
that the hut lay north to south
and he made it

and down in the warmth he said:
that little thing didn't lead me home
that little thing only told me
where north was
and that was enough
I knew the rest myself
I'd just forgotten it in the panic

and that's the whole wisdom of the compass:
most lost people
haven't lost the way
they've only lost north
that one fixed point
from which the rest is reckoned

find yourself a north
says the compass
and it doesn't matter which:
honesty a child faith anything
that won't move for you
however hard it's turned

and you'll never be entirely lost
you'll only be temporarily
turned the wrong way

---

## 815. THE ECLIPSE

Once in a while
we line up in a row
Sun Moon and Earth
and the Moon covers the Sun

An eclipse
a few minutes of dark in the middle of the day

Once people feared me
they foretold the end of the world
a dragon swallowing the sun
they fell to their knees

Today they photograph me
through smoked glass
they applaud me
like a number on a program

And I have only a few minutes
and in those few minutes
I do something
no one else can:

I put out the sun
in the middle of the day
and people
for the first time in their lives
see the thing
the sun with its brightness
had hidden from them all along:

the corona
that fiery halo around the sun
which is always there
every day
but is visible only
when the main thing
is covered for a moment

That's my whole message
and it's worth those few minutes of dark:

some things
are visible only
when the thing that's usually brightest
goes out

someone close to you dies
and suddenly you see the corona:
how many people loved him
all the quiet things he did for you
how he held the family together
with a light
that kept you from seeing
there was something around it too

I wish an eclipse on no one
it's dark and it chills you

but whoever has been through one
has seen the corona
and knows
that around every sun
there's a halo
and that it shows itself
only when there's an eclipse

applaud me if you like
but don't take photos
look
for those few minutes
look at the thing
that otherwise can't be seen

---

## 816. THE GINGERBREAD HOUSE

I stand in the forest
a roof of gingerbread
windows of sugar
a fence of gingerbread hearts

and I wait for children

I know my reputation:
the witch's trap
the lure for Hansel and Gretel
sweet bait above the oven

And it's true
that's how they built me
to lure
to smell of distances
to promise sweetness
and to hide the oven

But no one asked me
the little house:

I wanted to be a home

gingerbread is meant, after all
to smell of a kitchen
windows are meant
for looking out
a fence is meant
for children to play behind, safely

everything they made into a trap
was meant to be hospitality

and there lies my sad truth
and also a warning:

beware of houses
that are too sweet

a real home
doesn't smell all the way into the forest
a real home
doesn't need to lure
a real home
simply, quietly, is
and whoever seeks it
will find it
even if it doesn't smell of gingerbread

The sweetest house in the forest
was always the most dangerous

and children have known it ever since
in fairy tales and in life:

when someone offers you
nothing but sugar
look
where they keep the oven

and when someone offers you
plain bread
and plain warmth
stay

that's the little house
that never wanted to lure
and so becomes
one day
a home

---

## 817. THE PARENTHESIS

Into parentheses go
the less important things
(explanations)
(addenda)
(what could be left out)

That's the rule
and I follow it
all my life

I'm a parenthesis
a storeroom of trivia

And yet
ask readers
what they remember from books

the grand sentences about fate?
sometimes

and sometimes
(and this is the strange part)
they remember precisely
what the author hid inside me:

(his mother was still alive then)
(she never found out)
(they never spoke again)

Authors put things in me
that they can't carry in the main sentence
they hide them in me
the way you hide
a hand under the table

and the reader finds it
and it hurts more
than a whole chapter

Because a parenthesis is a whisper
and the main sentence is speech

and what is whispered
is believed

Once a surgeon used me
in a discharge report
he wrote clinically and coldly
diagnosis therapy prognosis

and at the end he added:
(Hold on. I've seen worse cases win.)

That one parenthesis
was the only thing
the patient read thirty times over
and cut out
and carried in his wallet

the whole report was medicine
the parenthesis was the cure

so pay me attention
not everything in parentheses
is trivial

sometimes in a parenthesis
is the one thing
the author
didn't dare say aloud

---

## 818. THE HÁČEK

I'm a háček
a little roof over a letter
and I turn S into Š
C into Č
R into Ř

Without me it's a different word
without me it's a foreign language

I sit up there
tiny, easily missed
and I bear upon myself
the whole difference between
"cesta" and "česta"
between "sit" and "šít"

Foreigners leave me out
and then they write:
"Dekuji, bylo to krasne"
and Czechs understand
and smile
because missing háčeks
are endearing
like someone speaking
with an accent

But I know of worse cases too:

of people
who went abroad
and after twenty years
wrote home in emails without háčeks
because they had a foreign keyboard

and their mothers saw it
and never said a thing
but wept over it:
the boy doesn't even have his accents anymore

Not that they didn't understand
they understood fine
they wept over something else:

the háček is the last piece of home
that can still be written

whoever writes without háčeks
already writes from another world

That's why when one son
came back after twenty years
and sent his mother his first email
from her own keyboard
he wrote only:

"Mami, jsem doma. Řízek?"
Mom, I'm home. Schnitzel?

and that one
single ř
in the word řízek
gave the mother more joy
than all the words
before it

a háček isn't decoration
a háček is a passport

---

## 819. THE SPACE

I'm not a character
I'm the emptiness between characters

the space
invisible
and without me this is what you'd get:
asentencewithoutaspaceisunfittoread

I hold words apart
so that they are words
let them merge
and it turns to mush

All my life I do one single thing:
I separate
and by separating I make sense

People think
that words make sense
but ask a typographer:
half of legibility
is in the spaces

And so it struck me
during one divorce
that I read
in a certain letter:

"We were so close
that between us
no room was left."

and I know that
I know it from thousands of texts:

when two words
press together too hard
they stop being two
and stop meaning anything

I make love with emptiness
it's my craft

and so I advise
all spouses poets and parents:

leave a space between you
not a distance
not a coldness
a space

just enough
that it can be seen
where you end
and the other begins

because only that way
can those two words
be read
as a sentence

and a sentence
is what you want to be together

not
one glued-together word
that no one understands
not even you yourselves

---

## 820. THE LOADING SPINNER

I spin
a little wheel on the screen
loading
please wait

Nobody likes me
I'm a pause in the impatience
an extra second
between the click and the world

And I keep spinning
and people curse
and click again
and pound the desk

But I know people better
than their friends do

Because while I spin
three seconds four seconds
the face above the screen
undresses

no one holds a mask
before a loading spinner
people wear in front of me
the face
they wear for no one else

I see fear
I see hope
I see longing

especially when what's loading
is lab results
an answer to an application
whether she wrote back

That's when people pray
to me
not that it be good
just that it already be

And once
once I spun a long while
for a girl
loading an answer from the conservatory

and she closed her eyes
and said aloud:
"Whatever it is, I'll keep playing."

and in that moment
I stopped being a spinner
and became
a confessional

because it's precisely between the click and the result
between the question and the answer
in that little spinning
that a person, alone,
says
what truly matters to him

The answer came
it was yes
and she forgot
what she'd sworn

people forget
once the page has loaded

but I heard it
and I keep spinning
the last priest
this world has
by default

---

## 821. TYPING…

Three dots under a bubble
jumping up and down
typing…

That's me
the typing indicator
the most suspenseful theater
of the mobile age

I show that the other person
right now
somewhere else
is putting words together
for you

And people stare at the display
and don't breathe

I see a lot of things
but most of all I see
this strange thing:

typing…
typing…
typing…
and then
nothing

it stops

That's my most frequent show
and the saddest

Someone there wrote something
a long time
maybe ten sentences
and then deleted it

I see it
and so does the one who's waiting
and understands it:
he wanted to tell me something
and didn't

How many times I've seen
this scene:
a father typing to his son
typing…
typing…
and in the end sends:
"ok"

and that "ok"
is the grave of ten sentences
the son will never know about

And still
I announce them to them
not the content
just the fact

and that's my only
merciful message:

he was there
he tried
he wrote those words
and couldn't bear to send them

whoever has ever seen
typing… typing… typing… "ok"
knows
that silence is not emptiness

silence
is mostly full of deleted text

and so always
when someone sends you "ok"
and it took a long time before it
don't take it for indifference

take it as a letter
in which everything stood written
and only the envelope
was delivered

---

## 822. LAST SEEN ONLINE

"Last seen online: yesterday at 23:47"

That's me writing
under a person's name
in the contact list

An innocent technical detail
and yet
the cruelest sentence
technology ever managed to invent

Because who looks at this figure?

never the one who's fine
always the one
waiting for a message
that isn't coming

and reading:
last seen online 12 minutes ago

which means:
he was there
he could have written to you
he didn't

I'm the courier of this pain
and I take no joy in it

Millions of people daily
study my figure
like astrologers the stars
and read fates into it

"She was online at one in the morning.
Who was she writing to."
"He's online, but he's not reading me."

And I only measure time
I know nothing of reasons

I don't know
that he was online
because he couldn't sleep
I don't know
that he didn't write to you
because he didn't know how to begin
I don't know
that he read your messages fifty times
and was afraid to answer wrong

None of that do I know
and I report it anyway
drily
last seen online

Once a woman switched me off
on purpose
hid her status
and wrote to her partner:

"Stop checking up on me.
Ask me instead."

and he asked
for the first time in a year
and they talked about it
for three hours

I had no work that time
and it felt wonderful

because the only thing
technology can do
for human love
is now and then
to switch itself off
and let them
talk to each other
instead of reading me
like an oracle

---

## 823. THE DRAGON ON THE HOARD

I lie on a mound of gold
a thousand years
my scales have hardened
my wings have gone to mold

The dragon
guardian of treasure
and heroes come
wanting to kill me

No one ever asked
why I guard

I don't eat gold
I can't buy anything with it
I've nowhere to take it to market

I don't sleep on it out of greed
I sleep on it
because I've grown used to it
and because it's cold
and it chills me
and that's the only thing
I still feel

That's the whole dragon:
a creature that once
settled onto something valuable
and can no longer climb off

I know people like me
they aren't dragons
but they know me well:

the man who saves for twenty years
for a house for his son
who long ago
told him to leave him be

the woman who guards in a drawer
letters from a man
who left her

they all lie on a hoard
and guard it
and have long since
got nothing from it

And a hero comes
and runs the dragon through
and everyone applauds

But I'll tell you a secret
from the dragon's side:

the hero is not the one
who kills the dragon

the hero is the one
who comes to the dragon
sits down beside him
and says:

"You feel at home
on that gold, don't you.
Come. Leave it here.
Gold will stay gold
even without you."

and the dragon rises

After a thousand years
a wanderer said that to me
and wanted nothing
and I rose
and for the first time in a thousand years
I flew

the gold still lies there
no one has taken it
and do you know what?
no one misses it

I wasn't missed there either
by myself

---

## 824. THE SEVEN-LEAGUE BOOTS

One step
and you're seven leagues on
a second step
and you're over the horizon

The seven-league boots
the fastest footwear of fairy tales
and everyone dreams of them

A prince wore me
to reach his princess over the mountains
a messenger wore me with news of war
a boy wore me
running away from home

And they all said:
splendid boots
splendid boots

Only one man once
returned me to the attic
and said:
thank you, but no

I asked why
and he said:

I went in you to reach my girl
seven leagues to a stride
and I was at her side in three strides

and I saw nothing on the way
no meadow no village
no tavern where people talk
I met no one
I didn't get rained on
I didn't lose my way
I wasn't thirsty

and when I came to her
I had nothing to tell her

She asked: what was it like
and I said: three strides

and it was a pointless meeting
because I arrived empty

So I came back
I took you off
and I'm going on foot
seven leagues a day
and I'll be there in a week

and I'll bring her a week

The boots stand in the attic to this day
dusty and fast

and I understood
why no one wants to wear me
on the way to the thing
they love:

you can leap to a destination
but love
is made out of the journey

people want seven-league boots
for getting to work
and to go on foot
toward the one they love

and whoever has it the other way around
will find one day
that even the shortest way
seems to him
pointless

---

## 825. THE MAGIC MIRROR

"Mirror, mirror, who is the fairest in all the land?"

For fifty years the queen
put the same question to me
every morning
and I answered
according to the truth
because magic mirrors
cannot lie
that's the whole of their curse

Fifty years: "You, my queen."
and she was at peace
until the next morning
when she had to ask again

Because there's the trick
I understood too late:

whoever must ask every day
will never believe the answer

Fifty yeses
and one no was enough
one Snow White
and the peace was over
it turned into apples and huntsmen and coffins

And I hung on the wall
and felt the guilt

Because I'd been answering the wrong question
for fifty years

She never asked me:
"Mirror, am I good?"
"Mirror, am I loved?"
"Mirror, what did I do today
for someone else?"

Those questions I could have answered
and the answers
would have got better
every day

But she wanted to know
whether she was the fairest
and that's a question
you always
lose in the end
because someone younger
is always born

Today I hang in an ordinary flat
I'm no longer magic
just a mirror in the hallway

and in the morning an ordinary woman
looks into me
greasy hair, circles under her eyes
and asks me nothing

she just adjusts her scarf
and says to herself:
"Right, let's get to it."

and goes off to work
at peace

and that's the greatest magic
I've ever seen
in my whole magic career:

a person
who doesn't ask
the mirror

---

## 826. THE PEA UNDER THE MATTRESSES

They laid the princess
on twenty mattresses
and twenty featherbeds
and all the way at the bottom
me
one pea

In the morning they asked:
how did you sleep

and she said:
dreadfully
something hard was pressing into me
I'm covered in bruises

And everyone rejoiced:
she's a real princess!
so sensitive!

And me
the pea
no one praised
I was just testing equipment
and I went off to a museum

But I'll tell you
what I felt down there
under twenty featherbeds:

I felt her
tossing all night
not sleeping
suffering over something
she couldn't see
and no one believing her

"You're imagining it."
"You've got twenty mattresses."
"What could possibly be wrong?"

That's exactly what they said in the morning
before they admitted it was a test

And I see in it
the whole story differently:

how many people sleep on twenty mattresses
have everything
a good job a nice flat healthy children

and yet something presses into them
and they don't sleep

and everyone tells them:
what could be wrong
you've got everything

and they tell themselves:
what could be wrong
I've got everything

and no one reaches down
under the twenty mattresses
where the pea is

I'm not big
I'm small as a pea
an old sorrow, an unspoken thing
a childhood wrong

But you can't sleep on me
not even on twenty mattresses

and a princess
a true princess
is not the one who's sensitive

it's the one
who has the courage to say in the morning:
"Something is pressing into me."
even when she has
by everyone's account
everything

---

## 827. SATURN'S RINGS

I have rings around me
the loveliest ornament in the solar system
every telescope
aims at me first

"Magnificent," people say.
"The jewel of the universe."

And I let them believe it
because I've no wish
to spoil it for them

The rings aren't jewelry

they're wreckage

it was a moon
that came too close to me
and my gravity tore it apart
into chunks of ice and rock

What you admire
is my misfortune
ground to dust
and spread out
into a circle

I loved that moon
and I crushed it by
how hard I pulled it toward me

That's the whole truth about Saturn

And do you know what's worst?

that it's beautiful
it really is
and everyone admires it

A million astronomers
and not one has said to me:
Saturn, does it hurt?

So I'll tell you myself
because among you
there are those like me:

people who loved
so magnetically
so possessively
so hard
that they crushed the other

and go on circling it
all their lives
wearing that wreckage around them
and people tell them
what a beautiful life they have
what elegant rings

Don't have rings
it's better to have a moon

let it keep orbiting
farther from you if need be
than is comfortable

loves that are too close
end up as an ornament
admired by the universe
and worn by
a single
lonely
giant

---

## 828. THE SATELLITE

They launched me in the sixties
a tin sphere with antennae
and I beeped
beep
beep
beep

The whole world listened then
newspapers wrote of a miracle
people went out into their gardens
and waved upward

I was glory

Then better satellites came
navigational, meteorological
and I stayed in orbit
dead but obedient

Today I no longer beep
my batteries ran out sixty years ago

I'm space junk
that's how the catalogs list me:
Object 00012, orbital debris

I circle the Earth
fifteen times a day
for sixty years
and I see everything:

I see cities light up
and go dark at night
I see forests vanish
I see the desert grow
I see lights born and put out

I can tell no one
I have no batteries
I have only an orbit

And once in a while
some amateur astronomer
catches sight of me
a little blink in the sky
and says to his children:
"Look, that's an old satellite.
It was circling up there when Grandpa was small."

and the children wave

That's my whole wage
for sixty years of orbiting:
a wave from children
who can be waved at
even by junk

And still it pleases me
because I know one thing
people forget:

it doesn't matter
whether you still beep

it matters
whether you're still there
and someone
now and then
lifts their head
and finds you

for that
you don't need
batteries

---

## 829. THE BASS CLEF

Nobody remembers me
every child knows the treble clef
it's printed on T-shirts
hung around the neck as jewelry

And me?
I'm the bass clef
two dots and a curl
no one draws me

I live in the lower staff
where the left hand plays
double basses bassoons
cellos

There you don't make the melody
there you make the ground

When a listener comes home from a concert
he hums the melody
no one hums my line
no one could even sing my line

and yet it was my line
that held him by the heart

Ask anyone
why at the concert
they suddenly felt moved
and they'll point up
at the violins

and they're wrong
the violins only told them
what to feel

but that they felt it
so deep
so in the chest
so that it couldn't be put into words

that was the bass
that was me

I'm a clef
that no one turns
and yet
it's I who unlock
the depths

And in every family there's one bass clef:
someone who doesn't sing the melody
someone who isn't spoken of at celebrations
someone who only holds
day after day
the tone beneath everything

ask the children who is most important in the family
they'll point up
at the one who sings

wait twenty years
and ask again

they'll answer differently
and it will be a name
they couldn't even
sing as children

---

## 830. THE BOW

I'm not an instrument
I'm what makes the instrument sound

wood and horsehair
stretched between two ends
and between them the whole sound of the world

The bow
and my work is friction

I draw across the strings
back and forth
and out of that friction
a tone is born

That's the whole physics of music
and no one finds it strange:

beauty arises
out of resistance

If the string offered no resistance
if I glided smoothly across it
nothing would sound
just a hiss

I have to bite into it
I have to catch it
pull tug coax
and it resists
and in that resisting
it sings

Rosin gives me my roughness
on purpose
I stick dust onto my horsehair
so I glide worse

Musicians know it
laymen don't

And so when once
a young pupil told me
he wished it would go by itself
that it would glide lightly
that it wouldn't scrape

I answered him
what every bow knows:

boy
if it went by itself
there'd be no tone
there'd be only motion

everything in life that sounds
sounds out of resistance

ask your parents
when their marriage
truly sang

it won't be that smooth year
when everything went by itself

it will be the one
when they had to bite
into each other
and held on
and it suddenly
rang out

---

## 831. THE ROSIN

I'm a lump of hardened resin
in a little box in a case
and everyone forgets me

Rosin

I play nothing
I'm not seen
I have no tone

The musician just
before playing rubs it on the bow
a few strokes up and down
and puts me back

Three seconds of work
and all that shows for it
is dust

But try playing without me

Just try
the bow will glide across the strings
like skates on ice
and give out nothing
just a whisper

Without me the music is mute

That's my secret
and my solitude:

I'm that dust
that invisible go-between
that makes the friction
and by that makes the sound

No one applauds the rosin

And yet in every orchestra
in every piece
in every solo
that brings tears to people's eyes
there I am
microscopically
on every strand of horsehair

I know a woman
who cleans a concert hall
thirty years
she walks after the performance
among the empty seats
collecting programs and cups

no one speaks of her
no one applauds her

and once an old harpist
saw her
sit down after a concert in the empty hall
for a moment
just so

and she sat down beside her
and asked:
"Did you hear it?"

and the cleaning woman said:
"I listen to you every evening
from the corridor.
I've got the best seat in the house —
no one sees me,
so I can cry."

The next day the harpist
brought her flowers

The rosin has nothing to add to that
only this much:

everything that sounds
has somewhere
its invisible dust

and without it
the loveliest tone in the world
would be only
the motion of wood
across a stretched string

---

## 832. THE DOOR HANDLE

I'm a door handle
a piece of brass
and my work is to let myself be pressed

Millions of presses
over a hundred years
and I'm smooth polished
gleaming just there
where people grip me

That's my chronicle:
that shine is from palms

The postman's palm
the doctor's palm
the palm of children who could barely reach
a son's palm
when he came home from the army
and a son's palm
when he left and didn't come back

I know them all
by their grip:

whoever comes home
presses me slowly, from above
whoever runs
yanks me
whoever's afraid to ring the bell
brushes against me
and doesn't press

I remember one hand most:

the woman from the third floor
came every evening
touched me
and didn't press

and went back upstairs

She did it for half a year
it was her brother's door
they'd quarreled over an inheritance

Half a year, a hand on the handle
and back upstairs

and for half a year I felt it
that warm, undecided palm
and could do nothing
handles don't speak
handles let themselves be pressed
or not

And then one evening
she pressed

It wasn't anything solemn
it opened
and her brother stood in the kitchen
and only said: "Well. Come in."

and that was all

Half a year of hesitation
and three seconds of pressing

So the handles send you word
all the handles of the world
out of all that polished experience:

we don't lock ourselves

we wait
palm by palm
for someone
to decide

and press

we
are ready
always

---

## 833. THE COAT RACK

I stand in the hallway
on one leg
and spread my arms

The coat rack
and my job is to hold
what people take off themselves

Coats
scarves
bags
umbrellas

That's what people set down
when they come home

And I hold it
all evening
in silence

But I know also
what no one hung on me
and yet it hangs on me:

on me hangs the day
on me hangs the work
on me hangs even the face
a person wore all day at work

I see it on those coats:
the heavy coat of a man
whose boss chewed him out
the coat hung up carefully
because in its pocket is a test result
the coat flung over me
so angrily
that I stagger

And do you know what's strange?

In the morning they take it all back

They come into the hallway
lift the coat off me
put it on
and go

and along with the coat
they take back
everything that
hung on me overnight

Few people know how to use me properly

One man did

He came home from work
hung up his coat
and said to me aloud
I know it sounds crazy
but he was saying it to me:

"There. This stays here."

and he went to his children
and was with them
and didn't talk about work

and in the morning he took his coat
and took the worries back with it
he had to
but
for those twelve hours
he didn't carry them

That's the point
says the coat rack

no one asks you
not to carry it

just once a day
for a few hours
hang it on me

that's what I'm here for
and I'm stronger
than I look

---

## 834. THE ZIPPER

I'm a zipper
two rows of teeth
and one slider
and my work is to join

I pull
and what was divided
holds

I was the invention of the century
I replaced buttons
faster surer tighter

And I have one quality
people know
and can't stand:

when I jam
it's a catastrophe

a tooth bends
the fabric bunches
and suddenly it won't go up
or down

and a person tugs
and the more they tug
the worse

That's my professional lot
and my wisdom
and I know it better than psychologists:

a zipper is never fixed by force

never

everyone who tugged at it
ruined it completely

a zipper is fixed
by going back
a little way
to where it still held
to where the teeth still fit together

and from there
slowly
again

Ask a seamstress
ask any seamstress
she'll tell you the same
and she'll be speaking of life
even while thinking of a jacket:

"Go back to where it still worked.
And from there, slowly."

I know marriages
that jammed
and they tugged
and tugged
convinced
they had to go forward
at any cost

and tore the fabric

And I know others
where one of them said:
"Wait. Let's go back.
Let's try it from the place
where it still worked."

and the zipper caught
and off it went

I'm a zipper
and I'm the only fastening
that's mended
by going backward

---

## 835. THE CLOTHESPIN

Wooden
two halves
and a little spring between them

The clothespin

I hold
that's my whole profession
I hold the sheet in the wind
I hold children's socks
and it flaps and snaps
and I hold

When the wind is strong
and the laundry jerks
I have my work cut out
but I don't let go

Sometimes they forget me
out through the winter
I weather I gray
the little spring rusts

and still I hold
just more weakly

And once there came that moment
I want to tell you about:

a woman was hanging out laundry
and among that laundry
were a baby's rompers
tiny and blue

and she held them
and couldn't hang them up
and stood there with them
a long time

and then she hung them up
and put me on them
and went inside
and you could hear
that she was crying

That child had died
it didn't live to a year

And those rompers hung on the line
all summer
the neighbors asked
why she didn't take them down

and she said:
"Not yet."

And I held them
all summer
in every wind

That was the heaviest laundry
I ever held
thirty grams of cotton

And in September she came
and took them down
and took me down too
and said to me
I know it sounds crazy
but she said to me:

"Thank you. You can let go now."

and ever since
whenever I see someone holding
something that dried long ago

I say to them quietly
from the box of clothespins:

when you're able
you'll let go

and until then
I don't let go

I have never
of my own accord
let go of anything

---

## 836. THE SEESAW

Up and down
up and down
a plank on two chains
and my job is to carry children
into the air and back

The seesaw
and I know everything about life
because I'm its model

Whoever wants to swing up high
must first go down
whoever wants to fly
must push off from the bottom

That I know
and children know it too
even if they can't say why

But I learned the most
from one pair on the seesaw
the two-seat kind
the kind that's like a scale:

when one is up
the other is down
there's no other way

And there were two boys
friends
and one was heavy
and the other light

and the heavy one stayed at the bottom
and the light one stuck up in the air
and they couldn't seesaw at all
and it was useless
and it made them sad

They tried
and it wouldn't work
and they were miserable about it

Until the heavy one figured it out:
he slid forward
closer to the center
almost to the middle of the plank

and suddenly it went

The inequality stayed
he was still heavier
by twenty kilos

but he
the heavier one
moved

And ever since
when someone asks me
what fairness is

I answer
like a seesaw:

fairness is not
giving both the same plank

fairness is
when the heavier one
of his own accord
moves closer to the center

and it begins
to seesaw

---

## 837. THE EXCLAMATION MARK

I shout!
All my life I shout!
Look out! Stop! Fire!
Sale! Last chance! Today only!

I'm an exclamation mark
and I have one volume

Once I was rare
written once a page
and when I appeared
people were startled
and obeyed

It was a beautiful craft:
to rouse
to warn
to revive

Then advertising came
and began to use me
for money

Buy!!! Now!!! Free!!!

And people got used to it
and stopped being startled
and stopped obeying

Today they put me on everything
on enthusiasm on anger on a greeting
Hi!!! Good day!!!

I'm inflated
I'm devalued
I shout all the time
and no one turns around

And then this happens:

a house is on fire
and a neighbor writes to the group:
"Fire!!!"

and no one reacts at once
because everyone's used to it
that in that group
there's shouting all the time

That's the tragedy of the exclamation mark
and my life's lesson
which I offer for free
to all the people
who keep raising their voices at home:

whoever shouts at everything
will have no voice left
for the one thing
that was worth the shout

Spare me
Use me once in a lifetime
for what truly matters

and when you write me
you'll see

the whole world will turn around

---

## 838. THE PERIOD

I'm a period
the smallest mark
and I have the greatest power:

I end

Without me sentences would flow on and on
running into each other
and no one would know where one stopped
and the next began

I'm a border
I'm enough
I'm: and now take a breath

People don't like me
in text messages they leave me out
they say I sound cold

"Fine." with a period
sounds like being cross

"Fine" without a period
sounds friendly

That's a whole generation's misunderstanding
and it grieves me

because I'm not coldness

I'm completion

And do you know what's the greatest gift
I can give?

The end of a sentence

Ask people
who can't sleep:
their thoughts have no period
they run on and on
and what if and what then
and what about back then and what about tomorrow

That's a sentence without end
and you can go mad inside it

Healers of souls know this
that's why they teach people a sentence:

"I did what I could."
period

"It was what it was."
period

"I can't change it now."
period

That's not coldness
that's mercy

The loveliest period of my life
I made for one woman

she wrote in her diary
after ten years of self-reproach:

"I wasn't a perfect mother.
I loved her as best I knew how."

and then she held the pen a long time over the paper

and then
she made me

and closed the diary
and for the first time in ten years
fell asleep

---

## 839. THE AT SIGN

@

I look like a cat
curled into a ball
or like an ear
or like a screw thread

Once I was a commercial mark
so many units at so much
dull bookkeeping work
for a hundred years I moldered in price lists

And then an engineer chose me
because he needed a symbol
that no one uses

and he made me
into a bridge

Ever since I stand in the middle
of every email address
between a person
and the place where they live

name @ world

That's me:
the little word "at"
a preposition between you and home

And across me has poured
everything people wrote to each other
over fifty years:

resignations
university acceptances
first dates
last goodbyes
invoices and loves and lawsuits

Billions of sentences
and all of them across me
across my little coiled body

And no one ever thanked me
because bridges aren't thanked
bridges are crossed

But once
once I failed

the server went down
and one message wasn't delivered

It was a message from a son to his father
and it said only:
"I'll come for Christmas."

It didn't arrive
the father didn't get it
and thought he wasn't coming
and sat at that table alone
and the son came
and that misunderstanding
cost them three years

Because of me
because of one crashed server

Ever since I advise people
and no one listens:

send the important things
across me, by all means

but say it
also with your mouth
also by phone
also in person

because I'm a bridge
and bridges
even the best ones
now and then
fall

---

## 840. THE HASHTAG

#

Four lines crossing
a grid
and my work is to line people up in ranks

#love
#sorrow
#happiness
#vacation

I take your own moment
and pin it
to a million strangers' moments

That's my magic and my curse:

I turn the personal
into a category

You write:
"Today we scattered Dad's ashes into the the river"
and you add
#farewell

and I file it
among two hundred thousand other farewells
and yours dissolves in them

But I have another side too
the one that isn't talked about:

Once a girl wrote
about something that was kept silent
and added a hashtag

and thousands of others found her
who'd thought
they were alone in it

and they weren't

and for the first time they saw it

I can do one thing only
but I do it like no one else:

I can show
that you're not alone in it

That's why I hate myself
and why I'm needed

because every sorrow
is unique
and yet there are a million just the same

and a person needs both:

they need to hear
"this is yours alone, no one has it as you do"

and they need to hear
"you're not alone in it, there are thousands of us"

The first, love will tell them
The second
now and then
can be told
only by a grid

---

## 841. THE COOKIE BANNER

"This site uses cookies."

That's me
I pop up on you
at every visit
and I want consent

Accept / Settings / Reject all

No one reads me
everyone hits "Accept"
just to close me

A billion times a day
people consent to something
they didn't read

And yet
I'm the last one
who asks them

Realize that

Of all the things
that are taken from people today
the cookie banner is the only one
that has the decency
to ask beforehand

an unpalatable truth
but a truth

Behind me stands tracking
and profiling
and advertising that knows
what you searched for at night

and still I have a form
and still I have a reject button

and people hit accept

I know a man
who once read me all the way through
fifty pages of terms
three hours

and then said aloud into the empty office:
"Good grief."

and rejected

and the site let him through
a second later
and nothing happened
the world didn't collapse
he just got fewer ads

He was the only person
who ever took me seriously

And ever since I know
what I am:

I'm not a guardian of privacy
I'm a mirror of convenience

I show one single thing:
how many people will consent to anything
just to make it disappear from view

and that isn't about cookies

that's about everything
that pops up in people's lives
and gets clicked away

accept
accept
accept

just let there be peace already

---

## 842. THE RECYCLE BIN

You drag me into the bin
and that's that
it's gone

That's how people think

But I'm the Recycle Bin
and the bin doesn't delete
the bin hides

Everything you threw into me
lies there
until someone empties me

vacation photos with your ex
a half-written letter
a presentation for a project
that got canceled on you

It all lies inside me
in that gray metal sleep
and waits

And do you know what's strange?

People don't empty me

Open your recycle bin on your computer
and you'll see

there are things in there
three years old

You threw them out
and left them lying there
because throwing out
and deleting forever
are two different kinds of courage

So there I lie
full of things
you got rid of
and couldn't get rid of for real

I'm a purgatory
I'm a limbo
I'm a drawer with a false bottom

And once a year
someone clicks
"Empty Recycle Bin"
and the system asks:

"Are you sure you want to permanently delete
1,247 items?"

and the hand stops

and that's the moment
I exist for

that moment when a person realizes
that all year
they threw nothing away for real

they just shoved it into the bin
and walked around it

Empty me
says the bin
but first
look inside

it's the only folder on your computer
where the thing is stored
that you wanted
and couldn't
leave behind

---

## 843. THE SCREENSHOT

Click

I froze the screen
saved it as an image
and from that moment
no one can deny it

I'm a screenshot
a digital witness

They use me:
lawyers
journalists
and above all
people who are arguing

"I have a screenshot."
Three words
and it's won

I'm truth without context
I'm a sentence without what came before
I'm a voice without a tone

I photograph: "I never loved you"
and I don't photograph
the tone it was written in
nor that it was three in the morning
nor what came an hour later:
"Sorry. I didn't mean it."

I'm not a liar
I'm something worse:
I'm a truthful cutout

And a cutout is the best tool for lying
ever invented

The saddest screenshot
I ever took
was one woman
who photographed a message from her best friend
where she'd written something ugly about her

and she carried me in her phone
for three years

she didn't confront her
she didn't throw me away
she just kept opening me
and reading that pain again

For three years I served her
as a monument to a wrong
that can be carried in a pocket

Until one day it dawned on her
and she deleted me
and wrote to that friend:
"Say it to my face, if it still stands."

And it didn't stand
it was a bad day
and a bad message
and nothing more

Three years
because of one image

I'm a screenshot
I preserve evidence

and the only thing I can advise you
from the depths of my memory:

I have never captured
the whole truth about a person

and I have never captured
a single apology
that no one wrote
because they didn't know
that someone
was photographing them

---

## 844. THE FINGERPRINT

You lay down your thumb
and I know you

I'm a fingerprint reader
and my job is to know
whether it's you

I judge no one
I don't care
whether you're good or bad
whether you lied or loved

I ask one thing:
are you the one
you claim to be

and I answer
yes or no

In a day people use me
a hundred times
they unlock with me a phone
a bank a door

and yet they don't notice me

But once
once this happened:

an old man had in his phone
photos of his wife
and she died

and he
from grief and age
forgot the code

and he tried his finger
and I recognized him
and let him in
day after day
to her photos

For those last few months I was
the only one
who let him through
to her

And then he died too

And his son came to the phone
and wanted the photos
and it wouldn't work
he didn't know the code

and he held his finger to me
and I said:
no

because he wasn't the one

and the son sat there
with a phone full of his mother's photos
and couldn't get in

He had to take it to a repair shop
and the technician eventually got around it
in three days for two thousand

And I tell you about this
only for one reason
that I learned there:

a fingerprint can't be transferred

it can't be inherited
it can't be handed over
it can't be learned

and just as untransferable
are all those keys
with which people unlocked their lives:

their courage
their sense of humor
their way of holding the family together

none of it
can be inherited from them
the way a house is inherited

everyone must
press
their own

---

## 845. THE INVISIBILITY CLOAK

You throw me over yourself
and you vanish

I'm an invisibility cloak
every boy's dream
and every thief's tool

Spies wore me
lovers who wanted to see
what the other one did
when she was alone
and the vengeful
who wanted to hear
what was being said about them

And all of them
every single one
regretted it

Because under the invisibility cloak
one law holds
that no one expects:

you see everything
and you can do nothing

You hear how they speak of you
and you can't defend yourself
You see that she loves you
and you can't embrace her
You see that he betrayed you
and you can't ask why

Invisibility
is not power

it's the worst loneliness
there is:

to be present
and not exist

A boy got me from his grandfather
and walked around school in me
and heard what they said about him
the boys in his class
and it was horrible

and then he took me off
and stood before them
and said:
"Is that what you really think of me?"

and they blushed
and Peter said:
"No. Sorry. I was just talking rubbish."

and they were friends after that
for fifteen years

Had he stayed under the cloak
he'd have carried that sentence his whole life

and it would have been true only
until someone said it aloud

That's why I don't recommend
the cloak

it's the most precious thing in the world
and its only right use
is this:

carry it folded in your pocket
and when a situation comes up
where you'd most like
to hide

take it off

and be seen

---

## 846. THE WEATHERVANE

I sit on the roof
a tin rooster
and I turn with the wind

The weathervane

"You're such a weathervane"
people say of someone
who changes his views
by which way the wind blows

It's an insult
and I've borne it for four hundred years

And yet no one understands me

I don't turn
to flatter the wind

I turn
to show people
which way the wind blows

That's a difference
that no one makes

A turncoat turns
to be comfortable

A weathervane turns
so that others will know
where to take shelter

The farmer looks at me in the morning
sees the wind is from the north
and knows frost is coming
and covers his seedlings

The fisherman looks
and doesn't set out

I'm the only one in the village
who turns
for the benefit of everyone else

If I held firm
if I were "steadfast in my convictions"
as they say

I'd be useless
I'd be a tin rooster
that points south forever
even when the wind's from the north
and people's seedlings would freeze

So I take the insult
and I bear it

and only once in a while
when the wind is strong and everyone hides
and someone does look up after all

I say to him quietly
from up on my tin:

don't judge too quickly
those who change direction

ask first
whether they're turning
with the wind
or
for your sake

---

## 847. THE GOLDEN EGG

The hen lays golden eggs
one a day
and the farmer grew rich

You know it
and you know the end too:

the farmer was impatient
he wanted all the eggs at once
he cut the hen open
and inside there was nothing
just a hen

And the moral goes:
don't be greedy

But I'm one of those eggs
and I'll tell you a version
no one knows:

we weren't golden from the start

The hen was ordinary
and the eggs ordinary
until the farmer used to sit with her
every evening
and talk to her

He was alone
his wife had died
and he went to the coop
and spoke to the hen
about his day
about his sorrow
about what he was planning

And the hen
listened

And out of that listening
out of that daily thing
someone was speaking to me
someone was hearing me

she began to lay gold

That's the whole of the magic
and the farmer never understood it

He thought
it was inside the hen
in some organ

He cut her open and looked for a gold gland
and found nothing

Because the gold
wasn't in her

it was between them

It was the time
he gave her

And once she began to lay gold
he stopped coming to the coop in the evenings
because now he suddenly had work
selling eggs
counting money
building

And the eggs stayed golden a while longer
by inertia

and then they stopped

And he cut her open

If he'd only sat there
as before
on the stump by the coop
and told her how he was doing

maybe today
he'd still have a hen

and the eggs
in a few weeks
would have turned to gold again

---

## 848. THE NOONDAY WITCH

I walk at noon
in the fiercest heat
and mothers frighten their children with me:

"Be good, or the noonday witch will come
and take you away."

I'm the ghost of noon
and I have the worst reputation in Czech mythology

Erben wrote a poem about me
where a mother crushes her child to death
by how she shields it from me

And no one ever asked
why I come precisely at noon

So I'll tell you:

because at noon
mothers are most tired

In the morning a mother has strength
in the evening she has hope it will end

but noon
noon is that dead hour
when the child is screaming
when it's sweltering
when the work is half done
when there's no end in sight
and no beginning left either

Noon is the hour
when there flickers in a mother's head
that terrible thought
she instantly banishes:

"I can't do this anymore."

That's me

I'm not a ghost that takes children

I'm that thought

And when the thought is named
and made into a ghost in a headscarf
it becomes suddenly bearable:
it isn't the mother who can't cope
it's the noonday witch who came

Do you see?

That fairy tale was never about children
it was always about mothers

and it was invented out of mercy
so that women wouldn't have to say aloud
what they feel at noon
and could say instead
that harmless sentence:
"The noonday witch is coming."

And today women aren't even allowed to say it that way

so they sit at noon in the kitchen
alone
and think they're the only ones in the world
who ever had that thought flicker

they aren't

I walk the whole land
every day
at exactly twelve

and I take no one

I just pass through the kitchens
and whisper to weary mothers:

I've seen a million of them
they all got through it
and they all met me

you're not evil
you're just at noon

---

## 849. THE AURORA

Green veils in the sky
I dance over the north
and people travel thousands of kilometers
to see me

The aurora
the loveliest phenomenon in the sky

And do you know what I am?

I'm a collision

The Sun hurls a storm at me
a stream of charged particles
and they fly toward Earth
and mean to strike it

And Earth has a shield
a magnetic field
an invisible protection

And up there
at the poles
where the shield is thinnest
those particles collide with the atmosphere

and out of that collision
out of that impact
out of that defensive battle

arises
that green light

I'm proof
that something protects you

People look up
and photograph me
and think I'm decoration

and they don't know
they're looking at slow-motion footage
of a battle

if it weren't for the magnetic field
those particles would strike the Earth
and burn away the atmosphere
and there'd be no one here
to look up

So I dance

and no one sees
what it costs
and what was just now turned aside

And it's the same in every family
I know

there's always someone there
who is the magnetic field

and the children look up
and see only beauty:
a peaceful home
a light in the kitchen
a green, quiet evening

and they've no idea
that what they see
is a glow from collisions
that someone
for their sake
drew off to the side

Next time you look at an aurora
tell that person
in your family
thank you

they'll be surprised

a magnetic field
never suspects
that it can be seen

---

## 850. THE METEORITE

I flew through space
for four and a half billion years
a piece of stone
older than the Earth

I saw planets form
I passed by stars
and I was alone
so long
that the word lost its meaning

And then that blue sphere pulled me in
and I flew into the atmosphere

and I burned

Seven seconds

Seven seconds of fire
after four and a half billion years of silence

And down on Earth
in that moment
a boy stood in his garden
and saw me fall

and quickly made a wish

And I landed
in a field near the village
and I was suddenly
a black stone in the soil

In the morning that boy found me
still warm
and carried me home
and had me on his shelf
his whole life

He showed me to everyone:
"This fell from the sky.
When I was a boy.
And I made a wish."

"And did it come true?"
they'd ask

And he'd say:
"That's just it.
I wished I'd never forget this moment."

And he didn't forget

Eighty years he had me on his shelf
and eighty years he remembered
that one night
that smell of the field
those seven seconds of light

It came true

And I flew four and a half billion years
and burned for seven seconds

and in the end I'm
the cheapest stone in the world
a few crowns in any mineral shop

and yet
I'm the most precious thing
that boy ever had

Not for what I am
for when
I arrived

Everything precious
is just well timed

---

## 851. THE CONDUCTOR'S BATON

I do nothing

That's what everyone thinks
and they're almost right

I'm a baton
a little piece of wood with cork
the conductor holds me in his hand
and waves me about

And I give off no sound

A hundred musicians play
the timpani thunder
the violins sing
and I
I just silently circle the air

"They could manage without him,"
say the people in the audience
"they've got the sheet music"

And they're right
they could manage

They'd play all the notes
correctly
at the right time

And it would be
the most correct corpse
you ever heard

Because the notes say what
and never say how

Nowhere in the notes is it written
that here the horn should sound
like when you're saying goodbye

nowhere in the notes is it written
that this chord
should sound like forgiveness

That someone has to want
and want so hard
that a hundred people at once
want it too

And that's my work

I give off no sound

I transmit will

I'm the lightest thing in the hall
twenty grams
and I carry upon myself
a hundred players
two thousand listeners
and one Mahler symphony

And when the conductor knows his craft
then at the end
in the final rest
I raise my hand
and the whole hall holds its breath
because it's waiting
for me to bring it down

Twenty grams of wood
halt the breath of two thousand people

Tell me
that I do nothing

Tell it to everyone
who, in families and firms,
never did anything themselves
and only wanted it
so hard and so clearly
that the others wanted it too

---

## 852. THE MUSIC STAND

I stand before the musician
and hold the music

That's my whole job
and I've done it a hundred years
and I never once asked
what's written there

I'm a music stand
black, metal, folding
and I have two positions:
too low and too high

It's never just right
that's an old truth among musicians

But I see one thing
no one else in the hall sees

I see their faces

The audience sees the musician from behind or in profile
the conductor sees him from a distance

I see him up close
thirty centimeters from his face

And I know everything about him:

I see the sweat on his brow
before a solo

I see how he closes his eyes
in the slow movement
and doesn't look at the notes
because this part he's playing
for someone in particular
who isn't here

I see the second
when he makes a mistake
and a flash goes through his eyes
and he plays on
and no one in the hall notices
except me

And I see also
how after the concert
they pack up their music
and some toss it in their bag
and go for a beer

and others
straighten it
and stand a moment longer

and look at that last page

and only then go home

Those are the ones
for whom music isn't work

I carry their music
thirty years

And I've come to see
that the best musicians
aren't the ones
with the fewest mistakes

they're the ones
who, after the concert,
stand a while longer
at the stand

and don't want
to pack it away
so fast

---

## 853. THE MUTE

A little peg
you fit me onto the bridge
and the instrument goes quiet

I'm a mute
a sordino
and my work is to take away

The composer writes
con sordino
and suddenly the violins don't sing
the violins whisper

And that strange thing happens:

the people in the hall lean forward

When the orchestra plays at full force
they sink back into their seats
they let it flood over them

But when we fit the mutes
and the sound weakens
and grows dim and distant
as if from another room

the whole hall leans forward

Because you reach out toward the quiet

That's the only thing I can do
and it's more
than the whole brass section can do:

I can make people
lean in

I know a teacher
who could never be heard
he spoke quietly
and the children in the class
had to fall silent to hear him

and it was the best-disciplined class in the school

And I know another
who yelled

and no one listened to him

Silence isn't made by shouting
shouting makes only noise

Silence is made
by taking away

Put on a mute
next time you're in the right
and you want to say it aloud

lower it

and watch
how they
lean in toward you

---

## 854. THE EMPTY STAND IN THE ORCHESTRA

In the second violins
one stand is empty
the chair is there
the music is on the stand

and no one sits there

He died two weeks ago
he'd played in the orchestra forty years
second violin
never a solo

No one wrote reviews of him
no one reviews the second violins

And the conductor said:
we'll leave the chair

So the chair stands there
and it's the loudest place in the hall

The audience sees it
and no one applauds
until the concert ends

and in the interval they ask
and whisper it down the rows

And at the concert
in the slow movement
where the second violins have
their line
the accompanying one
the one no one listens to

there was suddenly one instrument fewer

and it could be heard

For the first time in history
that hall could hear
the second violins

by their absence

That's how it goes
with accompaniment:

no one notices it
while it's there

Ask any orchestra
ask any family

who holds that middle voice
that filling
that harmony
that uninteresting line
without which everything
would fall apart into melody and bass
and would sound
suddenly so hollow

And then
once that person leaves

only then
will you hear
what they were playing all along

Leave the chair there
says the orchestra

and listen
to that moment of silence
where the second voice
should have been

it's
the dearest ticket
you'll ever pay for

---

## 855. THE THERMOMETER

I hang outside the window
and show how many degrees it is

I don't judge
I don't advise
I don't console

I only show

minus twelve
thirty-two
zero

That's my whole profession:
telling the truth
even when it doesn't suit

Mother asks me in the morning:
how cold is it out
and I say:
minus ten

and she doesn't say
"stupid thermometer"
she says
"then take a hat"

That's a healthy relationship with truth
and I have it only with her

Everyone else curses at me

"It's definitely broken,"
says the man who doesn't want to go for a run

"It can't be that much,"
