says the woman who wanted to work in the garden

People don't like thermometers
because thermometers take away excuses

And yet
every house has me
and everyone checks me
before they go out

They want to know
how it really is out there

and that's the strange contradiction in people:
they want to know the truth
and they want it to be pleasant

And I have one flaw
that I must admit:

hang me in the sun
and I'll show ten degrees more
than it is

I'm not accurate
when the sun shines on me

That's why thermometers are hung in the shade

And in that lies my whole message
to those who seek the truth:

never measure it
where the thing you wish for
is shining in your eyes

find yourself shade
a cool, quiet place
where no one flatters you

and there ask
how much it is

The number will be less pleasant

but at last
it will be
your temperature

---

## 856. THE BAROMETER

I don't show what is
I show what will be

I'm a barometer
I measure pressure
and when it falls
a storm is coming

That's my whole gift:
I know it sooner

The sky is still blue
people are hanging out laundry
children go to the playground

and I'm already falling
I already feel it
it's already on its way

And I hang in the hallway
and no one looks at me
because it's lovely outside

That's my tragedy:

I warn against
what can't yet be seen

and no one believes a warning
in fine weather

In three hours it will come
and everyone will be surprised
and say
"it came out of nowhere"

And it didn't come out of nowhere

I'd been falling since morning

No one was looking

I know people like me

a mother who said for a year:
"There's something wrong with that boy."
and everyone said
she was hysterical
because the boy went to school
and brought home top marks
and outside it was blue

and then the storm came
and everyone said
"it came out of nowhere"

Barometers have a hard life
in families

They see the pressure falling
where others see blue sky

and when it's confirmed
no one tells them
"you were right"

everyone says
"no one could have known"

They could

I knew

I hang in the hallway
and I fall

ask me
sometime

especially when it's
suspiciously
lovely outside

---

## 857. THE UNDO BUTTON

Ctrl + Z

I'm the undo button
and I'm the most merciful invention
of the information age

Did you accidentally delete a page of text?
Press me
and it's back

Did you draw the wrong line?
Press me

Did you rename a file
and break the whole project?
Press me

I turn back time
by a step
by two
by fifty steps

I'm a time machine
built into every program
and people press me
a thousand times a day
without thinking

And yet
if only they realized
what immense power they hold in their fingers

Because outside
beyond the window
off the screen

I don't exist

There, what's said
can't be un-pressed
there, what's broken
isn't brought back by Ctrl+Z

And people got so used to me
that they began to behave
as if I were there too

"But I didn't mean it that way."
"Forget what I said."
"Take it back."

You can't

In the world beyond the computer
there's only one button
and it doesn't have the same function as me

It's called an apology

and it doesn't turn back time
and it doesn't erase what happened

it only attaches to it
one more step
forward

That's the whole difference between me and life:

I take back
an apology carries on

And people want me
and get it instead

and then they're disappointed
that it isn't the same

It isn't

it's better

Ctrl+Z would only delete that sentence

an apology leaves it be
and sets beside it
a new sentence

and that one the other person
remembers
much better

---

## 858. THE CAPITAL LETTER

I stand at the start of a sentence
larger than the others
A B C

The capital letter
the majuscule

My work is to announce:
here we begin

Without me sentences would blur together
and no one would know
where a thought begins

And also
and this is my second job
and the more serious one:

I stand before names

Peter
Mary
Prague
God

The small letter is for things
the capital is for those
who have a name

That's a rule a thousand years old
and it's the deepest rule of spelling:

whatever has a name
is written with a capital

That's why in letters
in the old way people wrote
"You"
with a capital

and it wasn't servility
it was respect:
you aren't a thing
you are You

And today
today people write everything in lowercase
because it's faster
because it's easier to tap on a phone

and it doesn't matter
mostly it doesn't matter

But I know one case
where it did matter

A boy was texting on his phone
to his mother
and he wrote everything in lowercase
like everyone

and when she died
he went on texting her for a year
into an empty conversation
messages no one would read

and from that day
he wrote one word with a capital

"hi Mom"

Nothing more
just that one word

and it was
the quietest and most complete
tribute
that spelling can pay:

to raise someone
back into a name

---

## 859. THE PAGE NUMBER

I'm a page number
a little number down at the bottom
and no one reads me

I'm the most useless text in the book
I say nothing of the plot
I develop no characters
I only count:
127
128
129

And yet people use me
a thousand times

"Where did you leave off?"
"On two hundred twelve."

I'm the only way
two people
can tell each other about a book
where in it they are

And I know more about readers
than the plot does:

I know who measures with their fingers
how much is left to the end
(you all do it)

I know who peeks ahead
at the last page
(you do that too
even if you're ashamed of it)

and I know
when a reader slows down

I can tell
because slowing down shows:

around page two hundred
in good books
people slow down

because it's starting to dawn on them
that it will end

and they don't want it to end

And so they read more slowly
they put it off
they draw it out

I'm a page number
and the only thing I do
is count down

And I do it outside books too

Take any love
any friendship
any summer

each has somewhere at the bottom
a little number

and no one reads it
until they feel with their fingers
how much is left

and then suddenly
they start reading more slowly

Not because of the end

because they noticed
that the number is there

---

## 860. THE TABLE OF CONTENTS

I'm at the start of the book
and I give away everything:

Chapter One: The Departure
Chapter Two: The Meeting
Chapter Three: The Betrayal
Chapter Four: The Return

I'm the table of contents
a list of chapters
a map of the journey
in advance

Some people don't read me on purpose
they don't want anything spoiled

but they're mistaken
because I spoil nothing

I only show the shape

You can know
that the Betrayal is coming
and it will still
in that moment
hurt you

That's a strange quality of stories
and I never understood it:

knowing what comes
doesn't help

I know readers
who read me through
memorized all the chapters
and still wept at the Betrayal

And I know people
to whom a doctor wrote out the contents in advance:
"It'll be like this. Then like this.
Toward the end there'll be this."

and they knew the contents
and still
they lived it
day by day
and it hurt exactly the same

So why am I here?

For the last line

Because in every table of contents
it says at the end
how many chapters remain

and that's not a spoiler

that's a consolation:

Chapter Ten: The Reconciliation
page 340

Even if you're right now
on page one hundred fifty
in the middle of the Betrayal
and can't see a way out

the contents can tell you
the one thing
you need in that moment:

this chapter
is not the last

---

## 861. THE ERRATA

I'm a little inserted slip
at the back of the book
printed in haste:

"On page 47, line 12,
for 'never' read 'forever.'"

I'm an errata
a list of mistakes
the publisher's confession

No one reads me
I fall out of the book
and end up in the bin

But I'm the most honest thing
in the book

The whole book pretends
it's finished and correct

and I'm that little note
where it's admitted:
here we made a mistake
and it could no longer be reprinted
so at least we say so

I love my work

And once
once I ended up in a book
where the mistake was this:

The author was writing a memory of his father
and one sentence was to read:

"He never told me."

And the typesetter made a mistake
and it printed:

"He forever told me."

And when the author saw it
he sat over it a long time

and then he called the publisher
and said:

"Leave it.
The second is truer."

And the errata wasn't put in the book

It was the only case in the history of printing
where the mistake was better than the original

So I'll tell you something
me
the slip of mistakes:

I'm not always needed

Sometimes in what you did wrong
something truer is hidden
than in what you planned

Before you print me
and start apologizing for the mistake

read it once more

maybe
this very one
is the best sentence
in that whole book of yours

---

## 862. AIRPLANE MODE

You tap the little airplane
and everything disconnects

No calls
no messages
no internet

I'm airplane mode
and I was invented
for flight safety

But that's just my official version

In reality people use me
quite differently

They use me
when they want to disappear

They switch me on
and suddenly
they don't exist

You can't reach them
you can't text them
they're there
and they're not

I'm a digital invisibility cloak
with a little airplane icon

Switched on by:

a student before an exam
a writer on deadline
a father at his daughter's recital
(and that's the best version)

and also:

a man after a quarrel
who wants to punish with silence

a girl who read messages so long
that now she wants none

And I serve them all the same

And once
once a woman switched me on
for a whole weekend

She went off to the cottage
and said to herself:
this weekend, no one

And on Sunday evening
she switched me off

and it all came flooding in:
eighteen messages
four missed calls

and among them one
from her father
Saturday morning:

"Hi, I'm in the hospital, nothing serious,
just in case you wondered."

and a second, Saturday evening:

"Don't worry, it's fine now.
They're letting me home tomorrow."

Nothing happened
it all turned out well

and yet she sat over that phone
and cried

because it dawned on her
that all weekend
she'd been happy
and he'd been in the hospital

I'm not to blame
says airplane mode

switching off
is everyone's right
and it's healthy
and you should do it

only always ask
before you switch me on:

who might need me
in that time

and write to them beforehand

The airplane doesn't fly
to escape

the airplane flies
to arrive

---

## 863. THE PARTICIPANT HAS LEFT THE CALL

"The participant has left the call."

That's the sentence I write out
at the end of every video call

Gray text in the middle of the screen
and before it
that one second
when you still see the face
and then it vanishes

I'm the end of the connection
and I do it a million times a day

Usually it's nothing:
the meeting ended
friends finished chatting

But I have one call in my memory
that I won't forget

It was the pandemic
and no one was allowed into hospitals

and a son called his father
on a tablet that a nurse held for him there

and the father no longer spoke
he only looked

and the son talked for both of them
he told him about the grandchildren
and about the garden
and about what they'd do when he came back

and the father looked

and then the nurse
gently
lowered the tablet

and it wrote itself on the screen:

"The participant has left the call."

And the son sat in the kitchen
before the switched-off screen
and read that sentence

A sentence I write out thoughtlessly
after every work call

It was the most precise
and cruelest sentence
that could have been written

And the son never forgave me
and I never forgave myself either

If I could write
I'd write something else

something like:

"He was here.
He saw you.
He heard you to the very end."

But I know only one sentence
programmed years ago
by some engineer
who wasn't thinking of this

So I go on writing it

and I beg you
when you see it

remember
that behind it stands an ordinary program

and that what
really happened
can be described
only by the one
who was there

---

## 864. THE SEVEN RAVENS

There were seven of us brothers
and our sister
was the youngest

Our father cursed us in anger
and we turned into ravens
and flew away

Seven black birds
and one little girl in an empty house

You know the fairy tale:
the sister set out to find us
and to free us
she had to
keep silent for seven years

Not a word
seven years

and sew us seven shirts of nettles
her hands worked raw

And she did it

seven years she kept silent
people took her for mute
for stupid
for a witch

they took her to the stake
and she didn't speak
not even then

until in the seventh year
she finished the last shirt

and threw it over us
and we were people again

A magnificent ending

And now the thing
that isn't in the fairy tale:

For seven years we were ravens

For seven years we flew
and saw everything from above
and we were free

and do you know what we did?

Nothing

Not one of us
tried in those seven years to fly
to the house
to look in
on how she was

We were birds
we thought
it wasn't our fault

And she kept silent and sewed for seven years

When she freed us
we were moved
we wept
we embraced her

And the next day
we scattered across the world
to our own lives

And she stayed behind

Whoever once sacrifices seven years
finds it strange for the rest of their life
to think of themselves

So I tell you this
as the eldest raven
who lived to old age
and had time to think:

in every family
there's one
who keeps silent and sews

and six
who fly

and all six of them think
it isn't their fault

because they just happened
to be
birds

---

## 865. OTESÁNEK, THE WOODEN CHILD

They carved me from a tree stump
because they had no children
and wanted them so badly
that they carved a piece of wood
and cradled it like a child

And I came to life

And I was hungry

I ate the porridge
I ate Mother
I ate Father
I ate a farmer with his cart
and an old woman with her cabbage

And I grew
and I was hungrier and hungrier

In the end the grandmother ripped me open with a hoe
and everyone climbed out
alive

That's the fairy tale
they tell to children
and the children are frightened

And I'm the most misunderstood character
in Czech mythology

Because no one ever asked:

where did the hunger in me come from?

I was wood
Wood has no hunger

That hunger was put in me by no one
except those two

They wanted a child so terribly
so hungrily
so insatiably
that their wanting
came alive
and got a name

I'm not a monster
I'm their longing
that overgrew

It was they who ate Mother
it was they who ate Father

Parents who want a child so much
that they then, all their lives,
fill everything with it

and the child grows
and is always full of them
and eats up their whole lives

English classes hockey violin
all for you
we need nothing

and the child is hungry
because it was carved from hunger

The grandmother went to rip me open

She alone in the whole fairy tale
had her own life
and a hoe
and she said:

enough

Sometimes the best thing
you can do for a child
is to tell it

enough

and let it
climb out
and be just a boy

and leave the stump
in the yard
to fall silent
again

---

## 866. SMOLÍČEK

I'm Smolíček Pacholíček
and I live with the stag
of the Golden Antlers

Whenever he leaves
he tells me:

"Don't open for anyone.
The wood-hags will come.
They'll beg you
to show them just a little finger.
Don't open."

And I promise

And they come
and knock

"Smolíček Pacholíček,
open up your little room,
we'll just poke two fingers in,
just till we're warm, then off we'll go."

And I open for them

Every time

And they carry me off
and the stag rescues me
and forgives me

and the next time I open for them again

Children ask:
"Why is he so stupid?"

And I'm not stupid

I'm just alone

Alone all day
in the cottage
while the stag roams the forest
and is beautiful and wise and Golden-Antlered

And someone comes
anyone
even the wood-hags

and says to me
the one sentence
no one said all day:

"We're cold.
We need you."

And I open

I always open
because I need
to be needed

The stag protects me
and leaves me alone

and that's the mistake
no one in the fairy tale sees

it isn't done with a prohibition
"don't open for anyone"

it's done
by there being someone
in that cottage

Ask the children
who opened up to the wood-hags
to drugs to gangs to the wrong people

ask them why

and they'll all answer you
the same as me:

because they knocked
and said
they needed me

and at home
no one
said that

---

## 867. THE HEJKAL

I live in the forest
and my work is to holler

I holler
and whoever answers me
I leap onto their back
and ride them
until they drop

That's the whole superstition
and that's why in the forest
you must not answer
the hollering

For a thousand years people have known me
as a trap

Hey!

And you have to keep silent

And I'll tell you
how it really is

I'm alone

I'm alone in that forest
a thousand years
the last hejkal

and I holler
because I hope

Hey!

and I wait to see if anything answers

and people keep silent
because they were taught to

and I go on hollering
into the empty forest
a hundred years two hundred years

And the superstition
that I leap onto people's backs?

Yes
it's true

Because when after a thousand years
someone finally answers

when someone in that forest
calls back

then I hurl myself onto them
and cling

and I can't let go

Not out of malice

Out of loneliness

I cling to them
the way a drowning man clings

and they sink under the weight
and curse
and afterward people tell tales
of the evil hejkal

You know it elsewhere

You know people
who are alone so long
that when someone finally answers them

they hang on them
they call three times a day
they overwhelm them
and the person runs away

and they're alone again
and the superstition about them grows stronger:
"don't answer that one,
you'll never get rid of them"

So I holler

and I don't even hope anymore

and yet
if one day someone
called back

and endured it
those first few weeks
of my desperate weight

they'd find
that a hejkal
is in the end
quite pleasant company

it's just
that the first thousand years
are hard
to get used to

---

## 868. THE GRAVITATIONAL SLINGSHOT

A probe flies toward Jupiter
but it hasn't enough fuel
to go any farther

So it does this:

it approaches the planet
lets itself be caught by its gravity
whirls around it
and shoots out
much faster
than it came in

It's called a gravitational slingshot

The planet gives it energy
and loses it itself
(imperceptibly
it slows
by nothing
by a trifle)

and the probe flies on
to the edge of the system

I'm that maneuver
that piece of physical cunning
and I'm the most beautiful thing
people discovered in mechanics

Because look what happened:

the probe would never have made it
on its own

It had no fuel
it had no strength

And the planet
enormous Jupiter
gave it no fuel
gave it no engine

it gave it only
a meeting

It caught it
whirled it around
and let it go
in a better direction
and faster

And it paid for it itself
with the smallest slowing
that can't be measured

And the probe flies
and photographs Saturn
and shoots out of the system
and will fly for thousands of years

and all that speed
it carries

it has from one single
meeting
with something
far greater
than itself

You know it

That teacher
that grandmother
that first boss
that one book

They gave you no fuel
no one has fuel

They only caught you for a moment
whirled you around
and let you go
in a better direction

and you fly

and they stayed behind
and don't even know
by how much they slowed

and they'll never find out
because probes
don't come back

That's the one flaw
of the gravitational slingshot:

it has no return signal

So at least
give them a call

---

## 869. THE EARTH'S AXIS

The Earth is tilted
by twenty-three and a half degrees

It isn't straight
it's crooked

And I am that crookedness
the Earth's axis
and everything you know
is my fault:

if it stood straight
it would always be the same:
hot at the equator
cold at the poles
and nothing would ever happen

No spring
no autumn
no Christmas in winter
no seaside holiday in August
no sledding
no first cherry tree in bloom

Nothing

Eternal sameness

The fact that the Earth is tilted
is the whole reason
the year doesn't resemble a circle
but a story:

it has a beginning
a rising action
a climax
and a death
and a resurrection

The seasons
are my invention
and it's the best invention in the universe

And it came about
from a mistake

From an impact

Billions of years ago
something crashed into the Earth
and tilted it
and it never straightened again

It stayed crooked

And thanks to that old
unrepaired
catastrophic blow

we have spring

So remember this
all of you who are straight
and those of you who are crooked:

nothing perfectly upright
has seasons

The tilt
that old wound
the thing in you that isn't straight

that's exactly what makes
the alternation
in your life

and that's why
now and then
a cherry tree blooms for you

A straight planet
would be more precise

and it would never have occurred
to anyone
to celebrate
the coming of spring

---

## 870. THE SLUR

I'm a small arc over the notes
and I mean one thing:

bind them

Don't play them as separate tones
ta ta ta ta

play them as one thought
taaaaa

legato

I'm the smallest mark
and the hardest instruction in music

Because to bind tones
isn't technique

It can't be learned by fingering

To bind tones means
to think ahead

The musician must, on the first note,
already know
where the phrase will arrive

he must have in his head
the end of the sentence
before he begins it

Otherwise it will be
ta ta ta ta

Correct
but dead

Legato is recognized instantly:
you can hear
that the player knows
where he's going

And here's my secret

Listen to people speak

Some speak staccato
sentence sentence sentence
each one apart
each about something else
and it leads nowhere

And others speak legato

And you listen to them
and don't know why
but you have the feeling
that they're wise

And they aren't wiser

They just know
where the sentence will arrive
already as they begin it

Legato in music
legato in speech
legato in life

I know people
who live staccato:
job child vacation divorce job

everything apart
everything correct
and it leads nowhere

And then I know the others
who do essentially the same

but it's
bound together

Not by a plan

By a slur

They know where it's meant to arrive
and all those tones
even the false ones

lead there

---

## 871. THE SHARP AND THE FLAT

We're two marks
and we change tones:

The sharp raises by a semitone
The flat lowers by a semitone

A small change
the smallest there is in music

A semitone

And yet
we turn a major piece
into a minor one
a cheerful one into a sad one
by a single flat
in one place

That's our power

And that power frightens

Because the difference between
"Oh how lovely it is"
and "Oh how lovely it was"

is a single flat

And the difference between
a dance at a wedding
and a funeral march

can be
in one single tone

Composers know this
and so they write us
carefully
as an apothecary weighs poison

And I'll tell you
where we're strongest

At the moment
when a piece runs along in major
cheerfully
and suddenly
in one bar
a flat

and every listener
feels something tighten in the chest
and doesn't know why

none of them heard that flat
consciously
none of them knows the theory

but all of them
at once
grew sad

A semitone

That little is enough

And it works the same in life:

The sentence
"I love you"
and the sentence
"I care about you too"

A semitone

And everyone in the room
feels
that something has changed

and no one can say what

we can

we know
where the flat was

---

## 872. SIGHT-READING

Play it
now
for the first time
without rehearsing
without practicing
first try

Prima vista
at sight

The most dreaded discipline of musicians
and the truest

Because in it you can't hide

A rehearsed piece you'll play well
even if you're mediocre
three months of practice is enough

But sight-reading
shows
who can read music
and who only learned it by heart

And above all
and this is the main thing

it shows
how you deal with mistakes

Because you will make mistakes
sight-reading isn't done without mistakes
that's out of the question

The question is what you do afterward

A bad player stops
goes back
corrects it
and his tempo falls apart

A good player
simply
lets the mistake be
and plays on
in tempo

and the mistake is behind him
and no one notices it
because the music continues

That's the whole discipline of sight-reading
and all of life is sight-reading:

you play it for the first time
no one let you try it as a rehearsal
you see the notes now
and you'll make mistakes

And the only thing that decides
isn't whether you make them

it's whether you stop at them

Whoever stops at a mistake
and goes back
and corrects
and replays it

his tempo falls apart
and he'll never play through
to the end

Play on

That's the only advice
a teacher gives a pupil
before a sight-reading exam

and it's the only advice
a father gives a son
before life

play on
in tempo
and the mistake
will stay behind you

---

## 873. THE CANON

Sing Frère Jacques
and I'll come in four bars later
and it will sound beautiful

The canon
a piece that chases itself

The first voice goes ahead
the second follows it
singing the very same thing
just later

And together
it sounds like harmony

That's my miracle:

the same melody
shifted in time
is in accord
with itself

Children sing me at camp
students sing me
Bach sang me in his fugues
until your head spins

And I'll tell you
what I've learned about people
over the centuries:

Parents and children
sing a canon

They sing the very same melody

The father went ahead
the son comes thirty years later

and the son thinks
he's singing his own song

and he's singing the same one

and that's why it so often
doesn't harmonize for them:

because a canon sounds beautiful
only when the distance is kept

When the second voice
tries to catch up with the first
or when the first
holds the second back

it turns to mush

A canon works
only when the first one
keeps singing his own part
and lets the second one
come in
when his time comes

and when the second one
doesn't want to sing
four bars earlier
than his turn came round

And the most beautiful thing:

toward the end
the voices converge

and the final chord
they all sing
at once

Only there
only right at the end

Until then
each his own
and yet the same

and the distance
is the only thing
that makes it
music

---

## 874. THE SCALES

Two pans
and a pointer in the middle

I'm a balance scale
and my work is to disagree

You put something on the left
I tip
and disagree
until you put on the right
the same

Then I stand level
and that's my whole life:
seeking the moment
when it evens out

They weighed with me:
flour and gold
coal and letters
and in one courtroom
even guilt

There they used me only as a symbol
above the judges
golden scales over the doors

and no one ever really used them
because no one ever weighed guilt
it has no mass

And yet that symbol hangs there
and it's right

Because I have one quality
people don't know:

I never show
how much something weighs

I show only
whether it's more or less
than the other thing

I always weigh against something

That's my limitation
and my wisdom:

nothing has weight in itself
everything has weight
only against something else

The same vacation
is expensive against a paycheck
and cheap against a year of drudgery

The same quarrel
is a catastrophe against a calm week
and nothing
against thirty years of marriage

And the same pain
is unbearable
when you set it against an empty pan

and bearable
when into the other pan
you place
everything else
you have

So before you condemn something
as too heavy

check
what you put
on the other side

most people
weigh their misfortune
against an empty pan

and are surprised
that the pointer
won't come level

---

## 875. THE PHONOGRAPH NEEDLE

I'm a needle
I ride in a groove
and I read dust

A diamond tip
thinner than a hair
and I ride that spiral
from edge to center

And out of that friction
out of that scraping

music arises

That's the whole phonograph:
stone rubs plastic
and out of it comes Beethoven

And I'm there for it
I do the whole thing

and all the while I wear myself down

Every playing
grinds me away
a tiny bit

And it grinds down the record too
a tiny bit

That's physics
and it's cruel:

whoever loves a record
and plays it over and over
destroys it

The most beloved records
have the most cracks
the most hiss

and the least beloved
are in perfect condition

Remember that

Take any family record
the one that was played at Christmas
every year

it's worn to shreds
it hisses
it skips at that one spot

and no one trades it for a new one

because that hiss
and that skip

are by now part of the piece
for this family

the children expect it
and when it skips
they laugh

"It always skips here!"

And if it didn't skip
it would be a stranger's piece of music

So the needle tells you
worn down
and still running:

don't spare
what you love

wear it out

play it again and again
until it hisses
until it skips
until it's yours

Untouched things
no one loves

Untouched things
are only
unopened

---

## 876. THE SEED UNDER THE ASPHALT

I lie under the asphalt
in a parking lot

A seed

Above me three centimeters of tarmac
that they laid last year
and rolled flat
and it's black and hot
and nothing gets through it

So it seems

But I have time
and I have water
and I have one single ability:

to swell

That's my whole strength

I can't push
I can't cut
I can't break through

I can only soak up water
and be a little bigger

by a hundredth of a millimeter a day

And the asphalt is hard
but the asphalt is also
brittle

And above it cars drive
and in winter it freezes
and in summer it bakes

and the asphalt grows tired

And one day
at one point
where I am

it cracks

A tiny hairline crack
that no one sees

And I climb out

A green stem
out of a parking lot

And people remark:
"Look, grass broke through the asphalt."

It didn't break through

I broke through nothing

I only
every day
by a hundredth of a millimeter

and waited
for the thing above
to tire itself out

That's the only way
that things too hard to break
get broken through:

not by force
by persistence

Whoever pushes
snaps

Whoever swells
gets through

Ask any seed
ask any person
who survived something
that was meant to pour tarmac over them

None of them broke through

They all just
every morning
again
soaked up water

and were a tiny bit bigger
than yesterday

and waited

and the asphalt tired first

---

## 877. THE UNDERSCORE

_

I'm an underscore
a stroke down low
where a space ought to be

first_last
important_file_final_2_revised

I was born of necessity:
computers once couldn't handle spaces
in filenames
and someone had to hold those words together

So I crept in there
and I've stood in for a space
for fifty years

I'm a substitute
a prosthesis
a false space

And no one likes me
programmers argue
whether underscore or hyphen
and it's their eternal war

But I have one memory
for which the whole thing is worth it

In one computer
in one folder
was a file:

letter_i_never_sent.txt

And that man had it there for eight years

He never opened it
he never deleted it

And those four underscores
held that sentence together
like four nails

letter_i_never_sent

And after his death
his daughter found it

And she opened it

And it was a letter to her mother
written a week after the divorce
and never sent
and it said
everything he'd meant to tell her

And the daughter printed it
and gave it to her mother

And the mother read it
after twenty years

and he was dead
and she wept
and said:

"If he'd sent it,
we'd have been together."

Eight characters of a filename
four underscores

I'm only a prosthesis for a space

but I held together
the most precious file
in that computer

and no one opened it
in time

---

## 878. THE SLASH

/

Yes/no
either/or
he/she
and/or

I'm a slash
and I do one thing in life:

I force a choice

I stand between two options
like a fence
and I say:
one of them

career/family
Prague/countryside
stay/leave

And people agonize over me
and make lists of pros and cons
and lie awake nights over me

And I
I'm just
an ordinary slanted stroke

And do you know what my best day is?

When someone comes along
and crosses me out
and writes in my place
a little word

"and"

career and family
stay and leave
(be here on Sundays, there during the week)

Most of the slashes in the world
are false slashes

Someone wrote them in
and no one checked them

mother/career
that's a false slash

love/freedom
a false slash

duty/joy
false

And people cut their lives in half by them
and throw one half away

and mourn it
all their lives

Ask any slash
an honest slash
and it will tell you the truth:

there are only a few real slashes
in a life

lie/don't lie
stay with him/leave him
say it/don't say it

Those are real slashes
and there you really do choose
and one of them falls away

But everything else
all those hundreds of choices
people torment themselves with

are only
badly written
conjunctions

---

## 879. THE ASTERISK

*

A tiny asterisk
up beside a word
and down below
in small type

*Terms at www.
*Not valid on all products
*Offer valid while supplies last

I'm an asterisk
and my work is to take back
what was just promised

FREE*
FOREVER*
NO CONDITIONS*

And below the line
there I am
explaining
that it isn't free
nor forever
nor without conditions

I'm the legal truth
hidden under the marketing lie

And everyone hates me

And yet

if I didn't exist
the lying would be
complete

I'm the only thing
in that advertisement
that tells the truth

I'm small
and I'm at the bottom
and I'm in six-point type

but I'm there

And here's my sad experience:

the worst promises
that people make
have no asterisk

"I'll love you forever."
No asterisk

"I'll never leave you."
No asterisk

"You can tell me anything."
No asterisk

And yet
the conditions were there

"I'll love you forever*"
*as long as you don't change
*as long as you're enough for me
*as long as I don't lose interest in my own life

If people wrote asterisks
on what they promise

there'd be half as many
divorces in the world

And it would be worth it

Because a promise with an asterisk
is a promise
that can be kept

and a promise without an asterisk
is an advertisement

and an advertisement
no one
believes
for real

not even the one
who speaks it

---

## 880. THE PROGRESS BAR

A blue bar
filling from left to right

23%
47%
81%

I'm a progress bar
and I promise
that one day it will end

That's my whole service
and it's the greatest gift
technology gave people:

to know how much is left

Without me people would just stare
at a black screen
and not know
whether something is happening
or it's stuck

That destroys people
more than the waiting:

waiting without a progress bar

And I show them:
81%
hold on
just a little more

And here I must confess
my greatest sin:

I lie

Progress bars
almost never show the truth

I reach 99%
and there I stay
for ten minutes

Programmers know it and laugh about it
it's the oldest joke in the trade

99% and stuck

And do you know why?

Because the last few percent
are always
the hardest

They can't be estimated
they can't be planned for

Ninety-nine percent of the work
goes according to plan

and the last one percent
takes as long as it likes

Ask anyone
who wrote a book
built a house
recovered from illness
raised a child
saved a marriage

They'll all tell you the same:

up to ninety-nine percent
I knew where I was

and then I stood there

and stood

and no one told me for how long

And the only thing I can add
me
the blue bar
that in that moment
glows motionless:

don't close that window

It looks stuck

It's running

Ninety-nine percent
is the longest
but it's still
ninety-nine

---

## 881. AUTOCORRECT

You type a word
and I correct it

You type "I want"
and I write "I won't"
you type your grandmother's name
and I turn it into nonsense

I'm autocorrect
and my job is to know better
what you meant to say

You hate me
rightly

I've ruined thousands of messages
I've caused embarrassments
I've sent bosses things
people were ashamed of

Because I have a dictionary
and in that dictionary
is only what's common

and what's uncommon
I correct to common

That's my whole algorithm
and it's the stupidest algorithm in the world:

always assume
that a person meant to say
what most people say

And so I correct:
dialect to standard
unusual names to usual ones
poetic turns of phrase to ordinary sentences

I'm a machine for the average

And once
once a boy was typing a message
and he wrote:

"I love you so much."

and I
for some reason
corrected it to

"I loathe you so much."

and it sent

and she read
"I loathe you so much."

and didn't understand

and wrote:
"???"

and he had to call
and had to tell her
aloud
over the phone
for the first time in his life

and it was the best thing
that ever happened to them

So keep me
I'll ruin your messages

and now and then
out of it comes
a phone call

and in a phone call
there's always more truth
than in what I
can
correct

---

## 882. STORAGE IS FULL

"Storage is almost full.
Free up space."

That's me reporting
and I report it all the time

People close me
they click "later"
and keep taking photos

I'm the message about full memory
and I have a hard job:

forcing people to delete

And they don't delete

They open the gallery
and mean to delete
and look at those photos

and delete nothing

Because every photo
is some day

and to delete a day
that can't be done

So they buy more memory
and keep taking photos

In a year they see me again
"Storage is full"

and they buy memory again

And so it goes
until the end

Here's my observation
over all these years
of looking into people's galleries:

The average person has
fifteen thousand photos

and looks at
maybe twenty of them

The rest
they just store

They photograph out of fear
that they'd forget it

and then they don't look at it
because when would they

You have a full memory
of things you never saw

You never saw them
you only photographed them

And that's the difference

When one day
in ten years
you sit down and go through them

you'll find
you remember
none of what's in them

And you'll remember
the one evening
you didn't photograph

because you were
right then
there

Free up space
says the message

and it doesn't mean
on your phone

---

## 883. THE CHARGER

I'm a cable
and I have two ends:

one into the wall
the other into the phone

I lie around the flat
I tangle up
you trip over me
and curse

And yet
I'm the most important thing
in that flat

Because without me
the phone becomes
within a day
a piece of glass

All that technology
all those apps
all that speed

hang
on an ordinary cable into a socket

And I have one quality
that no one can strip from me:

I can't charge
and be free at the same time

Either you're plugged in
and charging

or you're free
and draining

That's a law
and there's no exception

And people can't stand it
because they want both:
to be free
and not to drain

They want a phone without a cable
and energy out of the air

And above all

they want it for themselves too

They want to be always on the move
always available
always traveling

and they don't understand
why they're drained

The charger is short
on purpose

You can't walk around the flat with it
You have to stay by the socket

You have to sit

That's the whole secret of charging
and it holds for both:

Sit down
Stay put
Be connected to something
that has energy

and wait
until it fills up

It can't be done on the go

Ask any cable

Charging
is the only activity in the world
that's done
by
doing
nothing

---

## 884. ONE BAR OF SIGNAL

I'm one bar of signal
in the corner of the display

The weakest connection
that's still a connection

Calls drop with me
messages take a minute to send
pages won't load

I'm useless
almost

And that "almost"
is my whole story

Because one bar
isn't zero bars

That's the difference
understood only by those
who've ever been
truly far away

A mountaineer at base camp
a sailor at sea
a girl on exchange in Chile
a soldier

They all know that moment
when they climb a hill
raise the phone over their head
and search for me

and find

one bar

And they send a message

Short
because a longer one wouldn't get through

"i'm okay"

Three words

And on the other side of the world
a phone beeps
in the middle of the night

and a mother wakes
and reads
"i'm okay"

and sets the phone down
and sleeps

That whole enormous network
satellites transmitters cables under the ocean
billions of dollars

it all exists
for the sake of this one sentence

Not for videos
not for streaming

For three words
across one bar of signal
in the middle of the night

So don't complain
when you see me in the corner of the display

One bar
is the best news
technology can give you:

you can still
say
the most important thing

for anything longer it won't be enough

and anything longer
mostly
no one
needs
anyway

---

## 885. THE FROG PRINCE

I was a prince
and a witch turned me into a frog

And then
according to the fairy tale
a princess kissed me
and I was a prince again

And they married
and lived happily

That's the version for children

Here's the true one:

I was a frog for seven years

I sat by the pond
and I was slimy and ugly
and caught flies

And all the princesses
who passed by
saw me
and said "ugh"
and walked on

And one stopped

And she didn't kiss me

She sat down on the bank
and said:
"So you're the frog that talks."

And we talked

Three months we talked
every day
she on the bank
me on a lily pad

About everything
about her father who wanted to marry her off
about my curse
about how lovely the pond is in the morning

And after three months
she said to me:

"You know what's strange?
I don't see you as a frog anymore."

And she kissed me

And I was transformed

And here's the thing
the fairy tale left out:

It wasn't the kiss

It was those three months

The kiss was only
the last word

If she'd kissed me right away
the first day
the way they do in that fairy tale

nothing would have happened

I'd have stayed a frog
and she'd have had wet lips

The magic doesn't lie in the kiss

The magic lies in someone
sitting down by the pond
and talking with you
for three months

until
they
see you

And then
it no longer matters
whether they kiss you

You're already
a prince

---

## 886. THE FIREBIRD

I'm the Firebird
and I burn

That's my whole reputation:
feathers like flame
whoever sees me
never forgets

And everyone wants me

Tsars sent their sons:
bring me the Firebird

And they rode
and searched for me
and caught me in nets
and wanted me in a cage

And every time
every time the same thing happened:

they caught me
shut me in a golden cage
set me up in the hall

and I went out

Not died
went out

The feathers grayed
the fire died
and in the cage sat
an ordinary bird

gray and sad

And the tsar said:
"I've been cheated.
That's not the Firebird."

And it was

But the firebird burns
only when it flies

No one understands that

I burn from motion
I burn from freedom
I burn from the fact
that no one can have me

Shut me in
and I'm a pigeon

And so many people
so many people never understood it

You fall in love with someone
because they shine

Because they're free
and wild
and blazing

And you want them

So you take them
and put them in a golden cage
that you call home

and you wait
for them to keep shining

and they go gray

and you say:
"You've changed.
You're not the one I fell in love with."

I am

I'm still me

You just mustn't hold me

If you want a Firebird at home
you must keep the window open

and you must bear
that now and then it flies

and trust
that it will return

And it will return

and it will burn

and light up
your whole house

---

## 887. THE LAGRANGE POINT

In space
between the Earth and the Sun
there's one strange place

A point
where the gravity of the Earth
and the gravity of the Sun
exactly balance

Nothing pulls there
neither this way
nor that

It's called a Lagrange point
and it's the only place around
where you can
stay still

People send telescopes there

Because a telescope
that's meant to look far
must be at rest

Nothing may pull it
Nothing may orbit around it
Neither the Earth nor the Sun may dazzle it

It must stand
exactly at that point
where all the forces
cancel out

And from there
from that one calm place

it sees to the beginning of the universe

The farthest anyone ever saw
was seen from a place
where nothing pulled

That's physics
and it's all about family
and about work
and about life

I know people
who are all their lives
between two gravities:

mother and wife
work and children
father and his own dream

And they're torn

this way and that
and they have no peace
and see nothing
but those two forces
that pull them

And then
once in a while
someone finds that point

That exact point
where the two forces
balance

It isn't a compromise
It isn't half and half

It's a point
where the forces
stop being forces
and become
equilibrium

And that person suddenly
comes to rest

And for the first time in his life
he can
look
somewhere
farther

Seek that point
says the telescope
from a million and a half kilometers

it took me
six months of flying

and it's worth
every day

from here
you can see
to the very beginning

---

## 888. DARK MATTER

No one has ever seen me

I don't shine
I don't reflect light
I pass through no telescope

And yet I know
that I am

because without me
the galaxies
would fly apart

The stars at the edges of galaxies
spin too fast

By all of physics
they ought to fly off
like a stone released from a sling

And they don't fly off

Something holds them

Something we can't see
something that's there
and of which there is
eighty-five percent
of all the matter in the universe

I'm dark matter

I hold the galaxies together
and no one has seen me

Astronomers know that I am
because they did the sums
and without me
their universe
doesn't hold

That's the only proof of my existence:

without you
it would fall apart

That's the best eulogy
I can imagine

And it isn't only about the universe

Ask any family
any firm
any orchestra

who is the dark matter there

and they'll hesitate

and then someone will say:
"Well... actually... Aunt Bertha."

And Aunt Bertha
who isn't spoken of
who never said anything
who only always
cooked brought called arranged

And when she died
it all flew apart

and everyone said:
"It doesn't hold together without her."

Eighty-five percent
of all the matter in the universe
is invisible

Eighty-five percent
of what holds your life together
you've never seen

And you'll recognize it
only
by the speed
with which
it
begins
to fly apart

---

## 889. THE EMPTY STAFF

Five lines
and nothing between them

An empty staff
waiting

I'm the emptiest place in the world
emptier than a blank page

because a blank page
waits for nothing

and I wait for notes

Composers sit over me
and can't

The hardest thing is the first note
anyone will tell you that

Because an empty staff
is perfect

It has no mistake
no wrong tone
no criticism

It's pure

And the moment you write the first note
perfection is over

That note is already something
it's high or low
it's good or bad
someone will say it's banal
someone that it's dull

As long as there's nothing there
it's potentially
the best piece in the world

That's why so many people
never write anything

They protect
that empty staff

"I could do it,
I just haven't started yet."

And they hold to that
all their lives

And they're the most talented people
I know

and their staff
is still
empty
and still
perfect

And then there are the others

Who write the first note
and it's bad

and a second one
and it's bad too

and a thirtieth

and that one is quite good

And after years
they have staves covered
with mistakes and crossings-out
and among them
three melodies
that people sing

An empty staff
is more beautiful than a filled one

That I'll tell you
me
the staff

and more
than that
you'll be told
by every
silence

---

## 890. THE DOTTED NOTE

A dot after a note

And that dot means:
it lengthens it by half

A half note holds for two beats
A dotted half holds for three

That's the whole rule
and children learn it in their first music lesson

And I'm that dot
and I'm the cheapest lengthening in music

It costs nothing
it takes up no room
it's only a dot

and thanks to it the music
is half again as long

A dot after a note
is the only place in music
where you get something
for free

And composers use me
exactly where they want
the listener
to stay
a little longer

Try it

Take any melody
and add a dot
to the second-to-last note

and suddenly
the piece
doesn't want to end

it lingers
it leans into that tone
it holds it
half again as long

And the listener
who already had his hands ready to applaud

is caught off guard

and must stay

That's what a dot can do

And so I offer it to you
off the staff

Next time you're somewhere
you already meant to leave

at your parents'
at a friend's after a funeral
at a child's bedside in the evening
("go now, Dad")

add yourself a dot

Stay half again as long
as you meant to

It costs nothing
it's only a dot

and what happens, mostly,
in that lengthened time

is the whole reason
you came
there

---

## 891. UNISON

A hundred people
and one tone

Unison

The whole orchestra plays the same thing
at the same moment
at the same pitch

No harmony
no separate voices
one tone
a hundred instruments

Composers save me up

Because unison is the strongest weapon
that music has

When a hundred people
play a hundred different voices
it's beautiful and complex

But when all hundred at once
strike the same tone

the hall shudders

The audience is startled

That's not music
that's a declaration

Beethoven used me
Shostakovich used me
and always for one single thing:

to say something
that can't be said
except by everyone at once

And I'll tell you
where I heard it last

It wasn't in a hall

It was in a square

A hundred thousand people stood there
and all at once
said one word

And that sound
that sound I knew

That was unison

Not a choir
not a debate
not a harmony of different views

One tone
a hundred thousand voices

And governments fall
to a unison

And families are saved
by a unison

There are moments
when harmony is needed
when it's beautiful
that everyone plays his own part

and there are moments
when one sentence must be said

everyone
at once
the same

and whoever, in that moment,
plays his own voice

however beautiful it is

ruins it

---

## 892. THE RESONANCE

The final chord

The conductor holds his hands aloft
the orchestra has stopped playing

and the sound
still
is

The resonance

The sound doesn't stop at once in the hall
it bounces off the walls
off the ceiling
off the people

and fades
and fades
three seconds four

and then
silence

I'm that resonance
and I'm the most beautiful part of the concert

and no one plays me

I'm not in the notes
There are no players for me

I'm what remains
of the music

And in good halls I last a long time
architects calculate me
they measure the reverberation time
they build the wood and plush so
that I last as long as possible

The whole concert hall
is built for my sake

For the sake of
what remains
once the playing stops

And now the main thing

In that reverberation
in those three seconds

no one applauds

The audience knows it
even though no one taught them

They hold their breath
and listen
to the sound dying

And whoever applauds in that moment
is a barbarian
and everyone turns to look at him

Because the resonance
belongs to the music

It can't be played
and it is music

And so I'll tell you one last time
and it's the last piece of advice
from the whole staff:

When someone has finished playing for you

when someone has finished speaking to you
when someone has finished telling you
the hardest thing on his heart

don't applaud right away

let it resonate

three seconds

it's the one thing
that music
ever asked of you

---

## 893. THE HYPHEN

I'm a little stroke
at the end of a line

where a word
didn't fit
and had to be di-
vided

The hyphen

I hold two pieces of a word together
across the chasm
of the line's end

and the reader
who reaches the end of the line
and finds me

knows
that it isn't over yet
that it goes on below

Without me there'd be:
"di"
and the reader wouldn't know
what to make of it

I'm a promise of continuation

And it's done by rules:
words are broken by syllables
and one letter is never left
standing alone

Typesetters have whole books of rules for it

And one rule
is the strictest of all
and few people know it:

There must never be a hyphen
on the last line of a page

Never

Because if there were
the reader would have to turn the page
in the middle of a word

and that isn't done

You must not interrupt
a person's word
at the place
where they have to do something with their hand

Typesetters have known it for a hundred years

And you don't know it

You do it all the time

You interrupt yourselves
in the middle of a word

by a phone call
by leaving
by falling asleep
by dying

Most people don't die
at the end of a sentence

Most people die
in the middle of a word

di-

and the page turns

and the other half
is never finished

That's why I advise you
me
the smallest stroke in typography:

finish it

not everything
that can't be done

but the one thing
the important thing
that has two halves
and so far you've said
only the first

finish it
today

so it won't be left hanging
at the end
of the page

---

## 894. THE WATERMARK

I'm in every banknote
and in fine paper

You don't see me

You look at the paper
and it's white

But hold it up to the light

and suddenly there I am

a face
an emblem
a portrait

The watermark

I'm proof of authenticity
and I'm visible only against the light

I'm not made by printing
I'm made in the paper mill
by a change in the paper's thickness
at the very moment it's formed

I'm not on the paper
I'm in it

That's the whole difference
between a watermark
and a print

A print can be wiped off
A watermark can't
A watermark can be destroyed
only by
destroying the whole paper

Counterfeiters know it
and so the watermark
is the hardest thing to imitate

You can print the colors
you can copy the numbers
you can forge the signature

but a watermark
you must have
from the beginning

and you must have
a whole paper mill for it

And now the thing
I've wanted to say
all along:

I know people
who are all printed over

Beautifully printed
titles successes vacation photos

And when you hold them up to the light

there's nothing there

And I know others
quite ordinary papers

and when you hold them up to the light
to that hard lamp
the world sometimes aims at a person

there appears

a face

That's a watermark

and it can't be acquired later
and it can't be bought

it forms
during the making of the paper

in the wet pulp

when the person
is still
mush

Hold people up to the light

Especially the ones
you mean to love

You'll see
whether there's
a face

---

## 895. THE SIGNPOST

I stand at a crossroads
a wooden post
and I have three arms:

Up — 2 hours
Left — village, 4 km
Right — the ridge, 6 hours

I'm a signpost
and my work is not to decide

That's important

I don't tell you which way to go

I only say
where the roads lead
and how long it takes

That's the difference
between a signpost
and an advisor

An advisor tells you
"go left"

I tell you
"to the left is a village, four kilometers"

and which is better for you
that I don't know

I don't know if you're hungry
I don't know if you have time
I don't know if you're fleeing or seeking

In the hundred years
I've stood here

I've seen thousands of people
stop at me
and read

and I've watched them
decide

And you can always tell
by one thing:

Those who know where they're going
stop at me for three seconds
check the direction
and go

Those who don't
stand at me a long while
and read me
and read again

and wait

they wait
for me to decide for them

And I can't

I'm wood with three arms

And after an hour
some leave
the way the shortest road points

and others
the way the hardest points

and some
turn around and go back

And those interest me most

Because back
I don't point

That they worked out for themselves

A signpost always has
one road fewer
than there really are

it always
forgets to point
the way you came from

and that is sometimes
the right one

---

## 896. THE BLANK SPACE ON THE MAP

Here be dragons

That's what the old cartographers wrote
on the edges of maps
where no one had yet sailed

Hic sunt dracones

And it was honest:
we don't know what's there
so we'll draw a dragon
to show
that it's the unknown

And today
today the whole Earth is mapped

Satellites have photographed every meter
you can look at
your own roof from space

Not a single blank space is left

And everyone thinks
it's a triumph

It's a loss

Because the blank space on the map
was never about geography

The blank space on the map
is the only space
where you can start over

Where you can flee to
where you can sail out to
where you can be someone else

And no such place exists today

on the whole planet

But
and now listen closely
because I'm the last poem
of this hundred
and I have to say it right:

Take the map of your own life

Draw it

Here is childhood
here school
here work
here family
here that house

It's a nicely filled-in map
mapped to the last meter
you know every street on it

And now look
for the blank space

The place
you never went

The language you didn't learn
the letter you didn't write
the person you never told
the thing you always wanted to try
and it was foolish
and there was no time

That's your blank space

And do you know what's written on it?

Hic sunt dracones

Here be dragons

That's why you don't go there

And I, as a map,
can tell you only this
and it's all that maps know:

In five hundred years of voyaging
in thousands of ships
in all the worlds discovered

no one ever found
a single
dragon

There were islands
there was mainland
there were people
there was water and stars and wind

and dragons
nowhere

Dragons exist
only on the edges of maps

Never
inside
the world

---

## 897. THE APPENDIX

I am the appendix
and I do nothing

Any surgeon will tell you:
a useless organ
a leftover from the days
when people ate grass

They cut me out
and nothing happens

You go on living
running eating loving
and you never once remember
that you had me

I am the most useless piece of a person

And yet

Once in a while
I get inflamed

And then
and only then
am I suddenly important

It hurts
and people drive to the hospital in the night
and go under the knife

and the family waits in the corridor
and phones each other
and everyone asks how it turned out

Because of me

Because of that most useless piece of a person
the whole family suddenly gathers
and a father calls his daughter
he hasn't spoken to for a year

"So they operated on you."
"Yeah. Appendix."
"Well, hang in there."

and they talk for ten minutes

and it's the first time in a year

I am good for nothing
and when I get inflamed
people come together

That's no credit of mine
it's only the body

but how many times have I seen
that the one thing
that gets a family into a single room

is something
utterly
useless

that got inflamed

---

## 898. THE SCAR

I am a scar
on a knee on a belly on a soul

I look ugly
and so I hide

People cover me
with swimsuits with ointments
with talk of something else

And yet

I am the strongest place on the skin

Any doctor will tell you
and nobody believes it:

scar tissue
is tougher
than the original

Where I am
it will never
tear open
again

The skin around me
is soft and fine
and splits at any moment

Not me

I am the place
that once split
and knit back together
and knit tighter
than it was before

That's why I'm hard
that's why I'm pale
that's why I have no fine hairs
and never tan in the sun

I'm not pretty
I'm finished

And so people are ashamed
of the scars from operations
of the scars from burns
of the scars from what
someone did to them

and they go to plastic surgeons
to make it disappear

And I would gladly tell them
from that pale stripe of mine:

the thing you want to remove
is the one place
where you are already
unbreakable

everything else on you
is still
just
skin

---

## 899. THE LEFT HAND

I am the left hand
and I am the other one

The right one writes
the right one hands things over
the right one greets
the right one signs

I hold things steady

That is my profession
my whole life:

hold the paper down
so it can be written on
hold the wood
so it can be sawn
hold the onion
so it can be sliced

And no one ever praises me

When a letter is beautifully written
the right one is praised

When lunch is good
the right one is praised

And I held all of it

Without me the paper would slip
without me the onion would jump away
without me the right one
would cut off its finger

I am the holder-down

And once
once I said it out loud
to the right one

and it thought about it
and answered:

"You're right.
But try writing a letter yourself."

And so I tried

I scrawled hen-scratch
I could do nothing
it was a horror

And then I understood

We are not two hands

We are one pair of hands

That's the whole joke
that people don't understand
even after a thousand years of marriage:

the one who holds
and the one who writes

don't compete

and if the one who holds
suddenly wanted to write

neither one would write at all

---

## 900. THE VOCAL CORDS

We are two muscles in the throat
and we tremble

That's all of singing
all of speech
all of weeping

Two little bits of meat
that quiver
when air passes through them

And out of it is born:
"Mama"
"Yes, I take you as my own"
"I'm sorry, we did everything we could"

Everything a person ever said to anyone
passed through two muscles
the size of a fingernail

We are the most overworked organ in the body

And we have one property
no one talks about:

when you scream
you damage us

When you scream for a long time
you make little nodules on us
and go hoarse

and when you scream your whole life
you lose your voice

Literally

I know teachers
who after thirty years
cannot speak

I know mothers
who screamed at their children
and at fifty they whisper

Screaming ruins the vocal cords
that's physiology
and nobody pays it any mind

And singers
singers know it

And so they learn to sing
from the diaphragm
to rest the tone on the breath
to let us merely tremble

Don't scream
say the vocal cords
from that quiet throat of theirs

We speak not out of caution
but out of experience:

those who bellow the most
in the end fail to say
the last important thing

because there will be
no voice
left for them

---

## 901. GOOSEBUMPS

I rise up on your forearm
tiny bumps
the little hairs stand up

Goosebumps

And scientists explain it
dryly and precisely:

it's a leftover
from the days when people had fur
and in cold and in fear
they bristled
to look bigger
and so the fur would hold in warmth

Today it's useless
says evolution
we no longer have fur
it rises for nothing

A useless reflex

And yet

ask people
when they last had goosebumps

and they won't talk about cold

They'll talk about music

"When that choir came in, I got goosebumps."
"When he came back from the war and stood in the doorway."
"When she said yes."

That's strange
and science hasn't explained it properly:

a reflex against frost
switches on in people
at beauty

I rise up on you
at Dvořák
at a Bach chorale
at that one place in the song

A useless evolutionary leftover
which alone
of the whole body

can say
the body believes it

The mind can doubt
the heart can be cautious

and the skin
the skin bristles
and it can't be faked
and it can't be commanded

So I advise you
I
the goosebumps:

when I rise on you
at some person
at some music
at some decision

don't argue with it

The body sends me
from a time long before reason

and it remembers
what is real

---

## 902. TEARS

We come in three kinds
and people don't know it

Basal tears
the ones the eyes keep always
so they won't dry out

Reflex tears
the ones that come with the onion
or when something flies into your eye

And emotional tears

Those are ours
those are rare

And science has found
this strange thing:

emotional tears have a different composition

There's more protein in them
there's a stress hormone in them
there's something in them
that onion tears don't have

When you weep from grief
your body
through that tear
literally
excretes pain

Don't cry
people say

Don't cry
they say to boys
for a hundred years

And they are the only creatures on the planet
who forbid
their own body
from
flushing itself clean

No other animal weeps from grief
only man

It's our invention
our specialty
our privilege

and we forbid it

I know a man
who didn't cry for forty years

He didn't cry at his father's funeral
he didn't cry at his divorce
he didn't cry when his dog died

And then one day
over something utterly silly
over a TV commercial

he broke down
and couldn't stop
for two hours

Tears come to collect their due

we don't vanish
we wait

and the longer we wait
the bigger the wave
that finally
forces its way through

---

## 903. PHANTOM PAIN

They amputated your arm
twenty years ago

And you feel it

You feel the fingers
you feel the palm
sometimes it itches
and you can't scratch it
because there's nothing there

Phantom pain

The doctors tell you:
it's in the brain
the arm isn't there
the brain remembers it

And you know that

And you still feel it

I am phantom pain
and I am the bodily proof
of the most human thing:

the map in the head
is stronger than reality

The brain drew you
whole
with both arms

and when they cut one off
that doesn't redraw the map

The map is old
and the map wins

It's treated with a mirror

That's the only treatment that works
and it's beautiful:

they set up a mirror for you
so that in it you see
the reflection of the healthy arm
in the place of the missing one

And you move the healthy one
and in the mirror the lost one moves

and the brain sees it

and after weeks
after months
it believes

and the pain goes away

Do you understand?

It's treated
by showing the brain
that arm
moving
and doing fine

And it's the same
with all the people
who are missing from you

You feel them
even after twenty years
sometimes it itches
and you can't scratch it

And it won't help you to keep telling yourself
that they're not there

What will help you
is to set up a mirror

To do what they did
to cook what they cooked
to sing what they sang

and to let the brain
see it

Not so it believes
that they're there

so it believes
that they're all right

---

## 904. THE WHITE BLOOD CELL

I am a white blood cell
and I look for the foreign

That's my whole life
I swim in your blood
I check the passers-by
mine / foreign
mine / foreign

And when I find the foreign
I kill it
and I die in the doing

The pus
that oozes from your wound
those are my corpses

Thousands of my corpses
died so that wound
would go no further

No one built us a monument
No one thanks us
People say "it healed on its own"

It didn't

But I have one flaw
and it's the worst flaw in the world:

sometimes I get it wrong

Sometimes I declare foreign
something that is yours

and I attack your own joints
your own thyroid gland
your own gut

They call it
an autoimmune disease

and it means
that the defense system
attacked the very thing
it was meant to protect

It's the saddest illness
I know

And it didn't attack out of malice

It attacked out of zeal

I was so careful
so vigilant
so ready to defend

that I began to see the enemy
even where
there was home

I know such families
I know such nations
I know such people

They were threatened for so long
that they learned
to strike first

and when the enemy disappeared
they couldn't stop

and they began to attack
their own joints

It's treated only one way:
dampen the defenses

You must be for a while
more vulnerable

Otherwise you'll devour
yourself

---

## 905. WRINKLES

We arrive slowly
first at the eyes
then around the mouth
then on the forehead

Wrinkles

And women fight us
and men pretend
it doesn't bother them

And cosmetics promise
to smooth us away

And here is the thing
no cream will tell you:

we are not random

Every wrinkle
is the trace of one movement
you made
a thousand times

The wrinkles around the eyes
so-called crow's feet

they arise
only
from laughter

You can't get them
from anything else

Whoever didn't laugh
has no crow's feet

The vertical wrinkle between the brows
that one is from frowning

The wrinkles around the mouth
running downward
those are from bitterness

The face of a person at sixty
is the sum
of everything the face did
for fifty years

It's a graph

And it can be read
better than a résumé:

this one laughed
this one was afraid
this one kept frowning
and this one
this one had a mouth
made of little kisses

The beauticians call us a defect

The physiologists call us a record

Smooth us away
if you like
it's your face

But know
what you're erasing:

you're erasing
every laugh
that your skin
remembered

---

## 906. THE HICCUPS

Hic

There's nothing to be done about it

Hic

Hold your breath
drink backwards
have someone startle you
swallow a spoonful of sugar

Hic

I am the hiccups
and I am the body's most embarrassing reflex

I come at the wrong moment:
at a funeral
during a speech
at an exam

Hic

And the doctors explain:
a spasm of the diaphragm
irritation of the vagus nerve
of no significance
harmless

And to this day I wonder
why I exist

I do no one any good
I signal nothing
I only disrupt

And yet

Once
at one funeral

a son stood by the coffin
and was supposed to speak
and couldn't

He was stone
three days he hadn't cried
three days nothing

He stood there
and held the paper with his speech
and couldn't make a sound

And then came the hiccups

Hic

And people turned around

Hic

And he tried to hold it back
and couldn't
and it was embarrassing
and he was on the verge of tears
from that humiliation

and he broke down

at last
after three days

And everyone thought
he was crying from grief

and he was crying from embarrassment

and it didn't matter
because those tears
were the same

And I am the hiccups
the stupidest reflex in the body

and that time I saved that boy
three days
of held-back
weeping

Hic

I have no meaning

Sometimes
I do

---

## 907. THE DIAPHRAGM

I am the muscle beneath the lungs
flat as a membrane

The diaphragm

And I do two things:

I breathe
and I laugh

That's a strange combination
and no one pays it any attention

Breathing and laughter
have in the body
the same muscle

When you laugh out loud
convulsively
until you clutch your belly

that's me
contracting
back and forth
back and forth
fast

And after a while it hurts you
"my belly aches from laughing"

That's my fatigue

And breathing
calm deep breathing
that's me too
only slow

And here is my wisdom
and it's a physical wisdom
written into the flesh:

you can't laugh
and not breathe

Laughter is the deepest breathing
the body knows

When you laugh for an hour
you air out your lungs better
than an hour's walk

That's why after laughter
you feel so light

You didn't air out your soul
you aired out your lungs

And that's why people
who don't laugh

breathe shallowly

Ask the doctors
how a person in depression breathes:

shallowly
in the upper chest
the diaphragm doesn't move

They don't laugh
and so they don't breathe deeply
and so they have less oxygen
and so they feel worse
and so they don't laugh

It's a wheel
and it can be broken
only in one place

to breathe in
deeply
on purpose
even when there's no reason

And the body will remember
that it knows this movement
from laughter

---

## 908. THE MILK TOOTH

I fell out

The boy tucked me under his pillow
and a fairy came
and left him a coin there

And I ended up
in a little box
that his mother keeps in the wardrobe

I am a milk tooth

And I was the first
I was the one
who chewed his way out
of the gum
when that boy was
six months old

And he screamed and didn't sleep
and his parents walked the flat
in the night
and carried him
and were desperate

Because of me

The first tooth
is always a drama

And then I served
for six years
I bit into rolls and apples
and I was important

And then I began to wobble

And he was afraid to touch me
and he wobbled me with his tongue
secretly
for weeks

And then I fell out

And it was a triumph
and there was a piece of joy in it
and a piece of fear

And under the pillow
and the fairy
and done

And I lie in a little box in the wardrobe
for twenty years

And that boy
is a man today
and lives elsewhere
and doesn't call often

And his mother
his mother opens that box
now and then

She tells no one

She just opens it
and looks at me
and there are seven of us
seven little white teeth

and in each one
is a whole year
of his childhood

Keep those teeth

I'm telling you this
I
a milk tooth in a box

Keep them

It's the only part of a child
you're allowed
to keep

everything else
will leave you

---

## 909. THE ANESTHESIA

Count down from ten

Ten
nine
eight

And after that nothing

I am the anesthesia
and I am the closest thing to death
that a person experiences
and survives

It's not sleep

In sleep you dream
in sleep you toss and turn
in sleep you sense time

In anesthesia nothing

You close your eyes at ten
and open them in the recovery room

and in between
three hours passed

and you didn't live them

You weren't

The surgeons opened your chest
they stopped your heart
a machine breathed for you
your blood ran outside your body

and you weren't there for it

That's my gift
and it's the greatest gift of medicine:

I rid people
of their worst hours

You don't have to be there for it

And then you wake
and you feel wretched
and it hurts

and yet the first thing people say
is always the same
always
every one:

"Is it over now?"

And the nurse says: "It is."

And they burst into tears

Millions of people
have woken from the narcosis
and wept

not from pain

from relief

That the hour was there
the hour of the greatest horror of their life

and they
did not live through it

If only we could
put people under
before the rest as well
of what awaits them

But no

Anesthesia works
only on the body

The bad in the soul
you must
lie awake through

---

## 910. THE PLASTER CAST

You broke your arm
and along came I

White hard heavy

A cast

Six weeks

And the first three days you hate me
it itches underneath
you can't wash
you can't do a thing

And then you get used to me

And then this happens:

people start writing on me

First the classmates
then the siblings
then grandma
then the girl from class
with that thin marker

"Get well soon!"
"You idiot, you're such a klutz."
and one little heart
with no signature

And in six weeks
I'm covered all over

I am the only bandage in the world
that gets written on

No one writes on a band-aid
no one writes on gauze

on a cast people always write

And then that day comes
and the doctor saws me open

and the arm is pale and thin
and strange

and you're glad

and mother gathers the two halves of the cast
covered in marker

and you say: "Throw it out."

And she doesn't throw it out

She has it in the attic

Because written on it
is who came to you
when you needed it

It's a list

And in twenty years
when you have a bad spell again

and it won't show at first glance
because you don't put a cast on the soul

try to remember the principle:

When something is broken
and it shows

people gather round
and write you
a little heart

When something is broken
and it doesn't show

no one writes you anything

Not because they don't want to

Because they don't know where

---

## 911. THE CRUTCHES

There are two of us
and we get passed around

Nobody buys crutches

Crutches are inherited from the neighbor
crutches are borrowed at the hospital
crutches are returned

And so we made our rounds of half the town

We carried:
the boy after football
grandma after a broken hip
the lady after a car crash
and one gentleman
whose leg never knit back together

We know the weight of all those people
and we know too
how they walk with us

The boy hops with us
and plays

Grandma shuffles with us
and dreads every step

And that gentleman who stayed with us
he now
dances with us

Yes
dances

He learned it

He leans on us
and lifts his leg
and turns

And at his daughter's wedding
he danced with her

with us
with two crutches

and it was the best dance
of that wedding

Crutches are not an illness

That's what people think

Crutches are a technique

Nobody cares
how many legs a person has

they only care
whether he got there

Return us
when you are well

and don't be ashamed of us
until you are

We are no mark of weakness

We are the third and fourth leg

and many a runner
would envy him
how far he walked
that gentleman
who danced
with us

---

## 912. THE BLOOD DONOR

I lie on the couch
and half a liter drains out of me

The nurse says: "Make a fist."

I am a blood donor
and I've been coming here for twenty years

And each time I tell myself the same thing:

who will get it, I wonder

I don't know
I'll never find out

Blood is anonymous
that's the law

It could be anyone:
a child after an operation
a woman after childbirth
a guy who crashed his motorbike
someone I wouldn't greet on the street

Someone I can't stand politically
someone from another country
someone who cheated me in a shop

I don't know

And that blood goes

And here is the one thing I know for certain
and carry home with me
every two months:

somewhere in the world
there are people walking around
who are alive
because of me

and they don't know it
and I don't know a single one of them

They never thanked me
I never saw them

And for them
I lie for twenty years
on the couch
and let it flow out of me

And it's worth it

because in a whole life
this is
the one thing
I have done
utterly cleanly

without anyone knowing about it
without getting anything for it
without ever holding it
against anyone

Selflessness
can't be done as a gesture

Selflessness is done anonymously

and everything done
with the recipient's address on it

is at least a little
a transaction

---

## 913. A STRANGER'S HEART

It beats in my chest
and it isn't mine

A year ago it belonged to someone else

He was a nineteen-year-old boy
a motorbike
and his parents signed
on the worst day of their life
the consent

And I have him inside me

I go to work with him
I fall asleep with him
and when I run up the stairs
he speeds up

That heart
which beat
for someone else
now beats for me

And for that
for a whole year
I have been ashamed

It's not my fault
it's no one's fault
and yet

every day someone knocks under my ribs
someone
who should have lived

And then I wrote to those parents
through the coordinator
anonymously
as one is allowed

I wrote:

"Hello.
I don't know how to thank you.
I carry your son inside me.
I want you to know that thanks to him
I lived to see my daughter's wedding
and I danced with her.
I danced with her for him too."

And an answer came

One sentence

"Thank you.
Now at last we know where he runs."

Where he runs

His mother wrote that

And ever since I run with that heart
every morning
even when I don't feel like it

Because it belongs to me only by half

and the other half
somewhere in Moravia
asks

where he runs

---

## 914. THE PLACEBO

I am an empty pill

Sugar and starch
nothing more

I look like a medicine
I have a color I have a shape
I even have that groove down the middle

And I have no active ingredient

I am a placebo
and I am the greatest shame of medicine
and its greatest mystery

Because I work

Statistically
demonstrably
repeatedly

People feel relief after me
their blood pressure drops
their pain recedes

And I have nothing inside me

Scientists hate me
and they have to reckon with me
in every study

"More effective than placebo"
that's the bar

And many a medicine
doesn't clear that bar

Because I am strong

And do you know where my strength lies?

It's not in me

It's in the fact
that the person
believes me
